With Srila Prabhupada in early days is a collection of letters written to Satsvarupa Dasa Goswami by His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada and his commentary upon them. This book, volume 1 in a series, discusses the letters he received between 1966-1968. Because Srila Prabhupada was always present with his disciples at 26 Second Avenue in 1966, his letters don’t actually begin until 1967. Therefore, he has simply told some highlights from their service in separation, and they begin (as a letter written to all his New York disciples) on January 19, 1967, after he had left for San Francisco.

# SP in the Early Days

## Dedication

To my disciples that they may gain further inspiration to become fixed in Kṛṣṇa consciousness and serving the order of their spiritual master in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

## 1: Meeting Śrīla Prabhupāda

Śrīla Prabhupāda had a process for taking people out of material consciousness and making them servants of Kṛṣṇa. I am one on whom his process worked. By evoking his life and activities, I would like to describe how I came to him in the summer of 1966.

I was born in a second-generation American family. My father was born of Italian immigrant parents, my mother of an Irish immigrant family—both Roman Catholic. My father was a fireman for the New York City Fire Department, and he went to war during World War II when he served in the U.S. Navy and worked his way up to become a Lieutenant Commander.

During the war years, my mother raised my older sister and me in a crowded neighborhood in the borough of Queens. Sometimes my uncle took me to see the Brooklyn Dodgers play at Ebbets Field, and I became a great Dodger fan. I also read a lot of comic books and played marbles in the city gutters. After the war, my father managed to get a mortgage to build a small Cape Cod house on Staten Island. In 1948, when I was eight years old, our family moved to our own house.

Staten Island was more countrified, and we could pick blackberries and run in the woods. I became a Cub Scout, and my mother leniently signed her name in my Cub book to certify that I could jump, build a fire, swim, and draw the flags of forty nations.

My mother was pious. She went to church regularly and she wanted the rest of our family to be good Catholics always, although my father didn’t go to Mass or think much of the priests. She kept a statue that she worshipped of Jesus in the form of “the little King,” an image in which he appears as a young boy dressed in a crown and robes. My mother used to tell me that even if you commit sin, God will forgive you if you repent; God is merciful.

I had a well-known picture, a scene showing Jesus Christ preaching to elder men at the temple. I was most attracted to this Biblical story. Here, too, Jesus was young; his mother and father had been traveling, and he had become lost. His parents found him at the temple preaching religious truths to ancient-looking scribes. I was a young boy, and in this picture Jesus was a young boy—but he was glorious. I received this picture for good attendance at church, and Sunday school teachers had put stars on it. Just as a young boy who has a crush on a girl sometimes writes, “I love you” on a book or on a card, on the back of that picture I wrote, “I love Jesus.” Then underneath it, as children sometimes do, I wrote “true.”

I went to public high school. In those days there wasn’t much promiscuity or drug abuse. We fooled around a lot in class, but there was no violence against the teachers. I wasted my high school years, but somehow managed to enroll in Brooklyn College.

During high school my father controlled me. He wanted me to enroll in the U.S. Naval Officers’ Training School. I hated the military, however, and my friends turned me against U.S. foreign policy. When I was seventeen, though, my father got me to enlist in the Naval Reserve.

As soon as I went to college I underwent an intellectual revolution. Any religious sentiments I had gained from my mother were driven away by my college professors, who were dyed-in-the-wool Marxist intellectuals, Americans from the 1930s. They taught me their intellectual and atheistic views, and knocked aside my religious worship, saying it was sentimental. One of them said theology could never satisfactorily explain why evil was present in the world. I was attracted to their philosophy because my parents were not intellectual and had never aroused my intellectual capacity. But my professors opened up a whole new world for me. I became eager to study philosophy and literature. I came to see for myself that the church was hypocritical: in the foyer of our church the priests regularly raffled bottles of liquor (they called them “baskets of joy”).

I became dissatisfied with the Catholic Church because it could not provide answers to my questions. I went to church one last time to see a young priest who was supposed to be exciting for young people. In confession I told the priest, “Actually I don’t believe in this confession I’m supposed to make.” The priest was behind the black curtain. He said, “Oh, why don’t you come and see me?” I agreed. Later in his room he said, “So what are you thinking? What is your philosophy?”

“Well,” I said, “how come there is so much evil in the world?” And I mentioned the Korean war and racism in America. “Oh,” he said, “you have a lot of love in you, I can see.” He was impressed by what I had said. Then I asked him some questions, but he couldn’t answer them. I was disappointed, and after that abandoned my religious sentiments. I thought God and the Bible were myths.

My college professors encouraged me to develop my writing abilities, which they thought were special. At eleven I started writing satirical stories, imitating things I had read. I had written humorous stories inspired by children’s books like Booth Tarkington’s book for boys, *Penrod and Sam.* In my high school years, I had always done well in English. When I was seventeen, I had started seriously keeping a diary in which I’d written heartfelt expressions of my confusion and my search for the truth. A conflict had begun during that time between my parents and me. I had been aspiring for higher things in life; their plans had limited me. I had expressed my feelings about this conflict in my journal.

I wrote in thick notebooks. Writing was a way for me to think, to meditate, and to express thoughts. My thoughts would become solid when I put them down on paper.

During my college years I read many books on my own and became influenced by the works of many novelists, books like Nietzsche’s *Thus Spake Zarathustra.* During my last two years at Brooklyn College I joined the literary magazine, *Landscapes,* and published my stories and poetry. For several semesters I won prizes for my writing. I would spend whole summers writing short stories and novels. With my college friends, who were almost all Jewish, I discovered the Lower East Side. Then abruptly, I had to end my New York City involvement with professors, college literary friends, and the Lower East Side and enter the Navy.

It was January 1962. Just like anyone in New York who joined the Navy, I was taken to the barracks in Brooklyn, where I stayed for a couple of weeks, waiting anxiously to see where I was going to be sent. Then my orders came. I was to sail aboard a ship called the *U.S.S. Saratoga,* a super-carrier. The ship left from its home port, Mayport, Florida, which is near Jacksonville, and crossed the Atlantic to visit Mediterranean ports in Spain, Italy, and France. We used to visit Cannes regularly, and France, Naples, and Italy. In Spain we visited Barcelona. Then we went further—to Greece, Cyprus, Beirut, and Istanbul. During the rest of the time I spent aboard, the ship traveled to ports in the U.S. Sometimes we spent twenty days at a time at sea.

When I first came aboard, they decided to put me in the gunnery department, but I told them I had journalistic abilities. I had heard there was a Public Information Office on board, so they agreed to place me in that department. My writing became occupational. I started a newspaper using articles from the UPI, which we got over the ship’s wire receiver. We also published a magazine in which I printed articles and reviews of intellectual books, most of which were over the heads of most sailors. I tried to continue the same kind of writing I had done when I had been in college with my literary friends.

I wasn’t like a low-class brute sailor. I never used obscene language, and I hated to hear others using it. This may have been partly because I was associating, through literature, with sensitive individuals—authors and poets.

Being in the Navy was like being in prison, and I simply counted the time I had before my discharge. I regularly got letters from my intellectual and literary friends in New York City. They had moved to the Lower East Side and taken up a life of creative freedom. I was just waiting to get away from the sailors and difficult naval life so I could join them.

Although I hated the Navy, it was somehow good for me. It toughened me up and made me more democratic. I saw that even the rough sailors were human beings—I wasn’t so special. I felt matured having associated with all kinds of people. Having endured my Naval service, I felt I could endure other difficult situations. Still I hated my two years in the Navy. It was *not* a series of romantic Mediterranean cruises. Like other “short-timers” (those who weren’t career sailors), I simply counted how much time I had to serve in the Navy. It was a dull ordeal, but it whetted my appetite for going to the Lower East Side, where I thought I could lead a free, romantic life. A few months after the death of President Kennedy, I was honorably discharged, and without visiting my parents on Staten Island, I went directly to the Lower East Side.

By then, the Lower East Side was, in my mind and in the minds of my friends, the most mystical place in the world. Just out of the Navy and free from my parents and school, I took to my new freedom with a vengeance. The neighborhood was Puerto Rican and Ukrainian, a slum, but people like me were living there—young writers, poets, painters—and everyone was experimenting. The neighborhood was a symbol of freedom and revolt from stupid, middle-class values. But everything was becoming corrupt. I was quickly losing my innocence. With my friends I continued my mystical search for the truth, but without any real direction. Those were naive days, when young people took LSD in search of spiritual truths.

Following an accident, I had to stay in bed for six weeks in leg casts. I had written to the Vedanta Society for some of their paperback books, and I began reading books on Eastern philosophy: *The Way of the Tao, Sayings of Confucius, Bhagavad-gītā*, and some of the *Upaniṣads.* One day, my mother came to my room and said, “You don’t really believe in these things, do you?” But I did like the *Bhagavad-gītā* and the *Upaniṣads.* I was attracted to some of the stories and aphorisms about the oneness of Brahman and the *ātmā,* the self beyond the body.

For six months after I came to the Lower East Side, I lived on the money I had saved from working in the Navy. Then I took a job as a case worker for the Department of Welfare. Social work is not a money-making business; it’s humanitarian work, and I was attracted to the opportunity to help people. In my job, I had to visit welfare recipients or recent applicants and see if they were eligible for welfare, witness their troubles, and listen to their sad stories. As their friend, I had to help them get money and to reform their lives. And there was always the bureaucratic paper work.

At the same time, I continued my literary career. I wasn’t interested in commercial writing or news reporting. I wanted to be an artist. For me, writing was a way to understand the truth. One of my writer friends said to me, “Well, you know, you have to write commercially. You can’t be idealistic.” But I used to always write about the creative process itself. I tried to explore the wonder of inspiration: how is it that somebody wants to write? Where does it come from, this creative drive and the ability and desire to express one’s self in words? What is the meaning of life? In a crude sense I was looking for spiritual life through writing, and I was attracted to writers who, even if not religious, wrote for higher purposes. I liked Kafka, for instance. Although people think of him as agnostic, he practiced his writing as a religion. God was not revealed to him, but neither was he interested in material success. He tried to express truth through writing. I tried to follow the writers interested in spiritual things such as the ultimate human predicament, and read books by Dostoevsky and other authors who wrote on these topics. In the crudest sense it was a spiritual quest. I identified these people as spiritually motivated compared to the materialistic workers or even priests, who weren’t as honest as these truth-seeking authors.

Yet despite my struggles to find the truth through writing, my health was deteriorating, and I was unhappy with everything. I had come to believe in Van Gogh’s statement, “Misery is eternal.” What more could you expect in an absurd universe? I felt lost, but so did great philosophers, painters, and musical composers. I certainly didn’t think some guru was suddenly going to appear and save me. I was too cynical. Yet I was regularly reading versions of the *Bhagavad-gītā* and the *Upaniṣads.*

Ironically, one week before the gift shop at 26 Second Avenue changed into Śrīla Prabhupāda’s temple, I was standing in that very doorway with a *Bhagavad-gītā* in my back pocket, waiting to meet a friend. Somehow we had chosen 26 Second Avenue as a meeting place. At that time, I had no idea what was about to happen.

It was July 1966. I often traveled on foot down Second Avenue between my office and where I lived. On Second Avenue, at the corner of Houston Street, was a little store, one of those nostalgia gift shops. Over the door was a painted sign, “Matchless Gifts.” In the store were matchboxes with pictures from the 1940’s Hollywood movies. One day while walking, I noticed that the gift shop was now empty. The “Matchless Gifts” sign was there, but the place was completely empty. There was a small piece of paper in the window announcing evening classes on the *Bhagavad-gītā*, by A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. There was also a painting displayed in the window of holy persons dancing with their arms upraised. I was attracted because I had been reading the *Bhagavad-gītā*.

The first night I attended the *Gītā* class was a warm summer evening, and in the storefront the back windows and front door were opened wide. It was seven o’clock, and myself and about a dozen people had gathered. Several of us young men were dressed in denims and wore button-down sport shirts with broad, dull stripes. We had left our worn sneakers at the door and were now sitting on the floor. Most of us were from the Lower East Side; we hadn’t had to go to great trouble to come to the storefront. The little room was bare—no pictures, no furniture, no rug, not even a chair—only a few plain, straw mats. A single bulb hung from the ceiling into the center of the room.

After I sat on the floor, one of the young men asked me my name. We talked and I felt at ease; here were gathered other young persons like myself. I had a feeling this group had already met a few times. Then suddenly, Prabhupāda opened the side door and entered the room.

He wasn’t wearing a shirt, and the saffron cloth that draped his torso left his arms and some of his chest bare. He had pointy shoes, and his complexion was a smooth golden brown. I watched him, his head shaven, his ears long-lobed, and his aspect grave. He reminded me of pictures I had seen of Lord Buddha in meditation. He was old, yet erect in his posture, fresh and radiant. His forehead was decorated with the yellowish clay markings of the Vaiṣṇavas. Prabhupāda recognized big, bearded Howard (Hayagrīva) and smiled. “You have brought your friends?”

When he spoke I could understand his words, although he spoke with a heavy Indian-British accent. He gave out several pairs of *karatālas,* and the young men began to play them: one, two, *three, . . .* one, two, *three. . .* Hearing the *karatālas* ringing in a slow, rhythmic pace, I felt even more that this group was already somewhat established—they knew how to play the instruments. Then Prabhupāda began to chant. I was fascinated, Hare Kṛṣṇa Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa Hare Hare/ Hare Rāma Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma Hare Hare. I couldn’t memorize the chant on the first evening, but I went away from the storefront chanting part of the mantra. I was oblivious to the sounds going on around me; I was thinking of the word “Kṛṣṇa.” I had heard this word pronounced before, but not so many times at once, and not by Swami Bhaktivedanta. The way he said it, “Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, Lord Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa”—it was a very different word. The chanting went on and on in my mind.

It was very convenient for me to go to the storefront. My mother or father weren’t there to tell me not to; I was grown up, had my own job and apartment within a few blocks of 26 Second Avenue, and if I wanted I could become a member of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. Yet when I first attended, I never thought I would practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness as an exclusive commitment.

During one of the very first nights I attended Prabhupāda’s *kīrtana* and lecture, I asked a question. Prabhupāda had invited questions, and Howard asked why Prabhupāda had described Lord Buddha as an atheist. I also raised my hand. Prabhupāda recognized me, and I began speaking in a roundabout manner by first apologizing that my question was not very clear. I finally got it out: “Is misery eternal?” I was thinking of a statement I had read in the collected letters of Van Gogh. Prabhupāda replied by first describing how there is no end to misery in the material world. He said that if a person breaks his arm and goes into the hospital and gets it repaired, his medical treatment will not prevent him from later breaking another limb or becoming ill and suffering in another way. Suffering goes on continually; but then Prabhupāda described a state—Kṛṣṇa consciousness—where one could attain relief from all misery. That completely spiritual state of Kṛṣṇa consciousness is eternal. I received Prabhupāda’s reply and did not argue. I was satisfied.

I remember soon after that going to see Śrīla Prabhupāda in his room and asking, “Is there a level in spiritual advancement one can make from which he doesn’t fall back?” I had sometimes thought I had achieved spiritual heights, but I later had to admit to myself that, after reaching such so-called spiritual heights, I had again fallen down to the worst kind of consciousness. I could realize that mine was not genuine spiritual life. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s one word reply, “Yes,” left a great impression on me. It wasn’t a “Yes” that *anyone* could have uttered. From that moment I was convinced that there was real spiritual life—a steady transcendental platform—and I was convinced that Prabhupāda had achieved that platform. After his lectures Prabhupāda would say (referring to Kṛṣṇa consciousness), “It is such a nice thing,” and he would urge us to practice it. I remember thinking, “Whatever that nice thing is, which I see he has achieved, I want to achieve it also.”

One of the first times I went to see Śrīla Prabhupāda was from my office during lunch hour. I had decided to stop by and purchase the Swami’s book, *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*.

I was dressed in a summer business suit with a shirt and tie. I went into the storefront and asked about buying the books. One of the boys said I should go up and see the Swami about it. I went up to his apartment and found him at his sitting place with a few boys in the room.

Sitting, smiling at ease, Prabhupāda was very attractive. He seemed strong and healthy; when he smiled all his teeth were intact and his nostrils flared aristocratically. His face was full and powerful. He was wearing a *khādī dhotī,* and he sat cross-legged, his smooth-skinned legs partly exposed. He wore no shirt, but the upper part of his body was wrapped with a *khādī* cloth shawl. His limbs were quite slender, but he had a protruding belly.

I must have interrupted Prabhupāda while he was talking with the young men. I asked him if I could purchase the three volumes of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* he had translated. Someone got the books from the closet opposite where he was sitting. I handled the books and asked Prabhupāda how much they cost. He said they were six dollars each. I thought that was rather expensive, but I wanted them. I was not bargain-hunting. Taking out my wallet I produced twenty dollars and gave it to him. He seemed the obvious one to ask the price of the books and to whom to give the money, because none of the other boys had come forward to represent him; they just sat and listened to him speak.

When I saw that Prabhupāda had to personally handle the sale of the books, I did not want to bother him. He mentioned something about change, but the boys said they didn’t have any, so I quickly asked him to please keep the change from my twenty dollar bill. “These books are commentaries on the scriptures?” I asked, trying to show that I knew something about the books. Śrīla Prabhupāda said yes, they were his commentaries. The books were a special color—a reddish, natural earth color, like that of a brick—unusual for books in America. I took the three volume set without any bag or wrapping.

I stood up and prepared to leave when Prabhupāda said, “Sit down,” gesturing that I could sit opposite him, like the others. He had said, “Sit down” in a heavy tone, indicating now that the sale of the books was complete, I should sit like the others and listen to him speak. He was offering me an important invitation—to become one of the others, who I knew spent many hours with him during the day when I was usually not able to come because of my job. I envied their leisure. They were able to learn much from the Swamijī and become intimate with him. By ending the sales transaction and asking me to sit, he clearly assumed that I had nothing better in the world to do than stop what I was doing and hear from him.

All this was true, except that I was on my lunch hour and was soon expected back in the office. I had planned just enough time to buy the books, go to lunch, and return to work. I didn’t want to argue with Prabhupāda but I couldn’t possibly stay. “I am sorry, I have to go,” I said definitely. “I am only on my lunch hour.” As I said this I had already started to move for the door. Prabhupāda responded by suddenly breaking into a wide smile; he looked very charmed and happy with me. He seemed to appreciate that I was a working man, a young man on the go. I had not come to see him because I was unemployed and had nowhere to go and nothing to do. Approving of my energetic demeanor, he allowed me to take my leave. In retrospect, I think he must have seen that I could use my karmic tendencies to do some active service for Kṛṣṇa.

I was interested in reading his books, and began almost immediately. I had developed a habit of glancing down a page, reading feverishly; certain modern authors, as well as my friends and I, had developed a writing style to catch at all costs the reader’s restless mind, swiftly moving him from sentence to sentence. I immediately perceived that to read Prabhupāda’s books, I would have to slow down and read more seriously. Although at first I was reluctant to slow myself, I felt willing to make the adjustment because I could understand that the subject matter was important. His was an intelligent philosophical presentation of the spiritual—God consciousness.

The book was filled with printing errors. I could understand that whoever had printed the book was not familiar with the English language. The rough, reddish material of the hard-bound cover, the paper, and the book binding—everything about it was strange.

In the Introduction, Prabhupāda apologizes for these material errors; but because the message of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* is so urgent, the book will be appreciated by honest persons despite mistakes. He even quotes a verse from the scripture to support this, and gives the example that if there is a fire in the house, one will be able to inform the residents of the emergency, even if he does not speak their language. This explanation made me sympathetic, and soon the very printing mistakes, the crude brick-colored cover, and the use of phrases like “the Lord,” “the Supreme Personality of Godhead,” and “Krishna” began to increase my interest in reading the book.

I took the book with me and read it wherever I went—on the bus, in my Suffolk Street apartment, and even at my office desk. I thought the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* was the most direct and authentic book about God I had ever read. It was filled with logical arguments to prove God's existence, and constantly praised His glories. I read quickly, retaining little, but feeling good whenever I read it.

A few nights later I went to see Prabhupāda and told him I was reading his book. One thing that had especially caught my attention was a section where the ancient sage, Vyāsadeva, author of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, admitted he was despondent. Although he had compiled all the *Vedas* and had done great service for humanity, although he was learned in spiritual techniques, he was dissatisfied. At first Vyāsadeva was puzzled about why he was unhappy; but then his spiritual master explained to him that although he had written many books, he had neglected to fully glorify God. After hearing this, Vyāsadeva compiled *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*.

When I read this, I identified with Vyāsadeva as a writer because I considered myself a writer, and I also was despondent. “This was very interesting about the author Vyāsadeva,” I mentioned to Prabhupāda when I met him one evening. “He wrote so many books, but still he was not satisfied because he had not directly praised Kṛṣṇa.”

Although at that time I had little understanding of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Śrīla Prabhupāda opened his eyes wide and seemed surprised that I was speaking about such an exalted subject from *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. He seemed pleased I was trying to enter into the mystery of *Bhāgavatam.* I had only begun my acquaintance with him, but when he was pleased, those moments became unforgettable.

However, the same evening I had asked such a serious question about Vyāsadeva’s despondency, I also asked Prabhupāda why Indian women wear a dot on their foreheads. He answered that this showed they were married, but then he gave me an odd look, as if to say, “Why are you asking me such a mundane question about Indian culture?”

In an early meeting with Prabhupāda I wanted to show my appreciation for spiritual India, so I expressed to him that I had read Gandhi's autobiography. “It was glorious,” I said.

“What is glorious about it?” Prabhupāda challenged. There were others present in the room. Although I was a guest, he had no qualms about challenging me for saying something foolish. I searched through my remembrance of Gandhi’s autobiography to answer his challenging question—*What is glorious*? I began to relate that Gandhi, as a child, although raised a vegetarian, took meat on the inducement of some of his friends. That night he felt that a lamb was howling in his belly.

Prabhupāda dismissed this at once saying, “Most of India is vegetarian; that is not glorious.” When I could not think of anything else to say about Gandhi, Prabhupāda said, “His autobiography is called *Experiment With The Truth,* but that is not the nature of truth. Truth is not to be found by someone’s experimenting; it is *always truth.”*

Although a blow to my ego, being exposed or defeated by Prabhupāda always seemed to be a gain for me, and I wanted to bring many things before him for his judgment, just to see what he would say about them. I showed him the paperback edition of the *Bhagavad-gītā* I was reading. He took it in his hand and perused the back cover. Printed was a reference to “the eternal faith of the Hindus.” Prabhupāda took the phrase apart. The word “Hindu” is a misnomer—a word invented by the Muslims to refer to the people of India. It is only a mispronunciation of the river Indus. It does not occur anywhere in Sanskrit literature. He also said that the Hindus’ faith could not be considered eternal.

Another one of the first times I met Prabhupāda, I challenged him by saying that he always gave answers according to the conclusions in the Vedic literatures. I said this was a narrow way to approach the truth. I cannot remember his exact reply, but he answered in a keen, logical, philosophical way that satisfied me. When I challenged that his replies were dogmatic, he did not offer me a dogmatic argument, but one based on logic. He told me that the Vedic scriptures reveal absolute truth, but he was ready to argue his statements from any dynamic viewpoint, not depending on merely quoting the scriptures.

When I came to Prabhupāda, my opinion was that the truth is always relative, and no one person or source of knowledge is a repository of absolute truth. This was a personal conviction I had gained by meeting different persons and authors. It seemed to me that the differences between people were demonstrated more by their style or their ability to dominate over others by argumentative power. I did not believe that anyone actually *knew* the truth. It was only a matter of one person appearing more convincing in his style than another. I had thought that this was the ultimate secret for people getting along in the world.

Before too long, however, Prabhupāda had me appreciating his authoritative presentation of the *paramparā.* Once, I became exasperated when Prabhupāda said that Kṛṣṇa had married 16,000 wives. “I am trying in good faith to understand Kṛṣṇa,” I said to him, “and I am trying to understand His teachings of the *Gītā.* But how do you expect me to understand when you say He married 16,000 wives?”

Śrīla Prabhupāda answered back: “*you* do not understand? Even the greatest *scholars* cannot understand.” And then he fully explained how Kṛṣṇa’s marrying 16,000 wives was neither mythical nor an example of material lust.

Another time, after one of his lunches, I went to Prabhupāda in his back room. There I sat next to some of the other devotees. While sitting I assumed a cross-legged pose of yogic meditation. Unlike some of the others, I had never done any disciplined meditation or yoga *āsanas,* but somehow, as if just to expose my foolishness in Prabhupāda’s presence, I sat in this pose. He looked at me as though I was emanating a bad odor, and I at once felt exposed in my absurd yoga pose. I understood that I didn’t know anything about yoga. He could expose this kind of foolishness with just a glance.

Prabhupāda satisfied my intellectual hunger with his philosophical presentation. By that time, I had become, like many intellectuals, eclectic. I had been inclined to read about spiritual things. I had read translations of the *Bhagavad-gītā* and the *Upaniṣads.* I had even read novels that were spiritually oriented, like Hesse’s *Magister Ludi: The Bead Game*, which is a fictional account of living with a guru in an *āśrama.* I had thought that book wonderful. But I remained eclectic.

I assumed Prabhupāda was an eclectic teacher. The first night I heard him speak on *Bhagavad-gītā,* I thought, “I wonder what he will teach next week? Perhaps the *Tibetan Book of the Dead.*” I was surprised when Śrīla Prabhupāda powerfully asserted what nobody else did: “There is an Absolute Truth, and you don’t have to search for Him in different places; you can learn everything from *Bhagavad-gītā*.” I accepted this presentation.

Before meeting Śrīla Prabhupāda, I never imagined I would be able to accept one philosophy, but I gave up my different ideas, one after another, and I came to trust him. Although I was twenty-six, I began to approach him more like a small child approaching his father.

A week after I began to regularly attend Prabhupāda’s *kīrtanas* and began coming to see him on nights when he didn’t hold *kīrtanas*, I felt reluctant to visit my parents in their summer home during the weekends. The last time I went to visit them, I first asked Prabhupāda’s permission. He said yes, of course I could go. I then deliberately expressed to him that I didn’t want to do anything without his permission. He nodded, smiled, and appreciated my submission.

I took a bus to Jersey and reached my parents’ house on Friday night. I immediately went to the ocean for an evening swim. I floated on my back, looking up at the faraway moon and stars. Suddenly, all that I had been learning and hearing from Prabhupāda filled my mind and being, and I could even hear him speaking within me with different voices. Even the phrases from his books were swimming through my mind. I became aware that my new experience with Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa consciousness was intense; I was getting supreme knowledge. I sensed it was very precious, and I became eager to return to the city to take part in my ongoing meetings with him.

My apartment on Suffolk Street was a scene of despair and hopelessness. I decided to leave it. I managed to rent a new apartment located on First Street, only a block away from Prabhupāda’s storefront. By Lower East Side standards, my new apartment was better. I let loose my pet cats and abandoned my record collection—I could not bear to take my belongings, even to sell them later. I would not return.

My motive was to move nearer to Prabhupāda. Now I could attend his morning class by walking only one city block; after his class I had to walk only four blocks to work, the welfare office at 330 East Fifth Street, between First and Second Avenues. After work I had only a five-block walk to my apartment, and from there to Prabhupāda’s in the evening.

I decided I would do things in my new apartment conducive only to spiritual life. I bought a sleeping bag, slept on the floor, and bought a few new pots to try to cook like Prabhupāda. I was already taking lunch at his place, but I wanted to cook rice and *capātīs* as a spiritual practice.

One of the few belongings I allowed myself to take from my old apartment was my portable typewriter, and in my new “spiritual” apartment, I began my first typing assignments from Śrīla Prabhupāda. After the morning class, I would return home and try to remember everything Prabhupāda had said. Then, sitting on the floor, I would set my typewriter on an improvised desk and type notes. I would type in terse sentences, a kind of shorthand. I showed these notes to Prabhupāda, and he approved of them. One morning he discovered after giving the lecture that his tape recorder had not been functioning. “That is all right,” he said, and he gestured toward me. “*He* is a tape recorder.”

One night, one of my former acquaintances from Brooklyn College visited me at the storefront just before the evening *kīrtana*. He had not been one of my close friends, but he was the first of my old crowd who came to see me at the storefront. I tried explaining the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy to him, but as soon as I described the nonmaterial, he rejected my statements. When I used the word “Brahman,” he said, “What are you talking about?”—as if I had gone crazy. I suggested that perhaps he should hear Swamijī explain these things, but he said he didn’t want to see him. It was as if he had come on behalf of my friends to see what I was doing. He stayed for the *kīrtana,* which he said he liked, but I don’t think he gave a good report to my other friends.

Soon after that, Murray, whom I had admired very much, came to the storefront. We had met during my first year in college, and I had always shown him my writings and confided in him—even during the years when I was away in the Navy. I looked up to him as a virtuous poet, a true Lower East Side sage. I was anxious that he like Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and I was nervous when he sat and chanted with us. I watched him to see his reaction. Afterwards, we left the storefront together, and he commented, “Well, anything that helps you communicate is good.” I told him that the Swamijī was speaking the same Vedic message spoken by spiritual masters in India thousands of years ago. It was the same message Kṛṣṇa had spoken when He appeared on earth; therefore, hearing from Swamijī was the same as directly hearing from Kṛṣṇa.

Murray listened respectfully for a minute, but then said that immediate things were more important. We spent the evening together walking around the Lower East Side and trying to visit friends. But no one was home. We talked about life and literature, and I tried tactfully to convey to him that I had great hopes about my entrance onto the spiritual path.

At one point, I began humming a popular song from a Broadway musical, which had the lines, “Stick to your own kind, one of your own kind.” Murray angrily turned to me and said, “Then why don’t you stick to your own kind if these are your new friends?” Then he wanted to see what was happening at a well-known tavern, and when I didn’t want to join him, we parted on the street.

He visited me once more, this time at my new apartment. I prepared some sweet, hot milk the way Prabhupāda made it. Murray asked me why the Hindus hold the cow as sacred. I explained that because we took the cow’s milk, she is like our mother, and why should we kill our mother? He smiled and said the milk I gave him was very nice, but our ways had parted irrevocably. He left after a short visit. We were parting for life.

Another time I visited a friend who was living with his girl friend in Greenwich Village. His way of life seemed aimless and sordid. I told him how I was attending Swamijī’s classes and typing for him. I distinctly remember that after speaking with him I went downstairs and hailed a taxi. As the taxi drove from my friend’s part of town back to the Lower East Side, a very strong impression overwhelmed me. With every block I passed I felt I was leaving behind an entire world, never to return again; I was leaving behind something that had a very deep grip on me, and I was entering a new consciousness—I was becoming Prabhupāda’s servant.

I began visiting Prabhupāda whenever I could. At first I invented different pretexts or formed specific questions to ask him before I would see him. One day, I decided to tell him that I wanted to be a trustee of his society. One of Prabhupāda’s early plans for supporting ISKCON was to create trustees who would each pay twenty dollars a month and be considered one of the pillars of his society. My main reason for wanting to do this was so that I could see him more often.

I headed over to 26 Second Avenue, but met Prabhupāda before I reached the storefront. He was out walking on the street by himself also heading for his apartment. He greeted me, and we walked to his apartment together. He was wearing a reddish sweatshirt with his *dhotī.* I stood behind him while he put the key in his apartment door; then we went inside. In his room, I began to express my intention to become a trustee. He acknowledged me while removing his sweatshirt, revealing his slender torso and protruding belly. “I am an old man,” he said. “I may die very soon. But you are young. You have your whole life, so you should give your life to Kṛṣṇa.”

I replied, “I may be younger, but still I could die very soon, and you could live much longer.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda replied, “That would be an accident. But the natural condition will be that you could live much longer.”

Gradually, by moving my apartment, and by cooking like Prabhupāda, I came to mold myself as his follower. I still had my job, and I kept the money to myself. I had collected about $600 with the idea that after a year or so, I would resign and go off to an island in Canada where it was free and solitary and do some writing. Instead, I began to buy things for Prabhupāda. I went shopping on Orchard Street and purchased some turtleneck jerseys for him, which he began wearing along with his *sannyāsī* clothes. These jerseys were not the best, and after a while the necks began to stretch, but he continued to wear them.

In his lectures he mentioned that mangos were the king of all fruits, but that they were not available in America. It occurred to me that I could bring him mangos, which he missed from his own country. At that time, mangos were not common in stores, but there was one store on First Avenue that kept a special stock in the rear refrigerator—one dollar each. I began to regularly purchase a mango each day and bring it to Śrīla Prabhupāda.

One day, when I brought the mango to his room, he was surrounded by many devotees. I gave him my mango, sat down, and he said, “Very good boy.” He said it as if I was just a tiny boy, and this made everyone in the room laugh. I felt foolish. Śrīla Prabhupāda, however, then changed his mood by saying to them, “No. This is actually love. This is Kṛṣṇa consciousness.” And they stopped laughing. Over the years, when Prabhupāda recalled the days of 1966, he often recounted, “And Satsvarūpa used to bring me a mango.”

I became very attracted to how Śrīla Prabhupāda would tell Bengali proverbs and stories from the *Vedas*. They were wonderful, and even more wonderful was the way he told them. Before meeting him, I had stayed up many nights listening to radio raconteurs, and, of course, reading many novels—anything from Charles Dickens to Thomas Wolfe. No one fascinated me as much as Śrīla Prabhupāda. The examples he gave—the hog that eats stool, the deathly ill man who, when asked how he is, says he’s fine, the many humorous stories he told—all captured my mind.

I began to realize that spiritual life was something strong, unlike my previous conception that spiritual ideas were as delicate as a glass toy, something that could break at any second. Hearing from him, I became hopeful. One day, on leaving the storefront, I said to him, “I am feeling very hopeful,” and Prabhupāda smiled and agreed that spiritual life *was* hopeful.

Before meeting him, I used to feel intimidated by the very massiveness of the buildings in the city. After hearing about Kṛṣṇa, however when I would walk on the streets, I could sense an ultimate illusion in the buildings; they no longer oppressed me. Spirit is real; matter, false.

One day, while he was talking, I suddenly realized the unique position of Kṛṣṇa in relation to other incarnations and saintly teachers. I said to Prabhupāda, “It seems that the special feature of Kṛṣṇa is that He so relishable, even in comparison to Jesus Christ and others.” Prabhupāda naturally agreed with pleasure that I had stated Kṛṣṇa’s actual position.

I had been giving occasional donations to Prabhupāda from my bank account, sometimes as much as twenty or thirty dollars. One day I decided to withdraw the entire balance, about four hundred dollars, and present the sum to Prabhupāda. It was a big surrender for me. I went into his room and walked up to his desk. Shyly, uttering that it was my entire savings, I presented my money to him. When Prabhupāda saw how much it was, he smiled with delight. I figured my donation could very well have represented next month’s rent or the devotees’ daily maintenance for food since, as far as I knew, Prabhupāda had almost no income at all. “Don is also giving us money,” Prabhupāda said, smiling.

I had squatted down beside him to give him the money, and I then stood up and walked a few steps from his desk to the other side of the room. By the time I sat down and looked at him again, his face had completely changed. He was like a different person. No longer was he brilliantly smiling, gratefully receiving the money, but suddenly he was stern, seeming to penetrate me with his glance. That look banished my self-satisfaction at being the magnanimous donor and patron and reduced me to whatever honesty I possessed. Although he did not speak, it was as if he had clearly said, “It is not very wonderful that you have given me a little money;. You still have to surrender to Kṛṣṇa.”

Approaching Prabhupāda as a person and becoming attached to him meant approaching Kṛṣṇa and Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Whatever influence he had gained over me was to draw me into devotional service. He openly proposed, repeatedly, that this was his mission. As one entered a relationship with him, one discovered that this was true. Prabhupāda was actually a pure devotee of Kṛṣṇa, and on behalf of his spiritual master he was personally trying to engage everyone he could in the Lord's service. Even from the beginning I was not cynical towards Śrīla Prabhupāda, nor did I suspect his motives. I had immediately sensed his kindness and devotional surrender to Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

My own doubts were intellectual: did God actually exist? I suffered from a deep-rooted agnostic mentality. Besides that, I had my own neurotic condition: unless someone showed me special treatment and loved me with personal concern, I could not come forward to surrender my love to Kṛṣṇa. My craving for being treated as special is a need everyone has, but that didn’t make it any less *my* own need. If I were treated like another case, not appreciated or personally touched, then I would not have been able to respond, no matter how intellectually convincing the philosophy was.

Yet I realized I could not demand this. Prabhupāda, however, knew perfectly well how to draw Kṛṣṇa consciousness from me. He always had time to see me, and he was always patient and tolerant. His sincere desire to situate me in devotional service was stronger than my reluctance to surrender. Many times he offered me the special treatment I required.

One day at the welfare office, my supervisor told me I would have to work until one o’clock instead of taking my usual lunch hour at noon. This meant I would miss taking lunch with Prabhupāda and his followers. I could not cope with that idea; yet I had no choice. I called Prabhupāda from pay phone in the hall outside my office. This was the first time I had ever telephoned him. I felt I was being presumptuous, considering him my equal and therefore easily accessible.

Prabhupāda answered. I asked him if he remembered who I was. He remembered my name. Then I informed him that I would not be able to come for lunch and requested that he please save me some *prasādam*. He said he would. That done, I felt pacified and able to work the extra hour.

At one o’clock I went straight to the storefront. Prabhupāda was alone; all the boys had already eaten. We talked for a little while, and then he asked me to go into the other room. The wooden floor was bare, the rug rolled back to one side (a daily routine for keeping the rug clean during lunch). Prabhupāda asked me to sit down. He left the room, while I sat. Soon he returned with a plate of *prasādam*. He bent over and placed it on the floor in front of me.

I was still not completely used to the Indian custom of eating off a plate on the floor. It seemed the way one would treat a dog. Although it was awkward for me, I was not resentful when he placed the plate before me. I felt grateful that he had specially saved me *prasādam* and that he was not simply feeding me lunch (I could have filled my belly in any one of the innumerable luncheonettes in the area), but that he was saving me from material life. Before eating, I bowed down in front of him and the plate of *prasādam.* This was the first time I had ever bowed down, although I had seen a few others do it. When I bowed down, I heard him say, “Yes.” It was a perfect moment for me, as he recognized and accepted my surrender. It had occurred just the way I had required—a special, intimate setting in which I was not just one of a crowd, but an individual soul personally receiving mercy.

Śrīla Prabhupāda expertly encouraged me in my confused state of mind. In a gathering in his room before at least a dozen others, I posed an intricate, personal question to him. He had said pleasing Kṛṣṇa was the perfection of our lives. Therefore I thought, “What kind of person would Kṛṣṇa actually like me to be?” In my own experience of love and friendship, a friend or lover could present him-self as any number of personalities just to please or flatter the beloved. Just as I had thought there was no absolute knowledge, I had also thought that a person had no true self, and that, gain the satisfaction of another, he could more or less prostitute himself by being one of many different selves, each assuming different masks out of the cleverness of self-interest. I finally tried to express this subjective rigmarole out loud.

“Swamijī,” I said, “I feel I can be many different persons, but how do I know which person I should be to please Kṛṣṇa? If I have many different selves, which self would He like me to be?” Looking at me and then replying before the room full of people, Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “This boy Steve is very nice. He does typing for me, and sometimes gives me donations. So you should all do like this.”

I was startled that Prabhupāda did not directly address my psychological question, but he answered it in his own perfect way. I was immediately gratified by his singling me out with praise, and for the entire evening I was ecstatic. Yet I began to think, “Why do I demand special praise?”

I was not alone, however, in being dependent on Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expressions of pleasure. Among Prabhupāda’s followers, we knew that if he was pleased with us, our life was successful. The same thing is stated in the *śāstras*, that if one pleases the spiritual master, Kṛṣṇa is pleased. Prabhupāda also made no secret of the fact that he did not love us for our own attractiveness, but was trying to capture our hearts to engage us in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

Sometimes Prabhupāda related even more specifically to me and my idiosyncrasies. One time I told him I wanted to speak about something practical, and he gave me a private interview. When I met him and repeated that I wanted to consult him about a practical matter, we both smiled at my implying that the *Bhagavad-gītā,* which he was always discussing, was in some way not practical. Prabhupāda smiled in what seemed to me an imitation of my own sarcastic mood and its inference that he deal more practically with me. I suddenly felt embarrassed. At the same time, Prabhupāda made me feel so honest that I somehow persisted in trying to make the presentation of what was actually on my mind, even if it meant I would appear foolish and in need of correction.

I told him I had written a book and that a publisher was interested in printing it. It was an autobiography, but it ended before I had come to the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. I asked him if I should write an ending, showing how I had became a devotee, and then try to sell it. Prabhupāda said, “Yes, go ahead and do it like that, but be sure they pay you well. People think that because we are religious we should not get paid. A religious person should get paid *twice* as much.”

Actually, it was Prabhupāda who was more practical. I was still dreaming of becoming a famous writer, and perhaps I was trying to impress Prabhupāda with my literary abilities, whereas he was thinking my book was good only if I could secure money for Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Shortly after this exchange, I decided my whole literary career had become a concoction. I suspected that even if I did become a famous writer and made money for Kṛṣṇa, I would be diverted from pure consciousness. With a spirit of neophyte renunciation, I gathered up my accumulated writings—years’ worth of stories and short novels, literary fables of my self’s journey—and heaved the hundreds of pages of manuscripts through a small door into the incinerator at 26 Second Avenue. Writing, I thought, was just an expression of the false ego. The sooner I got rid of it, the better.

Prabhupāda’s presence also went with me to the welfare office. I would keep the *Bhāgavatam* at my desk. Sometimes I would open it, read a page, and then close it. I had to be discreet because I was working in a large, open room with several rows of desks, and my supervisors and the other workers could easily notice whether my attention was focused on the welfare work or on something else. One time I related to Prabhupāda that since hearing him speak about Kṛṣṇa, I had become transcendental to the situation in my office. I told him that now I felt different from the others because I could understand that life was beset with birth, death, disease, and old age, and that none of the other workers and none of the leaders could solve these problems. When I expressed my genuine realization, Prabhupāda smiled and showed such strength in his face that my mind brought forth images of iron and of a powerful bull.

I asked Prabhupāda whether it was all right for me to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa at the office, at least to myself, when other persons were speaking to me. I said that people sometimes spoke complete nonsense, and rather than actually listen to them, I would only superficially hear their words and chant within. Śrīla Prabhupāda answered, “Yes. Not only the people you say are speaking nonsense, but *everyone*—even the greatest philosopher—is speaking nonsense. There is no real knowledge except Kṛṣṇa consciousness, the knowledge of the soul beyond the bodily conception.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda gradually attracted me, especially by his personal dealings. I would also deliberately cultivate these dealings with him. Once, at one of our evening meetings, a crazy Puerto Rican entered the temple and danced around in a mad, nondevotional manner. Prabhupāda tolerated him, and he eventually left the storefront. A day later, when I was walking in the Lower East Side neighborhood to the welfare office, I saw this same man. He was now standing in the middle of the street with a broom, imitating a traffic policeman by dramatically conducting traffic. I went to Prabhupāda’s apartment and told him, “Swamijī, you know that man who was dancing wildly in our kīrtana? I just saw him on the street, and he was directing traffic.” At first Prabhupāda questioned me what I meant by directing traffic, and I told him that the man was crazily imitating a policeman.

“Oh, is he a madman?” Prabhupāda asked.

I would purposely try to bring up odd topics like this because I delighted in hearing Prabhupāda talk about seemingly ordinary things. I had quickly learned to hold him in high reverence, but hearing him comment about ordinary affairs made me feel more love and friendship for him. Our developing relationship was a mixture of absolute philosophy and personal affection, with a firm basis of love and trust. Prabhupāda had said that unless we were *acquainted* with Kṛṣṇa, how could we love Him? The same seemed true of our relationship with Prabhupāda.

However, one could not be casual when he spoke with Prabhupāda. He was not another Lower East Side buddy, Navy buddy, or Brooklyn College buddy. I was always aware that he was elderly and only interested in discussing the Absolute Truth.

Some of the boys who came to see Prabhupāda used to behave irreverently, and sometimes they even made jokes at his expense. They would have little give-and-takes between themselves, even in Prabhupāda’s presence, thinking he was unaware of their jokes. They certainly didn’t take everything he said as absolute truth. When I saw their lighthearted attitude toward Śrīla Prabhupāda, I resented it from the beginning. I empathized with Prabhupāda. I didn’t tolerate any word-play or frivolity at his expense.

Once he was sitting in his room with a big pot someone had just given him, admiring it. “This is a first-class pot,” he said, “and this is first-class rice.” He had some *basmatī* rice that he was going to cook in the new pot. He would say things like this with so much innocence. He was, in one sense, vulnerable to the New York City hip mentality, and when the boys joked in a subtle way among themselves while in Prabhupāda’s presence or even in another room, I would feel estranged from them. Not that I was a serious devotee—I wasn’t—but I was attracted to Prabhupāda. Without even really knowing his philosophy, I had immediately responded to Prabhupāda, who was serious and elderly, and I could sense some of his infinite kindness upon me.

Prabhupāda’s grasp of Kṛṣṇa knowledge was clear and evident, but to speak to him was not always easy. Because of language differences, some of the boys who spent more time with him, such as Keith \[Kīrtanānanda\], would have to help the others who could not communicate properly with Prabhupāda. But language was only part of it. If we said something with a roundabout preamble he would say, “What?” or “Hmmm?” We had to speak clearly and simply and make good inquiries. We could never use slang or repeat trite phrases like, “You know, you know” and expect him to sustain us out of politeness. We felt impelled to approach Prabhupāda as a spiritual master and ask him serious, philosophical questions.

Bit by bit I got a chance to express different doubts and make inquiries that were personally important to me. One evening I asked Śrīla Prabhupāda, “There are different writers like Nietzsche and others who have actually misled people. *Now* I can understand that they have terribly misled people. Their evocations are powerful, and they try to be honest, but they express a miserable point of view. Do they have any God consciousness?”

Prabhupāda said, “Their sincerity is their God consciousness.” What he said was important to me. If he had said, “Oh they’re rascals, they’re fools, they’re completely worthless, they’re just going to hell”—that would have been hard for me to take because of my attachment. Instead, he expertly weaned me away from them by giving them credit, even though they remained in darkness and were pitiable persons. Prabhupāda explained that at least they were trying to look for the truth; they weren’t trying to enjoy anything. Still, they were ignorant, addicted to mental speculation. In this way I was able to make the inevitable break from thinkers who had greatly influenced me, and at the same time I retained compassionate regard for their lives of dedication.

Another time when I arrived too late for the general lunch, Prabhupāda again served me separately. While I was taking *dāl* and *capātīs* in his presence, I expressed to him how much I liked the *prasādam* and that I particularly liked to break up the pieces of my *capātī* and put them in my *dāl*.

“Oh, you do?” Prabhupāda said, smiling and seemingly surprised, as if he was interested in such a trivial habit. One advantage we had in those days was that there were only a few devotees, and Prabhupāda was able to pay each of us much attention. At least in my case, I cannot see how I could possibly have become attached to Kṛṣṇa without Prabhupāda’s personal cultivation. Who else would have been able to see in my enthusiasm for eating *dāl* and *capātīs* a spark of love of God? Who else could have assured me and led me on?

One morning, after Prabhupāda’s class was over, I went outside the temple and sat on the curb, looking down Second Avenue, wondering what I would do. It was Sunday, and I did not have to work at the welfare office, but at the same time I wasn't committed to spend all my time on Prabhupāda’s behalf. Prabhupāda was still in the storefront talking to some boys. From the window he must have seen me sitting on the curb, because he asked someone to come and get me. I was pleased to hear that Prabhupāda wanted to see me, and I went inside. He was still sitting on his dais, and said, “We are having a feast, and I am inviting you to come this afternoon. Do you have any engagement of work, or can you come?”

“No,” I said, “today is Sunday. I can come.” I had heard indirectly that they were having a feast, but I thought it was just for insiders. In the beginning, I had also thought that morning classes were just for insiders, so I didn't attend for weeks. But when Prabhupāda personally invited me to the feast, I was happy to come. If he had not personally picked me out of the gutter, I would not have come to the feast on my own.

Śrīla Prabhupāda personally cooked and served us the Sunday feast preparations. We sat in rows in his upstairs room, and he walked up and down the lines barefoot, spooning *prasādam* onto our plates from different pots. He asked which preparations we wanted, and then served us with pleasure. These preparations were not the same as those we ate at the regular luncheon, but sweets and savories with special tastes. The best preparation that day was his sweet rice. Even after we took full plates, Prabhupāda came back and asked us to take more. He stopped in front of me. Would I like some more sweet rice? In my early spiritual life, I had the misconception that I should deny myself what I liked best. I asked instead for some more plain rice. But even that a plain rice, was fancy yellow rice with cheese balls.

I used to phone my mother and father a few times a month just to assure them that I was all right and to talk about my job and their activities. Around that time, Prabhupāda printed an informative brochure on Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and I sent a copy in the mail to my parents.

The next time I phoned my family, my mother was extremely upset about the brochure. She understood that I was taking Kṛṣṇa consciousness seriously, and because it wasn’t Catholic, she was opposed. I tried to explain that I was not rejecting my Catholic beliefs, and that all religions were actually the same, but she didn’t seem to care. Both she and my father were upset because I was practicing a different religion (although my father didn’t attend services in “our religion”).

I told them over the phone, “There is no real religion in the world. People don’t follow religion. The world is so sinful, but Swamijī, *he’s* teaching us how we can follow the real religious life. I’m not rejecting my religion, I’m actually following it. Kṛṣṇa consciousness is the same thing the Pope teaches, but people don’t follow the Pope. Swamijī is teaching us that we should follow his orders. I’m doing that, so you should be happy.”

My sister argued, “Well, you say that the people don’t follow the Pope, but what about you? Why don’t *you* follow the Pope? Why criticize the Catholics for not following the Pope? What about *you?* You are one of them, and you don’t follow. You’re guilty of the very thing you’re accusing others of!”

Then my mother proceeded to say things against Prabhupāda, although she didn’t know anything about him. I did not want to continue hearing her criticize Prabhupāda, so I tried to change the subject.

My mother didn’t understand what I was doing. My family was sectarian—they thought that unless you’re part of the Catholic church, you’re disobeying God. I hadn’t been able to follow the Pope because he gave only vague instructions: “Be God-conscious.” I could follow Prabhupāda, however. “Why should my parents object?” I thought. Obviously I wasn’t following Catholicism, but I was following religious commands, as taught by Prabhupāda.

Although there was underlying friction with my relatives, I kept up contact with them, but my relationship with them had changed. I decided to take a portfolio we had made to my older sister. Prabhupāda had devised a scheme to solicit large donations from high-class people. We had gathered materials to convince prospective donors of our authenticity—photocopies of news articles like the one that had appeared in the *New York Times* about our chanting in Tompkins Square park, the *National Inquirer* article entitled “New Indian Religion Gets You Higher Than LSD,” and the favorable article from the *East Village Other*. I didn’t know many respectable people besides my relatives, so I decided to try preaching to them first.

I took the New York Central Line out of Times Square and headed north for an hour. I brought with me the original edition of Prabhupāda’s *Easy Journey to Other Planets.* Reading this book, I had a supramundane experience. While waiting for the train and during the ride, I actually perceived spiritual reality. I became completely convinced of what Prabhupāda wrote—that the existence of transcendental reality is separate from material surroundings. It was exciting to read this.

As soon as I reached my sister’s home, I immediately preached Kṛṣṇa consciousness. She said, “Oh, this is typical of you. Whatever you get into, you really get into it completely. How long is *this* going to last?”

I said, “Oh, no, this is *it.*” I explained to her that we should not be attracted to lumps of flesh. The spiritual world is reality. She listened without arguing.

Then her husband came home, and he was friendly. He was a stock analyst quickly rising to success in the New York Stock Exchange. I preached to him that wealth is not everything in life. I quoted the Bible. Christ says that it is harder for a man attached to his wealth to get into the kingdom of heaven than it is to put a camel through the eye of a needle. He didn’t like this, although I was quoting Christian scripture. Both he and my sister tolerated me until I asked for a donation. They flatly refused.

It was late afternoon, and I thought to myself, “Well, that’s it. I tried. Swamijī sent me. I preached the best I could.” I said to them. “So, I think I have to go now.”

My sister became alarmed. “Wait a minute. Just stay and sit and talk. We’ve heard you out. We’ve talked about your spiritual life. Now let’s just relax. There are other things.” She wanted me to relate to her as her brother, but I persisted in wanting to leave. My sister continued, “You’ve talked only about Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Are you some kind of fanatic? Is that all you can talk about? Why are you saying you have to go now, just because we won’t give you a donation?”

“I’m sorry,” I said, “I have to go. There is nothing for me to do here. I don’t want to talk about anything but Kṛṣṇa consciousness.”

I may have been slightly fanatical because I was clinging to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, which Prabhupāda had only recently given us.

My brother-in-law drove me to the train station. “This isn’t just something sentimental,” I told him. “It’s not foolish. It’s transcendental loving service to the Lord.”

He said, “I understand, I understand.” He tried to show me that he reflected seriously about spiritual things.

That was the last time I saw my sister. When I returned to Prabhupāda, I was happy to tell him about my preaching mission. “Swamijī’s my real father,” I thought. “Everything else is *māyā.”* Having experienced the world of *māyā,* I happily entered Prabhupāda’s room, although I had failed in getting money. After returning from my sister’s house, I valued the Kṛṣṇa conscious atmosphere at 26 Second Avenue much more. Prabhupāda’s room was as precious to me as the inner sanctums were to the Egyptian kings. It was filled with intense vibrations of his familiar love for us.

I told Prabhupāda about my visit to my sister, and he was glad I had tried, even though I had returned without a donation. Prabhupāda’s compassion was one of his greatest qualities. He had no attraction for characters like us, but he didn’t show it. He only saw the spirit soul within us. That’s the wonderful caliber of a spiritual person. Prabhupāda let us come close to him; he told us about Kṛṣṇa. He treated us so nicely that we wanted to give up our relatives and live with him.

As the weeks went by and I came more to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, I simply couldn’t hide it from my parents. Neither did my parents hide their intolerance. I tried to be casual about it, continuing to tell them news of my job at the welfare office and inquiring about their life in Staten Island, but when they heard I was still seeing the Swami, they didn’t like it. Finally my father telephoned me and said that if I was going to insist on taking part in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, he and my mother didn’t want anything to do with me. I couldn’t believe what he was saying. How was it possible? *Was* it possible that they could terminate our relationship? On this one point—and what was that point anyway, that I was interested in a different religion?—they were prepared to break off all relations with me.

For years they had struggled to maintain a middle-class status, but they knew they would have to continue struggling to keep it. That I was willing to leave their *karmī* religion was to them heresy. It was as if I had done the worst, shocking, scandalous, criminal act. Even though I was their son, they couldn’t tolerate it. What had I done? I had renounced the *karmī* world. For them, however who were staunch adherents to the fruitive path, that was everything.

I felt sad after this conversation, and after hanging up, I cried. Then I went into the temple and chanted. It was the afternoon, and there was no regular program going on. While I chanted, I thought of breaking up with my family, and I cried some. Then I went to see Prabhupāda in his room. I told him I had spoken to my father, and if I remained in Kṛṣṇa consciousness my father did not want to have any more connection with me.

As I sat across from Prabhupāda and informed him of this, he seemed concerned that I was emotional about the incident, and at the same time there seemed to be a kind of distant, almost imperceptible amusement in his eyes. He did not explain much to me, but just by talking with him, I felt assured that I should stay with him. He was my spiritual father.

Talking with Prabhupāda, I thought of how Kṛṣṇa had smiled upon hearing Arjuna’s lamentations just before He had given him transcendental knowledge. At least Prabhupāda could see I was confiding in him. For myself, I saw clearly that he was my guide. He had knowledge, and I was his boy.

In retrospect, I can understand this was Kṛṣṇa’s arrangement. Some senior Godbrothers have also told me this. Nārada’s mother died when he was five so he could advance in spiritual life. Similarly, since becoming a devotee, I have never had any difficulty with attached parents because they mysteriously rejected me. My upbringing was not liberal, with loose democratic relationships between family members. The bond was tight and strong, and I was subservient to my parents. My attachments to my family would have lasted my whole life. After I joined Śrīla Prabhupāda, however, my parents gave me an ultimatum: “Take your choice. Be a devotee, or live with us.”

I said, “All right, I’ll be a devotee.” I stuck to my side and they stuck to their side. After that I realized, more than ever before, that they were dedicated *karmīs; gṛha-vrata* was their religion, not Catholicism.

When I mentioned this family matter to one of my new Kṛṣṇa conscious friends, he said that every devotee had experienced the same thing with their parents, and it was always a cause of distress. I was not the only one. I saw that Prabhupāda sometimes personally dealt with his followers’ parents. A large percentage of his followers were Jewish, and their mothers were usually attached to their sons. (One of the devotees once remarked to Prabhupāda that he had noticed there were many Jewish boys among his followers. Prabhupāda saw the statement humorously and said, “Yes, we are a Jewish concern.”)

After Bruce (Brahmānanda) and his brother joined, their mother came to meet Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda requested her to help his mission by making a donation. She became angry and said, “I have already given you two sons! What more do you want?” Another time the parents of nineteen-year-old Michael (Madhusūdana) came to see Prabhupāda. They were concerned that their son become a doctor rather than a devotee. Prabhupāda told Michael’s father that there were already so many doctors, but devotees of Kṛṣṇa were rare, and the world has a greater need for them than doctors.

Śrīla Prabhupāda also became acquainted with Chuck’s (Acyutānanda’s) mother. She was friendly and was writing a term paper in her psychology class about why young people come to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Her theory was that they were products of broken homes. One time, Chuck’s mother was chatting with Prabhupāda about how she had just attended a meeting in a room where the heat was unbearable. A few minutes later, Prabhupāda expounded to her how material life is full of different kinds of sufferings, and she replied, “No, I don’t think that’s true. Material life isn’t suffering.” Prabhupāda then reminded her that a minute ago, she had described a condition of unbearable suffering.

Prabhupāda said that all the mothers of his followers were actually pious ladies; otherwise they could have not produced sons who later became devotees.

Even while Prabhupāda gave me special treatment, I still had doubts. One evening, in an informal gathering in his room, Prabhupāda was talking about the residents of Vaikuṇṭha, who have four arms. There was an Indian man present, as well as a number of Lower East Side friends at the temple. Prabhupāda had just shaved his head, which was gleaming, giving extra prominence to his artistically-applied Viṣṇu *tilaka* markings. I began to mentally reject that the residents of Vaikuṇṭha could have four arms. It sounded so incredible that it was beyond whether I believed it or not—it was inconceivable. But Prabhupāda’s Hindu guest was nodding patiently, as if Prabhupāda’s description of the residents of Vaikuṇṭha seemed normal to him.

Prabhupāda said that on Kṛṣṇa’s planet, Kṛṣṇa has two arms, and the residents have two arms. This planet is higher than the Vaikuṇṭhas, although in Vaikuṇṭha the liberated residents and Viṣṇu Himself are four-armed. The Indian man also agreed with this statement, but I could only think, “There are no persons with four arms anywhere!” At the same time, seeing Prabhupāda’s smile and gestures, and his effulgent, newly-shaved head as he sat erect in a cross-legged yoga posture, I could see that *he* was inconceivable. Yet he was in our midst, talking confidently about the residents of Viṣṇu’s planet. I knew that if I spoke out and scoffed, “Swamijī, what you say about Vaikuṇṭha is impossible,” he would have had stiff answers for me to explain why it was *certainly* possible, even if I didn’t believe it.

His talk and appearance were a combination of inconceivable and yet realistic existence. This was especially so when he would speak *Bhagavad-gītā* hour after hour in his room after his evening lecture. I knew that Prabhupāda, although dealing in many worldly affairs, was actually a person absorbed in supramundane consciousness. To him, Kṛṣṇa in the eternal spiritual planets, where all the residents enjoyed states of consciousness beyond superego and mental speculation, was more real than the gas stations and monument stores of Second Avenue, or even the Empire State Building.

As he sat before us on his mat with its decorations of little elephants, he waved his hands and widened his eyes and spoke transcendental knowledge. Sometimes he seemed to me like a person I thought existed only in storybooks, who possessed supernatural abilities to fly on carpets or to appear out of Aladdin’s lamp. He was not, however, a storybook character, but our affectionate Swamijī who had created a very real, transcendental nest at 26 Second Avenue, where we were also residents and partakers in higher consciousness.

In the evenings, the room would often be crowded, and I would sit in a corner, observing with awe how Prabhupāda was solidly situated in the inconceivable consciousness in which *yogīs* and saintly persons could do whatever they liked. He could see Kṛṣṇa face to face. I was doubting, but I was also appreciating.

I had other kinds of feelings of resentment and reluctance. Even while sitting together in the noonday comradeship taking Prabhupāda’s rice, *dāl,* and *capātīs* to our hearts’ content, I would become envious that Swamijī had some milk and a banana in addition to what we had. Although he sat amidst us on the same level and partook of the same meal except for those simple additions (themselves a concession to his elderly needs), I harbored such unworthy thoughts. I would mentally kick myself, “Why should I be envious of my spiritual master, who is helping me serve Kṛṣṇa and giving his whole life to his disciples?”

But I had many such envious and unworthy thoughts in the beginning. Sometimes my mind would think of an obscene word or thought while I was in Prabhupāda’s presence, almost in defiance of his purity. At first I was helpless before such occasional obscene mental screamings. There was no recourse except to endure being tortured by the mental voices of opposition. This only added to my fundamental trust in Prabhupāda. The very fact that my opposition took the form of inner cursings and obscenities proved to me that he was pure and had to be protected from the kind of demons that arose within me. I knew, be-cause of my recent indulgence in sinful acts, I would have to suffer for some time from these impurities.

As I became attracted to the activities of Śrīla Prabhupāda and his followers, I began to resent the time I was spending at my job, which kept me away from Prabhupāda’s association for eight hours a day. The other boys, who had no jobs, could see him all day. They would sit around him in his clean, sunny apartment while he talked about Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead. He was asking them to help him spread love of Kṛṣṇa, and together they were making plans. Although I was already pledging all of my money to Prabhupāda to run the temple and apartment, I began to feel I was missing out. I decided I could no longer keep my job. I wanted to join Prabhupāda and renounce the material world with its birth, death, and old age. I decided it would be a further commitment on my part to resign from my work and live as a full-time disciple like the others.

Without consulting Prabhupāda or anyone else, I obtained an official resignation form, filled it out, and handed it to my supervisor, Mr. Rice. I gave the welfare department two weeks notice and stated my reason for resigning: “To study the teachings of the *Bhagavad-gītā* with my guru.” Young people were regularly resigning from the welfare office, so my resignation did not create a commotion, although my unusual reason did raise some eyebrows. During my lunch hour I ran to Prabhupāda. He was seated on his mat behind his small desk, and the boys were gathered around him asking questions and listening.

“I quit my job,” I told him.

“Oh, why is that? You are offering such nice service.” He seemed disappointed in me.

“But I want to come daily and be part of the camp. I want to learn Kṛṣṇa consciousness.”

Seeing my predicament, the boys present said they would take jobs and let me spend more time listening to Prabhupāda. They suggested taking a job and alternating it with each other. Prabhupāda told me a story. There was once a faithful wife who had an ugly husband with a morose disposition. One day his wife asked, “Why are you morose? I do anything you want, and still you feel morose. Why is that?”

“I wish to have sex with a certain prostitute,” he admitted, “but she costs $1,000 just for one night and I cannot afford her.”

The faithful wife said to her ugly husband, “Don’t worry. I shall arrange it.” She immediately went to the prostitute’s house and began personally attending her—cleaning her room and performing other services.
