When the prostitute came home and noticed her activity she asked, “Why are you working here?”

The woman said, “My husband desires to enjoy you, and I hope you will take my services as payment so that he might spend one night with you.”

The prostitute laughed, “Don’t you realize that I cost over $1,000 a night? How can you ever raise that much money?” So, in addition to serving the prostitute, the wife herself turned to prostitution and eventually raised the required amount of money. She turned to her husband and said, “All right, you can go to that prostitute now,” and he gladly went at once.

Prabhupāda stopped and said that although people could criticize the wife as crazy and immoral, nevertheless, she was unquestionably faithful to her husband and tried her best to fulfill his desires. Prabhupāda didn’t have to say more. I got the idea that although I did not think it was spiritually beneficial to work at the welfare office with people whose association was abominable and boring and kept me away from him all day, he thought that by contributing the earnings from my job, I was rendering the best possible service. If that was the service he required from me, I should do it, and that would please Kṛṣṇa.

I reported back to my office and reassured my supervisor that I had changed my mind and did not want to resign. That afternoon, visiting clients on my job, I walked through the streets in the bliss of responsibility. I felt deeply entrusted with a duty from my father, my spiritual master, and I was convinced that to discharge it faithfully was my eternal duty. From then on, I worked at my civil service job with a sense of eternal life, because it was God’s work, as confirmed by my spiritual master.

In my spare time, and even sometimes when I was supposed to be out visiting clients, I would sit at a typewriter in my apartment and type out newly edited sections of Prabhupāda’s *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* translation and commentary. Sometimes I would get restless, but I would keep typing; the difficulty I experienced was an austerity of yoga. I thought I had to sit in front of my typewriter in a yoga position and carefully type Prabhupāda’s manuscripts by the hour.

When the first initiation was to be held, I decided I was not ready. I consoled myself that if I stayed home and did Swamijī’s typing instead of receiving initiation, that would not be so bad. “There’s nothing wrong if I don’t become initiated,” I thought, “this is also a fit engagement.” I knew I should probably have gone to the initiation, but I had withdrawn because of my old neurosis, thinking I was not paid enough personal attention. I also did not want to surrender my life to Kṛṣṇa just yet.

The next day I went to Prabhupāda’s apartment with the manuscripts I had typed. I rang the doorbell, and he personally answered; he was the only one in the apartment. He seemed glad to see me, but he said, “You didn’t come yesterday for initiation.”

“No,” I said.

“That’s all right,” he said kindly, as he let me inside. I said that I had stayed home to type and did not give any further explanation or excuse. When he sat down at his mat I then placed before him a substantial amount of typing, and he thanked me. He then gave me a few large grapes. For a moment I thought, “I have worked so hard, and I am only getting a few large grapes!” But then I kicked my mind as Prabhupāda said, “Kṛṣṇa will bless you.” And he picked up the manuscripts and looked through them approvingly. I knew I needed Kṛṣṇa’s blessings desperately, and I could almost feel the benediction coming immediately. I had sincerely and faithfully wanted those blessings from Kṛṣṇa, and received them with a thrill.

While I was present, Prabhupāda’s phone rang, and he answered it. He began discussing arrangements for a marriage. After he hung up the phone, Prabhupāda said to me, “So, there is a marriage ceremony tomorrow night, and I am personally inviting you to come. Two of my new disciples are to be married.”

I told him I would be happy to come. I felt gratified that he had personally made sure I would attend. He gave me more manuscripts to type, and I began to take my leave.

He walked me to the door of his apartment, and then said, “This is not automatic. Don’t simply take this work and do it mechanically.” I knew what he meant. Then he said, “If you love me, then I’ll love you.” With those words I left him and ran down the stairs and onto the street. I was suddenly so happy. I realized that he loved me just as Kṛṣṇa loves everyone. He had crashed through my neurotic mentality. In my sunken material condition, I had been thinking that no one could love or understand me, but now I felt differently. If I wanted to love Prabhupāda, I had to voluntarily give my love to him, even at the risk of being cheated. But with him there was no risk. He would not cheat me. Still, it was up to me to overcome my doubts, distastes, or whatever reasons I had for withholding myself from completely surrendering. If I loved him, he would love me. I didn’t want to stay rotting and sulking in the material world without Kṛṣṇa. I wanted to have a personal relationship with Kṛṣṇa through his representative and be released from stoneheartedness, false ego, and hallucinations. I knew I could get relief by being loved, but to be loved was only half the exchange. Prabhupāda was loving us—freely giving us *prasādam,* Kṛṣṇa-philosophy, and chanting. But as a person I could also *give* my love, and this love could overcome every obstacle. When I understood this, my desire to take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness greatly increased. I became determined not to let anything hinder me from engaging in transcendental loving service for the pleasure of my spiritual master.

Two weeks after the first initiation I heard that Prabhupāda was holding another initiation. I decided that this time I would not miss out. Prabhupāda was going to hold the second initiation on September 23, 1966, on the appearance day of Rādhārāṇī, Kṛṣṇa’s eternal consort. A week before that date, I went to Prabhupāda’s apartment and asked him if I could be initiated. “You know you’ll have to be a strict vegetarian,” he said.

“Oh,” I replied, “I already am.” Then he said, “Yes, it is all right.”

The other first initiates had already been chanting regularly on their beads. One evening, a few days before my initiation, I asked if we should count the beads as we chant. He said Vaiṣṇavas did have a counting method. He then asked me to purchase small beads and string them and show them to him when I was finished. As soon as he said this, I walked up to Fourteenth Street to Tandy’s and within a couple of hours of his request I returned with the little beads. He laughed and said, “You are very punctual.” The other devotees in the room also laughed. I took it that he was laughing because I exhibited such a minor virtue as punctuality.

I had an extra day off coming to me from the welfare office, and I took it on the day of initiation. Prabhupāda had not requested us to dress in any special way or to cut our hair, but I had decided to get a regular haircut at the barber’s. That day, I also wrote a letter to one of my ex-college professors, who was in Europe, informing her that I was becoming initiated into spiritual life on the occasion of Rādhāṣṭamī, the appearance day of Rādhārāṇī. I did not fully understand initiation and partly thought I was now becoming a Hindu. At least I understood that I was becoming a disciple—of Prabhupāda.

Three other devotees were going to be initiated also: Bruce, Chuck, and Keith. Keith had been at the storefront from the beginning and had become Prabhupāda’s personal cook, but on the occasion of the first initiation he had been detained. Attempting to get money for the temple he had gone to the welfare department and asked for money. Someone there had asked him, “Who told you to come here?”

When he had replied, “God told me,” they thought he was insane. They had not asked him to explain himself further, but told him to go to Bellevue Hospital to get psychoanalytical clearance to prove he was mentally stable. The welfare office then called the hospital to forewarn the staff that a man was coming who claimed he could talk to God. When Keith arrived they put him into an observation ward and detained him. He had therefore missed the first initiation.

Like the others, I had bought large red beads and strung them with a knot between each bead, as Prabhupāda had shown us. I had sewn an old cut-up green shirt into a bead bag, but when Prabhupāda saw it he said, “Get a proper bag.” There were, however, no other bead bags around at that time.

On the evening of the second initiation, Keith put on an Indian *dhotī,* which was the first time any of us had done that. It was made of yellow cloth and after Prabhupāda helped him put it on, he told Keith he looked like a Hindu *brahmacārī.*

Before the ceremony I expressed some of my doubts to Keith. Understanding personalism was hard. With faith, Keith recited to me a verse from the *Bhagavad-gītā:* “All truths are resting on Me as pearls are strung on a thread.” Hearing this, I felt encouraged.

At the initiation ceremony, Prabhupāda chanted on our beads. We handed him our beads and he chanted a full round while we watched him, and then he handed the beads back to us and said, “Bow down.” When my turn came I bowed down before him and then heard him say, “Repeat after me.” One word at a time he recited a prayer that began *“nama.”* I repeated the word *“nama.”* Then he said, *“oṁ.”* I repeated each word until I had recited the whole prayer, which was later translated: “I offer my obeisances to His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, who is very dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa on this earth, having taken shelter at the lotus feet of the transcendental Lord.” When I recited, word by word, the Sanskrit to this prayer, my eyes were closed, and I plunged into feelings of devotion and sincere surrender. I experienced an intense feeling that I was entering eternal life. He gave me my name: Satsvarūpa dāsa.

In a lecture that night, he described the significance of our initiation:

Lord Caitanya asked His disciple Rūpa Gosvāmī to go to Vṛndāvana to preach and sustain His mission. This is disciplic succession. Not that one thinks, “I have understood from my spiritual master. Now let me sit tight.” That is also nice, but Lord Caitanya’s mission is to spread the teaching—it is your duty. When a disciple receives instruction from the spiritual master, he has an obligation to the spiritual master to preach. After being instructed, the disciple will offer, “My dear master, what can I do for you? I am indebted to you for the knowledge you have given me; therefore, I must serve you.”

When I heard this, I was astounded. I hadn’t yet realized what a complete commitment I had made.

Present at the second initiation was Bruce’s mother. She sat on a folding chair in the corner of the room. After the ceremony, in which Prabhupāda had given Bruce the name “Brahmānanda,” Prabhupāda said to him, “Now offer your obeisances to your mother.” He bowed before her, and she seemed embarrassed. Later, Prabhupāda spoke with her in his room before the rest of us. Prabhupāda gave the name “Kīrtanānanda” to Keith and “Acyutānanda” to Chuck.

He didn’t comment on the spiritual significance of my Sanskrit name that night, but a few days later when I was in his room I asked, “Swamijī, what does my name mean?” He said, “The inner truth of reality.” (Later, during Kārttika, we were burning candles on his coffee table. I came in late; everyone was already gathered, burning their candles. When I entered the room, Prabhupāda said, “Oh, here comes the truth personified.”) At my initiation, Prabhupāda told me to chant twenty-five rounds, but shortly afterwards he reduced the number to sixteen. After my initiation I faithfully executed Prabhupāda’s order to chant sixteen rounds a day.

Prabhupāda also gave me some new duties. One of my minor duties was to regularly change the white letters in the sign posted in the front window, which announced the topics of Prabhupāda’s talks. He would give me the name of a topic on a small piece of paper. I would change these topics a couple of times a week.

One day, shortly after initiation, I shaved my head with the help of another devotee. We went to Prabhupāda and showed him our heads. He smiled warmly and said, “Thank you very much.” That was significant to me; he *thanked* us. I felt I had done it for Prabhupāda.

I asked Prabhupāda, “Should I wear *tilaka* to work?”

He said, “Yes,” so I showed up at work, my head shaven with *śikhā* and *tilaka*. This caused quite a stir, and sure enough, the boss called me into his office and said, “What is this?”

I replied to my boss, “It is my religion.”

“I don’t think you should do this,” he said.

But I was persistent: “I have to do it,” and he accepted, because New York City was cosmopolitan. One is supposed to be liberal towards the Puerto Ricans, the blacks, the Jews—and everybody. My boss couldn’t be prejudiced, although he really didn’t like that I was a devotee. He had to accept me.

Rūpānuga also worked in the Department of Welfare, and his boss called him in one day for wearing *tilaka* and carrying beads. His boss told him to stop, but he said, “No, this is my religion. There’s another Kṛṣṇa conscious devotee like me working in the Lower East Branch.” The boss didn’t just accept Rūpānuga’s word but called the branch where I was working. The supervisor told him, “Yes, we’ve got one of them here too.” Rūpānuga’s boss had to accept him too.

I used to follow Prabhupāda around as much as possible. For a while ,I carried the collection basket up to his room after his evening lecture. One time I counted the money in front of him (which amounted to about five dollars) and placed it beside him. Prabhupāda folded his palms before the money and bowed his head. He then looked at me and explained, “Money is the goddess Lakṣmī.”

One night after the lecture, while walking behind him from the temple to the courtyard, I noticed there was a brilliant full moon surrounded by a white aura. I was attracted by this sight and praised the evening moon. Prabhupāda also looked at the moon and then at me with slight distaste. I interpreted that to mean that my attraction to the moon was inferior and especially inappropriate after hearing Prabhupāda’s lecture and *kīrtana*. He wanted us to see the beauty of nature only in connection with Kṛṣṇa. The moon, as beautiful as it may be, is just an insignificant planet in the material universe. When was I going to cast my longing vision upon the planet of Kṛṣṇa?

Shortly after this, Prabhupāda informed Brahmānanda that we should hold our first election of officers for the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. He further suggested that in his opinion, the president should be Brahmānanda. Brahmānanda’s brother should be the treasurer, and I should be the secretary. Brahmānanda came down to the storefront and told the devotees that we should hold an election, and he mentioned Prabhupāda’s opinion. Naturally we voted unanimously in accord with Prabhupāda’s wish, and in this way I became secretary. My new duties were to take minutes of weekly meetings and to sign invoices for all the temple purchases. My main service, however, continued to be my full-time employment at the welfare office, which brought $400 a month for Prabhupāda. I also continued typing for him.

One night Prabhupāda gave me a rough manuscript for his introduction to *Gītopaniṣad.* He said he wanted to immediately mimeograph the essay and distribute it separately from the rest of the *Bhagavad-gītā*. I felt the pressure of the emergency request and therefore stayed up all night typing page by page, working carefully not to make mistakes. I was absorbed in the transcendental subject matter. After I returned the completed work in the morning, I waited to see the completed manuscript rushed into print, but Prabhupāda kept it for a few days before printing it. I understood that I had been given the overnight assignment to practice austerity.

Another austerity I performed during this time was volunteering my apartment for devotees’ use. The devotees had been inconveniencing Prabhupāda by using his bathroom, so I thought they could use my apartment, which was just around the corner from the temple. My apartment immediately turned into an annex of the temple, and devotees regularly came to shower, and one even moved in with me. When I had offered my apartment, I knew I would lose the solitude with which I had protected myself for many years, but it had also been a calculated exchange. I gained the community of the Vaiṣṇavas, and I figured I came out ahead.

Although crazy things happened in the East Village slums of New York City, we were living in a pure state. It was like living in a simple Indian village. The only reality for us was Swamijī and preaching for him.

I would rise early in the morning, chant on my red beads, and walk to the storefront for the class. After the class I usually didn’t see Prabhupāda, and if I did, it was briefly. Then I would walk to work. During my lunch hour, I would return to the temple to see Prabhupāda. I was at the office most of the day, but I had regular contact with Prabhupāda every day, and on Monday, Wednesday, and Friday evenings we attended class. We would gather in his apartment around six o’clock and sit with him while he performed *ārati*, a ceremonial offering to the Lord of food, lamps, flowers, fans and incense. We would then have philosophical exchanges. At seven o’clock we would move to the temple room for the program.

The devotees had formed a community. It was complete and wonderful. We lived together like a family. Sometimes there were squabbles, but Prabhupāda sat in his room on the other side of the window ready to solve any disagreements. We were neophytes; we didn’t know what *bhakti-yoga* was. There wasn’t a “*bhakta* program.” Although we were with Prabhupāda, he wasn’t revealing everything to us at once, but he was cultivating us by letting us render service to him, which was all we knew.

Because of taking on different responsibilities, Prabhupāda used to honor me by allowing me to be consulted when we had to make an important decision for our growing ISKCON. One time, Śrīla Prabhupāda and some of us went uptown to look at a possible new building for our society. After we toured the building, we stood on the street and Prabhupāda asked me what I thought of it. I made a comment about the neighborhood. I felt like a responsible son of the family. I had never had that feeling with my own family.

Prabhupāda made me feel that important things depended on me and that I was expected to do my work and hold up my end of things. He arranged that the maintenance of our society appeared to depend on my service. All I had to do was perform it and my progress was guaranteed. I had begun a utilitarian relationship with Prabhupāda. This was the most solid method of rendering regular service and the most satisfactory basis for exchange, and it was not sentimental. The intimate moments with him were always the essence of sweetness. Sometimes he would call me into his room just to help him sort out some of his personal papers, instructing me to rip up certain things and put other things back into their envelopes. By simply being his instrument for these functions, I would feel entirely satisfied.

One night I found him alone in his room before his type-writer. I had been reading a book about Vaiṣṇavism in which Kṛṣṇa was described as the darling of Nanda. I asked what “darling of Nanda” meant, and he gave me a brief explanation. Then I said that I had just read a statement by Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura that the perfect theist sees the spiritual form of the Lord in the Deity of Kṛṣṇa. Śrīla Prabhupāda patiently heard me, but after some time said, “Now you go down and let me do my work.” I realized that I was simply detaining him from his more important work of translating the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. Still, he considered both personally encouraging us and delivering to us the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* as equally important.

Because I typed for Prabhupāda, I was sometimes in his room at late hours. One night, after everyone had left, I was still typing when he came in and took his late-night *prasādam.* He took a bowl of puffed rice with milk. While he ate his *prasādam,* he looked over at me with a surprised and meaningful look, and his eyes widened. Prabhupāda’s expression was vivid, but it was not easy to know exactly what he was thinking. I thought this expression meant that he was surprised that at this late hour I was still typing in his room. I was showing enthusiasm, and as a result I gained a glimpse of him in his more private moments.

The Sunday feasts, which Prabhupāda had introduced, became a regular happening and a great success. Prabhupāda had us charge money at the door. Both Kīrtanānanda and Acyutānanda knew how to cook, and they prepared “Brahmin Spaghetti”, *halavah, gulābjāmons, puṣpaṇa* rice, sweet rice, and vegetable dishes. They were excellent feasts. Honoring the feast was an intense experience for the devotees. In the beginning it was a considerable struggle to subdue our senses, follow the regulations, and rise early, and the Sunday feast was a kind of reward of spiritual austerity. Before taking the feast, I would make an intense prayer of resolve to the Lord: “Please let me remain in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Let me serve.” Sunday feasts were times of rededication and the enjoyment of transcendental pleasure, and we did enjoy the feasts with open relish and spiritual sense gratification.

Seeing the guests eat was very wonderful. We didn’t mind in those days, however, if no guests came, because *we* would feast like anything anyway.

During the week, Prabhupāda gave us permission to keep a big bowl of *gulābjāmons* in his room, and we were able to go at any time of the day to that pot and take a couple of *gulābjāmons—*just to keep ourselves going in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Prabhupāda was well aware of the attraction of these milk sweets, and he called them “ISKCON bullets” or sometimes “golden bullets.” He would even refer to them in his lectures.

The Sunday afternoon *kīrtanas* in Tompkins Square Park had become legendary. They began in October 1966. We got permission from the Department of Parks to hold a *kīrtana* every Sunday for three hours in the afternoon. Some of the devotees would go there before Prabhupāda arrived. We set out a large oriental rug and a harmonium, sat down, and began chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. Kīrtanānanda, who had been the first to wear a *dhotī* and shave his head, was no longer the only one. I remember I also dressed like that. In those days it was extremely innovative to walk in such a costume, but this was our way of life, our religion. We thought there was nothing more exciting than *kīrtana,* and we expected that other people would at least respond.

Several people gathered around us as we chanted. Then Śrīla Prabhupāda arrived with a few devotees. He walked the blocks from the temple to the park, which was about a ten-minute walk. Then he sat down and joined us. He played drum himself, and he chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa for at least an hour. The rhythms would rise and fall like the waves in the ocean, and we would rise and fall with them. He would lead, we would respond, and then he would lead again. Sometimes I would look up to him, sometimes at the crowd, which grew to many people, and the chanting never stopped. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s voice was strong. He kept the same tune, and we kept chanting as if we would never stop: Hare Kṛṣṇa Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa Hare Hare/ Hare Rāma Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma Hare Hare.

At least on a couple of occasions, Allen Ginsberg and his friends and other young people of that hip culture, as well as the non-hip immigrants and young children of Polish and Puerto Rican extract, joined in chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa.

After chanting for a long time, Śrīla Prabhupāda would get up and give a talk. He spoke in a voice that could hardly be heard except by the few people nearest him as he had no microphone. He introduced a few philosophical concepts about the chanting and then asked people to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. The great majority of the people there couldn’t hear him or else had difficulty understanding his accent. After speaking briefly, Prabhupāda again led the *kīrtana* with his drum.

Later, Śrīla Prabhupāda often recalled how Brahmānanda and Acyutānanda were the first to dance at these Tompkins Square Park *kīrtanas.* Sometimes he would also ask the rest of us to dance. He once looked over at me and gestured with his head that I should dance. At first I thought he must have been gesturing to someone behind me. I looked behind, then looked at him again, and Śrīla Prabhupāda repeated the gesture, which was undoubtedly meant for me. On his direct but unspoken order, I got up and danced. Tompkins Square Park was one of the few places where some of my old friends came and saw me. They seemed to be observing me sarcastically, but I didn’t care. I got up wearing my robes and danced with my hands in the air before all the people. When the *kīrtana* was over, we dispersed and walked back to the temple with Prabhupāda.

After our first *kīrtana* there, the *New York Times* had carried an article with a picture of us. Śrīla Prabhupāda took that as a milestone. Later, we also received coverage in the local newspaper, the *East Village Other.* We were on the front page with the headline: “Save Earth Now,” and a picture of Prabhupāda standing and speaking by a tree in Tompkins Square Park. Prabhupāda had us read this article to him aloud in his room, and he especially appreciated that the writer had said, “Most people say God is dead, but the Swami on the Lower East Side shows us that He’s very much alive.” Prabhupāda appreciated the newspaper writer’s recognition that we were alive and chanting. We had been chanting in the park the day the *East Village Other* newspaper article had appeared. After the *kīrtana,* a wave of interested people came back to the storefront. Prabhupāda spoke from the *vyāsāsana,* and he had us distribute cups of sweet rice to everyone who came. We felt Kṛṣṇa consciousness was a new movement with good potential for gaining a strong following among young people.

Around this time, the poet Allen Ginsberg took more interest in our movement and donated some money. He became a kind of patron, and his interest in us was to our advantage. I remember being with Śrīla Prabhupāda after Allen Ginsberg had visited, and Prabhupāda asked us to understand that he was prophylactic, not affected or contaminated by Allen Ginsberg's association.

Prabhupāda wanted us to get a good typewriter, an IBM. One devotee heard that IBM would donate a typewriter to an educational institution. Prabhupāda told me, “Yes, go there and try to get that typewriter. Tell them we are an educational institution.” Of course, if an IBM representative investigated 26 Second Avenue, he wouldn’t find a big school, but I went to IBM and spoke to a company representative. He said, “You are from a school—Kṛṣṇa consciousness? What is it? What is the subject?”

I said, “Well . . . ”

Then he understood. “Oh,” he said, “it’s a religion. We can’t give to religious organizations. This is for bona fide schools.”

I said, “But we *are* studying in Kṛṣṇa consciousness; everything is included. In different schools the students study mathematics, philosophy, or science, the source of all knowledge is the Absolute Truth. In that sense, Kṛṣṇa consciousness is not a religion. It’s the king of all knowledge. If you actually have a program for giving to educational institutions, we qualify.”

The man I was talking to became perplexed. He brought me to his boss, and I talked with him. He said they would come by to visit us. but they never came. Prabhupāda was just giving us another chance to preach to nondevotees.

Prabhupāda made up a saying that those who didn’t take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness were “simply rascals.” He said we should make up a button that says “S.R.” on it, and when we meet someone who doesn’t believe in God or Kṛṣṇa, we can give them this button and say, “Here, you are S.R.” Sometimes Prabhupāda referred to someone as an “S.R. Man.” This was our inside language.

Śrīla Prabhupāda had great concern for his new society. One time we were in the storefront, and Śrīla Prabhupāda walked by himself from his apartment to the storefront (a rare occurrence). He had come because he had seen a light on in the bathroom. He taught us by his example. Another time, one boy staying in the temple was found to have marijuana. We told him to leave the temple, but the next day he came back to the front door while Prabhupāda was giving class. Śrīla Prabhupāda had a concerned look on his face when he saw the boy and said our institution had to be protected.

When Prabhupāda started *Back To Godhead* in November, he gave the editing to the most prominent literary devotees. In the back of my mind, I thought it would be nice if I was one of them, but I couldn’t claim to have the credentials they had. Hayagrīva was a college English professor and Rāyarāma was a professional writer for comic books. They both wanted to be editor, so Prabhupāda made them co-editors. Rāyarāma encouraged me to write.

Before Śrīla Prabhupāda started *Back To Godhead,* I mentioned to Hayagrīva that since I used to write out of false ego, I shouldn’t write any more. He said, “Oh, I am not doing that,” and he kind of laughed. “I am going to write like anything about Kṛṣṇa consciousness.” I thought the purest thing was not to write at all because it involved my false ego. Shortly afterwards, however, Prabhupāda began his magazine. I retained my propensity to write, so I began writing again—but without false ego. My first essays were “Experience of *Karma Yoga.*” I became a contributing writer for *Back To Godhead.*

As early as my teens I had been a disciplined writer. I could spend an entire day writing. I would revise material, write, type, try to form a story with a beginning, middle, and end. The discipline of writing, regulated habits, and attraction to a straightforward use of language—these were a kind of preparation Kṛṣṇa had given me so that one day I could write something for Kṛṣṇa.

One day I again met one of my former friends outside Prabhupāda’s storefront. He had heard that I was not interested in my old literary pursuits but was writing for *Back To Godhead.* “So,” he said, “you’re working on the Swami’s magazine, huh?” I could tell by his tone of voice that he was ridiculing me. We were supposed to be free, independent spirits, but my search had ended and now I was writing like a menial servant for an Indian swami.

But I was determined, even if my old friends laughed at me. I said, “Yes. You’re right. This is where I wound up, working on the Swami’s magazine”—and I thought to myself, “Yes, whether you like it or not. I may not be an avant-garde writer by your estimation, but I don’t care because I’m helping the Swami.”

I was loyal to Prabhupāda, and I was prepared to tell anyone that I was his disciple. Previously, I had been searching for my actual identity. Now I had not just philosophical understanding of my eternal identity, but I had an occupation to experience it. “What am I? Am I a writer?” Now I knew what I was.

In addition to *Back To Godhead,* Prabhupāda produced a little pamphlet, “The Peace Formula,” which was in tune with the peace movement and the different peace marches that were held in New York City. Prabhupāda stated the profound peace formula of the *Bhagavad-gītā*, that everything belongs to God; He is the well-wisher and the maintainer of everything. If the people of the world would recognize that the world belongs to God and read the *Bhagavad-gītā*, they would have peace.

We distributed BTG and “The Peace Formula” on the street, and soon after, we printed two more pamphlets, “Krishna, The Reservoir of Pleasure,” and “Who Is Crazy?” We had good luck selling these on the streets. I would go to St. Mark’s, stand in front of the movie theaters and hawk as loud as I could: “Get your copy of *Back To Godhead*! New magazine of spiritual life. This issue contains an article by Allen Ginsberg on chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa.” I would sell a small number of copies, and that gave Prabhupāda pleasure.

In December we recorded the *Happening* album. One night, Prabhupāda and some of the devotees went to a studio in upper Manhattan. We had to wait a while for a group of young men to finish their recording before we could use the studio. Prabhupāda had borrowed a big *mṛdaṅga* for the occasion, and we had Allen Ginsberg’s harmonium. Kīrtanānanda played the four notes of the tamboura, and another devotee played the harmonium as a drone. A couple of us played *karatālas,* and Prabhupāda sang and played the drum. After our first *kīrtana,* the studio producers asked us to sing again; something was wrong with the balance. I remember thinking that it was going to take many revisions to make our record, but the studio men were pleased with the second version, and they said they would use it. However, we still had to make the other side of the record. Some of us dispersed, and Prabhupāda sat with just a few devotees and sang for the other side. He also spoke the message he had written, the “Hare Kṛṣṇa Address.”

Mr. Kallman, who was helping us produce the record, asked Prabhupāda after he made the first side whether he was tired, and Prabhupāda said no, but after the second address, Prabhupāda said, “Now we are tired.” And it was an exhaustive evening for all of us. The studio men played our recording back, and we sat and listened. Then Śrīla Prabhupāda got up, raised his hands, and began to dance, stepping back and forth. We also got up, raised our arms, and began dancing around the studio, joining in on the chanting that was being replayed over the studio speakers.

Although I was twenty-six, I looked to Prabhupāda as my father and tried to please him as his son. He was old enough to have been my grandfather, and this was very favorable for my spiritual development. I had never known anyone that old or that wise in my family. Now my parents had rejected me completely, so Prabhupāda was also my father. Within a few months of meeting him, I knew I had to dedicate my life to Prabhupāda, my eternal guru.

During these months, one devotee said to me, “I’ve always been looking for the topmost thing, the topmost knowledge, so I accept the Swami. That’s why I’m here. But if I later see something else as the topmost knowledge, then I’ll accept that.”

I thought, “What? You shouldn’t say there is a possibility of something else.” I was sold on Kṛṣṇa consciousness and Prabhupāda.

Another time I was walking with a different devotee. He said, “So this is our spiritual life, and we have a guru, and in the future, maybe we’ll have another guru.” Although I didn’t have a complete philosophical understanding of why I should accept only one guru, I said, “How can you have another guru?” I thought the idea was obnoxious. “No,” I continued, “that’s wrong. Swamijī is our guru. For us there is no other guru.” I quickly became loyal to Śrīla Prabhupāda.

To some degree, the *Bhāgavatam* will always remain theoretical to me, but my guru is not theoretical because I’ve seen guru—and not just an ordinary guru; I’ve seen the best quality of guru. Since that’s my experience with my spiritual master, I can’t help passing it on to my disciples. It’s in my heart. I cannot be a guru according to an institutionalized version or a group decision of what a guru should be. I cannot minimize the importance of the spiritual master in the lives of my disciples while knowing how important Prabhupāda is in my life. The spiritual master and his disciples form a loving, spiritual family, just as we had with Prabhupāda on 26 Second Avenue.

By the end of December, Prabhupāda had received an invitation to visit San Francisco. I have described in *Planting the Seed* how the devotees in New York felt about his proposed trip:

Although in his letter Mukunda had enclosed a plane ticket, some of Swamijī’s followers refused to accept that Swamijī would use it. Those who knew they could not leave New York began to criticize the idea of Swamijī going to San Francisco. They didn’t think that people out on the West Coast could take care of Swamijī properly. Swamijī appearing with rock musicians? Those people out there didn’t seem to have the proper respect. Anyway, there was no suitable temple there. There was no printing press, no *Back To Godhead* magazine. Why should Swamijī leave New York to attend a function like *that* with strangers in California? How could he leave them behind in New York? How could their spiritual life continue without him? Timidly, one or two dissenters indirectly expressed some of these feelings to Prabhupāda, as if almost wishing to admonish him for thinking of leaving them, and even hinting that things would not go well, either in San Francisco or New York, if he departed. But they found Prabhupāda quite confident and determined. He did not belong to New York, he belonged to Kṛṣṇa, and he had to go wherever Kṛṣṇa desired him to preach. Prabhupāda showed a spirit of complete detachment, eager to travel and expand the chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa.

I was of the party who was skeptical about his going to San Francisco. We talked amongst ourselves. “Why should Prabhupāda go? The devotees in San Francisco are making plans for him to attend some dance hall with rock 'n' roll musicians. We don’t ask Prabhupāda to go to such functions in New York. Maybe they don’t appreciate him.” We were attached to Prabhupāda and attempted to somehow convince him to stay in New York, but when we saw he definitely wanted to go, we accepted. In *kṛṣṇa-līlā,* the *gopīs* thought of Kṛṣṇa in Mathurā. They understood Mathurā was a higher-class society than Vṛndāvana. They were sorry, in a sense, for themselves and their relationship with Kṛṣṇa, but they also thought it wasn’t right that He had left them.

In the last days of the second week of January 1967, final plane reservations were made, and the devotees began packing Prabhupāda’s manuscripts away in trunks. Raṇcora, a new devotee recruited from Tompkins Square Park, had collected enough money for a plane ticket, and the devotees decided he should accompany Prabhupāda as his personal assistant. Prabhupāda explained that he would only be gone a few weeks and that he wanted all the programs to go on in his absence.

On the day Prabhupāda departed for San Francisco, devotees went into his room. Brahmānanda sat next to Prabhupāda, and other devotees gathered around. For a while I stood in the next room, by the window, looking outside at Second Avenue. I felt separation from Prabhupāda, even though he was in the next room. Somehow I didn’t desire to join the other devotees who were talking to Prabhupāda. I thought, “That’s not my place, in the room with him. Yet I feel I *am* with him. This is *my* relationship, serving him in separation.” I didn’t feel soured or offensive; rather, I felt completely dedicated to him. I didn’t feel isolated or distant from him; I loved him. It was a positive feeling, but one of separation.

I describe Śrīla Prabhupāda’s departure for San Francisco in *Planting the Seed:*

He waited in his room while the boys arranged for a car to take him to the airport. The day was gray and cold, and steam hissed in the radiators. He would take only a suitcase—mostly clothes and some books. He checked the closet to see that his manuscripts were in order. Kīrtanānanda would take care of his things in his apartment. He sat down at his desk where, for more than six months, he had sat many times, working for hours at the typewriter preparing his *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, and where he sat talking to so many guests and to his followers. But today he would not be talking with friends or typing a manuscript, but waiting a last few minutes alone before his departure.  
Prabhupāda looked at his watch. He put on his tweed winter coat and his hat and shoes, put his right hand in his bead bag, and continued chanting. He walked out of the apartment, down the stairs, and through the courtyard, which was now frozen and still, its trees starkly bare without a single leaf remaining. And he left the storefront behind.  
He left, even while Brahmānanda, Rūpānuga, and Satsvarūpa were at their office jobs. There was not even a farewell scene or a farewell address.

## 2: 1967, January 19 - December 30

## January 1967

I have begun my story with my spiritual birth in July 1966, and I have discussed some highlights. During the fall of 1966, there was no correspondence between Śrīla Prabhupāda and any of his few disciples since they were all with him daily. This also reveals to us that communicating through letters is a crucial part of serving the spiritual master in separation. When one is with his spiritual master every day there is usually no need of writing him letters. My letters from Śrīla Prabhupāda tell a story of service in separation.

In telling this story, I will attempt to relate honestly the situation in which I exchanged letters with Prabhupāda. I will have to reveal some of my faults and misunderstandings in executing devotional service as a neophyte disciple under the direction of my spiritual master. In any case, the disciple appears as a fool before his spiritual master; and even Lord Caitanya, although experiencing the highest ecstasies of love of God, placed Himself as a fool before His spiritual master. These letters, which start from the beginning of my spiritual life, show that I made some mistakes. It was proper that I saw myself then (it’s also proper that I see myself now) as a student receiving correction from Śrīla Prabhupāda. There is really nothing wrong when a father helps his young child who is just be-ginning to walk, but who sometimes slips. This is the very nature of the sweet mellows between a parent and his little child. The parent is also pleased with whatever little advances the child makes.

Let me therefore proceed with this autobiographical commentary of my relationship with Śrīla Prabhupāda. Our text is the letters I received from Prabhupāda. I shall present one letter at a time, and then I shall give background commentary. I hope this will bring out Śrīla Prabhupāda’s wonderful qualities even more, and surely I will become purified by remembering my fortunate connection with Śrīla Prabhupāda in my otherwise hopeless existence.

\* \* \*

January 19, 1967

My dear Brahmananda, Hayagriva, Kirtanananda, Satsvarupa, Gargamuni, Achyutananda, Jadurani,

Please accept my greetings and blessings of Guru Gouranga Giridhari Gandharvika. You have already got the news of our safe arrival and good reception by the devotees here. Mr. Allen Ginsberg and about fifty or sixty others received us at the airport, and when I arrived in my apartment there were some press reporters also who took note of my mission. Two or three papers like the *Examiner* and the *Chronicle,* etc. have published the report. One of the reports is sent herewith. Please find. I wish that 1,000 copies of this report may be offset printed at once, and 100 copies of the same may be sent here as soon as possible.

I understand that you are feeling my absence. Krishna will give you strength. Physical presence is immaterial; presence of transcendental sound received from the spiritual master should be the guidance of life. That will make our spiritual life successful. If you feel very strongly about my absence, you may place my picture on my sitting places, and this will be a source of inspiration for you.

Sriman Rayrama is cooking well and distributing prasadam to the devotees, numbering sometimes seventy. It is very encouraging. I think this center will be a very nice branch without delay. Everything is prospective. Hope you are all well, and I am awaiting your early reply.

Yours affectionately,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is a group letter and I am one of the recipients. I have described how the devotees in New York City received this letter in the third volume of Prabhupāda’s biography:

On January 19, just three days after his arrival in San Francisco, Prabhupāda had written back to his New York disciples. They were his spiritual children and were very dear to him. Although far from his homeland, India, he hadn’t thought first of writing anyone there. Since he was a *sannyāsī,* he had no interest in writing to any family members or relatives. And as for writing Godbrothers, there was not much importance in that, since they had repeatedly shown their reluctance to help. But being in a new city among new faces and having met with an initial fanfare of success, Prabhupāda had wanted to share the news with those most eager to hear from him. He had also wanted to reassure his disciples whom, after only a few months of training, he was expecting to conduct the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in New York.

The letter had helped—especially the second paragraph. Brahmānanda had posted it in the storefront. Now Swamijī had clearly enunciated that they were still with him and he was still in New York with them. It was something special—service to the spiritual master in separation—and even the devotees in San Francisco, who were with Swamijī every day, could not yet know its special taste. While the devotees in New York performed their daily duties, they often quoted from the letter and thought about it: “Krishna will give you strength. Physical presence is immaterial; presence of the transcendental sound received from the spiritual master should be the guidance of life. That will make our spiritual life successful.”

Although Prabhupāda had written that they could place his photograph on his seat, no one had a photograph. They had to ask the devotees in San Francisco for one. A boy took some poor color snapshots and sent them to New York, and the devotees placed one at Prabhupāda’s sitting place in his apartment. It helped.

For Prabhupāda also, the letters to his disciples in New York marked a milestone. This was the basis on which he hoped to conduct a world movement. He could travel from place to place and yet be simultaneously pre-sent in many places by his instructions.

Brahmānanda, as president of the New York temple, frequently phoned San Francisco. “The chanting is the focal point,” he told Hayagrīva. “We can always sit down and chant. We’re beginning to understand what Swamijī meant when he said that worship in separation is more relishable.”

—Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta Volume 3, pp. 52–3

Śrīla Prabhupāda had left us with the responsibility of managing the New York temple in his absence. Part of that meant we had to give evening classes on the *Bhagavad-gītā* to the visitors who would come to the storefront. I have described this and the first Kṛṣṇa conscious lecture I gave in an article I wrote for *Back To Godhead* entitled “Second Birth”:

Prabhupāda had instructed us how to lecture in his absence, so the first night after he left, one of his more advanced pupils gave a talk. He cited in his talk the same examples that Prabhupāda gave, and it was satisfying. He simply tried to present the teachings of *Bhagavad-gītā*. After the boy’s talk, when we chanted, it was just as transcendentally satisfying as if Śrīla Prabhupāda was there. In fact, it was more poignant for his students; there was ecstasy in the feeling of separation.

On another night I was chosen to speak. I remember looking at the people who had gathered and praying to God for the courage to be able to say something pure and effective and not let down my spiritual master and my assembled Godbrothers and Godsisters—and to actually impart the Absolute Truth to the audience. After all, we students knew the great value of these teachings of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but all the others did not. So it was up to me to present them. I prayed to cast off all desire to show off my own learning; I wanted just to say what my spiritual master was actually teaching. This left me no room at all to speculate or concoct. I attempted to remember what he had said. For me personally, it was a very grave occasion.

As I spoke, I said, “Kṛṣṇa can be compared to the sun. The sun is a planet, a material thing in one place in the sky, and its energy, the sunshine, is diffused, spread out all over the universe. This is a material example. Similarly, Kṛṣṇa is the source of all energies and all emanations and creations. That is the definition of the Absolute Truth—that from which everything is coming. The Absolute Truth is intelligent and conscious, and according to the Vedic literature, He is a person, the Supreme Person, God—Kṛṣṇa. Just as the sun has a form and is local, so Kṛṣṇa has a form. But His form has inconceivable energies. All spiritual and material existence is His energy. We say that God is great, but how great He is, is inconceivable. But we can study this in the Vedic literature to get some idea of the greatness of the Supreme Person.”

As I spoke I became somewhat reassured and tried to remember more. But I felt inadequate and understood that I was not a very good disciple. I was not able to speak more than five or six minutes before I was unable to go on. So I stopped. The advanced disciples who heard my talk said it was nice, especially because I had not deviated from what our spiritual master had said, or hadn’t made anything up. This made me happy, and yet, at the same time, I did not think I had done anything at all, because actually I had not. It was not my wonderful additions, my brain power, my good looks, or my speaking ability that had made my little talk acceptable; it was not within my power to make people begin to think about Kṛṣṇa and to take the philosophy seriously—but it was in the power of His Divine Grace, and I had transmitted some of it.

After the talk there was a question. Someone asked, “How do you *know* that Kṛṣṇa is God?” Remembering what Śrīla Prabhupāda had said in answer to that question, I answered, “How do you know who is the President of the United States? By his credentials. He can show you that he actually has that position. In the same way, all the Vedic literatures describe that Kṛṣṇa, who appeared as the son of Devakī and performed many wonderful acts, is actually the Supreme Brahman, or the Personality of Godhead. It is stated in the *Bhagavad-gītā* by Kṛṣṇa’s disciple, Arjuna, that not only did he accept Kṛṣṇa as God, but Kṛṣṇa is accepted by all the great spiritual masters in disciplic succession, such as Nārada and Vyāsadeva. Also, when Kṛṣṇa appeared on the earth He displayed all the opulences of the Personality of Godhead—full strength, beauty, knowledge, fame, wealth, and renunciation.”

After answering the question, I invited the audience to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. I had preserved the format of my spiritual master, and I had preserved his words, which are the words of the *Bhagavad-gītā*.

After Śrīla Prabhupāda left for San Francisco, there was a feeling of emptiness: he was not in his sitting room, he wasn’t holding *ārati,* the apartment was dark. Although we followed the schedule he gave us, it didn’t seem the same. We continued printing our essays and poems in *Back to Godhead,* and among ourselves we talked about Kṛṣṇa. We reluctantly acknowledged that there was another branch now, which Prabhupāda liked. I had my job, so I had no time for being idle. I went on with that, I went on with my rounds, and life went on in separation. Separation is natural. We survived, and even increased.

\* \* \*

January 30, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I understand that Neal has left us and you have taken the responsibility for typing the records. Please let me know how many tapes are with you. I think you have five tapes with you because I have only got three with me. See that the tapes do not miss. You are a sincere devotee of the Lord, and certainly He will bless you with auspicious advancement in the matter of spiritual understanding.

I hope you are in full cooperation in the service of Krishna. I shall be glad to hear from you. Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

As a complete beginner I was thrilled, and my life was changed by one statement in this letter: “You are a sincere devotee of the Lord, and certainly He will bless you with auspicious advancement in the matter of spiritual understanding.”

There may sometimes be a tendency for devotees, both new and old, to take Prabhupāda’s instructions as commonplace. This is because devotees are accustomed to regularly hearing them in his letters, lectures, and purports. Similarly, although the holy name is the rarest thing, devotees may take chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa for granted because they chant *kīrtana* every day. One of the advantages, however, of being at the beginning stages of spiritual life is that the devotee is usually fresh and keen to hear from the spiritual master; the danger of contemptuous familiarity had not yet entered that relationship.

It is a common phenomenon that when one begins to learn anything, he makes advancement by leaps and bounds compared to what he knew before; further improvement takes time. If a man doesn’t know anything about playing golf, the first time he goes through a course he’ll make great advancement over his complete ignorance of the game. However, he still has to get his score down to par. That may be difficult for him, and his progress will not be as quick between each successive game as it was from complete ignorance to the first game. Since I was beginning my spiritual life, I did not regard this statement as ordinary or trite; it was very important for my spiritual development.

Prabhupāda was not simply making a general statement. He was answering a question I wrote him in a letter. At the time we did not have Prabhupāda’s translation of the *Bhagavad-gītā*. We had attended his *Bhagavad-gītā* classes, where under his instruction we had been reading the verses translated by Dr. Radhakrishnan. Prabhupāda had been there commenting on Radhakrishnan’s translations in his lectures, and we had been trying to understand the *Bhagavad-gītā* from those lectures. I accepted *Bhagavad-gītā* as transcendental knowledge. I could also perceive that the subject matter is subtle, and to understand it requires concentrated, subtle perceptive power (or intellectual power) but in a spiritual way. That is, by revelation or by the blessing of Kṛṣṇa.

Prabhupāda told us that unless one drank milk, the finer tissues of one’s brain would not develop to enable one to understand the transcendental topics. This implies that one has to live in goodness, like a *brahmacārī,* to understand this valuable subject. People dulled by sense gratification who attend a *Bhagavad-gītā* lecture will not even be able to understand the discussion. They have no idea what Brahman is, and they cannot understand the virtues of transcendental life.

I was yearning to understand the subtle, spiritual subject matter of the *Bhagavad-gītā*. I was an infant in spiritual life. With the innocence and confusion of an infant, but with full trust in my spiritual father, I approached him by letter and asked whether I could understand transcendental knowledge. This question is similar to the first question I asked Śrīla Prabhupāda when we were alone in his room: “Is there a level of spiritual advancement one can make from which he doesn’t fall back?”

A disciple’s question must be philosophically relevant to his advancement; that is the essence of inquiry. A disciple must also have the innocence of a child who asks his parents where he came from, or why the sky is blue, and then fully accepts his parents’ answers as knowledge. One has no real relationship with his spiritual master if one doesn’t accept his answers with full faith. Thus we have the statement:

*yasya deve parā bhaktir*  
*yathā deve tathā gurau*  
*tasyaite kathitā hy arthaḥ*  
*prakāśante mahātmanaḥ*

**Only to he who has implicit faith in the guru and Kṛṣṇa are the truths of the** ***Vedas*** **revealed. —Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad**

I was pleased and enthused to think that I was a sincere devotee. At that time, January 1967, what had I done? I had not done anything to be considered sincere, except for executing a few typing tasks and giving money. To be called a sincere devotee was, to me, high praise, and coming from Śrīla Prabhupāda I valued it deeply. Whatever Prabhupāda wrote was read by all of us who were living in the storefront—not only the person Prabhupāda directly praised or criticized in his letters—and all of it was regarded as most important. Although we were young students at that time, we did not think he was just speaking rhetoric. We had a great appreciation for Śrīla Prabhupāda and knew that he was close to Kṛṣṇa, that he was a grave personality, and that whatever he said was the absolute truth.

When I got this letter, I treasured it with all my heart—Prabhupāda said I was a sincere devotee! I could also understand the instruction: because he said I was sincere, Kṛṣṇa would therefore bless me with auspicious advancement. I knew that I could then understand the subtle subject matter of the *Bhagavad-gītā;* what had to me seemed difficult to understand, I now felt I could realize in the future. The key to that understanding would be my sincere service as a devotee, which meant my service to Śrīla Prabhupāda.

As for the other items in this letter, Śrīla Prabhupāda was reciprocating with me on a practical basis because I was transcribing his tapes. Neal, a student from Antioch College, came in 1966 to type some of Prabhupāda’s *Bhagavad-gītā* tapes as part of a work-study program. He typed quickly (two tapes a day), and that inspired Prabhupāda to also work quickly, but he was notorious because he left out all the Sanskrit and some of the English from the transcripts. When Prabhupāda went to San Francisco, Neal was supposed to join him, but he didn’t show up. Eventually he did come to the temple, but then he again disappeared. He wasn’t a real devotee.

Prabhupāda turned to me. I remember standing in the storefront reading a letter he had written another devotee. At the bottom he said, “So give the tapes to Satsvarūpa, and tell him to do the needful.” The devotees read it and laughed at the expression, partly because Prabhupāda referred to me as a menial servant who was expected to do his chores. Yet I experienced a warm feeling that only devotees could understand: it was nice to be an insignificant, trusted servant.

I hadn’t stopped my typing, but continued to type regularly in my apartment. Prabhupāda had kept thousands of single-spaced typed pages of the *Bhāgavatam* in his closet. Hayagrīva had been editing, and I had been re-typing them, but now I began typing the tapes Prabhupāda had sent from San Francisco. I used the Dictaphone in Prabhupāda’s room, making sure I took good care of the machine because if it were to become damaged, I knew Prabhupāda would be angry. He used to say, “You Americans, you rich men’s sons, if something like an expensive machine breaks, you throw it in the street and buy a new one; that is your idea. That is not good.” He also liked us to keep it covered when not in use.

I worked in the room outside Prabhupāda’s main sitting room at 26 Second Avenue. Many things went on at the same time in that room: one devotee painted pictures of Kṛṣṇa; Brahmānanda sat on the floor and examined invoices or talked and quarreled with his brother, Gargamuni; Kīrtanānanda worked nearby in the kitchenette. That’s also where the large jar of *gulābjāmuns* was kept, and devotees could come and take one whenever they wanted. Using earphones, I typed in one corner. It was exciting to hear incredible things that no one had ever heard. At the time, Prabhupāda was working on *Teachings of Lord Caitanya.* Sometimes I would stop the tape and tell the others what Prabhupāda was saying.

In Prabhupāda’s absence I was hankering to be with him. There was no question of going to San Francisco, however, so to be able to hear Prabhupāda speak was wonderful. I didn’t think reluctantly, “Well, let me make this sacrifice. *Someone* has to do it.” But I *loved* to be able to hear Prabhupāda. I greedily took the service, and once I took it, I didn’t give it up for years. Even after I was temple president (when I shouldn’t have been typing because I had too many other duties), I was dependent on regularly hearing his voice speaking into the Dictaphone. By typing his tapes, I learned the philosophy and produced manuscripts, and to serve Prabhupāda in this way was a great honor for me.

In this letter Prabhupāda refers to responsibility. As I mentioned earlier in the first chapter, in the beginning of my relationship with Śrīla Prabhupāda, before the exchange of letters began, I thrived on responsibility. My main responsibility was to keep my job and, along with Brahmānanda, give money to maintain the New York center. My other responsibility was to type Prabhupāda’s lectures, and later on I had the responsibility of typing his book dictations. During this time, he was recording tapes of his *Teachings of Lord Caitanya* and sending them to me from San Francisco.

Based on these experiences, I still preach to devotees about the necessity of responsibility, although it may to be menial. Typing nowadays is not considered an important service, but back then, typing was needed, since Prabhupāda’s main service was book production and there were few available typists or typewriters. Thus, my having a typewriter and being able to type was important. I could type, and I could give money—that was about all I could do—but Prabhupāda accepted these offerings as solid contributions. I accepted each responsibility he gave me as a hungry person accepts food. It helped solidify my link with my spiritual master.

Prabhupāda had a number of tapes in his possession (about nine), and he would rotate them. He always knew how many tapes I had and how many were in the mail. This was Śrīla Prabhupāda’s typical manner; he kept accounts of everything Kṛṣṇa gave him with great care. Thus he writes, “See that the tapes do not miss.” He did not want to lose any transcendental dictations.

This letter states the practical substance of my service: the responsibility of typing Prabhupāda’s tapes. (And Śrīla Prabhupāda also states that the transcendental result of such service; that is, Kṛṣṇa will gradually consider a devotee sincere and bless him. By the blessings of the spiritual master, one gets the blessings of Kṛṣṇa.)

In the last sentence of this letter, Prabhupāda did not simply sign off with formal rhetoric, but he gave another grave instruction, reminding me to be “in full cooperation in the service of Kṛṣṇa.” “Cooperation” meant he wanted me to get along with my Godbrothers. We know the propensity of Americans, even those practicing spiritual life, to fight with one another. Just as when a teacher leaves the classroom he does not know exactly how the students will behave, so Prabhupāda left us alone in New York, warning us to get along with one another. He said he would be glad to hear from me. Somehow or other in those days, we were getting along, cooperating in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. This is the desire of the spiritual master.

\* \* \*

## February 1967

February 15, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I hope you have received my previous notes sent to you in different letters. I have not as yet received the copy of *Narada Bhaktisutra* on which you want me to write commentaries. I hope you are duly editing the tapes of *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya*. While typing the records after your editing, make it in duplicate, and send me one copy so I can see how you are doing it.

I have now just five tapes with me, out of which I am returning one today. Please let me know how many tapes are there till today. Neal was to come here, but he has not come; therefore, I am sending you the tapes both for typing and editing. I hope you will do it nicely. May Krishna be pleased upon you.

I have advised Brahmananda to transfer money to my savings account, and please see that it is immediately done. I have sent him the letter to transfer; simply he has to sign it, or you may sign it and forward it to the bank. I allowed you to deal with the bank for two reasons: (1) that I may be free from monetary dealings, (2) that you may learn also how to deal with them. But recent indiscriminate issuing of checks has perturbed my mind. By indiscriminate issuing of checks the society’s money has been put into risk.

So far I can see from the correspondence of Brahmananda, it is not possible for us to get the house for so many reasons. The main reason is that we have no money to pay cash, and nobody is going to invest cash in that house because it is neither complete or has any income. It is simply utopian to think of possessing the house, and Mr. Price is simply giving us false hope. That is the verdict of devotees and trustees here, and for reasons I believe it is correct. Please see, therefore, that the above amount is immediately transferred. When actual sales contract is there, I shall again retransfer the amount, as I did it a few days ago.

You are all innocent boys without any experience of the world. The cunning world can befool you at any time. So please be careful of the world in Krishna Consciousness. When Krishna will desire, the house will come to us automatically. But we should not ask Krishna to give us a house; let Krishna give us when He likes. If Mr. Price is able to give us the house, it is so far very good. But from circumstances it appears that Mr. Price cannot get financial assistance from any businessman. If somebody gives us a donation for the right cause, that is a different thing. So we should only wait for Krishna’s mercy and should not jeopardize the hard-earned money for service of Krishna. I hope you will not misunderstand me; both you and Gargamuni must be careful about the accounts and see that checks above $50.00 are not issued indiscriminately.

Hope you are well and shall be glad to hear from you by return post. Please ask Rayrama to write to me. I am anxious to know if he had any talks with Mr. Ypalantin.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter Prabhupāda addresses me regarding the attempt by the devotees in New York City to buy a building. I dealt with this at length in Volume III of the *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta* in the chapter called, “The Price Affair.” Here I can reveal a little more of my personal involvement. This letter particularly exposed my foolishness.

When Prabhupāda wrote this letter, he was already aware that the real estate man was trying to cheat us. He had written letters to Brahmānanda and had had correspondence with other devotees. Kīrtanānanda was the first devotee to meet this real estate man. Brahmānanda really developed a relationship with him. The man promised us all kinds of help and said he would personally buy or donate the house for us. He promised so many illusory things, and everyone—Brahmānanda, Kīrtanānanda, myself, and all the other devotees involved—although we were occasionally a little skeptical, were following this cheater. He wanted a substantial down payment from us, which he planned to steal. (Later, in 1971, in negotiating to buy property in Juhu, Bombay, Prabhupāda also had to deal with a cheater who was trying to take a down payment from the devotees and keep the land. Just as in Kṛṣṇa’s time demons sometimes appeared with evil intentions and Kṛṣṇa had to kill them, so Prabhupāda’s devotees often proved unable to see through their deception. Prabhupāda had to personally overcome them.)

With this letter Prabhupāda, turned to me for help. He had already advised Brahmānanda not to give away money, and he therefore says he asked Brahmānanda to transfer $6,200 to his savings account. Prabhupāda was concerned that it might not be transferred as he had requested, and so he asked me, “Please see that it is immediately done.”

I was secretary of ISKCON New York then, Brahmānanda was president, and Gargamuni was treasurer. Normally, Prabhupāda dealt with this matter through them, but since he was becoming doubtful because of the real estate man’s bewitchment of Brahmānanda and the others, he turned to another devotee, namely myself.

Prabhupāda said in this letter that he could see from his correspondence with Brahmānanda that it was not possible for us to get the house. “It is simply utopian to think of possessing the house, and Mr. Price is simply giving us false hope.” Then Prabhupāda again personally addressed me to take individual action: “Please see, therefore, that the above amount is immediately transferred.”

I was not unaware of Prabhupāda’s intent when I received this letter. I immediately perceived the personal nature of it, how he was addressing me—not just as part of a group, but individually. On receiving this letter, I knew I had to convince the other devotees of what Prabhupāda wanted. In this way I could save the society’s money and follow the orders of my spiritual master. I could understand that Prabhupāda had attempted to withdraw from the agreement, at least to some degree, by requesting the other devotees to do so, but they had not listened.

I was, therefore, being given the charge of relaying instructions to the others about what must be done immediately. But Brahmānanda and Kīrtanānanda remained steady and continued to trust this real estate man. Initially I faced their disagreements and tried to convince them. They read Prabhupāda’s letter to me and understood what he was saying, but they said that things had changed since Prabhupāda had written the letter.

This was the nature of Mr. Price’s trickery. He knew we ultimately had to receive our instructions from Śrīla Prabhupāda, but he would deviously appear to agree with what Prabhupāda had said and convince us that Prabhupāda’s most recent instruction was now old. When Prabhupāda had objected to our dealing with Mr. Price, he would say, “Oh, because of the most recent change, that objection had now been removed. So you can deal with me.” But if we actually studied Prabhupāda’s letter, he was simply telling us that we should pull out and not deal with this man unless he met our terms.

I gradually felt my resolution weakened, and my Godbrothers convinced me that things had evolved since Prabhupāda wrote his letter to me, and neither the devotees nor I had to follow Prabhupāda’s specific directions word for word.

It was my misfortune that I renounced this direct charge Prabhupāda had given me; it would have saved the day. I also became one of the “innocent boys without any experience of the world,” and worse than that, I disobeyed my spiritual master’s order.

The highest priority is to follow one’s spiritual master. The spiritual master will never give an instruction to disrupt cooperation among his disciples, but sometimes he gives a disciple an individual instruction, and the disciple has to follow that in all circumstances. The spiritual master may say something contrary to the sense of everything in the whole world, but we have to always follow him and not be dissuaded by any other parties. All this takes considerable intelligence.

In the midst of an event as important as the purchase of a building—in which our entire New York savings might be lost—Prabhupāda was still, in a balanced way, dealing with different matters. This proved his transcendental equipoise.

In this letter he talks about his literary work. In 1967, at 26 Second Avenue, a few books on Hinduism were circulating (some volumes of the *Rāmāyaṇa,* as well as a couple of books from the Gaudiya Math, among them a translation of the *Nārada-bhakti-sūtra).* This *Nārada-bhakti-sūtra* interested me because it had very basic aphorisms about devotional service. Also, inside this book was a color picture of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa which I had developed into photographs and, with Prabhupāda’s permission, given to the devotees. I had once shown this book to Prabhupāda, and we had noticed that the author had translated *bhakti* as “divine love,” whereas Prabhupāda used the more active and personal term, “devotional service.”

I had requested in one of my letters that perhaps Prabhupāda might translate *Nārada-bhakti-sūtra*. In his reply he willingly committed himself. It is a touching and personal remembrance for me, that at my foolish request Prabhupāda was willing to turn his literary endeavors and his sacred and powerful attention to translating this book. He continued this work for some time but then stopped, considering it redundant, since he was simultaneously working on the *Teachings of Lord Caitanya.* This is the history of how he began *Nārada-bhakti-sūtra.*

In this letter Prabhupāda again shows his pleasure with my tiny efforts of assisting him by typing his tapes. He hoped I would execute the service well and said, “May Krishna be pleased upon you.” Prabhupāda was pleased even by a small amount of service, even from a foolish boy who didn’t know enough to carry on his head the order of the spiritual master no matter what anyone else said.

## March 1967

March 21, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. Regarding your question about the inhabitants of Brajabhumi or Vrindaban, it is to be understood that Vrindaban is the original abode of Lord Krishna, as He is the original Personality of Godhead. In that transcendental plane, He has His transcendental associates engage in different exchanges of humor. For ourselves, when we are in the perfect stage of devotional service, we can know our eternal relation with Krishna, and, as such, one of the associates of Lord Krishna becomes our ideal leader. This acceptance of leadership by one of the eternal associates of the Lord is not artificial. Do not, therefore, try it at present; it will be automatically revealed to you in proper time.

Regarding the house, you are already in trouble. The best thing is to stop this strain. Mr. Lerner told me that Mr. Hall has no sufficient money to purchase the house. I definitely instructed you if Mr. Hall enters into agreement with Mr. Tyler it is then only you could hand over the check of $5,000.00 to Mr. Hall or his agent. But you have not followed my instruction, and now you are in trouble. If Mr. Hall has no money to purchase the house, then his accepting our check is a clear case of cheating. So immediately inquire if Mr. Hall has at all the money to purchase the house from Mr. Tyler. If not, immediately demand back the check; otherwise consult our good lawyers and file a legal case. We cannot forego this cheating conspiracy because Mr. Hall has no money. He must either deliver the possession of the house immediately or must return the money immediately, or we will file a case against all the combined group, namely Mr. Price, Mr. Hall and Mr. Lerner. I do not know what is the actual case, but from my telephonic conversation with Brahmananda it appears to be an organized cheating case, and you have to face them with courage, without excusing anyone of them. We are not revengeful, but we cannot lose Krishna’s money for nothing.

Brahmananda has asked me whether the San Francisco branch will pay some money for purchasing the house. But where is the house and where is the purchase? So far it is talks of Mr. Price and company in which you innocent boys have been entrapped. I do not know how I can help in this great blunder. I can only hope that Krishna will help you.

Howard wants one of the mimeograph machines here so that copies of *Back to Godhead* required here may be printed here. What is the weight of the machine and what will be the cost of transport charges? I think you can spare one machine for San Francisco and charge them the cost of it.

Please ask Rayrama to get back the first chapter of *Geetopanishad* from the publisher. I have asked this several times, but there is no reply. I shall be glad to hear from you about all the points mentioned herein.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is the second letter Prabhupāda wrote me about the devotees being cheated by Mr. Price. I’ll mention further some of my faults and how one can avoid them.

(1) My mistake was that I didn’t follow the order of my spiritual master, and the result was that we immediately got into trouble. Although we were all neophytes, Kṛṣṇa was teaching us in a very perceivable, external way the bad results of not following our spiritual master’s order.  
(2) The order we neglected was not one of the general spiritual orders like chanting sixteen rounds and avoiding the four sinful activities (although it can be argued that our mistake was under the category of gambling). It was specific: he told us not to give money to these cheaters, yet we did. It was something like a father sending his young son on an errand and warning him, “Be certain you don’t do such-and-such. Do you understand?” Yet the small son becomes tricked by the clever world in the form of a cheater, who tells him, “Oh, don’t listen to your father. He’s a nice man, but he doesn’t know how things are done.” As young sons we ran back to our father, Śrīla Prabhupāda, crying that we’d lost our money and were in trouble. The world had tricked us, but we could have remained protected if we had followed our spiritual master’s instructions.  
(3) It would have been far better to have remained a fool in the eyes of Mr. Price than to leave Śrīla Prabhupāda’s order. A million times better! Later, Śrīla Prabhupāda told us the story of the man who had lost his caste to get a meal. There was a *brāhmaṇa* who was traveling and became so hungry that he decided to take a meal under forbidden conditions. A *brāhmaṇa* is only supposed to eat in the homes of other *brāhmaṇas*, but this *brāhmaṇa* stopped at the house of a Muslim and asked for a meal. The Muslim agreed and gave the *brāhmaṇa* what he could, but because the Muslim was a poor man, it wasn’t much. When the *brāhmaṇa* finished the scanty meal and asked the Muslim for more, the Muslim replied that he had no more. Then the *brāhmaṇa* lamented, “Oh no! I have lost my caste, but still I am hungry!”  
Our situation was similar to that. By following our spiritual master’s order we may appear foolish to the whole world, but we should not mind. We should not have given up the jewel of obedience to the spiritual master.  
Now we were stinging from our hurt: we had lost all our money.  
(4) If one disobeys his spiritual master, one will always go wrong, because in his heart he will develop a separate mentality from the guru’s and think, “Yes, of course the guru should be honored. That is the principle. But he is a man, and what he says may be wrong.” Even if we assure ourselves that by disobeying the spiritual master’s orders things will be corrected or will improve, the terrible result will be our *loss of faith.*  
(5) In this case, the result for me was exposure of my foolishness, not loss of faith. Prabhupāda, however, in pointing out my blunder, did not say that it was merely a worldly mistake due to naivetṇ, but he stressed the gravest thing of all: “You have not followed my instruction, and now you are in trouble.”  
Because we had no desire to disobey Śrīla Prabhupāda, however, he forgave us. It was a case of a mistake that one may occasionally commit; the spiritual master can forgive such a mistake. We were still his boys, fully engaged in his cause.
