(6) The way to recover from such blunders (of temporarily neglecting the order of the spiritual master) is to again fully follow the spiritual master’s orders.  
(7) We can also note that Śrīla Prabhupāda’s unprecedented success in spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness was all due to his faith in the order of his spiritual master. He followed that order “blindly,” even when it forced him to lose the company of his Godbrothers. We should all pray for full faith in the order of our spiritual master.  
(8) Another important point in this letter is Prabhupāda’s advice on what we should do after having been cheated out of our hard-earned $6,000: “It appears to be an organized cheating case, and you have to face them with courage, without excusing anyone of them. We are not revengeful, but we cannot lose Krishna’s money for nothing.” A devotee is peaceful and tolerant, but he has to defend Kṛṣṇa’s honor and property, even if this sometimes requires that he fight. Because of fear, laziness, or false renunciation, we cannot avoid fighting when there is no other recourse. The required courage is also not material. We have to pray to Lord Balarāma to compensate for our deficiency in strength.

In the first paragraph of this letter, Śrīla Prabhupāda answers a question I asked about a very advanced part of Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy, which I had heard him speak about in his dictations of *Teachings of Lord Caitanya*. Prabhupāda explained that before one assumes his *rasa* with Kṛṣṇa, he first goes to Vṛndāvana and follows a leader in his particular *rasa,* and that leader—a resident of Vṛndāvana—trains him to associate with Kṛṣṇa in that eternal *rasa*. When I heard this, I didn’t know what it meant, and I had innocently asked Śrīla Prabhupāda. Yet in this letter we see Prabhupāda has not elaborated on what he wrote in *Teachings of Lord Caitanya* in answering my question. He said I was not ready. I was prematurely asking about something that required advanced realization. We have seen over the years—during Prabhupāda’s presence and after his disappearance—that some of his disciples have not been satisfied with the amount of information Prabhupāda has given on different advanced spiritual topics. Sometimes they have gone to other devotees and spiritual masters in the Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava *sampradāya* to ask for further information. I would have, therefore, been disobedient if I had tried to delve further, since he explicitly told me not to.

Lord Caitanya also showed this example in relation to his spiritual master, Ṝśvara Purī. Ṝśvara Purī told Lord Caitanya not to study *Vedānta-sūtra* but to simply chant Hare Kṛṣṇa. Lord Caitanya did what his spiritual master said, and as a result He manifested all the symptoms of pure love of God.

We have to be faithful to our spiritual master, even if he appears to limit us in our spiritual advancement. There is no question that our spiritual master will actually limit us, but for our own benefit he may restrict us from inquiring into different points if we are not yet actually prepared to enter those areas of understanding. The advanced point being discussed in this letter refers to the *siddha-deha* or *svarūpa-siddhi* of the living entity. Each of us has an eternal *rasa* with Kṛṣṇa, which is revealed to us by our spiritual master, but we cannot realize our *rasa* just by hearing about it.

At this time, I had been initiated for only six months, and yet I was asking Prabhupāda for very advanced information. How valuable would it have been for my spiritual development if Śrīla Prabhupāda had told me more about this topic? Not having controlled my material desires, there was no question that I could understand my *rasa* with Kṛṣṇa—even if someone were to have told me what it was, it would have made no sense. If a so-called spiritual master tells his neophyte disciple his *rasa*, it will simply mislead that devotee. It will be a farce.

On the other hand, no one can say Prabhupāda didn’t teach us advanced things. He was writing them in the *Teachings of Lord Caitanya,* which was available in the very beginning of ISKCON to a neophyte like myself. While full understanding was beyond us, at least we knew the existence of such a higher state.

Naturally, I received Śrīla Prabhupāda’s letter soberly. I understood that Prabhupāda was being gentle and fatherly with me. He didn’t think I was being devious. I was inquiring about something I had read in his book. In a fatherly, concerned way he gently informed me that at the present time, there was no question of my trying to understand anything further about this topic. I was actually pleased when I read this; I was not at all disappointed.

A disciple only wants to get instructions or directions from his spiritual master. He has no predetermined desire about what he wants his spiritual master to say; he simply wants the right direction. The importance of these letters is that as a disciple, I received instructions that permanently formed my spiritual life. These are *my* letters, but Prabhupāda wrote letters to other disciples, and in each case whatever he said molded the life of that devotee.

Although my letters from Prabhupāda are specific and apply uniquely to me, others can also read them in a general way—as a guideline. We are therefore commenting on these letters, treating them as śāstric texts. For myself, they are certainly *śāstra.* Guru, *śāstra,* and *sādhu* form the direction for the disciple. They are the source of transcendental knowledge. The guru’s teachings are śāstric, or scriptural; therefore, the guru’s letters are śāstric. By the logic that things equal to the same thing are equal to each other, the guru’s letters are equal to *śāstra.* These different instructions are therefore a storehouse of direction for one in spiritual life.

One may ask, “What if a disciple, rather than asking questions that may be too advanced for his understanding at present, just waits patiently to get answers? Is this silent patience a virtue, or would it be better if he asked penetrating questions?” The basis of approaching the spiritual master is inquiry and service. One should inquire, but that is also automatically taken care of if one is eager to listen. The disciple is inquiring by hearing a lecture by the spiritual master or reading his articles or books. If in the course of hearing from his spiritual master or while reading Vedic literature he doesn’t understand a particular item, he can inquire directly about it. Or if he thinks it is a very advanced topic, he can wait, and his spiritual master will tell him about it at the proper time.

Prabhupāda’s own example was that he only asked his spiritual master, “How can I serve you?” and did not pose many philosophical questions. Prabhupāda was an ideal disciple: he simply wanted to serve his spiritual master. Sometimes there is an impure tinge in a disciple, and he poses as more intelligent than he actually is. He may even go so far as to test his spiritual master.

In the early days, Prabhupāda instructed us to hold *iṣṭagoṣṭhīs.* He told us the meaning of *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* was “to discuss what the spiritual master has said.” Prabhupāda would sit up in his apartment at 26 Second Avenue, and we would sit in the storefront temple room and discuss.

At one of our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs,* Rāyarāma discussed how Chicago would become a Kṛṣṇa conscious city, and how other cities would follow, and how eventually we would buy land in Alaska and other places. These were big visions coming from a little storefront where we could barely buy a pair of sneakers. We were talking about how we were going to take over the world. We knew Prabhupāda also thought like that.

Prabhupāda gave general ideas for expanded preaching. There should be preaching in different cities. “So,” he would say, “Brahmānanda, you will go to Moscow?” And everyone would laugh. Then he would say to someone else, “So, you will go to China?” and everyone would laugh. He would laugh too, but he seriously meant what he said.

After this particular *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* he chided us: “What are you talking about?”

I said, “We are talking about our goals.”

He said, “The goal is Kṛṣṇa,” and I had a feeling that we hadn’t used the *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* properly. Instead of speculating about taking over the world through cultural influence, we should have been discussing how to become pure devotees to preach Prabhupāda’s mission. Then Kṛṣṇa would become pleased to give us the power to take over the world. Another time he referred in a chiding way to some of the questions we had manufactured in one of our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs*. “I know your *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* questions,” he said. “You want to know how many miles long was the Pūtanā witch.”

If, however, one has a doubt about something—he doesn’t understand a text of the scriptures or doesn’t understand an instruction—he should definitely submit an honest question. The spiritual master is always pleased to supply the answer.

## April–July 1967

After March 21, 1967, Prabhupāda didn’t write me a letter until August. Because after three months in San Francisco, Prabhupāda returned to New York City. We greeted him with an ecstatic turnout on April 9, defying the airport authorities by holding a big *kīrtana,* with cymbals and *mṛdaṅgas.* We were all eager for Prabhupāda to catch sight of us, and somehow Prabhupāda singled out each of us with his glance. He was pleased to see each and every one of us.

I drove to the temple with Prabhupāda. When we arrived at 26 Second Avenue, no one else was there. The temple, however, was full of incense, which Gargamuni had set burning. The air was so smoky that you could hardly see into the room, but rather than finding it disturbing, Prabhupāda said, “This is like India.” He liked it. He also liked the improvements we had made in the tiny storefront temple.

When Prabhupāda was away, we had recruited two devotees who were ready for initiation. We introduced them to Prabhupāda. To Paul, (who later became Puruṣottama) he said, “You look like a born Vaiṣṇava.” The other boy’s name was Mike (who later became Madhusūdana). They both sat down, and Prabhupāda asked them, “How do you like this Kṛṣṇa conscious atmosphere?” They replied shyly that they liked it. When the devotees returned from the airport, Prabhupāda lectured and praised the Kṛṣṇa conscious process, noting that even in his absence the temple had improved, not deteriorated. He said this was a sign of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

With Prabhupāda’s return to New York, we went to different public engagements aside from the usual chanting in Tompkins Square Park. One of the more notable programs we were invited to attend was the Cosmic Love-In, held at the Village Theater to sponsor the presidential candidate Louis Ablofia. Mr. Louis Ablofia was a “peace and love” hippie who had rented the Village Theater for a couple of days. His program was to constantly provide free entertainment so that as many people as possible would come, and then he would make his political campaign.

Mr. Ablofia had printed a pamphlet announcing all the different persons who were going to be there, such as Timothy Leary and Allen Ginsberg. Timothy Leary had previously been renting the Village Theater for his psychedelic presentations. One night, while Prabhupāda was in New York, Timothy Leary advertised his lectures by having his men drive a Volkswagen minibus around the Lower East Side with a figure of Lord Buddha on the roof. When Prabhupāda saw this and heard about the proposed program, he became angry and later spoke to the devotees in his room in a moving way, praising Lord Buddha. Prabhupāda said that Lord Buddha was a beautiful young man, a prince who gave up everything for spiritual life. “Why is this rascal preaching this nonsense in Lord Buddha’s name?”

Still, Prabhupāda agreed to go to the love-in, and we were advertised in the pamphlet along with Ginsberg, Leary, and many others, including rock groups and activists associated with the peace movement. We were listed as “Swami Bhaktivedanta and the Hare Krishna Chanters.”

Some of Prabhupāda’s disciples thought he shouldn’t attend. They said it was not good for Prabhupāda to be billed on the same program with criminals and social deviants, and it might not be good for Prabhupāda’s case for permanent U.S. residency. We contacted our lawyer, and he agreed that it was not good for Prabhupāda to appear with such persons. At the same time, we were already advertised on the handbill as being part of the program. A back-and-forth dispute took place between some of Prabhupāda’s disciples: some thought he should go, some thought he shouldn’t go.

During the days before the love-in, Prabhupāda sometimes consulted with the two groups. One day he would decide to go, the next day he would decide the opposite. Prabhupāda’s decision was changing so much that after one temple evening lecture, our president, Brahmānanda, stood up and told the audience that we were going to attend the function at the Village Theater and that they should come and see us there. From the *vyāsāsana* Śrīla Prabhupāda corrected him, “No, that has been changed; we are not going.” Brahmānanda was confused. The very night that we were to appear at the love-in, Prabhupāda called different disciples into his room, took their advice, and at the last minute gave his opinion that we should go. “I have come here to preach,” he said.

The large theater was completely filled. When we entered there was a rock band on stage, and the group was dancing wildly around a figure dressed like a banana. They were celebrating the fact that if you ground the skin of a banana and smoked it, it would have the same effect as marijuana. They were playing disorganized rock music—rough, with electronic sounds. The place was in a state of pandemonium.

Śrīla Prabhupāda walked down the aisle, and his face became grave and stern against the display. He sat down, and after awhile we were told it was time for us to go on stage. We went on stage with Prabhupāda and chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa before a large audience. At first the organizers of the program did not want Prabhupāda to speak, but then they said he could speak for five minutes. After only a brief show, we had to leave. As we were leaving the stage, Louis Ablofia, who was wearing golden stars stuck to his face, took the microphone and asked everyone to give us a round of applause.

We left the theater loyally and affectionately surrounding Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda looked up at the marquee on which was written “The Fugs.” Prabhupāda asked, “What is this Fugs?” We explained it was a rock group. Then we walked the few blocks back to the storefront.

Up in his room we recalled some of the activities that we had just experienced at the love-in. Someone remarked to Prabhupāda that actually, Louis Ablofia was too young to run for president, so he could never win. Prabhupāda suddenly began to laugh silently, so much that his body began to shake, and finally he put his hand in front of his face. He went on smiling and laughing, recalling the foolish activities we had just seen. But he also expressed his disgust at what he called “that plantain group,” the group celebrating the banana, who acted like monkeys “dancing without any symmetry.”

Later, the same people who smoked banana peels approached one of the devotees and asked if we would accept free bananas from them for our *āśrama,* on the condition that we would return the peels to them for their nefarious activities. Gargamuni explained this to Śrīla Prabhupāda. “Should we accept this offer?” he asked.

“Yes,” Prabhupāda said, “We can accept the free bananas for Kṛṣṇa.”

During the days after Prabhupāda’s return to New York in 1967, there was a threat that he might have to leave the country. One day in his room Prabhupāda said, “Your country does not want me.” He said he could not get his visa renewed. This was very alarming to us, since we were such neophyte devotees and had no idea of what it would be like to try to be Kṛṣṇa conscious without Prabhupāda. It was bad enough that he had gone off to San Francisco, but if he had to leave for India, it seemed impossible. Then we contacted a lawyer and the threat was postponed.

While Prabhupāda was still in New York, the welfare workers in my New York office went on strike. Since I was working for Prabhupāda and we needed immediate cash each month, I did not go on strike with the others. Because of this the strikers harassed me. Every day the picketers would line up outside the welfare office, and they would shout and threaten the workers who were still reporting for work. I used to have to go through a line of shouting—and sometimes pushing—strikers while going to work in the morning, coming out for lunch, returning from lunch, and leaving at the end of the day. In those days, I went to work with a *śikhā* and Vaiṣṇava *tilaka,* as well as a little Deity of Lord Jagannātha around my neck. Because I was working for Prabhupāda’s mission, I was not afraid to face these taunters. They would shout names at me because I was a devotee, but I never stopped going to work.

One morning, Śrīla Prabhupāda was taking a walk in the Lower East Side neighborhood with Kīrtanānanda, and they passed the welfare office where the picketers were gathered. On seeing Prabhupāda they began to sing the melody of Hare Kṛṣṇa, which we had made popular, but instead of chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra to the melody, they chanted, “Money money, money money, money money, money money . . . ” At that time, our movement was not associated with any kind of money-collecting; the only meaning of their taunt was that they were criticizing Prabhupāda for sending me to work and breaking the picket lines.

I only heard later that the picketers had yelled at Prabhupāda. That same morning, I had taken breakfast in the storefront and hurried out the door, heading down the block to work. I had prepared myself psychologically to meet the picketers. Suddenly, from around the corner, came Prabhupāda and Kīrtanānanda, who had just been taunted by the picketers. As soon as I saw Prabhupāda, I got down on my hands and knees and bowed my forehead to the pavement. I then got up, my Jagannātha Deity jangling around my neck, and beheld my beloved spiritual master. Prabhupāda was smiling beautifully. He was completely happy, and he also seemed pleased to see me. He had not been disturbed by the picketers, but was pleased that despite their taunts I was going to work. Prabhupāda touched me lightly, and by that touch I felt transcendentaly energized. On his behalf I walked quickly and resolutely around the corner and straight through the picket line.

It was also at this time that I was chosen to go to Boston to open the first ISKCON center there. A group of devotees (I wasn’t among them) went to Boston for a big peace rally in which tens of thousands of people took part. This took place in the park near the zoo. The next day, on the front page of the Boston newspaper appeared a picture of a crowd of hundreds surrounding the devotees, who were chanting. When the devotees in New York saw this picture, they thought it was an auspicious sign. Rāyarāma especially thought Boston would be a wonderful place to start an ISKCON center because of the large student population there who could receive Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

When Prabhupāda heard the favorable prospects of preaching in Boston, he said some devotees should go there. Because I had a job and we knew of no other way to support a temple, I was asked to go. I said to Rāyarāma, “What am I going to do when I go there? Am I just going to say, ‘Well, here I am!’ I’m not a pure devotee. What will I do?” I knew I wasn’t a prepared preacher; I was a new devotee. Was I supposed to organize a mission for an entire city just as Prabhupāda did when he came to New York? I was unsure whether I could do anything.

One day, while Prabhupāda was still in New York, I traveled by bus to Boston to see if I could get a job there. I went to the welfare office in Boston and applied for a job on the basis that I had worked several years for the welfare department in New York City. I had in my record a letter from the Commission of Welfare of New York City commending me for my uninterrupted work during the recent strike. Boston is a smaller city than New York. It is in New York’s shadow. The employers at the welfare office in Boston were impressed that I was coming from the big city. They asked me, “Why are you coming to Boston?”

I said, “It’s too dirty in New York City; I don’t like it there. I just want to come to Boston.” To my surprise, I was promised a job that very day. They said I could begin in August.

When the job interview was finished, I still had some time before I had to catch the bus back to New York. I thought that when I did open a center in Boston, it would not be in the city but in Cambridge, where Harvard and M.I.T. are. I took a bus to Harvard Square. It was the first time I had been there, and I saw intense activity—Hell’s Angels with their motorbikes, hippies and students browsing through many shops. I discovered that Harvard Square was one of the hardest places in the country to acquire real estate. Nevertheless, I walked around campus, saw the exciting young people, and thought, “I *have* to get a place here.”

I took a bus back to New York City and then a taxi to the Lower East Side. I ran upstairs to see Prabhupāda in his apartment. “I got the job,” I boasted to Prabhupāda, and I proudly told him that it was unusual for someone to get a promise of a job in one day—yet I had done it. Prabhupāda reminded me, “By Kṛṣṇa’s grace,” and I immediately felt humbled. I had been boasting as if I had received the job on my own power, but Prabhupāda pointed out that it was only by Kṛṣṇa’s grace.

Sometimes when I was supposed to be seeing clients (which was part of my welfare work), I would come by the temple and type some of the tapes Prabhupāda had dictated. One time, while bringing the tape recorder back into his room after finishing with it, he asked whether I was working. I was dressed in a shirt and tie, and I explained to him that I was taking time off from work to type. I said I was like Sanātana because I was engaged in devotional service when I was supposed to be at my government work. I was making a little pun about Sanātana, about whom Prabhupāda was telling us in the *Teachings of Lord Caitanya*. “But,” Śrīla Prabhupāda smiled broadly and said, “you *are* Sanātana.”

I remember one time I accompanied Śrīla Prabhupāda with Rāyarāma to a lawyer’s office on Chambers Street. We took a taxi. Rāyarāma was the main spokesman, but I also went along because Rāyarāma had some business afterwards, and I would have to return to the temple with Prabhupāda. I remember sitting in the office with Śrīla Prabhupāda, and he reached over and took a big bookend modeled after the stern of a ship. He twirled the propeller playfully. Upon seeing a woman typing in the office, he said humorously, “So, this is woman’s liberation? That she is typing?” Then we went in to see the lawyer, and he discussed Prabhupāda’s immigration case with us. Later Prabhupāda commented that the lawyer was simply taking his money on the pretense of getting him residency status, yet all he got was months of delays.

After finishing our business with the lawyer, I accompanied Śrīla Prabhupāda back to the storefront. I suggested we take a taxi, and he said no. What was the use of spending so much money? We would take a bus. I did not know exactly where the bus stop was, but he did, so it was he who began leading me. I also tried to take care of him as we crossed the large street. I looked both ways for traffic. I commented to him that New York City resembled a jungle, but I said there were no snakes in this jungle. “What about Mr. Price?” Prabhupāda asked. Then we took the bus, which went up to Second Avenue. When I thought we were near our neighborhood, I said I would ring the buzzer, but Prabhupāda said, “No, it is one more stop.” He also pointed out to me where he had previously stayed and some other places with which he was familiar as we passed the Bowery. I felt happy to be with him so intimately.

When we were walking the few blocks back to the storefront, I impulsively asked him why a bogus organization like the Ramakrishna mission, although not genuine, was able to establish so many branches around the world, whereas we were not able to? Prabhupāda made no response. I was stunned that he didn’t reply to my question at all, and suddenly felt I had committed a wrong by speaking completely out of turn with a too-familiar attitude.

As we walked the last block to the storefront, I was suffering mentally because of the mistake of what I had said, and when we reached the storefront, Prabhupāda just went upstairs without saying anything, and I went in to the front part of the temple. Perhaps Prabhupāda was not really displeased, and he said nothing about the matter again. Maybe I just misunderstood the whole thing. But a disciple cannot demand that his spiritual master explain everything in the disciple’s own terms. The servant should simply serve.

After staying a month and a half in New York City, Prabhupāda suddenly had a paralyzing stroke. I elaborately described the incident of his hospitalization and recovering in *Only He Could Lead Them.* I have also described there how I took part in massaging Prabhupāda and standing watch in the hospital room with others. These were chances for intimate moments with Śrīla Prabhupāda. One day Prabhupāda took the vegetarian food the hospital gave him and mixed it with the *prasādam* we brought from the temple. Before he ate, he gave each of the devotees in the room a handful of *prasādam*, and he did not leave much for himself. From the hospital, Prabhupāda went to New Jersey to rest and recuperate.

Prabhupāda would sit on his bed in the little house we had rented in New Jersey while his servant, Gaurasundara, massaged him. One day, during his massage, Prabhupāda gave an example: “How can you say the universe has no God? The universe is like a man. A man may go to the doctor, and the doctor may examine him and find so many symptoms of life, like the man’s pulse, but if the doctor says in conclusion, ‘You are dead,’ he has certainly made a crazy analysis.”

There’s a photo of Prabhupāda that was taken in a simple studio. It shows him without a shirt, his *brāhmaṇa* thread over his chest, and nice *tilaka* marks. He appears to have an unhappy expression, at least superficially. During his stay in New Jersey, one of the devotees asked Prabhupāda why he hadn’t looked happy when this picture was taken. Prabhupāda said, “This is ecstasy.”

Prabhupāda tried to recuperate in New Jersey, but couldn’t. He returned to San Francisco. A few weeks later he was back again in New York, but this time he was on his way to India. He felt that was the only place he could recover his health.

I felt insecure about Prabhupāda’s leaving for India. One day, while Prabhupāda was upstairs in his apartment, I sat in the temple room and thought, “But if he goes, I’ll be like Arjuna describes himself in the *Bhagavad-gītā*—a riven cloud. Will I be strong enough to continue in Kṛṣṇa consciousness if he doesn’t come back?” Śrīla Prabhupāda preached to us, however, that we should continue, and I knew I had full engagement in which to absorb myself. Kṛṣṇa would protect me.

During the few days he remained in New York City, I took my leave of him to preach in Boston. He told me I should hold public *kīrtana* there. He said the *kīrtana* was like a big cannon, and if it sounded loudly, *māyā* would be defeated.

After Prabhupāda wished me well at preaching in Boston, I made my obeisances before him. While my head was bowed to the floor, Prabhupāda reached over and rubbed his hand several times up and down my back and neck. I could hardly believe my good fortune in receiving his transcendental touch. For months after, while I went through many different adventures in Boston, I repeatedly remembered the benediction of his hand on my head and back. I was convinced that by his touch I had received sufficient blessings to conduct all my activities to establish the beginnings of Kṛṣṇa consciousness in Boston.

In August I actually moved to Boston. I traveled alone with my Navy duffel bag. I didn’t know where to stay the first night, so I looked in a newspaper at ads for renting apartments. I was able to get one furnished room in a house in Central Square, which was not as wonderful as Harvard, but between Harvard University and M.I.T. It was just a place to stay while I worked at the welfare office and started a temple. I chanted on my beads, prepared simple *prasādam*, and went to work. It was rather lonely living without devotees. Sometimes I would talk to them on the phone. Soon after I moved to Boston, I received a letter from Prabhupāda.

## August 1967

August 29, 1967

Dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am so glad to receive your first letter to me in India, and also to receive your contribution of $10. You are always “first” in contributions, and therefore your contribution is also the first to come from the American centers. It is so stated in the *Srimad Bhagavatam* that the best use of the human form of life is to serve Lord Krishna by one’s life, resources, intelligence and words; so you are all good souls, especially your example is very nice, for you have practically given your life and soul to Krishna Consciousness. You are utilizing your resources for the society, and you are utilizing your intelligence and words for the furtherance of Krishna Consciousness. So I very much appreciate your effort, and surely Krishna will bless you more and more, and you will feel it yourself. I am very glad to learn that you have finished editing the *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya,* and if you send me copies by post, I will immediately begin printing here.

I am so encouraged that one branch in New Mexico and one in Boston are opened, even in my absence. This is wanted. Physically I may be present or not; but the work must continue. Our Guru Maharaja injected some of his spiritual vision in us, and the work is continuing in different branches as far as possible by Bon Maharaja, Tirtha Maharaja, my humble self, and others. Similarly, in the Western world Krishna has sent me some good souls like you, and I hope even in my absence that the spirit of Krishna consciousness will be spread.

You will be glad to know that Kirtanananda is now Tridandi Swami Kirtanananda. I have made him a sannyasi yesterday, on the birthday of Lord Krishna, and it was unexpectedly a very successful ceremony. The moment he was being offered sannyasa there was some ceremony of Janmastami in the temple, and hundreds of men and women congratulated the young sannyasi. Somebody remarked that he looked like Lord Chaitanya. He will be going back to the States very soon; in the meantime I shall try to utilize this “white sannyasi” for recruiting some members in India, and then he will go back. The field of work is certainly there; but if he can be used here, I will try for it.

I can assure you of your steady progress in Krishna Consciousness, as you are sincerely trying for the cause of Krishna Consciousness. In the conditioned stage there is every possibility of being mistaken, but even if there is a gross mistake, Krishna forgives His devotee who is very sincere. So go on working sincerely and Krishna will protect you always. Kindly make Boston a very nice center. The prospect there is very good due to the large number of students.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

(Received at 103 East Brookline Street, Boston)

Śrīla Prabhupāda wanted his mission spread by devotees who would take responsibility for preaching in various areas. Whether you call the devotee a temple president, a zonal leader, or whatever, the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement requires devotees who will dedicate themselves to their *prabhu-datta deśa.* Prabhupāda had explained this in a purport to a verse in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta:*

*sanātana kahe,—“bhāla kailā upadeśa*  
*tāhāṅ yāba, sei mora ‘prabhu-datta deśa’”*

**Translation: Sanātana Gosvāmī replied, “You have given me very good advice. I shall certainly go there, for that is the place the Lord had given me for my residence.”**

Purport: The words *prabhu-datta deśa* are very significant. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s devotional cult teaches one not to sit down in one place, but to spread the devotional cult all over the world. The Lord dispatched Sanātana Gosvāmī and Rūpa Gosvāmī to Vṛndāvana to excavate and renovate the holy places and from there establish the cult of *bhakti*. Therefore, Vṛndāvana was given to Sanātana Gosvāmī and Rūpa Gosvāmī as their place of residence. Similarly, every one in the line of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s devotional cult should accept the words of the spiritual master and thus spread the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. They should go everywhere, to all parts of the world, accepting those places as *prabhu-datta deśa,* the places of residence given by the spiritual master or Lord Kṛṣṇa. The spiritual master is the representative; therefore, one who has carried out the orders of the spiritual master is understood to have carried out the orders of Kṛṣṇa or Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu wanted to spread the *bhakti* cult all over the world *(pṛthivīte āche yata nagarādi grāma* \[*CB Antya-khaṇḍa* [4.126](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cb-antya/4.126)\]). Therefore, devotees in the line of Kṛṣṇa consciousness must go to different parts of the world and preach, as ordered by the spiritual master. That will satisfy Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu.

—Cc. Antya [4.144](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-antya/4.144)

One who takes a *prabhu-datta deśa* has to become responsible in all ways to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness in his particular place, and he should be prepared to go to any other place under the order of the spiritual master. Many sincere persons are needed, and only then can Kṛṣṇa consciousness be spread effectively.

Prabhupāda says here that there are four things at a devotee’s disposal: one’s life, one’s money, one’s intelligence, and one’s words. A devotee-preacher has to give all of these things to Kṛṣṇa. His is not a part-time occupation. In the early days of ISKCON, dedicating our whole life meant we had to give everything to Prabhupāda. Since Prabhupāda was personally present, it was not difficult for us to see that he was worth serving with our whole life. Just seeing him completely convinced us of this.

As for dedicating the money at our disposal, large amounts were not required back then. My own paycheck from working in the welfare department paid almost all our expenses, although we soon moved on to bigger things. Whatever money we had we used in Kṛṣṇa’s service; that principle is continuing today, even though the income and overhead has increased many fold and the methods of accounting have become complicated.

As for dedicating intelligence, that meant we had to be willing to preach. We had to be bold. Taking a *prabhu-datta deśa* meant we had to be willing to meet people—inimical as well as pious. We had to use intelligence to approach people, to respond to them and then lead them in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, free from *māyā.*

As for dedicating words in the service of Kṛṣṇa, we simply had to speak whatever Prabhupāda said.

After Prabhupāda’s letter to me on August 29, he wrote a letter to Haṁsadūta, who was at that time staying in Boston. In a letter to Prabhupāda, Haṁsadūta had praised Prabhupāda’s spiritual master. Prabhupāda’s reply:

Your appreciation of my spiritual master is very commendable. One who understands and appreciates the disciplic succession is certainly advanced, and we should always be careful to give full respect to those who have so carefully handled this divine fruit of transcendental knowledge before us. Even a slight change will spoil it. That is why I have always been so careful to give you only those things which I have heard from my Guru Maharaja.

When we devotees in Boston read these statements about Prabhupāda’s faithful transmission of knowledge and how he exclusively repeated only the words of his spiritual master, we were astounded and appreciative. We all resolved to do the same thing—preach only what Prabhupāda had written or spoken. Therefore, it is not small-minded if we persist in being uninterested in other philosophical presentations, even by other Vaiṣṇava scholars, and stick to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s presentation. This is the authorized method, and was the method by which Prabhupāda achieved great success: “Even a slight change will spoil it. That is why I have been so careful to give you only those things which I have heard from my Guru Maharaja.”

Prabhupāda again assured me of steady progress be-cause of my sincerity in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I was surprised, though, that Prabhupāda wrote me about possibly making a big mistake in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I thought Prabhupāda was reading my mind, looking with his transcendental vision from India to Boston. I tried to think what big mistake I might have recently made. Then I concluded that Prabhupāda was warning me of the future. He was also preparing me, in a positive sense, for the future—that when or if my gross mistake came, I should not despair. Kṛṣṇa forgives His devotee.

After receiving Prabhupāda’s letter, I was careful not to make a mistake. In his letter, Prabhupāda had given me a general instruction, which I took in a personal sense because Prabhupāda suddenly pointed it out to me. I accepted it as directly coming from Kṛṣṇa for my own benefit.

There is also a remark in this letter that Śrīla Prabhupāda’s spiritual master “injected his spiritual vision in some of us, and the work is continuing in different branches.” Prabhupāda mentions himself as “my humble self” along with several of his Godbrothers. In retrospect, we can see that his achievement deserves extraordinary mention, not just as one of several achievements. Prabhupāda’s success surpassed all others. In 1967 there were only a few U.S. ISKCON centers, but ISKCON was soon to explode into dozens of centers. Scores were later opened and maintained by Prabhupāda and his followers all over the world. Yet even by 1967, his work was outstanding.

Later, Prabhupāda himself would point out that he alone had preached actively in the spirit of his Guru Mahārāja. Of course, he preferred to be humble and to not declare himself better than his Godbrothers, but he also wanted it to be clearly known that active preaching was the way to receive the blessings of his Guru Mahārāja, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī. It pained him to point out that what his Guru Mahārāja had worked for was undone, and only he carried on the mission of spreading Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s mercy to every town and village, while his other Godbrothers did virtually nothing. Unless Prabhu-pāda pointed out his outstanding example and the reason for his success, his disciples might in the future become forgetful of or bewildered by how the mercy of Lord Caitanya actually did grow into a worldwide mission.

Prabhupāda states in his August 20 letter to Haṁsadūta, “Even a slight change will spoil it. That is why I have always been so careful to give you only those things which I have heard from my Guru Mahārāja.” Therefore, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s followers, working cooperatively in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, should know their success is to always glorify Śrīla Prabhupāda in his supreme position and follow his example, not the example of his Godbrothers, who mainly spent time in litigation over possession of properties, who slowed their spiritual master’s mission to a halt, and who stopped their own personal preaching, which they had conducted actively during Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī’s personal presence.

In my commentaries to these letters, I often describe Śrīla Prabhupāda out of my own affectionate bond as a disciple; therefore, I emphasize that he is my own spiritual master rather than expatiating upon how he is the founder-*ācārya* for the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. That is my intent in writing these commentaries (whereas the *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta*, the more important work, focuses on Śrīla Prabhupāda’s worldwide accomplishments). Even in this book, however, which is written in the context of my own affection for Śrīla Prabhupāda as my spiritual father—who saved my life by giving me Kṛṣṇa consciousness—I must make it clear that he is no ordinary spiritual master. He not only saved me, but he saved hundreds of thousands of others.

He single-handedly transplanted the *bhakti-latā* (Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu’s *saṅkīrtana* movement) from its original soil in India to the harsh Northern climates of the *mlecchās,* and, by his expert gardening skill, that *bhakti* plant has miraculously grown up, flourished, and spread. By 1967, this was already well underway; even while Śrīla Prabhupāda patiently recuperated with a few devotees in India, his disciples in half a dozen U.S. centers planted their roots and introduced the *mahā-mantra, prasādam,* and *Back to Godhead* magazine to their countrymen (especially the youth of America). In this letter, Prabhupāda simply desired that his own followers would imbibe spiritual vision from him in disciplic succession, “And I hope, even in my absence, that the spirit of Krishna consciousness will be spread.”

## September 1967

September 23, 1967

My dear Raya Rama, Satsvarupa, and Himavati,  
Please accept my blessings. I have received many letters from you, and to some I have replied. I don’t know whether I’ve replied to all your points. I’ll be glad to hear from you about the progress of the Boston center. So far as my health is concerned, definitely I’ve improved because I am now cooking and typing. I think I will be returning by the end of October. Please send a letter of invitation stating that my presence is urgently required in the States. This will help enormously in obtaining my permanent visa. I must produce concrete evidence of the necessity of my presence in America for this visa status.

ACBS

Prabhupāda’s letter of September 23 is addressed to three persons: Rāyarāma, myself, and Himavatī, who were in living Boston at that time. Brahmānanda and Rāyarāma had arranged that Rāyarāma would transfer to Boston and take charge while I would take a supporting role. Prabhu-pāda wrote to Rāyarāma, “I think by this time you have gone to Boston.” But Rāyarāma never came. Rāyarāma’s main engagement was publishing *Back to Godhead*, and Śrīla Prabhupāda gave, through him, enduring instructions for *Back to Godhead,* as he did in this letter:

I’ve received news from other centers that BTG is now in demand, and your idea for publishing 5000 copies is very encouraging. The news which we are publishing in Back to Godhead has certainly a novel presentation to the people in the Western world, who are hankering after spiritual enlightenment. So if you continue to write articles on the basis of *Bhagavad-gita* and *Srimad Bhagavatam*, certainly they will appeal to the core of the hearts of all human beings, and surely the people will respond. So, continue the policy scrupulously, and you will be successful.

Śrīla Prabhupāda gives the success formula for his magazine. He does not emphasize publishing a slick magazine for public relations, but he wants articles based on *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and *Bhagavad-gītā*. Prabhupāda appreciated that the magazine was improving. (“I have not seen a single copy, although I’ve heard from many sources of its improvement.”) *Back to Godhead* was just beginning to evolve from a crude, mimeographed magazine to a better-looking graphics magazine, and Prabhupāda was happy to see the improvement.

Here Prabhupāda says he was receiving many letters from me and he was not sure whether he was replying to all my points. How much I was chatting with Śrīla Prabhupāda! I was writing him more letters than he could reply to. What I wrote was probably not important. My letters were sometimes outpourings from my heart. They often contained reports of our activities in Boston, and praises of Prabhupāda. It was not important that he reply to every letter I sent him. Still, it was important for me to write. The other devotees and I were simply living for Prabhupāda; it was not possible to serve in silent separation. We could serve in separation, but not in silence.

Prabhupāda’s main reason for writing was to ask for an invitation from the U.S. to help him get his visa. Although we disciples of Prabhupāda were U.S. citizens and were sincerely testifying that unless this sublime, kind, and all-wise person, the representative of Kṛṣṇa, came back to the States soon, our spiritual lives would dry up and die, the immigration department didn’t consider our sincere requests important. The actual truth was *our* situation—we urgently required Prabhupāda’s presence. Yet we needed official recognition of Prabhupāda’s importance for America from a nondevotee institution. This illustrates the nature of the material world. Prabhupāda also asked that we send a personal invitation from our ISKCON center in Boston.

While Prabhupāda wrote from India, I continued serving him in separation. I used to take the elevated train a good distance—a half hour or so—to the welfare office, and I would chant on my beads while commuting. I was still a stranger to the city. I didn’t know anybody, and people in the office didn’t know much of my activities. Each day I would give the *Bhāgavatam* class in my apartment before I left for work. When I came back at night, the devotees liked me to give another class; I would read again from the *Bhāgavatam*. During the day, while at work, I would prepare for the morning class whenever I had spare time.

In Boston I had even more duties than I had in New York. This didn't improve my relationship with my bosses at the welfare office. One time I visited my clients briefly and then went back to the temple. My boss must have suspected me, because he called the temple, and I foolishly answered the phone. “What are you doing there?” he demanded. I went back to the office, and they grounded me with office working hours from nine o’clock to five o’clock.

Rūpānuga had shown me how he read at his desk typewritten notes of Prabhupāda’s lectures instead of the thick *Bhāgavatam*. Then the supervisors didn’t suspect he was reading Kṛṣṇa conscious literature. I followed his example. I also wrote articles at my desk. Once I composed an essay about Lord Rāmacandra, \[which was later printed in BTG\].

Because I rose early, I would tend to doze at my desk. My supervisors would complain: “We don’t want him. He’s always falling asleep.” They transferred me to another department. The head of my new department was a crazy, neurotic woman, but I got along with her well because I was a devotee and could tolerate different people. One of the supervisors called me into his office one day and commended me, “You have inner peace. No one else could work with this woman, but you have been able to, and we are very thankful.”

How did I do it? I wouldn’t get angry; I was introspective and would chant in my mind and think about the temple. No one, therefore, could bother me. The supervisors accepted me as a peaceful person who did his work, but was obviously not interested in a career.

I used to get many calls from the temple while I was at work. I would try to conduct temple affairs from my office. Once the supervisors rebuked me, “This is not a church rectory. Why are you taking all these calls?” The workers around me would wonder what work I was doing when I would speak on the phone, preaching to the devotees with problems and giving directions for management and *saṅkīrtana.*

## October 1967

October 3, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I’ve not heard from you in a long time. Please convey my blessings to Rayrama, Damodar, and others. Since I’ve come to India, I’ve not received a single copy of BTG. There is no stationery nor prospectus either. I asked for these as well. But I’ve received nothing. I am starting for Calcutta on October 9. You may send stationery, BTG, and the prospectus to my Calcutta address by air mail. My Calcutta address is:

c/o Madan Dutta  
76 Durgha Charan Dr. Rd.,  
Calcutta, 14.

In my last letter to Brahmananda and Hayagriva, I’ve already requested that Rayrama take the introductory letter from Kirtanananda, and as previously settled, Rayrama may go to London in November. In London, there is one Miss D.C. Bowtell, 27 Cranhurst Rd., Cricklecod, London, N.W.2. This old lady was given Hari Nama while my Guru Maharaja was on earth. Since then she is maintaining one apartment and may be doing some preaching work. So Rayrama may open some correspondence with her before going there. This will help in the matter of opening a center in London. Besides that, I shall be glad to hear whether my book *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya* is now complete. I am afraid that there is no contract for publication with Macmillan Co. I have already asked Brahmananda to send the manuscript of *Geetopanishad* to the following address:

Hitsaran Sharma  
c/o Dalmia Enterprise,  
Scindia House,  
New Delhi, 1.

Please ask Brahmananda if he has already dispatched the manuscripts to the above address. I do not wish to delay publication any longer, but I wish to publish here in India.

Regarding my return: I may inform you that I am now ninety percent cured, and I can immediately return, but I want to return with a permanent visa. Please therefore consult Mukunda, Brahmananda, and Company and do all the necessary action. I shall expect your reply touching all the points made herein to my Calcutta address. Hope you are well.

N.B. I have received the letter of Jadurany. Please offer my blessings for her. I shall duly reply to her letter touching all the points.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

The first statement in this letter, “I have not heard from you in a long time,” immediately made me want to leap forward in reciprocation. Although we were serving Prabhupāda in separation, he became newly present in our lives when he made such statements.

I can clearly remember receiving this aerogram in my apartment in Boston and showing it to the other devotees. How wonderful are our Godbrothers, with whom we can share the intimate understandings of the relationship between guru and disciple. Most people cannot share these confidential understandings. We ourselves must never be-come skeptical like the ordinary person who dismisses the affectionate dealings we have with our spiritual master. By receiving this letter and Prabhupāda’s personal, human request for his stationery, a prospectus, and BTG, the chance to again serve him personally loomed up as an all-important reality, even though the things he requested were relatively small. One’s relationship with his spiritual master is not based only on receiving *śabda-brahma* (knowledge) from him. Here *sevā* is also stressed. Prabhupāda not only gave us Vedic knowledge for our comprehension, but he turned to us for his personal needs.

This letter is similar to Prabhupāda’s letter of February 15 because he turned to me after his other disciples had not complied with his request. When he wrote this letter he had not gotten some things he had asked for—the latest issues of BTG, stationery, and an ISKCON prospectus.

*Why wasn’t the guru served?* The spiritual master’s requests should always be regarded with gravity. We should not decide that something he wants is not important and neglect giving it to him. We should not think, “Oh, he is very elevated, meditating on Brahman; therefore, his request for stationery is not very important.” We should not tolerate neglect of his request (although we do tolerate when our own requests are not fulfilled). We should not confuse our interest with our guru’s. Rather, the spiritual master’s wishes are never ordinary; they are always instructive. When he asks us for something, it is the same as if Kṛṣṇa were to ask for it. If Kṛṣṇa asks us for a drink of water, we would consider it very important to give Him that water. If we love Kṛṣṇa, we will also think that *Kṛṣṇa needs that water.*

Similarly, when I received this letter, I (as well as the other devotees in Boston) became alarmed and concerned: *Why wasn’t Prabhupāda being served? Why* was he being neglected? In this letter Prabhupāda offered me a chance to give him what he desired. He therefore concludes, “I shall expect your reply.”

I am aware as I write this commentary that someone might criticize me for presenting myself as a favorite devotee of my spiritual master or for over-stressing my love for him. Someone could say I’m sentimental. Perhaps I am at fault.. I *do* know, however, that while Prabhupāda wrote these letters, I was always serving him with my body, mind, and words; and in return he was protecting me. When I reminisce over these affairs I may express my sentiments, but there is nothing wrong with this. That I am dwelling on all the particulars of his instructions, stressing their importance, and fondly remembering these past occasions with my guru-preceptor are sentiments fully approved by guru, *śāstra,* and *sādhu.*

We recall the meeting of Lord Kṛṣṇa with His former *brahmacārī* school friend, Sudāmā Vipra. When Sudāmā recalled how he and Kṛṣṇa had faithfully discharged the order of their spiritual master, Sāndīpani Muni, both Kṛṣṇa and Sudāmā merged in transcendental bliss. We cannot accuse Kṛṣṇa of foolishly or sentimentally remembering the past or being too fond of His guru. Rather, Sudāmā’s statement is approved:

Kṛṣṇa continued: “My dear friend, you may remember that many such incidents occurred while we were in the *āśrama* of our spiritual master. Both of us can realize that without the blessings of the spiritual master, no one can be happy. By the mercy of the spiritual master and by his blessings, one can achieve peace and prosperity and be able to fulfill the mission of human life.”

—Kṛṣṇa, Volume III, p. 76

The personal mood in Prabhupāda’s concluding words is touching. Śrīla Prabhupāda always lived with his devotees, sharing with them all the ISKCON adventures. Prabhupāda, like Sāndīpani Muni, was always with his boys, caring for them and engaging them in his personal mission of spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

October 6, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you for your letter of 29 September 1967, and I have noted the contents very carefully. The letter which you have enclosed therein to facilitate my permanent visa appears to be nice, but if an invitation or provisional appointment as lecturer is possible, I think that will be still nicer.

I am going to Calcutta with Achyutananda and Ramanuja to visit Lord Chaitanya’s birthsite at Mayapur. So until further information, you can address my letters to the above-mentioned Calcutta address.

If it is possible to get an official or provisional appointment letter through the influence of Mr. Ross and on the strength of the above certificates, it will be easier to get the permanent visa.

Regarding manuscripts: you can hold on till my arrival or the next letter in this connection. The thing is that there is regular negotiation with Macmillan Co. for taking up the publication. Brahmananda, in his recent letter, has informed that the sanction is awaiting for the president’s signature. So let us wait a few days more for the final word.

I can understand that you have secured a very nice place in Boston and there is good possibility of pushing our movement amongst the student community there. Our movement is certainly very much appealing to the younger section of your country, and if we are successful in the matter of attracting the student community in your country, certainly this movement will scatter all over the world with the foretelling of Lord Chaitanya, that in every town and village of the world the Lord will be famous for His glorious Sankirtan movement. Please try for this with your heart and soul, and your life will be a successful mission.

I am always aspiring after returning at your care and overloading you with typewriting tasks. You have done this work always very nicely and sincerely, and a secretary like Satswarupa Brahmachary is a great fortune for me. I hope we shall very soon meet again and help each other in the matter of discharging Krishna Consciousness engagements. I am now ninety percent all right, and I think I can return now safely. This typewriting work is done by me. For two days I am alone and doing everything myself as experiment. This proves that I am now well. Please offer my blessings to all the boys and girls there.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Śrīla Prabhupāda refers here to a letter I enclosed. The story of that letter is told in *Prabhupāda-līlā,* “A Visit to Boston:”

Finally, after several days at Harvard, Satsvarūpa managed to reserve a hall in a fraternity house for a single evening in November. The most difficult part was to persuade the house clerk to type a letter on the fraternity letterhead acknowledging that Swami Bhaktivedanta would lecture at the hall for one night in November. Although it was only a tiny victory, Satsvarūpa was pleased nevertheless. He sent the “Harvard” letter to Swamijī, who gratefully acknowledged it, but added that an appointment as lecturer would have been better.

Prabhupāda gently pointed out that what I had sent him wasn’t really that useful. Of course, what he was asking for was a tall order. Imagine my getting him an appointment as lecturer on the faculty of Harvard University. To become a faculty member of Harvard is difficult. I actually tried, but I was given the run-around and sent from office to office. People were polite and showed some interest in the Swami, my spiritual master, but they simply sent me from one place to another.

I was actually blissful walking around Harvard Square, going to different places—even though I was rather inept at getting what Prabhupāda wanted. It was an opportunity for self-purification, having to talk to college administrators and professors to glorify my spiritual master. I was not as interested in arranging a lecture for Prabhupāda as I was in getting a letter from them. (Prabhupāda wanted a permanent visa or “green card,” and he thought if someone from Harvard University wrote this letter saying they wanted him to lecture regularly, this would help.) When I attempted to get anything in writing from the faculty at Harvard University, however, they refused.

In the course of doing this, I came to the office of Professor Ingals, whom I mentioned in *Prabhupāda-līlā.* He is still one of the biggest Sanskrit scholars in America. He gave me a little of his time, and I spoke to him about how our spiritual master told us that Jīva Gosvāmī wrote many books in Sanskrit. I was innocent in those days and would become happy just to meet someone who knew something about our philosophy. Not just myself, but the devotees in general were so innocent that we would become thrilled just to see an Indian man, or a woman on the street dressed in a *sārī*, and we would go up to them with folded palms and say, “Hare Kṛṣṇa,” expecting them to be devotees like us. We were often disappointed to find them cold and nondevotional. Similarly, to see an academic professor smiling and acknowledging that he had read Jīva Gosvāmī’s books was exciting, but when I pressed the point—just as when I would try to talk to the Indians on the street—I could see he was not at all devotional.

I told Professor Ingals the story of how Jīva Gosvāmī learned Sanskrit. He was the nephew of Sanātana Gosvāmī and Rūpa Gosvāmī. When he was very young, he had heard about Lord Caitanya from them and immediately wanted to join Lord Caitanya’s movement, but being only six he thought, “What can I contribute?” He therefore studied Sanskrit for ten years to become more qualified, and then he joined the two Gosvāmīs in Vṛndāvana.

When I told the professor this story, he seemed happy to hear it, and his face brightened. Then, however, I pressed the point that I would like him to write a letter stating that Harvard University wanted Prabhupāda to come as a guest lecturer.

The professor then said that one of his graduate students, Mr. O’Connell (who was living at that time in the World Religions Center), was more interested in Vaiṣṇavism than he, and he suggested that I approach Mr. O’Connell. Again in my innocence I became excited that there was a Vaiṣṇava at Harvard University. I left Professor Ingals and hurried across the campus to the World Religions Center. There I knocked on Mr. O’Connell’s door. His wife answered. The first thing I said was, “Are you Vaiṣṇavas?” The woman became visibly disturbed. This hurt me, and I became disappointed.

She let me come in, however, and I soon found that her husband *studied* Vaiṣṇavism. There is a world of difference between someone who studies Vaiṣṇavism and the real servant of a Vaiṣṇava. Prior to entering their apartment, I didn’t know this truth.

On the walls of their apartment were different pictures and artifacts; their shelves were filled with knickknacks, one of a little man playing a drum. Many of the artifacts were of Indian origin, but they were not devotional. My disappointment began to change to a determined, righteous feeling of loyalty to Śrīla Prabhupāda. I understood that devotional service was a rare gift that could only be given by a pure devotee, such as Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Mrs. O’Connell was accustomed to entertaining her husband’s guests. She talked with me for a while, but I had already realized I was on nondevotee ground. Then Mr. O’Connell came home. He was also cheerful and friendly with me. He was proficient in academic knowledge about Vaiṣṇavism, and the talk became argumentative. By Prabhupāda’s grace, although I had been initiated only a year, I was able to hold my ground with him.

I had never been to Boston until now, and I had never dreamed of having anything to do with Harvard University. I had grown up in New York City and had gone to Brooklyn College. What would I be doing with Harvard intellectuals? In fact, when I was sent to Boston, I was a little afraid that I might have to debate with someone from Harvard. I didn’t think I could speak with anyone who was very educated. An educated person might say things that went over my head and I would find myself in an awkward situation. Here I was in the apartment of a Harvard University Vaiṣṇava scholar, and I discovered that the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy easily countered his arguments.

Of course, he did not convert, but neither did that lessen my enthusiasm to make the points of Kṛṣṇa conscious *siddhānta*, and neither was I intimidated by his nondevotional, academic view. I could also understand that the professor could assist us as a friend of our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in Boston, so I remained polite. We developed an acquaintanceship, and he said he would be interested in visiting our temple. Subsequently, he did visit, and he also met Prabhupāda. For years now he has maintained friendly contact with the devotees. I learned a lot, however, from my first meeting with Dr. O’Connell: a scholar in Vaiṣṇavism is not necessarily a devotee—in fact, he is almost necessarily *not* a devotee. These were some of my adventures at Harvard when I tried to get Prabhupāda a permanent place on the faculty, without success.

Then I started the grass roots approach. One night I saw an advertisement for a dance at Harvard. I went, but the hall was so dark and the rock music so intimidating that I had to leave after speaking to only a few people and giving out some flyers.

At other times, however, I went to the freshman dorms and knocked on the doors. Different people let me in. I would say, “Would you like to talk about spiritual life? Have you heard of Hare Kṛṣṇa? If you have some time, I’d like to talk with you about it. I’m from New York, and we’re chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa.” They did not want to appear unhip or ignorant of things happening in New York City. (Even though I hadn’t been to Harvard, I had some edge over the students there when I described an avant-garde thing happening on the Lower East Side—Hare Kṛṣṇa and Swami Bhaktivedanta.) The students were a little interested in the mantra, and, when they would let me into their rooms, I would talk to them about the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy. Sometimes, several boys would gather. I was older than most of these college boys, so I could easily speak with them and try to increase their interest in what I was doing.

One student told me of a person on campus who had been instrumental in inviting the Indian lecturer, Krishnamurti, to the campus the previous semester. I found this person. He was in his room alone; he was a violinist and a little strange. We talked for a while, and I tried to interest him in inviting Śrīla Prabhupāda for a series of lectures. If I could work through the students, I thought, then Prabhu-pāda could lecture there, and maybe I could get the letter of invitation, the real thing I wanted.

The young man and I had a discussion. He said that from my description, Prabhupāda sounded like he was just a servant, whereas Krishnamurti was a master. I replied that he did not know the power of the servant of God. I then told him how Ambarīṣa Mahārāja, a humble devotee, had defeated the *yogī-*master, Durvāsā.

We became a little acquainted, left his room, and walked around campus. I told him a devotee has no material desires. “But what about the desire to serve Kṛṣṇa?” he asked.

I said, “That is the *one* desire you can have and it is not material.”

He also questioned me: “You seem to be saying everything in reference to what you’ve heard from your spiritual master.” I *was* repeating what Prabhupāda had said; I said nothing except that. I told the young man, “Yes, but I am doing it from my heart. I am not just a tape recorder.” Speaking with this person and other Harvard students was a test of the depth of my conviction and boldness as a preacher for Prabhupāda.

This student led me to another who worked with the *Harvard Crimson.* I went to the luncheonette and sat with him as he ate a hamburger and drank a Coke. I asked him about sponsoring Prabhupāda, but nothing came of our meeting.

I finally contacted another person who did help me a little. He was connected with Phillip Brooks House, a fraternity. He gave me permission, using his name, to tell the secretary of the fraternity that Prabhupāda could speak at Phillips Brooks Hall for one night. I then went to the secretary and quoted my sponsor, the Harvard student, to ask permission for Swami Bhaktivedanta to give a talk at their hall. I asked her to write a letter, but she didn’t want to, and there was a lot of red tape. When I finally got the student who said he would sponsor Prabhupāda to testify with me that Prabhupāda was a bona fide spiritual leader, the secretary wrote a letter. This is the letter Prabhupāda refers to when he says, “The letter which you have enclosed appears to be nice, but if an invitation or appointment as lecturer is possible, I think that will still be nicer.”

As it turned out, Prabhupāda was not able to come to America that year early enough for the lecture, which was scheduled in November; the few devotees in the Boston temple held the program without him. There was, however, a newspaper report that described us as prophets. We sent it to Prabhupāda and he was pleased with us. Even though what we may do is small, if we endeavor, we will become purified, and the spiritual master will be pleased with us.

The second paragraph of this letter refers to when Prabhupāda went with Acyutānanda to Lord Caitanya’s birthsite. We were excited hearing how Prabhupāda was going to different places around India. We were enlivened enough in our relationship with Prabhupāda that his being far away did not weaken it or make us morose. A letter telling what he was doing only enlivened us. And we were enlivened, in any case, to serve him in separation.

Service in separation is a sublime doctrine of Vaiṣṇavism, yet even as neophytes we were realized in serving in separation. This was the way Prabhupāda trained us, to serve in separation—and we did it in a personal way. There was no other person to vie with our loyalty for Prabhupāda. While he was away from us, we did not become unchaste or misled. There was no one else but Prabhupāda. Service in separation was unhampered by any disturbance, such as becoming unfaithful or listening to another teacher. There was only the world of *māyā,* which appeared vast to us, and there was Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa consciousness, which were our only hope in reality.

The next topic Prabhupāda discusses is my getting a place for ISKCON in Boston. This is discussed in *Prabhupāda-līlā:*

When Satsvarūpa had gained entrance to the storefront for the first time, he had come alone, had checked out the bare front room, the basement, and the oil burner. Just as he was about to leave, however, he spotted a blue aerogram lying on the floor. He considered how it had traveled from hand to hand, originating with Swamijī in his room in Calcutta, and had flown to America, mixed in with thousands of other letters in a large mail bag and had been separated and brought here to a building where no one lived. Yet the mailman had put it through the chute, and it had glided a few feet and landed on the floor where it had sat for days. This was, of course, no more than the miracle of postal service. But the postman could not have possibly appreciated that so much was being carried in that lightweight aerogram. Sitting on the window shelf, Satsvarūpa read Swamijī’s words.

I can understand that you have secured a very nice place in Boston and there is very good possibility of pushing our movement amongst the student community there. Our movement is certainly very much appealing to the younger section of your country, and if we are successful in the matter of attracting the student community in your country, certainly this movement will scatter all over the world with the foretelling of Lord Chaitanya, that in every village and town of the world the Lord will be famous for His glorious Sankirtan movement. Please try for this with your heart and soul, and your life will be a successful mission.

This incident was another touching one for me. Prabhupāda was engaging us as missionary workers, pointing out our life’s work. One could say that in 1967, Prabhupāda told me to work in Boston with the students, but since then that has changed. How, then is such an instruction eternal? When I reflect on this, I see that the same instruction—that I should preach—is still pushing me on. I must still try to reach the student community, and all nondevotees, in whatever way I can by pushing on Prabhupāda’s movement. Prabhupāda said, “Please try for this with your heart and soul, and your life will be a successful mission.”

Prabhupāda asked for big things. He was lenient and would be pleased with us and encourage us if we had done a tiny service nicely. Then he pushed us to increase that service hundreds of times. Prabhupāda was an expert psychologist in guiding us to surrender to Kṛṣṇa while trying to achieve something ambitious.

At the end of this letter Prabhupāda is kind to me. He says I am a good secretary and looks forward to working with me again. He describes us as if we were equals: “I hope we shall very soon meet again and help each other in the matter of discharging Krishna Conscious engagements.”

The disciple does not accept his spiritual master’s kindness as flattery. The spiritual master may give his benediction to hundreds of disciples, but when he gives it to one individual disciple, that disciple doesn’t think, “Oh, he’s just saying that. He says that to everyone.” If a disciple thinks that, he is cynical and is falling down. I had no doubt that Prabhupāda actually meant what he said to me in kindness, although I did not deserve to be described as nice and sincere. The Vaiṣṇava is naturally kind. Even if I wasn’t sincere, what was pleasing to me was that Prabhupāda said I was. If Prabhupāda was saying it, Kṛṣṇa was saying it. Somehow I’d done something—only by Kṛṣṇa and my spiritual master’s grace.

I remember reading this letter in Boston. At the time we had not moved into our new center. I used to live in the kitchen of an apartment I shared with four other devotees in the south section of Boston. On the kitchen wall we hung a picture of Prabhupāda. I remember feeling so impure that I did not even want to look at that picture. I used to think Prabhupāda was looking directly at me from that picture, and I felt impure. Yet I thought, impure as I am, Prabhupāda is writing me this letter saying he wants to come to Boston soon and work with me. I did not understand his statement theoretically. If your friend who is far away expresses some affection and says he would like to get together with you again, you look forward to it actually happening as soon as possible. By this letter I became enlivened and looked forward to my great master, Śrīla Prabhupāda, returning so that I could work with him as he said in his letter. I still hope it is possible for Prabhupāda to come back and allow me to work with him (or perhaps I can go where he is and work with him). The instructions he gives here are eternal. Perhaps I can bring this letter as an introduction and I can ask Prabhupāda, “Please give me Kṛṣṇa conscious engagement.”

Devotional service is sweet. The nondevotees or the academic professors of Vaiṣṇavism cannot relish devotional service. The relationship between the spiritual master and disciple is personal and transcendental. This, as much as anything, is the meaning of these letters. They are philosophical instructions, but they are dear to us because they are personal. By remembering them, we can rededicate ourselves as the tiny servants of our spiritual master, and the basis of that affection is service. If I hadn’t done any service, how could Prabhupāda have blessed me by saying I was sincere or that he wanted to work with me. If there had been no work, then there would have been no basis for our reuniting. But we had a via medium—my service to him. It was his benediction that he engaged me in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Prabhupāda speaks in this letter of our reciprocal dealings with each other: I am helping him in his mission and he is serving Kṛṣṇa. Together we are pushing on his movement, which is Kṛṣṇa’s movement, Lord Caitanya’s movement. This kind of togetherness forms the basis of our relationship. Prabhupāda’s letters are therefore personal—from person to person.

Although Kṛṣṇa is supreme and infallible, He also appears as a human being and becomes the son of Mother Yaśodā, the friend of the cowherd boys, and the lover of the *gopīs.* Similarly, Prabhupāda, the eternal teacher, appears to us as our elderly master. Thus in this letter he informs us that he’s feeling better: “I am alone and doing everything myself as an experiment. This proves that I am now well. I am now ninety percent all right.” By this kind of exchange we are able to love our spiritual master in a personal way. Prabhupāda was informing us that he was again feeling well, and naturally we were happy.

\* \* \*

October 27, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have already duly received the invitation from Harvard University. It is understood that they are scheduling me for 20 November between 6 and 10 P.M. I can start immediately on the strength of my visitor’s visa, but I am waiting for Mukunda’s reply; he is trying for my permanent visa.

Yesterday we have all come to Navadwipa. The place where I am staying is another establishment of one of my Godbrothers. It is a very nice and extensive place, and my Godbrother B.R. Sridhara Maharaja has spared one entire nice house for our stay. He has also agreed to cooperate with our society. We shall observe his birthday ceremony tomorrow, and the brahmacharis shall learn how to celebrate the spiritual master’s birthday. I hope to reach your country by November 20. Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda appreciated the quality of Śrīdhara Mahārāja’s *āśrama* and the warm reception Śrīdhara Mahārāja, his old friend and Godbrother, gave him there, but even while in Navadvīpa observing his Godbrother’s birthday ceremony, Prabhupāda had his mind and heart set on re-turning to the United States. He was only waiting to see if Mukunda could get him a permanent visa. Prabhupāda was still seriously considering coming to the engagement we had set up for him at Harvard, which he finally did not attend. As weakened as he had become in his health, he was eager to make the journey to return to us. He showed us in letters like these, dating from as soon as he had arrived in India, that his mind was not far away from us in his private meditation, and as our own front-line general, he had gone back to India for recuperation and would soon return.

Neither was he just resting behind the front lines. On arriving in India, he had at once explored the possibilities of expanding his new Western movement into an East-West movement by establishing his own places in Vṛndāvana and Māyāpur.

Prabhupāda was not keeping merely a token connection with us by dropping us a letter, hoping by some kind of remote control he could guide his mission though us. Rather, his letters were urgent calls—"Why didn’t you get the permanent visa? Where are the letters from the U.S. institutions?” Prabhupāda would also regularly tell us of possible dates of his return, even if in actuality he was not able to come as early as he would state.

Prabhupāda’s letters were vital because *Prabhupāda himself* was vital. He was the source of electrical power lighting the whole ISKCON, and we were simply light bulbs in different sockets, going on brightly due to Prabhupāda’s merciful charge. From Navadvīpa he churned our feelings of separation, charged us with new activities to perform on his behalf, and promised he would soon return.

## December 1967

One of the three devotees in our Boston center received a letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda dated December 12 that I would like to include here:

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of December 3, 1967. You have described in your letter that my presence again before you will be wonderful. I quite agree with you. Your sincere prayer to Lord Nrisimhadev is helping me to recuperate my health, and you will be glad to know that I am returning to San Francisco on December 14 at 12:45 PM by PAA flight #846.

I can understand that you are all thinking of me twenty-four hours a day, and therefore Satsvarupa had a dream that I had gone to Boston and was enjoying your company. Similarly, I also think of you all, especially of you because you are so nice and good.

I understand also that there was a kirtan performance given by Sri Purna das. You have rightly remarked whether they are devotees. You are right. These people are professional singers. Krishna kirtan is not for earning livelihood. Krishna kirtan is not meant for entertaining the public through demonstration of arts. It is dynamic service to the Lord. We do not therefore mind so much about the artistic presentation of Krishna kirtan, but we want to see how much a devotee is satisfying the Supreme Will.
