Śrī Pūrṇa was a professional *bhajana* singer at a psychedelic dance hall in Boston. We had arranged with the dance hall owner that we could hold our *kīrtana* on stage between one of the scheduled acts (most of the performers were rock groups). We had gone at night into the big hall, filled with people and pulsating with colored strobe lights similar to the scene at the Cosmic Love-In in New York. It was a place of hallucinatory sense gratification; but ironically, over the main stage were painted the words, *“Praise the Lord.”*

We set up our canvas painting of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu and His *saṅkīrtana* party in Navadvīpa, and we performed our Hare Kṛṣṇa *kīrtana* with drum and *karatālas,* which the audience fairly appreciated. Suddenly an Indian singer also came on the stage and joined us, but first he offered proper respects before the painting of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu. It was a pleasant surprise to see someone sit and chant with us. We left immediately after our *kīrtana*, but we understood from Śrī Pūrṇa that he was going to make a full performance there that night. He told us that he sang different *bhajanas,* as well as other songs. Later, one of the devotees wrote Prabhupāda to inquire about this singer’s actual position; we were so naive about such things. Prabhupāda’s reply is instructive as to the true nature of *kīrtana*: “It is dynamic service to the Lord. . . . We want to see how much a devotee is satisfying the Supreme Will.”

These statements by Prabhupāda immediately became definitive descriptions for me. I memorized most of the phrases about *kīrtana* in this letter, and I would quote them whenever we discussed or executed *kīrtana.* This is still my understanding of the essence of *kīrtana.* I particularly like Prabhupāda’s point that we should see *kīrtana* as “dynamic service to the Lord.” Arts are for show of talent or some aesthetic appreciation, “art for art’s sake,” but *kīrtana* is when we sincerely cry out for Kṛṣṇa, “Please engage me in Your service.” If many people take part in *kīrtana*, then all of them should congregationally be calling out to the Lord in this mood. *Kīrtana* should never become a mere show or performance, even if a virtuoso musician takes part. He also should emphasize not artistry but “dynamic service.” *Kīrtana* is not rhythm and blues or flashy Bengali showmanship. The instruments are to enhance the service, not dominate it. We therefore understood that because our lives were dedicated to Kṛṣṇa and Prabhupāda’s orders, our *kīrtana* was potent, whereas the singing of Śrī Pūrṇa, being a means to earn his livelihood, was not authorized devotional service.

\* \* \*

December 16, 1967

My dear Satswarupa,

Please accept my blessings, and convey the same to Pradyumna and others. I missed your telephone call, but I understand that your center is doing well. When I go to New York I shall go to Boston. I understand that Damodar has gone to New York with the manuscript of *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya*. I thank you for your sincere service. I pray to Krishna for your more and more improvement in Krishna Consciousness.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

To a devotee in Boston:

December 16, 1967

When I left your country on July 22, I had very little hope of coming back again. But Krishna informed me that I’m not going to die immediately; therefore I have come back again to get inspiration of Krishna Consciousness from you all good souls. Although officially I am your spiritual master, I consider you all students of my spiritual master because your love for Krishna and service for Krishna teach me how to become a sincere Krishna Conscious person.

I understand that there are only three persons at Boston, but each of you can do the work for 300 persons, so it is not the numerical strength that works, but it is Krishna Consciousness that works. A Krishna Conscious person never tires of working, and these symptoms I can see in you, as you want to be overloaded with work. This is the test of how one is advancing in Krishna Consciousness. Nobody becomes tired but wants to work more and more. Your Godbrother Satsvarupa also told me the same thing, that he may be overloaded with typewriting work. Similarly, Govinda dasi and Goursundar also want to be overloaded with Krishna Conscious work. But physically I am not as strong as you all young boys and girls are. You can pray to Krishna that I may get your strength to serve the cause of Krishna Consciousness.

Please convey my blessings to Satsvarupa and Pradyumna. Inform Satsvarupa that very soon I’m going to overload him with tapes for typing. My dictaphone is a little out of order (Gargamuni has taken charge of repairing it), and as soon as I get back my work on *Srimad Bhagavatam* will begin. At the present moment I have got some difficulty in sleeping. I cannot sleep more than three hours a night and one hour in the day. So if it continues like that, and if I keep fit, I think I shall have ample time to work on writing books.

Hope you are well.

These letters were the first Prabhupāda wrote the devotees in Boston upon his return to the U.S., after his illness in the late spring and recuperation in India. They begin a new series of exchanges—the beginning of Prabhupāda’s plan to visit Boston in May and the resumption of my regularly typing his *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* tapes. Only two days after his arrival in San Francisco, he was enlivening us with new tasks and anticipation. When Prabhupāda refers here to overloading me with work, it is in a jolly spirit. This is symptomatic of the spiritual energy: a devotee wants to work, and he does not get tired. Prabhupāda used Acyutānanda as an example in his lectures: “Previously this boy would not even go to the store for his mother, but now he is working twenty-four hours a day in the service of Kṛṣṇa.” Impersonalists don’t understand the activities of the devotees; they think they are material. However, even Prabhupāda’s neophyte disciples felt the quality of liberation in being able to stay free of *māyā* by working all day.

We used to post a sign at 26 Second Avenue: “Always be engaged in work for Kṛṣṇa. If you cannot find any work, then read or chant Hare Kṛṣṇa.” Prabhupāda said that when we took rest we should feel we have so many work assignments (tasks given to us by the spiritual master for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness) that we can’t do them all. Just maintaining our little center in Boston was for three persons constant engagement—cleaning, washing pots, earning money, and preaching.

Prabhupāda refers to the prerogative of *young* people to do this work. Of course, Prabhupāda also worked constantly (although his “work” was on the spontaneous platform), but because he appeared to be in an elderly body, he left the “legwork” chores to his disciples.

Young people who join the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement should therefore use all their youthful energy in Kṛṣṇa’s service to carry out needful tasks. never be lazy. All devotees can take up this spirit. If each devotee works “for 300 persons,” how much we can do!

Prabhupāda writes in a handwritten note, “I pray to Krishna for your more and more improvement in Krishna Consciousness.” This kind of encouragement was very meaningful to me; it gave me realistic hope. I was not perfect but in need of improvement, and Prabhupāda realistically addressed my position in his prayers to Kṛṣṇa: “This boy is sincere, but he needs much improvement. Please help him to do better.” By the combination of Prabhupāda’s prayers on our behalf and our own prayers and endeavoring to our fullest capacity, we can hope to make ourselves worthy servitors of Kṛṣṇa.

\* \* \*

December 23, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. Your letter dated 17 December is duly in hand. I am preparing the text in my dictaphone for sending to you as usual, but you must have a dictaphone there; otherwise, how you can hear the tapes? So arrange for this, either one to hire or on purchase system. And as soon as you let me know that you have one dictaphone there, I will send you the tapes regularly.

I have already written to Rayrama about *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya*, that there is no need of editing the final manuscript. But let us prepare for its printing. He had some correspondence with Dai Nippon Printing Company of Tokyo, and our Dwarkadish did all the negotiations. Please contact him immediately and ask him to send me the correspondence he had with the printing company.

I also do not like too much editorial work. This too much editorial work on *Geetopanishad* has created some misunderstanding between the members of the editorial staff. Anyway, in the future one man should edit it, and that will be sufficient for our printing. And I do not want that *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya* should be edited again and typed again and time wasted in that way. I have also informed Rayrama of this, and you can also inform him like this. The book should be printed immediately, without any waste of time. That is my desire.  
Regarding Boston and Harvard University kirtans: I quite appreciate your noble activities. Our Kirtan movement is genuine, and if the unsophisticated students take it seriously, it will be a great achievement. I know that all these bogus yogis, they are cheating the public; but at the same time your countrymen also want to be cheated. They are cheating the public by words of extravagancy, saying that anyone can meditate, even if he is a drunkard. These cheap words attract people, and these rascals become popular. So we don’t want cheap popularity; I was very glad to learn from Gargamuni that Brahmananda refused to place my identification with so many cheater-swamis. We shall always remain distinct from all these cheaters. If we can convert one person to Krishna Consciousness, that is our success of life. We don’t want too many rascal followers. Let us perform this movement sincerely with faith in Krishna, and people will gradually appreciate our service.

I thank you very much once more for your ardent desire to cooperate with my missionary work. Krishna will make you happy.  
Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

A few days after his arrival in San Francisco, a devotee phoned us from San Francisco and told us some of the details of Prabhupāda’s arrival there. He also told us that he might be able to arrange for Prabhupāda to speak to us briefly on the phone. One night the phone rang in Boston storefront and it was Prabhupāda. We each spoke to him, one at a time. Jadurāṇī, who picked up the phone, spoke first. She said she had heard that Prabhupāda had looked healthy on coming from the plane, and that he was walking quickly and was tanned from the sun. He informed her that he had come from India by himself, without any assistant. When he said that, she almost correctingly replied, “But Kṛṣṇa was with you.” I took the phone next and said, “Oh Swamijī, we miss you very much. It’s so nice to hear you on the phone.” I heard Prabhupāda’s voice over the line: “Yes, and I am hearing you also.” Next Pradyumna spoke to Prabhupāda and told him he was trying to advertise for the sales of Prabhupāda’s *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. Prabhu-pāda heard his ideas, appreciated that he wanted to render some service, but suggested some better ideas. After the phone call, we all exchanged what we had said to Prabhupāda and what we had heard from him, and we agreed that each of us had said at least one foolish remark. But everything *he* had said was wonderful. Pradyumna’s and my happiness was so great that we couldn’t control ourselves from rolling on the floor.

The first paragraph of this letter appears to be just some business. For me, however, it was actually the most nectarean business and the most important part of the letter. The “business” between the spiritual master and the disciple is full of the best, life-giving substance. I can remember, for example, in the early days sitting in Prabhupāda’s apartment when he would ask me to assist him in looking through different papers on his desk. He would direct me to rip up some of the papers, and he would take others and deal with them in different ways. Simply sitting with him and ripping up papers for him was blissful because I was serving a great personality. Even Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, in His topmost feature does things in Vṛndāvana that seem inconsequential in the mundane world. He does not act as a king or as the supreme ruler, but plays with His cowherd friends. Even business with the spiritual master that appears to be of little consequence helps solidify our friendly relationship with him.

Prabhupāda had just returned from India, where he was recovering from his stroke. While in India he had not dictated anything. In fact, since his illness (which had begun in June 1967), he had not worked on his all-important *Bhāgavatam* translation. Prabhupāda was patient. He understood that everything was under Kṛṣṇa’s control and that only by His will could he write. Now he was back in the U.S., however, and his health was ninety percent re-covered; he was ready to work again.

This was auspicious, and all the devotees were happy (especially myself, for I was the typist). In my letter to Prabhupāda, I volunteered my typing services, but I mentioned I didn’t have a Dictaphone. Prabhupāda therefore asks me how I could do his work if I didn’t have a Dictaphone. These were the small-time days of ISKCON—just to get office equipment was a big endeavor; we had little money and little experience. I had done his dictation a year before in New York, but I had used Prabhupāda’s own equipment. I worked in the room outside Prabhupāda’s room and would regularly type there using his Dictaphone.

He wouldn’t let the equipment go far from his room. When I finished, I would neatly wind up the cord and return it to his room. However, now I was in Boston. Prabhupāda mentions in his letter that I volunteered to do the tapes, but I had no Dictaphone. Prabhupāda even had to tell me how I should get one, but I was happy to receive this letter and his direct order.

I did not want to commit our limited finances to a Dictaphone unless I knew I would be typing for Prabhupāda. I did not take it for granted that I would continue as his typist, but with his letter Prabhupāda had assured me that I would continue as a typist–so get a Dictaphone!

I used to take a bus to work, and on the way there was a place that sold office equipment. I stopped there and rented a Grundig Dictaphone. These days we use cassettes, but back then, cassettes were not commercially popular, and Prabhupāda used a reel-to-reel machine. I took great care of the Grundig Dictaphone. That day I wrote Prabhupāda, and he began regularly to send me his tapes.

Since I was the typist and there was a considerable interim between the time Prabhupāda dictated the tapes and the books were actually published, I had the exclusive fortune of having advance information. At this time, Prabhupāda was working on one of the middle chapters of the Third Canto, which describes the birth of Hiraṇyakaśipu and relates how the Kumāras went to the spiritual world and saw Lord Nārāyaṇa. I even wrote a *Back to Godhead* essay on this subject and published it before the Third Canto was released. Prabhupāda was always glad to see such articles printed, as long as we continued to prepare his books for publication.

Our center in Boston was not prominent, but this was one of our opulences, that I was receiving Prabhupāda’s tapes. I also used to edit the transcripts there, and then I would make a photocopy of the transcripts in my welfare office. I distinctly remember copying many pages of the chapter in which Diti approached Kaśyapa Muni to conceive a child, and as a result, the twins Hiraṇyākṣa and Hiraṇyakaśipu were born. One day, my supervisor called me into his office and told me that my editing was just too much and I would have to stop. Still, after that, I used to regularly write articles and edit the *Bhāgavatam* at my desk.

In this letter, Śrīla Prabhupāda also congratulates us for our “noble activities,” our Harvard University *kīrtanas.* After our first engagement at Phillips Brooks House, the faculty agreed to let us use the hall for a series of engagements. Even though Prabhupāda didn’t come, we held *kīrtana*, lectured and answered all their questions. That Prabhupāda called these activities “noble” surprised us, and we felt encouraged to continue missionary work on his behalf. His word enlivened us and convinced us that although we were just tiny devotees, our efforts were the real noble activities for a human being—chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and preaching on Kṛṣṇa’s behalf at the university.

A human being doesn’t really get satisfaction from degraded activities because in his heart he knows he is acting in a debased, animalistic way. An ordinary man would like to do something noble if he could, but he’s so selfish and addicted to sense gratification that he cannot do anything noble, or he becomes cynical, thinking there is no such thing as nobility. But when someone who is actually noble-hearted, like Śrīla Prabhupāda, recognizes that his disciples’ activities are also becoming noble, then those disciples, despite their disqualifications of low birth and bad inclinations, feel purified and satisfied. Kṛṣṇa conscious activities are truly noble.

Prabhupāda was a little hopeful for the unsophisticated students, but he noted that the bogus *yogīs* were cheating the public. He specifically referred to Maharishi Mahesh Yogi. At the time there was a great wave of popularity for Transcendental Meditation. Maharishi had arrived with the Beatles, and there were many articles about him in *Look* and *Life* magazines. He had one big engagement at Madison Square Garden in New York City, and then he came to Boston and had an engagement at Harvard University. We went to that engagement and stood outside, giving out a leaflet I had written, which (without calling Maharishi by name) denounced Transcendental Meditation as inferior to chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and invited people to our temple. Some people criticized us for giving out these flyers at the engagement, especially since we declared we were involved in meditation. People thought we were part of Maharishi’s program, but when they read the flyer they could understand that we were criticizing the program they were about to attend.

During this period, there was not much distribution of literature in ISKCON. We printed a limited number of BTG’s, and we would give them out in Boston as quickly as we could; then we would resort to passing out flyers. I used to distribute the BTG magazines at the psychedelic head shops in Boston and Cambridge; some of the shop owners would take a regular consignment of ten magazines. They would always sell them and give me the money at the end of the month, when I would bring them another issue.

It is interesting that during the first few years of ISKCON, the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement was known as a psychedelic or hippie movement. Although we did not think of ourselves as hippies, we took advantage of the people’s misconceptions (just as nowadays we have vegetarian cooking classes, although we are not health-food faddists). We had this rather unwholesome association, but it did give us an inside line to the youth, who in the sixties were mostly hippies. I used to deliver BTG’s to the head shops, and then I would go to Harvard Square to sell them. There were hawkers in Harvard Square, and I soon became one of them. The Black Panthers, who were radical propagandists, sent their men dressed in combat boots and fatigues to Harvard Square, and in the name of selling their paper they would shout radical philosophy right on the street, accusing the passersby of being bigots and denouncing America with their rabble rousing speeches.

It was quite a job to catch people’s attention. I used to station myself at a good spot in Harvard Square and start hawking the magazine, as I had done on St. Mark’s place in New York, saying things like, “Newest yoga magazine! Magazine on transcendental life! Here’s a magazine about chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa!” These things interested people because they saw the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement as an exciting avant-garde group. Right around that time the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra was sung in the musical *Hair,* and people knew us as a kind of underground youth movement. We took advantage of this and met a mostly good reception. People would usually stop, but sometimes we would have to use sales techniques to get them to actually buy a magazine. I would open the magazine, show them the contents, and try to convince them to pay the quarter for a copy. We regularly distributed magazines and flyers and reported our results to Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda allowed us to remain connected with the hippie image until he heard from a professor in Germany that respectable people didn’t think highly of the devotees’ mixing with persons like Allen Ginsberg. Prabhupāda wrote to Hayagrīva that we should stop all tendencies toward mixing with the hippies and stand on our own. In New York we attended different functions to give out our magazine and to chant, and usually our tendency had been to attend functions connected with left-wing radical political movements. If there was a peace march or a war protest, we would attend, and we began to be known as part of the culture.

At the *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs* we held even as early as when I was in New York, we regularly used to debate about whether our identification with the hippie culture was good or not. I was of the opinion that we shouldn’t tag along after hippie groups but should have our own functions. We had a bad experience at one anti-war protest in New York. Some of our literature was ripped up by militant political rightists, and some of them even followed us, threatening us. That we should risk our reputation by being connected with leftist politicians didn’t seem right to some of the devotees. After this incident at the anti-war protest, Brahmānanda and Gargamuni decided we should also attend some of the rightwing functions. However, in our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī,* I made a proposal that we should have our own street *saṅkīrtana* (which we called parades), and we began marching all the way from the Lower East Side to the West Village with a little ragtag *saṅkīrtana* party. When Prabhupāda saw these ideas in our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* notes, he wrote some notes of his own in the margin. He said this was an excellent idea; we should not attend these political functions anymore, but should have our own functions like street *saṅkīrtana*. At least on the East Coast, we began to break away from the hippie scene. No longer did we simply attend the latest Be-In or peace movement rally where we tried to be accepted by the people. We began distinguishing ourselves as a time-less, transcendental movement by holding our own functions.

In this letter Prabhupāda criticizes the bogus *yogīs* in his reference to Transcendental Meditation. With considerable alarm, we saw the wave of popularity TM was enjoying grow. Even people to whom we had been preaching in Boston would turn away from us and begin to practice Transcendental Meditation. It was painful to see. We preached to persons that our movement was actually authentic, supported by the Vedic literatures, but they would say, “Well, Maharishi says that even if you do a little bit of good, it is good, because it is transcendental.” Several young people interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness turned away from our temple to take up this nonsensical meditation. We became disappointed, and perhaps some of the devotees became a little fearful or doubtful. It was important that Prabhupāda strengthened our conviction by telling us that Transcendental Meditation would come and go.

At the time something happens, it’s hard to have a long-range view of the situation. There we were, just beginning to enjoy a little popularity of our own for being a spiritual movement that performed meditation when TM came along and swept many people away. We became disturbed, but Prabhupāda assured us that TM *would* come and go. Now that has proved true. The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is strong; it has deep cultural roots in countries all over the world, and TM's popularity has dwindled. It has been exposed as a joke because of its levitation and money-making practices; it cannot be considered spiritual.

At that time, however, those following TM were making claims that theirs was the most exciting, mind-expanding, and blissful of meditative processes. Prabhupāda’s words should be understood in this context: we needed to hear them, and he did not say them cheaply. Almost everyone thought Maharishi was wonderful—he was the latest thing. At least they thought he was a true guru and a transcendentalist. On the other hand, Śrīla Prabhupāda’s position was as solid as a rock, and he gave us some of his own confidence. We thus became convinced and we went on preaching, even though we had little popularity compared to Maharishi.

I cannot overemphasize the seriousness with which we read Prabhupāda’s statements. When he said, “We don’t want to make rascal followers. Let us perform this movement with sincerity, and people will gradually appreciate”—we took these words as our sustenance. The devotees and I were all young in devotional service; there were no older or advanced devotees except for Prabhupāda, who was often somewhere else preaching. Our centers were not that well established, and even the local authorities, such as the police, didn’t know what to think of us. We had little money and no established means of income, such as collections from *saṅkīrtana*. Even though we had a small overhead, we did not know from day to day how we would maintain ourselves. Going on the streets with a *śikhā* and wearing Vaiṣṇava dress was still an extraordinary spectacle. We were Prabhupāda’s poor students, and we had given up all our family connections and hopes for careers and were now doing missionary activities for Prabhupāda. He had to encourage us in so many ways. Prabhupāda’s encouragement was like daily food for hungry men.

These letters were not casual communication with Śrīla Prabhupāda. Without his letters, how could we have lived? How could we have gone on preaching in a city like Boston or Cambridge, at a time when TM was popular—without becoming disappointed and disturbed—unless Prabhupāda had informed us of our actual position and the position of others?

Śrīla Prabhupāda ends this letter with sweet, encouraging words, thanking us for our ardent desire to cooperate with his mission. I remember thinking, “Prabhupāda understands I have an ardent desire.” Whatever ardent desire I had, *he* had created; but that he said we were trying to serve him with a little intensity and sincerity was comforting.

In those days the conditions were austere, and there were not many great successes to uplift us. We received this letter in our little temple in Allston, which was a small brick structure about as big as a car garage. We partitioned it into a temple room and kitchen, and there was a cellar in the basement. There we had a bathtub supported on cinderblocks, but there was no hot water. Those were our living quarters. My own living quarters in this temple were on a shelf that jutted out from the inner structure of the building. This shelf overlooked the kitchen, and I used to climb up there to sleep, read, and work.

Our temple had such a fragile existence. We were situated in the midst of the Boston University housing complex, and often there were different harassments from the students. Even worse than the students were the younger juvenile delinquents and local kids, who would regularly break our windows and were constantly giving us trouble. The first night we opened, a good number of students came. They understood we were a yoga group. Some of the women who came were wearing leotards, and the men were also ready for some kind of evening of athletic sitting postures and exercises. But we held *kīrtana.* Then I spoke about how I had come from New York and what the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement was. They were rather disappointed that it was not their idea of yoga. After the opening night, although we were in the midst of Boston University housing, hardly any of the students came, either in the day or at night. Most of those who did come, came from another part of the city.

Going to Harvard University and other universities and churches, preaching in Harvard Square, and doing whatever other small activities—these somehow added up in Prabhupāda’s mind so that he felt we had an ardent desire to spread this movement. Since those times were in some ways difficult and austere, his words gave us the courage we needed to go on.

December 30, 1967

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have received back the tape which you have typewritten. The inquiry about *pores* should be *force.* Today I am sending you two Krishna Conscious lectures, tape recorded into the dictaphone. Please write them properly and send me one copy. The best thing will be as soon as you type the tapes, send me one copy after editing, as you were doing formerly. I’ll keep one copy with me, you keep one copy with you and if further editing is required, another copy should be kept with you.

You will be glad to know that yesterday I have signed the agreement with Macmillan for publishing *Geetopanishad*, and also, Mr. B. K. Nehru was met by me the day before yesterday, and he has promised to help me in getting the permanent visa.

So far as my health is concerned, Goursundar is keeping me quite fit by massaging and Govinda dasi is supplying me opma. Perhaps you have never tasted what is opma? But if Jadurany can prepare it, I shall send the formula.  
Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

For me this was the essence of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s return to the U.S.—again he was translating *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, and again I was receiving tapes. Life was back.

The tapes began to arrive at the Boston Temple—plastic reels of Grundig dictation tapes, wrapped in a simple business envelope, stapled and addressed in Prabhupāda’s own hand. First they came at a rate of once a week, then more quickly, two or three a week. It was a strain for me to keep up while working full-time on my job and looking after the affairs of the temple. But the dictation work gave me pleasure and satisfaction. It was what touched me, delighted me, and intrigued me most. In the early morning hours, before going to work, even before the others had risen from bed, I would sit on the kitchen floor listening to Prabhupāda’s voice through earphones, and I would type. It was better than getting a letter. Prabhupāda alone in his room, was speaking the philosophy of Kṛṣṇa consciousness into my ears.

Sometimes while riding on the bus to work I would fall asleep and dream of Prabhupāda’s voice in my ears as I rapidly typed. It was very personal service.

—Prabhupāda-līlā, “A Visit to Boston”

In this letter Prabhupāda suddenly introduced me to the benefits of *upma prasādam*. He enticed me: “Perhaps you have never tasted what is opma?” and that made me immediately inquire for the “formula” from the devotees in San Francisco. *Upma* is now a great favorite of mine, mostly due to the mention of it in this letter. It is cooked with a procedure similar to that used for *halavā*. First farina is cooked in a pan until it is brown. Then instead of mixing the grains with sugar water, you mix them with vegetables (which you cook separately). The combination of simply cooked vegetables along with grains of *halavā*-like texture makes a nice hot dish of *prasādam.* In his letter, Prabhupāda gives us a glimpse into his daily life, which was pleasing to all of us. He took massage from his servant and directed others how to cook nice *prasādam,* like *upma.*

I had experimented on my own with cooking when I had had my own apartment around the corner from the temple at 26 Second Avenue. Before I had been initiated, I had tried cooking *capātīs.* That took a lot of time, great effort, and complete concentration. I had kneaded a big ball of dough with water, but when I had cooked the *capātīs* they had come out stiff and burnt. I had saved them, though, and every day when I came home from work I ate some of them.

I learned how to make *halavā* in Boston from Haṁsadūta. He had learned from Kīrtanānanda. Haṁsadūta showed me how to put the farina in a pan, gradually brown the grains, and then add butter. He said, “You keep adding butter until it looks like wet sand.” He showed me how to throw the farina into boiling sugar water. This was the first thing I learned to make.

After that I also learned how to make raisin chutney and different *sabjīs.* I learned only a few things from Haṁsadūta; I learned other things on my own. I also used to make *samosās*. We would follow recipes the devotees had circulated. In those days we used to cook oatmeal cereal with lots of powdered milk. The devotees loved these oatmeal cereals, and they made them even richer by adding fruits.

A few days before the Sunday feast, when I came home from work in the evening, I would make chutney, because it can be made in advance. I would put it aside in a jar, then I would make *gulābjāmons.* I used to stand over the wok, waiting for the *gulābjāmun* balls to bubble in the ghee and turn over. Then I would also save them in a jar.

For the Sunday feast, we would make ten or twelve preparations. I would cook most of them myself, with Pradyumna’s help. On Saturday nights we made the *samosās, halavā, kacaurīs,* and different kinds of chutney. Then at the feast we would put the large pots, filled with the various preparations, before our guests. I would lecture. With great effort I tried to relate to their situations or youthful mentalities as much as possible. It was a personal plea, based on philosophy, to bring them to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The visitors liked to chant *kīrtana* and take *prasādam* with us. The Sunday feast was our most successful preaching activity.

After this, we began looking forward to Prabhupāda’s coming to Boston. Now it was possible because Prabhupāda was in the U.S. We became fixed in our desire that Prabhupāda come, and we persistently asked him. In retrospect, I can see that our center in Boston was not developed enough to demand Prabhupāda visit us. If we had seen strictly from the viewpoint of Prabhupāda’s personal interests, we should not have been so insistent that he come. Actually, we were asking him to come for our own spiritual benefit. At the same time, however, our simple mentality was that since we were preaching on Prabhupāda’s behalf, whatever we were doing in Boston was also his mission, so he would probably want to come. Aside from that, we frankly and openly told him that we needed him to come to lead the charge. For us, inviting Prabhupāda was the most natural thing to do, and there was no question of stifling our strong invitation. Although we were somewhat brash and demanding in our request, we were also sincere in our hopes for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

## 3: 1968, January 17 - December 31

## January 1968

January 17, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have your letter of January 9, 1968 duly in hand, and I thank you very much for it. Yes, I will make plans to appear there on May 1, and before I arrive there on May 1, you can please arrange some lectures in different universities and colleges so that after reaching there I will find all the engagements there.

I thank you very much for your kind words, and you can pray to Krishna that I may be for some time with you. That is my desire. I have no objection to death. I am an old man; still I wish to live because I want to see that the mission I have started may be still stronger before I leave. So pray to Krishna for this.

Krishna can be known by His mercy only, as you will find in the text of the Kumaras. One thing: You will note that the Kumaras considered themselves sinful after cursing the two doorkeepers of Vaikuntha, and this was realized when they were fully conscious of the Supreme Lord. I have sent you one tape today. You will find the explanation there, and enjoy it. Yes, if it is possible to send carbon copies of *Srimad Bhagavatam* tapes to other centers, please do so. Also, please send a copy of *Bhagavatam* tapes, and also lecture tapes if it is possible, to Goursundar care of this address. Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

P.S. Today I have received your letter of the 16th and a tape. I have also sent another tape. ACB

Prabhupāda was coming! “Yes, I will make plans to appear there on May 1.” However, we had to make it worthwhile—“Please arrange some lectures in different universities and colleges so that after reaching there I will find all the engagements there.”

On receipt of this letter, I tried to secure engagements for Prabhupāda. At Boston University, I attended a meeting of one of the student organizations. They were social activists, so I read to them a section from Prabhupāda’s *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* that I thought they would find relevant. It was a passage where Lord Viṣṇu is described as the whole tree and all the living beings are the different leaves. In that passage, Prabhupāda describes that by watering the root of the tree, all the branches and leaves will be nourished automatically. Thus Kṛṣṇa consciousness is the one treatment that will cure all the different diseases.

I explained how Prabhupāda was teaching *bhakti-yoga*, which was actually a transcendental solution to all material problems. “It will be valuable,” I said, “for the students to hear from this great leader.” When I made my presentation, the student officers liked the idea of having Swami Bhaktivedanta come and speak at the university. They asked me how much it would cost. I asked for $100 and they immediately agreed. They then dismissed me so that they could go on with the rest of their meeting, and I went upstairs.

As soon as I was out of the room, I suddenly heard a loud burst of laughter from their meeting room. I couldn’t help but think that they had made some joke at my expense. As I went outside the university building and waited for the trolley to take me to Allston, I began cursing myself for asking for only $100 from them. I thought asking for such a small amount of money showed my lack of faith. If I asked for a little in Kṛṣṇa’s name, then He would only give me a little. I had been in so much anxiety about securing the engagement that I didn’t want to jeopardize it in any way by asking for too much money. But I had underdone it.

In the second paragraph of this letter, Prabhupāda thanks me for praying to Kṛṣṇa for his long life. He says he is not interested in a long duration of life for himself, but only to solidify the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. How human and touching these dealings seem today! Śrīla Prabhupāda in his personal form is now gone from this world, and to think of praying for him while he was here, asking him to stay, seems more poignant now than it must have felt when he was actually present. When I expressed this feeling in 1968, it was a way of expressing my affection; I had no idea then how difficult life would be in ISKCON without Prabhupāda. Now we are experiencing it.

Śrīla Prabhupāda said, “You can pray to Krishna that I may be for some time with you.” Now with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s permission, I wish to pray that since his inevitable disappearance has caused disruption and threats of splintering to his Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, may Lord Kṛṣṇa please protect this movement. I pray that Śrīla Prabhupāda please remain with us by our understanding of his instructions. Prabhupāda’s wish as expressed in this letter is the same wish that remains with us today: “I want to see that the mission I have started may be still stronger… So pray to Krishna for this.”

In this letter, there is a reference to some material on the tapes Prabhupāda was sending me. He was working on the Third Canto, and here he comments on the section where the Four Kumāras curse the doormen at the gates of Vaikuṇṭha. We could understand that when Prabhupāda was dictating, he was enjoying the material and taking note of it. He was not like a professor who goes through hackneyed material and mechanically writes it on the blackboard for his students to copy into their notebooks. Rather, Prabhupāda himself was most interested in the *Bhāgavatam.* He was enjoying it, and he wanted to extend that enjoyment to me.

I was delighted because our exchange was personal. At the same time, Prabhupāda was not dictating just so I could hear *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* on my earphones and enjoy it in Boston. He was dictating it for the whole world; I was the insignificant typist-editor through whom the wonderful *Bhāgavatam* was being passed and distributed. Prabhupāda, however, did not overlook or neglect me, but, as if nudging me in the side, he encouraged me to enjoy and note the instruction. The instruction in this letter is for the whole world, but in an intimate side moment, the speaker of the *Bhāgavatam* is turning to his typist and asking him to take special relish.

Prabhupāda said, “You will note that the Kumaras considered themselves sinful after cursing the two doorkeepers of Vaikuntha, and this was realized when they were fully conscious of the Supreme Lord.” I hadn’t written Prabhupāda anything about the attitude of the Kumāras. Prabhupāda picked out a particular instruction and pointed me toward it.

When I received this letter, I wondered, “Why is he saying this? Why should I pay special attention to this?” The instruction is one of humility. The Kumāras were justified in cursing the doormen because the Kumāras were spotless *brahmacārīs;* they did not expect to be barred at the door of Vaikuṇṭha. The doorkeepers offended them, and the Kumāras had a right to curse them. Afterwards the Kumāras felt they had overreacted out of pride in cursing the intimate devotees of the Lord. Although they were offended, they had responded with offense.

No one can know the mind of a Vaiṣṇava. Why Prabhupāda selected this passage from his tape to impress me I don’t know, but I accepted that since humility is an important instruction, he was advising me that I had better be humble, even if there was some occasion where I would be justified in cursing someone who had offended me. A devotee will become humble when he is “fully conscious of the Supreme Lord.”

I was dedicated to typing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s tapes. I used to rise early in the morning, before the other devotees, and sitting on the kitchen floor, I would put on the earphones of my rented Grundig Dictaphone and type on my manual typewriter. I made an extra copy with carbon paper. Despite the strain, I always rose early to type, and it gave me deep pleasure and satisfaction. It was what touched me, delighted me, and intrigued me most. It was philosophy, which I was eager to learn (especially since there was not much philosophy distributed in the early days), and the tapes were personal—the intimacy of hearing Prabhupāda speak in his Dictaphone in his own room.

When I received this letter, I was already completely absorbed in typing for Śrīla Prabhupāda, and he touched me further by telling me in advance that the tape he was enclosing that day in his letter was to contain some special nectar, and I should note it. Thus he says, “You will find the explanation there, and enjoy it.” Spiritual life is meant to be enjoyed and relished, and here Śrīla Prabhupāda tells me to go ahead and enjoy. That I was already doing, but when he told me to, I enjoyed going deeper into the ocean of transcendental happiness. I also noted his instruction about humility.

The devotees in the temple would constantly talk among ourselves about Prabhupāda’s letters and dictation tapes. We also became blissful hearing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s latest instructions. Prabhupāda’s instructions are always fresh, and we felt that they were the latest news coming from Vaikuṇṭha. When I talked to the devotees in New York and other centers, they would want to tell me the latest thing Prabhupāda was saying, as if it was the most exciting news. We are not to learn Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy from a dull textbook in a schoolroom setting. Rather, learning our philosophy is our exciting life. That is where the idea came from that I should not simply hoard Prabhupāda’s tapes, but I should send carbon copies to other centers. In this letter, Prabhupāda approved this idea by saying, “Please do so.”

\* \* \*

January 22, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have duly received back the tape from San Francisco. Herewith please find another tape. I have received back the edited papers on *Srimad Bhagavatam*.

Brahmananda and others cannot change the style. They can see if there is any grammatical discrepancy.

I understand that sometimes you feel urges and frustration. In the material world the sex urge is the binding force for material existence. A determined person tolerates such sex urges as one tolerates the itching sensation of eczema. If one cannot control the sex urge, then he can legitimately marry. Immoral sex life and spiritual advancement are incompatible. Your full engagement in Krishna Consciousness and constant chanting will save you from all inconveniences.

You have accepted me as father, so I have also accepted you as my dear and real son. The relationship of father and son on the spiritual platform is real and eternal. On the material platform such a relationship is ephemeral and temporary. Although I cannot give you anything as a father, still I can pray to Krishna for your more and more advancement in Krishna Consciousness. Your sincerity and service mood will always help you in advancing your general cause.

Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is a handwritten letter and is therefore especially valuable. Because it is a direct personal communication, a handwritten letter from the spiritual master is special for the disciple.

In the letter Prabhupāda writes, “Brahmananda and others cannot change the style. They can see if there is any grammatical discrepancy.” This refers to my desire to see Prabhupāda’s writings published almost exactly as he wrote them. I thought Prabhupāda’s utterances in his writings were perfect exactly as they came from him. I felt there should be only small punctuation and grammatical changes, and even phrases and sentences should be allowed to remain, even if they went against grammatical rules. Other disciples, like Rāyarāma and Brahmānanda, argued that the important thing was the instruction Prabhupāda’s writing conveyed, not the exact way he said it.

There is an example that determines the relative merit of these two attitudes. Once a disciple heard the spiritual master misuse a word according to the dictionary, and he thus concluded, “The dictionary is wrong.” This showed his faith in his spiritual master. Another disciple, however, on seeing the proper meaning in the dictionary, felt that in his spiritual master’s best interest his “mistake” should be corrected. Similarly, a disciple who wants to see his spiritual master appear nice in a photograph may step forward and request his spiritual master to adjust the flower garland he is wearing if it is not straight so that his Guru Mahārāja’s photograph will be first-class. The disciple who wants his spiritual master’s use of words to conform to the dictionary (or who wants to adjust his spiritual master’s garland) has a higher understanding than the other disciple.

Prabhupāda himself wanted his literatures presented without any grammatical discrepancies. My desire to publish his work partially unedited was, therefore, a more neophyte understanding. It is true, however, that editing the spiritual master’s writing is a delicate matter, and in the name of editing, the meaning should not be changed. Prabhupāda would become angry when he saw his disciples alter the original meaning of his writings.

In the second paragraph of this handwritten letter, Prabhupāda gives me advice how to handle sex desires. I had written him about these desires. In replying, he dealt with me individually, but I could understand from his letter that all living entities have this problem. “In the material world, the sex urge is the binding force for material existence.” Prabhupāda gives me a Kṛṣṇa conscious alternative: either be determined and tolerate sex urges “as one tolerates the itching sensation of eczema,” or if this is not possible, “one can satisfy the sex urge by legitimate marriage.” However, he clearly rules out immoral sex.

The spiritual master sometimes puts before us the fact that we have our own free will, but he gives us guidelines of what we can and cannot do. We are free to use our intelligence within certain alternatives, but certain acts always remain forbidden.

Prabhupāda’s statement in the last paragraph of this letter is the essence of personal dealings. I had written Prabhupāda, as many disciples do, acknowledging him as my true father. Prabhupāda confirmed this, putting himself forward without a doubt as my real father. He pointed out the wonderful relationship we both had entered at initiation: “You have accepted me as father, so I have also accepted you as my dear and real son.” We tend to be firmly fixed in material conceptions, and there is no greater illusion than the bodily connection between ourselves and our material mother and father. Prabhupāda boldly asserts spiritual reality, however, and discards the material connection as insignificant. We should therefore always remember that initiation is more important than our births in this material world. It is not something merely official—a priestly ritual, which has no real effect on our lives. Rather, a second birth is more important than our first. *Śāstra* states:

*janame janame sabe pitā mātā pāya*  
*kṛṣṇa guru nahi mile baja hari ei*

**In the transmigration of the soul through species, everyone gets a father and mother. The difficulty is to obtain a bona fide spiritual master and Kṛṣṇa.**

Even dogs and cats have mothers and fathers, but only in the human species can we get our eternal spiritual father.

Here Prabhupāda makes śāstric injunction personal—it is handwritten, addressed to me. In this way the guru is more merciful than Kṛṣṇa or *śāstra*. A disciple knows he can approach his spiritual master with his particular mentality, present his own case before him, and hear directly from his spiritual master the suitable application of *śāstra* on his own case. The spiritual master’s personal statements, therefore, are to be taken as absolutely as the *śāstra’s*. The disciple must remember and quote them, as I am doing here: “Although I cannot give you anything as father, still I pray to Krishna for your more and more advancement in Krishna Consciousness. Your sincerity and service mood will always help you in advancing your general cause.”

In repeating the spiritual master’s words, it may be said that the disciple is imitating or following him. Actually, the two terms are different. Following the spiritual master means to obey his orders; imitating him means to try to artificially surpass him or assume some affectation to be exactly like him. Most of Prabhupāda’s disciples however, have picked up his mannerisms. Some devotees, although Americans, now speak with an Indian accent like Prabhupāda’s. Especially Prabhupāda’s older disciples display many of his traits and gestures such as turning one’s head and hand in a certain way. These things, although an imitation of Prabhupāda, are not harmful. We disciples of Prabhupāda observed him and try to do everything as he did.

We also repeat his words; that is the duty of the disciple. However, if a disciple recites a realization his spiritual master has expressed without having that realization himself—that would be considered imitating the spiritual master.

I also try to follow Prabhupāda’s example with faith, realization, and dedication. For instance, I follow Prabhupāda’s daily schedule: rising early to write books, chanting *japa,* stopping my work before noon to bathe, taking rest after lunch. And most important of all, I try to follow Prabhupāda’s example of dedicating himself to the devotees, sacrificing, traveling, and preaching to the non-devotees. We can follow all these things.

\* \* \*

January 30, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am just in receipt of your letter of yesterday’s date, along with a copy of *Srimad Bhagavatam* 316/1, beginning from page 1708. I have sent one tape this morning; probably you will get it tomorrow. I have received your edited copy of *Srimad Bhagavatam;* it is very nicely done. For the last few days I am feeling a little trouble with my brain. There is a buzzing sound always, which sometimes disturbs me, and therefore my tape recordings in the dictaphone have become a little slow, but I will go on sending tapes as far as possible.

I understand that some of our students are in Boston, and they are not attending the Temple or cooperating with you. This means that they are under the clutches of Maya, more or less. After all, Krishna Consciousness is a difficult job for less intelligent persons. But one who understands Krishna Consciousness and acts accordingly, he should be accepted as spiritually the strongest man. In the *Chaitanya Charitamrita* it is said that persons who take to Krishna Consciousness are supposed to be the most intelligent men.

Regarding Maharishi Mahesh: I do not know much about his activities, but I have heard that he has got his particular type of mantra, which he sells for $35. And there are hundreds of students being cheated by him. Anyone who does not know the science of Krishna is a great fool, and if he poses himself as a spiritual master, he must be cheating the public without any doubt. Your process of discouraging friends in the matter of their being cheated by such cheaters is very good.

You know that out of many millions of people, somebody tries to be spiritually advanced. And out of many millions of spiritually advanced men, hardly there is one who understands Krishna Consciousness. So, in your country there are many who may be spiritually inclined, but because they want to be cheated Krishna sends them many kinds of cheaters to bewilder them. It is understood that Maharishi Mahesh says that one can drink and at the same time make advancement in meditation. This is clearly a cheating process. But what can be done? Your countrymen also want to be cheated like that. They want to continue their illicit sex relationships, intoxication habits, meat-eating, and all other nonsensical things, and at the same time they want to make spiritual advancement. This is a clear indication of being cheated; nobody can make any advancement in spiritual life while keeping himself conditioned by the above sinful activities.

So you can judge yourself what is the position of the so-called Maharishi Mahesh, and try to help your friends in such a way. There is no need of talking with people who are interested in Maharishi Mahesh, but if you have any sympathetic friends, you can let them know how the cheating process is going on. I do not think that his disciples’ claiming advancement in spiritual life is bona fide.

Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Here Prabhupāda acknowledged receiving pages from me in our ongoing exchange: he was sending me dictation tapes of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, and I was typing them and editing the English. As usual, Prabhupāda was checking the number of tapes he had released and those in his possession. Although one may say this reciprocation has now stopped, that is not actually true, for we perform devotional service eternally.

I once had an inkling of the eternality of our devotional service while with Prabhupāda in New York before I went to Boston. In a routine way, I would each day take Prabhupāda’s Dictaphone from his room, type his tapes, and afterwards return the Dictaphone. One time, returning to his room with the Dictaphone, I suddenly felt my duties becoming a tiresome routine. I caught myself and was somehow reminded, “No. This is *eternal.*" This thought was not just positive thinking or self-encouragement, but was a realization. Because I had been doing the work for a number of days, the material tendency was to think it tiresome; in actuality, I would be doing it not just for a few days but eternally. Neither was it tiresome.

One feels tired because one’s consciousness descends to the material platform, looking for varieties of sense pleasure. If you start devotional service with some material tinge of sense enjoyment, and later the material aspect no longer pleases you, you may think the devotional side of the service itself is tiresome. If you receive bona fide service from your spiritual master, even though part of a daily routine, you should not offensively think it tiresome or boring. That indicates a failure in your mentality. Similarly, you may chant Hare Kṛṣṇa endlessly. The pure devotee doesn’t find it tiresome. Even when the neophyte devotee feels it tiresome, he knows it is *he* who is the tiresome rascal. Because of his poor mentality, he has failed to taste the always fresh variety in chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa or in rendering service to the spiritual master.

Even in my neophyte position, however, I was not tired of this exchange with Prabhupāda; I wished I could go on forever. Now it appears I have stopped this service; I am not typing any of his tapes. But certainly the essence of devotional service continues. “He lives forever, and the follower lives with him by his instruction.” I may not be typing Prabhupāda’s tapes now, but in the future I may again be typing his tapes. In all circumstances, I must continue serving my spiritual master as he bids me.

In the second paragraph of this letter, Prabhupāda refers to some trouble he had in his brain. Because of a buzzing sound in his ear, he was disturbed, and his dictation was going slower. Prabhupāda was informing me why there might be a reduction in the number of tapes he was sending. By this example, we can understand how the pure devotee continues to work, no matter what condition he is in.

Someone might say, “Why do material disturbances inconvenience a pure devotee if he is supposed to be free from material conditions?” That a pure devotee is inconvenienced by material conditions is not an imperfection. The pure devotee may live in this material world, but if he fully engages himself in devotional service, he is liberated; if someone sees a devotee in a material context and finds fault with him, that is the observer’s mistake. From the Vaiṣṇava’s point of view, the spiritual master is actually disturbed because he takes the karma of the conditioned souls. Why was Prabhupāda bothered by a buzzing sound in his head? He was taking the karma of many disciples and leading them back to home, back to Godhead. The higher instruction however, is that despite difficulty—even though the difficulty itself was not material—Prabhupāda was pushing on, as he said, “I will go on sending tapes as far as possible.”

Rather than quarreling among ourselves or lamenting when we get a setback, we should continue serving. That is liberation. If a devotee becomes diseased, it is not an obstacle so long as he continues to think of Kṛṣṇa. Prabhupāda showed us by example how to remain Kṛṣṇa conscious even in distress. That is good instruction, applicable to all of us, since we are prone to receive some material distress. The real point is not to try to avoid all distress, but to remember Kṛṣṇa in all circumstances. Prabhupāda was not just teaching me a precept, but for all of us he was teaching by his example of working—despite difficulty.

In the next paragraph, Prabhupāda answers my question about why an initiated devotee living in Boston was not attending the temple. He answered a specific question for me, but his answer can also serve as a basic instruction about why people leave Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

One day at our Sunday feast I spoke with a visitor and learned that he was actually an initiated disciple of Prabhupāda. He had been initiated in California; since he had come to live on the East Coast, he had left Prabhupāda’s movement. I was at first astounded to find that the person to whom I had been talking, whom I had assumed was a *karmī,* was actually a devotee. I asked him what was wrong and why he wasn’t attending the temple. He had adopted a concocted philosophical explanation to justify what he was doing and why he was not able to serve his guru. I told him that I would write to Prabhupāda and inform him that I had talked with him. The devotee asked me, “Why are you going to do that? Why do you think you have to tell Prabhupāda everything that happens ?" Later, I thought over whether it was proper to inform Prabhupāda, but I did write him about it. I even told Prabhupāda in my letter that I felt it was my obligation to tell him everything important.

Prabhupāda therefore replies in this letter with several points: (1) The boy who was initiated but “not attending the Temple or cooperating with you” was in the clutches of *māyā.* That is the simple and blunt conclusion: the person must be in *māyā.* (2) Why had this boy left? Prabhupāda also gives the answer: it is very difficult for less intelligent persons to practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Whoever understands Kṛṣṇa consciousness and acts accordingly is most intelligent. This is a good definition of intelligence. Prabhupāda was philosophical, not sentimental, about one of his initiated devotees not taking part in his movement. In this short paragraph, Prabhupāda has summed up the reason why a person leaves the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, philosophically analyzing that reason so that at least I would not be bewildered.

This was the first initiated devotee I met who had left Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and it was a shock. Now, however, this incident is commonplace. We do not expect that a disciple will write his spiritual master with astonishment because he met one of his Godbrothers who is no longer a devotee. That does not mean, though, that a devotee’s leaving Kṛṣṇa consciousness is not unfortunate. And the answer Prabhupāda gave me is applicable today, even though the numbers may be considerably increased. We should not become discouraged or doubtful about the process of devotional service. We know from the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy that any devotee can become affected by *māyā* if he is not careful. If one is not serious or intelligent, he will leave the association of devotees.

We should not turn away such blooped devotees, but welcome them to associate with the devotees again and resume service in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. At the same time, we cannot avoid Prabhupāda’s conclusion that blooped devotees have fallen into illusion. Just as I could not accept the concocted philosophy by which the boy in Boston explained why he had blooped, so we cannot accept the different concocted philosophies that now appear in more variety among the various persons who have left Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We can always find justifications for leaving the society of devotees, but Prabhupāda wanted us to live in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is still intact, and devotees in the various temples are following its principles. If by one’s minute use of free will one chooses to again engage in materialistic activities and live separately from the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, he can only be suffering from a chronic disease of less intelligence under the influence of *māyā.*

The rest of the letter deals with the Transcendental Meditation fad. I have previously discussed this. Now Prabhupāda deals with it at length because he can understand we were getting concerned in Boston with the wave of popularity TM enjoyed. Step by step, Prabhupāda analyzes TM’s cheating process. He also says it is good that we have discouraged our friends from taking to it. Note that Prabhupāda says there is no need of going to people who are already followers of Maharishi, but among sympathetic friends we can speak openly and tell them that Maharishi’s is a cheating process.

I had talked with one TM follower, and when pressed he had admitted Maharishi was teaching only a popularization of real meditation. But according to the follower, Maharishi felt that if he introduced just a little spirituality or meditation, then that would be good. This was his claim: that a little bit of good was something favorable, and it could lead to further realization. The TM man had said it was unfair of me to simply discredit their process of meditation. His argument had somewhat bewildered me, and I submitted my doubts to Prabhupāda. Thus his answer: “I do not think that his disciple’s claiming advancement in spiritual life is bona fide.” Prabhupāda did not accept the man’s word jugglery to prove the validity of TM. Prabhupāda didn’t think they got *any* benefit at all.

Another point Prabhupāda makes exposing Transcendental Meditation in his letter is that the American people *want* to be cheated; they want to continue sense gratification and simultaneously advance in spiritual realization.

Prabhupāda said Kṛṣṇa had sent the TM cheaters to bewilder us. We had become fearful of Maharishi and how he had made gains, and we had wondered, “How is it that God is allowing this cheater, who is coming in the name of being a *sādhu* and a guru, to do this nonsense?” Sometimes a neophyte devotee becomes bewildered to see the success of a demon. Prabhupāda informed us from his high level of transcendental observation that actually, Kṛṣṇa had also sent Maharishi. As Kṛṣṇa had sent Prabhupāda to rescue the fallen souls and to bring them to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, so He had also sent Maharishi, because the Western people had a great desire to be cheated by enjoying sense gratification while thinking themselves meditators. Prabhupāda’s instruction was therefore well-rounded and gave us full satisfaction for both debate and peace of mind. Now we knew what to say to innocent persons (and, if necessary, to demons). We also knew more clearly that our position was completely fixed at the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa’s pure devotee.

This letter may not seem particularly astounding, but at the time it was necessary ammunition, and we immediately used it on the front lines. From the beginning of our movement, the devotees had complete openness before Śrīla Prabhupāda. Only he had absolute knowledge, so if we were troubled by something, we felt we must tell him so that we could get relief and go on with our service in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Although we did not have philosophical depth or learning, Prabhupāda had sent us to the front lines to preach. Periodically, we met with a stumping situation and had to write him for immediate help. This letter should be understood in that context—as arguments we adopted immediately for our preaching in Boston. Prabhupāda convinced us and freed us from our doubts, and he gave us logical arguments to defeat the arguments of cheaters.

We were dedicated to preaching, and anything that was a serious impediment we felt compelled to report to Prabhupāda. Our only motive was that Śrīla Prabhupāda would straighten us out so that we would be more effective representatives. Whatever Prabhupāda told us (for our good or as an instruction on how to perform our duty) we would do. Whatever impediments we encountered, we would relate them to him in hopes that he would tell us how to solve them. That is the benefit of having the spiritual master present.

During Prabhupāda’s first visit to Boston, I asked him how we could communicate with him after his disappearance. The first thing he said in response was, “You are inquiring from me by letters.” We settled many things by that method. It is valuable for devotees to consult the complete selection of Prabhupāda’s letters, especially letters from the early years, because Prabhupāda discusses many things such as preaching techniques, Deity worship, and even diet. If a devotee worked in the kitchen, he would ask Prabhupāda how he should offer *prasādam,* how the balance of the food should be given to the devotees, and what standard of cleanliness he should maintain. These questions and answers were on practical devotional service.

If when we preached we met someone who posed a formidable challenge that we could not answer so well, we would submit our doubts to Prabhupāda. We would then use his answers in our preaching arguments. We should not inquire unnecessarily; we should ask everything out of a desire for further enlightenment to carry on devotional service for our spiritual master. This is the ideal nature of inquiry.

If despite our repeated attempts to control *māyā* we find ourselves in difficulty, then we have to inquire from our spiritual master, confessing our particular situation (and not in a roundabout way). We have to actually tell our spiritual master what is happening; otherwise, the spiritual master cannot help us. We usually don’t want general instructions but specific direction.

Before signing this letter, Prabhupāda had already received another letter by me. His reply to my second letter is handwritten at the bottom of his typed letter of January 30:

The evening twilight first appeared as a beautiful girl to attract the demons but later on turned to be the evening twilight. There is no reality in the material beauty. What you think as real, that is also unreal because everything material transforms into matter at the ultimate stage. It is the spirit soul who is beautiful and not the matter.

This is in reference to an inquiry I had made about the section of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* I was typing. The Third Canto describes Lord Brahmā’s creation of the different species and personalities and the different reactions that occurred from them. I had written Prabhupāda for clarification of Lord Brahmā’s creation of a beautiful woman. In the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* her attractive bodily features are described. The lusty demons desired this woman, who appeared before them as evening twilight.

I was not certain whether Lord Brahmā had created an *actual* beautiful girl to attract the demons, or whether twilight itself was attracting and the demons had perceived it in an illusory way as a beautiful woman. Prabhupāda gave me a literal answer to my question. Lord Brahmā actually created a beautiful woman, and by her form she attracted the demons, but later she turned into evening twilight.

Although that completes the literal answer to my question, Prabhupāda gave me much more. A Kṛṣṇa conscious preceptor doesn’t just literally translate Vedic statements, but he always answers with ultimate conclusions in mind. My inquiry was not very important; it was a technical matter whether the girl was first twilight and later a girl or vice versa. The real essence of the example was that men are attracted to the female form, as demonstrated by the demons.

This is one of the passages in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* where the female form is described in a tantalizing way—bewitching looks, enticing smile, large, well-formed breasts, thin waist, and broad hips. I remember being bewildered at first by seeing these accounts in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. I thought, “Why is the *Bhāgavatam* attracting us to the form of a woman?” My inquiry to Prabhupāda did not really penetrate into the heart of this matter: the power of female attraction. If Prabhupāda had merely replied on the technical point, whether the girl was twilight or “real,” my dilemma would not have been solved. What is the sense of inquiring into whether she was *real*?

Prabhupāda, therefore, penetrates the ultimate illusion by stating that there is no reality in material beauty. *Any* girl, whether the one next door, the one whose picture appears in a magazine or a movie—*any* girl in the material world (even if described in *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*)—*is unreal.* To think twilight is a woman is illusion, but so is being attracted to a flesh-and-blood girl. A beautiful mannequin in a department store window is unreal, but so is the attractive girl walking down the street. “What you think as real,” Prabhupāda says, “that is also unreal, because everything material transforms into matter at the ultimate stage. It is the spirit soul who is beautiful and not the matter.”

There are spiritual female forms, such as the consorts of Kṛṣṇa, which *are* real, but we should never contemplate those forms with lust. Whenever we have the desire to enjoy the female form, we put ourselves into material consciousness to relate to the illusory, material forms. The desire to enjoy separately from Kṛṣṇa is perverted. Whenever the female form appears as described, lusty hearts are pinched and maddened, but a Kṛṣṇa conscious person will understand that the desire to enjoy the form is illusory.

In the last paragraph of the handwritten note, Prabhupāda mentions three disciples: Rūpānuga, Hayagrīva, and Pradyumna. After meeting Rūpānuga, I wrote Prabhupāda how faithful Rūpānuga was to his spiritual master. Prabhupāda was pleased to hear that Rūpānuga was “fully conscious about our movement and has full confidence in the spiritual master.” At the time, Hayagrīva had become estranged from his service, but Prabhupāda had recently received positive news from him that he was “feeling our separation.” And Pradyumna was recovering from surgery. Prabhupāda felt concerned for his different disciples. We should also inquire about the well-being of our fellow devotees in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. That is the personal way of life.

## February 1968

February 25, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in receipt of a copy of your last Istagosthi dated January 27, and I am very satisfied with your replies on the questions made in the Istagosthi. The meeting is called “Istagosthi,” not “Istagoshi.”

You may know also that Ramakrishna’s worship of Kali makes him a third-class man. In the *Bhagavad-gita* it is stated that one who worships the demigods like Brahma, Siva, Indra, Chandra, or demigoddesses like Kali, Durga, Sarasvati, etc. is described as a person who has lost his intelligence. Once a person has lost his intelligence, how can he become an incarnation of God? A person who has lost his intelligence is not even counted as being in a higher section of society. Ramakrishna is a bogus man according to the statement of *Bhagavad-gita*.

Vivekananda was also not even intelligent, because he accepted Ramakrishna as an incarnation of God. This Ramakrishna, who is actually Gadadhar Chatterjee, declared himself to be the same Rama, the original God, and Vivekananda accepted him as an incarnation of Rama and Krishna. Is he who accepts Ramakrishna as such a very intelligent man? Suppose a man comes before somebody and the man says that he is Pres. Johnson. If the foolish person accepts the pretender as Pres. Johnson, is that person to be accepted as a very intelligent man? Vivekananda was not even an intelligent man, because he accepted Gadadhar Chatterjee as an incarnation of God without any credentials. We accept Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, on the evidence of Vedic literatures. There is no evidence in the Vedic literatures that a common, less intelligent person, by worshiping a demigoddess Kali, can become an incarnation of God.

The goddess Kali is the divine mother for the conditioned souls, not for the liberated souls. There is no direction in the Vedic literatures that one can become liberated by worshiping the goddess Kali. She is the superintendent of the prison house known as the material world. One can derive some material facilities by worshiping goddess Kali or Durga, but nobody can become liberated by worshiping such demigoddesses, what to speak of becoming an incarnation of Godhead by such worship. So the propaganda that Ramakrishna became an incarnation of God by worshiping Kali is not accepted by any authorized acharya, and therefore it is bogus propaganda. Therefore, one who follows such a bogus propaganda is also misled.

You have to meet many opposing elements in the matter of preaching work; therefore, you should always be careful to follow the principles in *Bhagavad-gita As It Is.* You will be glad to know that our arrangement with Macmillan for publishing *Bhagavad-gita As It Is* is already complete, and the manuscript has been handed over to them. We should clearly preach that our Krishna Consciousness movement is purely based on *Bhagavad-gita As It Is*. Any other movement which does not tally with the principles of *Bhagavad-gita As It Is,* is considered unauthorized by us.

All these so-called yogis or other spiritualist movements imported from India that are in this country are all against the principles of *Bhagavad-gita*. It is our movement only which strictly follows the principles of the *Bhagavad-gita*. The last word in the *Bhagavad-gita* is to surrender unto Krishna, and Lord Chaitanya taught us how to surrender unto Krishna. His transcendental movement of chanting the Holy Name of Krishna and Rama is the sublime movement, not only for the present age but for all time—past, present and future.

Regarding your question: How could Vishnu appear from the nostril of Brahma? The answer is that Vishnu, being all pervading, He can appear from any place. He appeared from a pillar as Nrsimhadev. So one may question how Vishnu may appear from a pillar, but He actually appeared. He is all-powerful, with inconceivable potency, and therefore He can appear Himself from anywhere He likes. When the Lord appeared as a small hog from the nostril of Brahma, He began to expand Himself more and more and gradually became a gigantic boar.

So this is the inconceivable energy of the Supreme Lord. That does not, however, mean that Brahma’s nostril is the birthplace of Vishnu. The sun rises from the eastern horizon. That does not mean that the eastern horizon is the birthplace of the sun. In the *Bhagavad-gita*, therefore, it is stated that anyone who understands the transcendental pastimes of the appearance, disappearance, and activities of Krishna becomes liberated immediately after quitting his body.

Hope this meets you well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Here is an unusually long letter, entirely philosophical. Remarkably, it is not a reply to a successful preaching report from me, nor did I inquire about philosophy in my letter. Rather, Prabhupāda saw a copy of notes from one of our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs* in Boston. He showed interest in the discussion we had had about Ramakrishna’s philosophy, and he also thought we needed full instruction on the matter. The letter is a criticism of the philosophy that a person who worships a demigod can consider himself God.

We held our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* in Boston among four devotees and in separation from Prabhupāda; but now he joined us with the conclusive explanation of our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī*. We never thought we would get such a result from our talk; it was surprising, like a much-welcomed surprise visit.

A disciple never knows exactly which of his acts will please his spiritual master. We are obliged to serve, but we should do every minute part of our service diligently and with a personal, loving touch.
