The accumulation of acts rendered regularly and sincerely will eventually catch the spiritual master’s attention, and he will give his blessings. The blessings of the spiritual master may come as encouragement or praise in our service, or the blessings may come in the form of a stern reprimand. In either case, instruction is a blessing.

Here I caught my spiritual master’s interest by sincerely attempting to engage in *kṛṣṇa-kathā.* The spiritual master is pleased to hear of his disciples’ discussing *kṛṣṇa-kathā,* and he desires to enlighten their talks so they will come to the proper understanding.

The spiritual master’s responses are not always predictable, neither is he obliged to act in a certain way on our demand. I was present when a devotee brought Prabhupāda one of his books that had just been published in a foreign language. Printing and distributing books was certainly what pleased Prabhupāda most, but somehow—perhaps because of how he wanted to deal with this devotee, or maybe because of something on his mind at the moment—he received the new book without comment nor did he recognize the devotee. Yet on another occasion, he gave great attention and showed pleasure over what seemed like a small incident—receiving a flower from a child.

We cannot know the mind of the spiritual master. Neither should we be disappointed if we don't receive the flattery he expects in return for his service.

Once I remarked to a magazine interviewer, “Without Kṛṣṇa, life is hellish.” I was describing my life before I came to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He published my comment in a Boston magazine and it caught Prabhupāda’s attention. Prabhupāda became very happy and said I had delivered a “hammer blow to *māyā”* by making that statement. How was I to know at the time I spoke to the reporter just how it would affect my spiritual master?

We have to dedicate our whole lives to pleasing our spiritual masters. He is aware of our main service, and he is pleased with that. The spiritual master is ultimately a person, free to pay attention to any part of our service as he likes. He has the right; he is not obliged.

Anytime I hear the phrase “divine mother” referred to as the object of ultimate worship or meditation, I always think of the statement Prabhupāda makes here: “The goddess Kali is the divine mother for the conditioned souls, not for the liberated souls.” If there had been any trace of bewilderment in me from hearing a person speak about worshiping the divine mother, now it was banished. I could understand that just as a child may see his mother as the ultimate truth because she fulfills his material needs, so the ignorant person worships the superintendress of the material world for benedictions. But the grown-up liberated soul goes to the Supreme Father and asks to render service.

In this philosophical letter, Prabhupāda stresses that if we simply follow the principles in *Bhagavad-gītā* under the *ācāryas* in disciplic succession we will understand everything and have the topmost knowledge. The instruction was particularly appropriate to our gathering in Boston because one boy among us had a sentimental attachment to Christianity. He thought his own ideas should be accepted to be as good as a devotee’s presentation of the *Bhagavad-gītā*. He thought everyone could make some contribution to the truth; a quote from the *Bhagavad-gītā* might contain some of the truth, but maybe someone else’s ideas did also. Everyone should contribute his ideas, he thought, and such a hodgepodge mixture was *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī.*

I tried explaining to him that neither I nor any devotee was a complete repository of the absolute truth. However, if any one of us could recite the scriptural conclusion to answer standard philosophical questions, those answers should be accepted as absolute truth. Speculation could not directly oppose the *śāstra. Iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* was not speculation but (as Prabhupāda had told us) “discussing what the spiritual master has said.”

This boy also thought it would be good for us to study the Bible so we could speak to Christians on *saṅkīrtana.* Prabhupāda stresses in this letter, however, that we should strictly follow the *Bhagavad-gītā*. No other scripture is necessary. It was as if Prabhupāda was reading the mind of the boy in our company when he wrote, “You have to meet with opposing elements in the matter of preaching work; therefore, you should always be careful to follow the principles of *Bhagavad-gita As It Is*. We should clearly preach that our Krishna Consciousness movement is purely based on *Bhagavad-gita*. Any other movement which does not tally with the principles of *Bhagavad-gita As It Is,* is considered unauthorized by us.”

Prabhupāda ends this letter by correcting me. I prided myself on clearly understanding every word of his dictations, and I also thought I understood them philosophically. When I heard Prabhupāda say that Lord Varāha appeared from Brahmā’s nostril, I thought there was some mistake. Perhaps I had heard wrong. I inquired from Prabhupāda, and here he corrects me. I understood he was correcting not my ability to hear his tapes but my philosophical shortcomings. The point Prabhupāda makes is that although the Lord is the father of Brahmā and all other living entities, if He wishes He can appear as the son of Brahmā or any devotee. He can appear in any form and wherever He likes. He is not subject to material or spiritual laws. Even though He appeared from Brahmā’s nostril, He did not become inferior to Brahmā, as a son would have. Just to drive home his instruction, Prabhupāda added, “I hope you understand the transcendental appearance and disappearance of the transcendental Personality of Godhead.”

The full significance of receiving correction and instruction from the spiritual master is amplified by Prabhupāda’s last statement, where he says that whoever can understand how the Lord appears “becomes liberated immediately after quitting his present body.” This is the ultimate result of learning transcendental knowledge from the spiritual master. We are in the material world, confused and lost in purpose like Arjuna on the Battlefield of Kurukṣetra. We may speak learned things, and we sometimes think we are intelligent—even in the presence of the spiritual master. Only by *his* words in *paramparā,* however, can we be favored with the transcendental knowledge that will enable us to understand Kṛṣṇa’s appearance and disappearance. Thus we will be able to go back home, back to Godhead.

Our spiritual master does not have to correct our technical mistakes or defeat us in debate, so when our guru corrects us, we should not feel sorry, as if he has bested us. Rather, we should accept such correction. *Śāstra* enjoins, “We were standing in the darkness of ignorance with our eyes closed shut, and our spiritual master has come with the torchlight of knowledge and opened them.” We should always regard our spiritual master’s words as absolute and in our best interests.

\* \* \*

Undated

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in receipt of your letter dated February 7, 1968 along with *Srimad Bhagavatam* copies. Thank you very much for your unalloyed service. I am very much encouraged when I hear from New York and Boston that things are going nicely. Do not be worried about my material body. We are to serve through our spiritual body. The spiritual body has no connection with material bodily afflictions. One who is not afflicted by the material body or mental diseases is ready to enter into the spiritual kingdom of God.

You can arrange all my needs in Boston in May, as my program is to go to New York in April and to go to Boston in May, and then in June to go to Montreal. Herewith find one tape of 2/21/68. Offer my blessings to Pradyumna, to Jadurany, and to the other boy who has joined you. Be always absorbed in Krishna Consciousness, and He will give you all intelligence.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda begins this letter by thanking me for my “unalloyed service.” I would like to further explain how important this statement was to me. If I am to comment honestly on my letters from Śrīla Prabhupāda, I must not merely echo the philosophy of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but recall the emotional and spiritual reciprocation when I received his letters.

A letter by the spiritual master is, in one sense, above the scriptures. In no scripture does the Supreme Lord personally say, “Thank you for your unalloyed service.” The *śāstras* state that devotees who chant Kṛṣṇa’s names are dear to Him, but an individual reader of the *śāstras* may not know positively if this statement applies to him; and even if he does know, the injunction applies generally, to all devotees. But in this letter I am addressed. That very address, “My dear Satsvarupa,” is the name Prabhupāda gave me, the name by which I am known to Kṛṣṇa. This unique gift from the spiritual master is even more valuable than the gift of the *śāstras.* The *Mahābhārata* explains:

tarko ’pratiṣṭaḥ śrutayo vibhinnā  
nāsāv ṛṣir yasya mataṁ na bhinnam  
dharmasya tattvaṁ nihitaṁ guhāyāṁ  
mahājano yena gataḥ sa panthāḥ

Realization of the Absolute Truth cannot be attained by pursuing logic, since one’s logic may be defeated by a better logician; a philosopher does not become distinguished unless he defeats all the previous philosophers; learning about the Absolute from the *śāstras* is very difficult because of their variety and grave meaning; but the Absolute Truth is hidden in the hearts of the unalloyed pure devotees; therefore, one should follow the path of the *mahājanas*.

From this verse we can understand that receiving personal instruction from a *mahājana* such as Śrīla Prabhupāda is more valuable than simply studying many *śāstras.* But since I did not think my service was “unalloyed,” how was I to understand the statement in this letter? I understood that Prabhupāda was being kind, but he was not cheaply flattering me. Out of his liberal kindness, he thought my working full-time for him was unalloyed. I *wanted* to be an unalloyed servant, and Śrīla Prabhupāda’s blessings in this letter pulled me strongly in that direction.

On a certain level, my service and that of my Godbrothers *was* “unalloyed” and “pure”—and Śrīla Prabhupāda, in recognizing a certain purity in us (even though we were neophytes), was raising us upwards. Ultimately, he had inconceivably made us unalloyed servants. Our connection with Prabhupāda is inconceivable; therefore, it is also possible that one who works for him can, by his grace, become an unalloyed devotee. Prabhupāda says so, and this is confirmed by Sanātana Gosvāmī:

yathā kāñcanatāṁ yāti  
kāṁsyaṁ rasa-vidhānataḥ  
tathā dīkṣā-vidhānena  
dvijatvaṁ jāyate nṛṇām

As bell metal is turned to gold when mixed with mercury in an alchemical process, so one who is properly trained and initiated by a bona fide spiritual master immediately becomes a *brāhmaṇa.*

—Cc. Ādi 7.47

In this letter Prabhupāda also shows his concern for his fledging centers in Boston and New York. He warns me, however, not to misplace my concern for his health. “Don’t be worried about my material body,” he says.

There are several points to consider here. Prabhupāda is referring to a letter I sent him in which I expressed concern for his health and hoped he had been freed of his disease. He sees a defect in the way I stated my concern. There are a number of ways to understand this statement:

(1) Prabhupāda’s illness was not material because of infection of karma. His body was spiritual and his illness was not ordinary.

(2) Even if he appeared afflicted, that did not stop his Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Devotional service is uninterrupted and can be done even in one’s mind. Prabhupāda’s Kṛṣṇa consciousness was, therefore, unaffected—even in reverses such as illness.

(3) Therefore, even if a pure devotee is disturbed by apparent bodily distress, his mind remains pure in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and he is eligible to go back to Godhead. *Śāstra* states:

tat te ’nukampāṁ susamīkṣamāno  
bhuñjāna evātma-kṛtaṁ vipākam  
hṛd-vāg-vapurbhir vidadhan namas te  
jīveta yo mukti-pade sa dāya-bhāk

My dear Lord, one who constantly waits for Your causeless mercy to be bestowed upon him and who goes on suffering the resultant actions of his past misdeeds, offering You respectful obeisances from the core of his heart, is surely eligible to become liberated, for it has become his rightful claim.

—Bhāg. 10.14.8

(4) When Prabhupāda referred here to “mental disease,” he meant material desires. Prabhupāda impressed on me that if one is in pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness, he should never be called diseased or sick. Apparently, I had addressed Prabhupāda in that way. “Disease” is always material. Prabhupāda therefore noticed some defect in my concern.

Similarly, my Godbrother Madhusūdana once wrote Prabhupāda about Prabhupāda’s ill health. Madhusūdana also got a letter which began, “My dear boy,” and informed him that his thinking that Prabhupāda’s illness was like an ordinary victim’s was a misjudgment.

(5) Even though one is in good physical health, if he is afflicted by bodily attachment and a separate attitude from devotion to Kṛṣṇa, he is diseased.

(6) Thus we have to always see the spiritual master in terms of his spiritual position.

By saying, “Don’t be worried about my material body,” Prabhupāda also distinguishes between a material and a spiritual body. Actually, he had no material body, and his “affliction” was either a transcendental symptom of ecstasy or a result of the sinful activities of his disciples—or it may have been a chance for his disciples to serve him. However, the disciples should not think that their spiritual master’s illness is his contamination.

This letter also contains items of ongoing reciprocation. Prabhupāda announces he is going to come to Boston in May. Also, Prabhupāda encloses another tape. As I read this letter in preparation to write this commentary, I again feel the joy of receiving one of Prabhupāda’s tapes in the mail. Think of the spontaneous joy of a child, who may even jump up and down upon being released from school, or anticipating a summer at the beach, or receiving his desired Christmas present—that spontaneous joy, increased many times, was my satisfaction when I received these tapes. Receiving a tape meant I could again *hear* Prabhupāda speaking *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* and I could type it to my heart’s content.

Prabhupāda also writes at the end of this letter that I should “be always absorbed in Krishna Consciousness, and Krishna will give you all intelligence.” We often see statements like this in Prabhupāda’s letters. This confirms the philosophical point that the Supersoul is the *caitya-guru.* Here the guru is confirming the *caitya-guru*. We should therefore desire to contact the Supersoul to get intelligence to carry out the order of our spiritual master.

## March 1968

March 4, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. And offer the same to Jadurany and Pradyumna. I hope you are all doing well in the service of Krishna. I am leaving Los Angeles for San Francisco on the 8th of March, 1968, and therefore from the 6th of March 1968 you can send all mail to San Francisco. You will be glad to know that Jayananda is purchasing a nice house for San Francisco, and I hope I shall get the permanent visa also. At present I am feeling well. May Krishna give me strength to serve you more and more.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is also a handwritten letter. During this period of my correspondence, a handwritten letter from Prabhupāda was usually a note he had written to accompany a dictation tape.

This note, with references to leaving Los Angeles for San Francisco and plans for buying a new temple and getting a visa, shows Prabhupāda as the active founder-*ācārya* of ISKCON, moving on the different fronts. Although he moved quickly and had growing concerns, still he was keeping in touch with me. I was grateful to have a connection with such an active spiritual leader. Unless a devotee has some vital service link with his spiritual master, he may not be able to fully reciprocate with him.

Certainly my link was not a big thing, but I was doing some menial typing, and I was supporting a small center of three or four devotees. But that service link kept me always in touch with my spiritual master, even as he actively traveled to different cities conducting the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. A devotee should desire that link, which is established by utilitarian exchange. Somehow I had lodged myself into a corner of his heart—there was always another tape to do, and he would always respond to me on matters regarding our Boston center. That utilitarian connection, which makes it necessary for our spiritual master to deal with us regularly and give us instruction, is sweet and beneficial for the disciple.

In March we received at our Boston center a letter to a devotee named Yadunandana. This was the boy who came from New York and stayed only briefly with us in Boston, and then left the movement. (Prabhupāda gave the name “Yadunandana” several times at initiations.) Some of the points in the letter became important to me, and I always remember and quote them. One is Prabhupāda’s comments about solitude. I also was fond of solitude, and before coming to Kṛṣṇa consciousness I liked to live alone. I have already described how I gave up my apartment for the use of the devotees at 26 Second Avenue. At that time I knew I was giving up something to which I was attached, but I felt that gaining the devotee association was more valuable than solitude. Yadunandana had written to Śrīla Prabhupāda of his desire to go off in solitude and make spiritual advancement. Prabhupāda replied:

The tendency for solitude is a kind of reaction of our past material activities, but solitude is not very good for a neophyte. Maya is always trying to attack us, and as soon as she finds some opportunity, she tries to inflict her poisonous effects. The best thing is therefore not to seek solitude in the beginning but to remain in the midst of pure devotees so that even if there is an attack of Maya their association will protect you. If you are, however, always busy in the matter of preaching work, that is very nice. But to remain alone in a solitary place for a new man is not advisable.

Vrindaban is, of course, very conducive for devotional service, but it is not advisable to remain there alone either. I am trying to get a nice place at Vrindaban, and when the place is there I shall personally be present with some devotees and appreciate the atmosphere of Vrindaban. So long as we are engaged in devotional service with proper guidance, certainly we are always in a transcendental position, and the transcendental position is unlimited; it doesn’t matter whether you are in Vrindaban or the U.S.A.

But still, for everyone Vrindaban’s atmosphere is very sweet. But so long as we have not completely freed our material attachments, even residence in Vrindaban becomes uncongenial. This seeking for solitude is simply a reaction of our past nonsense activities, or it is negation, voidism. The sound position is always being engaged in the service of Krishna; that is the positive standing.

Solitude as a reaction to our past bitter experience of material life, and solitude as voidism—these teachings by Prabhupāda are important definitions. Of course, detachment from the mass of people is praised in *Bhagavad-gītā,* but this kind of solitude means avoiding the association of materialistic people, especially those attached to women and sense enjoyment. We should never avoid the association of devotees.

Unfortunately, Yadunandana did not take Prabhupāda’s advice. He said he knew of an idyllic spot in New England, a hidden place in the forest where there was a pond, and he said he would go there and meditate. So Yadunandana left us, and we didn’t see him for weeks. Then I saw him wandering around the Boston Common. He had grown a beard and was with some hippies. He seemed high on drugs. When I asked him what happened to his solitary meditation, he admitted that he had gotten bored and had gone to nearby Provincetown where he had met with some old friends. So much for Yadunandana’s solitude.

Yadunandana had been in the New York temple and had left to come to our Boston center where he had noted a different atmosphere. Śrīla Prabhupāda comments on the relative positions of big and small ISKCON centers:

The association of devotees is the only solace for our existence. New York might have been a little busier than Boston, but so long as one is under the guidance of an experienced devotee the affairs in both places are all right.

Following the typewritten letter to Yadunandana, there was a handwritten note to myself.

My dear Satsvarupa,

This morning I have sent you one note regarding Pradyumna’s hospital bill. I think I shall be able to send you $500.00 from this center. I understand that the hospital bills are also paid by small installments. We have to make an arrangement for future emergency, and I wish to organize a central board for such management. For me, it is not possible to look after the administrative affairs. Some of you should form a committee for this kind of management. In India the brahmacharis collect alms and subscription. But here it is not possible to do like that; therefore, all brahmacharis may work at least part-time so that our financial difficulty may be minimized.

Your ever well-wisher,  
ACB

I have already commented on this in *Prabhupāda-līlā:*

When Pradyumna had to enter the hospital for a hernia operation, Satsvarūpa didn’t know how they would pay for it. Pradyumna’s parents had refused. Having no one else to turn to, Satsvarūpa wrote Prabhupāda, asking him if he could pay the bill. Just as a dependent child, fearful of the world, turns always to his protective parents, Satsvarūpa had faith that Swamijī knew everything and could decide everything. And besides, no one else cared about Pradyumna or how the Boston center for Kṛṣṇa consciousness would pay a five-hundred-dollar hospital bill. Satsvarūpa concluded that if Swamijī thought it wrong for him to ask for the money, then he would tell him that also.

Śrīla Prabhupāda considered the hospital bill a botheration. Why should he have to worry about every item of business in ten different centers around the world? Although he assured Satsvarūpa that if necessary he would send the money for Pradyumna’s bill, he suggested that his disciples form a governing body to manage such problems. (This was the beginning or seed idea of the GBC.) His growing institution had to be properly managed—and not entirely by him. He should be left to carry on the duties proper for the spiritual master, not that he had to be simultaneously treasurer, chairman, trouble-shooter, and counselor for each problem of each devotee in each center. And yet that was happening as he accepted more disciples. They were turning to him for everything: medical advice, marriage counseling, financial assistance, as well as transcendental knowledge.

\* \* \*

March 27, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated March 18, 1968, along with enclosures of transliteration made by Pradyumna, and I thank you very much. Regarding your letter dated 17 March, it was with the tape received, and I have just opened it. There is no question of changing the Boston program. I am engaged here up to the 8th of April; after that I shall be free to go to New York. I do not know what is the program in New York because I have not received information of it. So ask Brahmananda to send me a letter when I should go to N.Y. I can start for N.Y. after the 8th of April, any day. So make your program conjointly, but for me, I am free from San Francisco after the 8th.

I have already sent you one note about Pradyumna’s hospital bill, and as soon as I hear from you, I shall arrange to send you $500 from my personal book fund. I am told by some of my students here that it is very easy to pay the bills in small installment payments, and if that arrangement can be made, you may see to it, if it is possible.

Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Here we see indication that the different centers were competing to get Prabhupāda to visit their temples. New York was the original center, and the devotees there expected to get priority. Boston was not yet significant. Yet I had made my bid as early as 1967, and here Prabhupāda promises he would come. The devotees in New York were surprised, because it would cut into the time Prabhupāda would spend with them. But I had gotten a prior promise. By this date I had told Prabhupāda that I had gotten engagements for him at different universities. I suppose I proved the point that if we want our spiritual master to visit us we have to attract him by making prior preparations.

## April 1968

April 1968

April 2, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated March 19, 1968, along with copies of *Srimad Bhagavatam,* and I thank you very much. The astronomical calculation is that every three years one month is added, so that the year in which one month is added, that is always calculated as consisting of thirteen months. Otherwise, generally it is twelve months in a year. At present I am keeping copies \[of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*\] as it is, and I shall see it again and do the necessary corrections.

I am very glad to see the Boston temple photos; and it gives me a nice idea. Offer Pradyumna my blessings and congratulate him on his coming back from the hospital. Please take care of him as best you can. You have not said anything how his hospital bills have to be paid. I have already written to you that if need be, then I can pay something from my book fund. Otherwise, the bills may be paid by installment, as it is learned from here. I am going to N.Y. on the 17th of April, and I think I shall have to attend some meetings in Philadelphia University on the 25th, and then I shall be free to go to Boston, and you can make your programs accordingly.

I am very glad that Jadurany is preparing signs on account of my visit there. Please offer my blessings to Jadurany, Pradyumna, Jadunandan, Jai-Govinda, and others who are there.

The boy of the name John, who was sometimes in Boston, he came here to meet me. I asked him to stay in the temple, but he’s not in the temple, nor have I seen him again.

Hoping you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

The first paragraph of this letter refers to a technical clarification of something Prabhupāda spoke on a dictation tape.

Prabhupāda states he is glad we are “preparing signs on account of my visit there.” The main sign was a big one over our little storefront which said “Hare Krishna.” There is also a description in *Prabhupāda-līlā* of our anticipation:

Prabhupāda had fixed the date of his arrival in Boston for May 1 and asked Satsvarūpa to begin arranging lectures for him in Boston’s many universities. When the devotees sent Prabhupāda a few photographs of the storefront interior, he wrote back, “It gives me a nice idea.” Although the photos showed only a small empty room with an unfinished altar, Prabhupāda had not been looking with the eyes of an interior decorator. Rather his “nice idea” was that his disciples in Boston were engaging in Kṛṣṇa consciousness to the best of their ability. The important thing was that they were serving Kṛṣṇa. By adding Kṛṣṇa consciousness, anything material could become valuable.

\* \* \*

April 8, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of 4/1/68, and I am glad that you have solved the hospital bill of Pradyumna. I am so glad to learn that he is taking part in the Istagosti. His answers are very intelligent. I have fixed up the date for going to N.Y. on the 17th.

One thing: You may inform all devotees that Maya cannot touch a pure devotee. When you find a devotee is supposedly in difficulty it is not the work of Maya, but it is the work of the Lord by His personal, internal energy. The Pandava’s tribulations in so many ways; Lord Ramachandra’s departure to the forest; His wife, the Goddess of Fortune, being kidnapped by Lord Ravana; Lord Krishna’s death being caused by the arrow of a hunter; Thakura Haridas’s being caned in twenty-two markets; or Lord Jesus Christ’s being crucified—all were acts of the Lord personally.

We cannot always understand the intricacies of such incidences. Sometimes they are enacted to bewilder persons who are demons. You should therefore discuss the Istagosti from current reading matters from *Bhagavad Gita* or *Srimad Bhagavatam*. We should only try to understand everything from the standard of devotional service. It is clearly stated in the *Bhagavad Gita* that anyone who is cent percent engaged in the service of the Lord is transcendentally situated, and the influence of Maya has no more action on such a person. The Lord and His pure devotees are always beyond the range of Maya’s action. Even though tribulations pure devotees suffer appear like the actions of Maya, we should understand such actions to be of Yogamaya, or the internal potency of the Lord.  
My blessings for you all. I am feeling all right.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is a handwritten letter. Prabhupāda is writing in reference to our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs.* Usually *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs* were divided into discussions of business and philosophy. It was usually the kind of meeting guests are excluded from and candid exchanges are allowed.

Not only would the president present his items, but others were also allowed to speak. *Iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* would be a chance for the cook to remind the devotees not to leave food out to spoil; it would be an opportunity for the *pūjārī* to make a Kṛṣṇa conscious speech about the importance of attending the *āratis* or how to develop a special relationship with the Deity by doing individual services; it would even be an opportunity for different *anarthas* or temple politics to be exposed.

Prabhupāda expected we conduct these meetings with a decorum befitting devotees; *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* is not a name-calling free-for-all. Yet we could treat festering issues openly, and it was the proper place for discussing controversial items, although that would also be done by respecting the jurisdiction of the temple authorities. *Iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* was a competent way to settle many problems that gathered during the week.

In the philosophy part of the *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī,* I began reading Śrīla Prabhupāda’s letters. I invited devotees to ask philosophical questions about things they didn’t understand. To draw every devotee into the discussion, one devotee would pose as a Māyāvādī and challenge the others in the room. If executed in a loving, lively, and *paramparā* fashion, the *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī,* “discussing what the spiritual master has said,” can still be a vital method of maintaining the ṇsprit de corps of the temple.

In one of our *iṣṭa-goṣṭhīs,* we had a discussion of how *māyā* affects the pure devotee, and Prabhupāda comments on that in this letter. He also hints that we may have been speculating in our discussion. He writes, “You should therefore discuss the Istagosti from current reading matters from *Bhagavad Gita* or *Srimad Bhagavatam.*” *Iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* is not an exchange for “free thinkers”; neither is it meant for speculating on extremely technical or academic philosophical points.

I found the philosophical statements in this short, handwritten note important. Although one may find the same things in Prabhupāda’s purports, I had not yet read such a purport when I received this letter. I still treasure this letter for its enlightening description of the great devotees who underwent trials and tribulations and the Kṛṣṇa conscious explanation of why they did so. Here the inner meaning of scriptures such as the *Rāmāyaṇa* and the *Mahābhārata* is revealed. If Kṛṣṇa had empowered these devotees, why did they (and even some incarnations of God) have to suffer? If we can understand why great devotees suffer, then we can better understand our own tribulations, even though we are engaged on God’s side of the battle against *māyā.*

Prabhupāda states that it is inconceivable and “we cannot always understand the intricacies of such incidences.” Thus we understand the crucifixion of Jesus Christ as the Lord’s plan to bewilder the demons. The demons feel God has been defeated—the crucifixion appears to confirm this. So they are plunged further into atheism. This is also done under the direction of the Supreme. Still, the devotees are not bewildered to see a great devotee undergo tribulations. Devotees understand such things as the plan of the Lord, although they may not claim to know the intricacies of His plan.

Bhīṣmadeva also discusses this philosophy when he explains to Yudhiṣṭhira why everyone had to die on the Battlefield of Kurukṣetra under the plan of eternal time. We must accept the axiom that the pure devotee is always “beyond the range of Maya’s actions.” Once we accept this, then we know that they are never under the influence of *mahāmāyā.* “Even though they appear like the actions of Maya,” Śrīla Prabhupāda says, “we should understand their action to be of Yogamaya, or the internal potency of the Lord.”

Philosophically, our spiritual master’s instructions can be found in his books and his letters. We should, therefore, carefully read our spiritual master’s books, and they will also become like personal letters, directing our soul’s individual progress. Likewise, many philosophical instructions I received in letters from Śrīla Prabhupāda have an indelible imprint in my memory.

\* \* \*

April 9, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for your letter dated 4/6/68 and have noted the contents. So far as your being given work, there is no need for you to feel concern; you are already helping on *Srimad Bhagavatam,* and permanently you can do so. So you are certainly included as a worker and editor for *Srimad Bhagavatam* already.

Certainly we are not going to say these things about the negro people publicly; we have no distinction between black or white, or demigod or demon; but at the same time, so long as there are demons or demigods we have to behave towards them in the proper way. Just like Chaitanya Mahaprabhu: He had no distinction between a tiger and a man. He was so powerful that He could convert even a tiger to dance. But so far as we are concerned we should not imitate and go to some tiger and try to make him dance! But still, a tiger is equally as eligible as a man.

So, you can understand that these talks are not for the public, as the people have not got the ability to understand. Basically we have not got hatred for anyone, but when one is demoniac or atheistic we should try to avoid their company. A preacher’s business is to love God, to make friendship with devotees, to enlighten the innocent, and to avoid the demons. This principle we shall follow. But in higher devotional life there is no such distinctions. The topmost devotee sees everything in Krishna and Krishna in everything. Generally, as preachers, we are middle-class devotees. So we should not remain as neophyte devotees. Neophyte devotees do not know how to preach. They simply go to the temple and offer everything with devotion to the Deity, and they don’t know anything else. So our devotees of the society should not remain in the neophyte position; neither should they try to imitate the topmost devotee. The best thing is to remain in the via media of middle-class position: namely to love God, to make friendship with devotees, and to enlighten the innocent, and to avoid the demons.

These differences of body according to karma are there, but a devotee divides them into the above groups, and so we have to divide them into these different groups and behave differently with each of the groups. It is true that we should not be attached to the differences of material bodies; but practically, in the practical field, our behavior should be as stated above.

So far as standard Sanskrit transliteration is concerned, that which Pradyumna is doing will be our standard. The spelling should also be standard, based on his work. So far as the word “Kshatriya” is concerned, this is the correct spelling. All these discrepancies are happening on account of my students being unaware of the Sanskrit language. Therefore, I requested Pradyumna to learn Sanskrit very seriously. He has got the aptitude, and I hope he may come out very successful.

Yes, everyone of us should behave ideally towards others so everyone can get impetus to make progress more and more. The college engagements sound very nice; I shall reach there by the 1st of May. Hoping you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\\“Au” can sometimes go as “ou.” The rest of the transliteration is standard.

In the first paragraph Prabhupāda tells me not to feel concerned that I will be left out of work on the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. I had heard an editorial board was being formed to produce the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, but my name had not been mentioned. I had written Prabhupāda in a somewhat foolish, anxious, and greedy way, as if my lifeline was being threatened, but here Prabhupāda informs me, “You are certainly included as worker and editor for *Srimad Bhagavatam* already.”

It is a fact that our service is our lifeline, but we should understand that it is supplied by our spiritual master. If for some reason we abuse our relationship, we may be cut off. Service is not guaranteed by our own merit; the spiritual master extends it to us magnanimously.

The spiritual master, however, is a kind of father who has no intention of cutting off the life of his dependent. As we execute our duty, we should thank our spiritual master for giving us activities which liberate us from the material world. Although it appears that the spiritual master is the order-giver, and it may even appear that he is benefiting from our labor, he is actually rendering us the greatest service by allowing us to work for Kṛṣṇa on his behalf.

In the next paragraph, Prabhupāda refers to something I had said about “the negro people.” I had been concerned that Prabhupāda had referred to Negroes in his books as an inferior race. In my letter I had pointed out how people in modern American society would never accept these statements. I may have inadvertently become doubtful, inferring that Prabhupāda’s statement was on the bodily platform. Because of my own material background, I had come to identify with the American Negroes’ right for social equality. This produced a so-called liberal mentality in me, and I misjudged Prabhupāda’s statement. We may acknowledge bodily differences, but all of us—whether materially higher or lower—are born *mlecchas* in families considered low and fallen by Vedic standards.

Spirit souls are, of course, all equally eligible for attaining love of God. In this connection Śrīla Prabhupāda discusses an *uttama-adhikārī.* The *uttama-adhikārī* makes no distinction at all; he sees all spirit souls as servants of the Lord. But even the distinctions the preacher *(madhyama-adhikārī)* makes are not because of prejudice or bigotry. Regardless of race, sex, or social conditions, all persons—*except the confirmed atheist*—are eligible to receive Kṛṣṇa’s mercy. Even atheists receive this mercy by the grace of the empowered spiritual masters. However, because people defend their bodily conceptions, we cannot always discuss these things publicly.

I remember once preaching in the street to a black man in Boston. I told him that he thought he was black and I white only because of “skin disease.” He became offended, thinking that I was saying he was black because of *his* diseased skin. He was so much in the bodily concept that he didn’t understand my point: if he thinks that he and others are their bodies, then he is suffering from skin disease.

People may, therefore, misunderstand us when we discuss the inferiority of different births. Generally we should not present this aspect of our philosophy when people may misunderstand, but whenever there is an opportunity for a full discussion, we can enlighten an inquisitive person. A preacher must have no material, racial, or sexual prejudice. He should not distinguish others as black, Indian, woman, or man, but with the vision of the *madhyama-adhikārī* he should see them as devotees, innocent persons, or atheists. These distinctions will enable the *madhyama-adhikārī* to preach effectively and to overcome the bodily prejudices of others. This vision will also allow the preacher to overcome his own prejudices.

This letter again opened my eyes to see that Prabhupāda’s statements were perfect, not products of material reasoning.

At the end of this letter, Prabhupāda confirms something I had written him: “Yes, every one of us should behave ideally towards each other, so everyone can get impetus to make progress more and more.” What he meant was that the most important method of preaching is the ideal behavior of the devotees. A leader will inspire newcomers or subordinates to behave in a certain way by his own ideal example.

This is the process of spiritual realization. All understandings must be confirmed by the spiritual master. At that early stage of my spiritual life, my “realization” could have been incorrect. Moreover, we should never think we have reached an advanced stage where we are above consulting our spiritual master and senior Godbrothers. Even Mahārāja Parikṣit, although convinced he should go to the Ganges and hear *kṛṣṇa-kathā* until the end of his life, consulted the sages to discover the best course of action.

\* \* \*

Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote another long letter to Yadunandana before he had abandoned the devotees' association. Prabhupāda referred to it in a letter to another devotee: “I have referred to Jadunandana’s letter, and I wish that you all should read that letter, because it contains some valuable information about our preaching method, and many intelligent questions by him have been replied to in that letter.”

This statement seemed odd, since this boy Yadunandana had not proven himself serious, but Śrīla Prabhupāda appreciated his questions and answered them at length for everyone’s benefit. Yadunandana professed to be a former devout Christian, although now he was a devotee. He had a particular interest in speaking to Christians whom he felt he was especially qualified to convert to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He liked to try this when we would go out on *harinām kīrtana* at the Boston Common. Here Śrīla Prabhupāda advises him about his plans to convert Christians into Kṛṣṇaites:

Your faith in the Christian religion is very strong, I can understand. So, when you want to convert Christians into Krishna Conscious devotees, you should understand first of all the philosophy of Krishna Consciousness. If we try to convert Christians into devotees, it will be an utter failure. We do not condemn any religion, because the *Bhagavatam* says that a religious process is the best if one can attain love of God by practicing it. So we are teaching love of God, not any particular type of religion. Our Krishna Consciousness movement is not a religious movement; it is a movement for purifying the heart. Modern civilization is defying the authority of the Personality of Godhead; the more a man advances in material adventures, the more he becomes covered by the illusory energy.

Yadunandana asked if a person became purely Kṛṣṇa conscious, would the material reality vanish from him? Prabhupāda replies:

When one’s material concept of life is finished, the material vision also vanishes. Actually there is one energy of Krishna, which is spiritual. Material consciousness of life means forgetfulness of Krishna; when one is fully Krishna Conscious, there is no more material existence in the vision of such an advanced devotee. We have to learn Krishna Consciousness step by step; just like we prepare prasadam. Ordinarily it is rice, dal and chapatis. But when it is offered to Krishna, it becomes prasadam. How ordinary rice, dal and chapatis turn into spiritual prasadam is to be understood by advancement in Krishna Consciousness, but actually anything in relation with Krishna is spiritual energy.

The mind does not dissolve. It changes its quality; or rather it becomes purified.

Yadunandana asked if his thoughts and feelings of love for Kṛṣṇa were just ego-projected. Prabhupāda replied that such thoughts were “not ego-projected or emotional, provided they are confirmed by the spiritual master.” Yadunandana also asked why Prabhupāda had written in one place that Brahman realization is considered catastrophic. Prabhupāda replied, “Brahman realization is considered catastrophic in the sense if one does not make further progress in Krishna Consciousness. If one finalizes everything by Brahman realization, certainly that is a catastrophe.”

Yadunandana asked the usual questions about how much *prasādam* we should take. Prabhupāda replied, “The more we minimize, that is good, but not at the risk of health.” Prabhupāda said that because we have to work for Kṛṣṇa, we have to maintain good health. “Yes,” Prabhupāda wrote, “you are right in saying that in the beginning of devotional service one can only see Krishna in the Deity and the prasadam that is being offered to Him. But anyway, if one has the tendency to eat more, then let him eat more prasadam and not any nonsense food. Eating more is not encouraged. But it is not that if I want more food, then artificially I shall eat less.”

Yadunandana asked repeated questions about preaching to Christians. Our experience was that the people in the Boston Common were rowdy. We would chant, and hundreds would gather around us, including hecklers, but the chanting had such force that most of the noises and distractions were subdued. No one could really compete with our *kīrtana.* When the *kīrtana* stopped, however, there was more of a chance for the hecklers to dominate. Sometimes we would start to give a lecture, and the hecklers would answer back, which they could not do while we were powerfully singing in *kīrtana.* Sometimes we would not even give a lecture, but take only a brief rest and then chant again. Yadunandana inquired from Prabhupāda about how to deal with these people, especially how to preach to them something that was connected with their Christian background. Prabhupāda’s reply:

Those who are agitated in the mind, they will hear neither Krishna Conscious philosophy nor Christian philosophy. So to make their minds smoother, you should chant Hare Krishna nicely. You cannot do it by changing the philosophical topics. The chanting will work. When there is no possibility of talking philosophy, we should simply chant, nothing more. Don’t talk anything. That will help both the singer and the audience. Your little speech is very nice; Chaitanya Mahaprabhu also said there are millions of Names of God, and one can chant any one of them he prefers. We chant Hare Krishna because Lord Chaitanya also chanted Hare Krishna. We recommend to chant any Name of God, but we prefer to chant the Holy Name of God, Krishna, following the footprints of Lord Chaitanya.

You can mention some parallels from Christian literature while explaining *Bhagavad Gita,* but you cannot find in any other scripture in the world the full information given in the *Bhagavad Gita*. We should not forget that we are preaching the science of God from *Bhagavad Gita,* which contains all spiritual instructions contained in other scriptures.

But if the public is unruly, simply to chant and not speak anything on philosophy is the best. At that time there is no use wasting time talking with them on philosophy. Better for you and the audience to chant and hear Hare Krishna.

During May, Śrīla Prabhupāda came to Boston. We rented a house about nine blocks from the temple where Prabhupāda could stay. The night before he came, a few devotees drove up from New York to help us clean the house. We stayed up all night, and the morning of his arrival I cooked for him, making the few things I knew: *purīs, halavah,* sweet rice. I couldn’t even go to the airport because I was so busy. When Prabhupāda arrived, the devotees said, “Prabhupāda is here!” I was still cooking. He came right into the kitchen, and I made obeisances, but I had to return to the stove. After he went into his room, his secretary came to the kitchen and said, “Prabhupāda wants to see you.”

“I can’t come,” I said. “I haven’t finished cooking.” I had been waiting for months for Prabhupāda to come, but when he came I couldn’t even see him at the airport—I couldn’t even go to his room.

Prabhupāda stayed with us in Boston for nearly a month, speaking at the temple and at university engagements. I have elaborately described his activities during this stay in *Prabhupāda-līlā,* Chapter Four.

## June 1968

June 13, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I thank you for your letter dated June 8, 1968, as well as for the booklet you have sent describing my activities in Boston. Your endeavor to utilize energy twenty-four hours for Krishna Consciousness is a great qualification. I am very glad to know that you are risking $350.00 a month for taking a nicer accommodation for our temple. I quite appreciate this attempt. And Krishna will surely help you. I have sent one letter to Miss Rose, and try to keep friendship with her. She will gradually become more and more interested in our temple affairs. Everyone had dormant love for Krishna, and we have to give one a possible chance to awaken it. Miss Rose was taking gradual increased interest in our temple, and I think if you flatter her a little bit more, she will come to the Krishna Consciousness platform and may be a great help to the center.

I am so pleased to learn that the new temple location will be near the business district. In business circles there is a popular English saying that you can earn more by sitting in one place than by traveling to many places provided you can sit down in a place with due consideration. Similarly, if actually you have selected a nice place, that place itself will act as advertisement for popularizing our Krishna Consciousness.

For the time being Devananda must stay with you; he should not come here for the present. When we shall form a Sankirtan party for the European tour I shall call him. Please show him this part of my letter, and I hope he will understand. He is a very good, nice, sober boy, and he will be a good help to your activities.  
In the meantime, I have received one letter from James. I am enclosing his letter. I do not know what he wants to know from me. This boy is a new boy, and he might have so many ideas within his brain, and if you can train him for your purposes, he will be a great service. We may always expect that we get always untamed boys and girls, and so we have to train them up. That depends on our capacity. So if you can tackle this boy, he may be also a good assistant. Our whole program is to train the untrained. No-body is in Krishna Consciousness, and we are trying our best to bring them to that platform.

Hoping you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This was the first letter Prabhupāda wrote me from Montreal after visiting us in Boston for the month of May, Prabhupāda refers to the booklet I sent him. During his stay I kept a journal of his speeches and activities, which ran to nearly fifty single-spaced, typewritten pages. I had entitled it *Swami in Boston* and sent it to him. (I have since drawn from that booklet to write *Prabhupāda-līlā.*) Prabhupāda’s thanking me for this booklet was an indirect approval of what I later expanded into my main service—writing about Prabhupāda.

I always had the urge to observe Prabhupāda’s activities and share them in writing with devotees. I also used to photocopy his letters and send them to other centers. This was curtailed, however, when Prabhupāda indicated that some of his letters were confidential; their instructions were not always meant to be applied generally.

In philosophic discussions about the emphasis of different services and the policies of ISKCON, we should be careful of proving our case by quoting Prabhupāda’s letters. I do not think they have the same weight in philosophical matters as Prabhupāda’s purports. Although they are also absolute, they were addressed to certain persons and were written at certain times. Prabhupāda’s purports, however, are addressed to all humanity and are applicable for at least the next 10,000 years.

With this letter Prabhupāda recalls some of the events he took part in while in Boston. Miss Rose, an elderly lady, who for a while showed some patronage to the devotees, also thought she herself should take up Kṛṣṇa consciousness. She was a neurotic, frivolous person, but had somehow developed an affection for the devotees in the storefront temple. When Śrīla Prabhupāda came to Boston, she increased her devotional activities. She sometimes drove Prabhupāda in her car, and she attended his lectures and *darśanas.* Because she was sentimental and not really deep-thinking or inclined much to inquire into the Absolute, and because she was also attached to her own material conditioning, Prabhupāda had to encourage her gradually. She was the kind of aging, middle-aged woman who is still concerned about appearing youthful. Prabhupāda often referred to her as young. Once Prabhupāda discussed old age and said that if a person is young “like Miss Rose,” then he can digest anything, even stones; whereas “an old man like me” finds difficulty.

One time Miss Rose told Prabhupāda she thought he was beautiful. He replied that she made such a statement only because she herself was in a beautiful state of consciousness and saw everything in beautiful Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Prabhupāda also asked her outright to donate her car and take a more active part in the temple, but she laughed in a flustered and nervous way and said, “I wish I could!”

Before Prabhupāda left, however, there was a rather traumatic scene involving Miss Rose. She came to Prabhupāda on one of his last days there, but Prabhupāda’s secretary, Govinda dāsī, did not allow her entry. Miss Rose became almost hysterical and barged into Prabhupāda’s room, crying and screaming about her mistreatment. Then she invented examples of different abuses she had suffered at the hands of the devotees. I suppose behind her misbehavior was a kind of attachment to His Divine Grace, and therefore she had become panic-stricken when Govinda dāsī had denied her permission to see him.

Śrīla Prabhupāda did not appreciate her wild behavior and uncontrolled crying. I was present and tried to speak patiently to her, asking her to behave properly and leave the room. She calmed down eventually, but after the incident her relationship with us waned.

In this letter, Prabhupāda remembers her with kind words. He is also seeing things from the viewpoint of the *ācārya* of our Boston temple. Prabhupāda advises me it would be to our advantage to remain friends with her. He himself had sent her a letter. Like everyone else, Miss Rose had dormant love for Kṛṣṇa, and we had to give her the opportunity to awaken it. Prabhupāda also gives tactical advice in dealing with her: “If you flatter her a little more, she will come to the Krishna Consciousness platform and may be a great help to the center.” Rūpa Gosvāmī says:

*yena tena prakāreṇa manaḥ kṛṣṇa niveśayet*  
*sarve vidhi-niśedhā syur etayor eva kiṅkarāḥ*

**"Somehow or other become Kṛṣṇa conscious; the rules and regulations can follow later."**

We can induce someone to take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and that may sometimes include flattering that person’s false ego.

In this letter we can also see Śrīla Prabhupāda acting as the Governing Body Commissioner. Prabhupāda was concerned, from a business point of view, with the location of our temple. He was always an expert manager. Therefore, he quotes a maxim about a salesman who can do better by staying in a good location than by traveling to different places. Prabhupāda also approved of our taking the risk of paying rent for a good place.

Regarding the statement about Devānanda staying with us: Devānanda wanted to leave our temple, and I asked Prabhupāda whether he could stay since I needed him. In his GBC function, Prabhupāda directed Devānanda to stay.

As for James, he was a crazy boy who stayed briefly in our temple and later wrote a letter to Prabhupāda complaining about us. Prabhupāda could see he was crazy and therefore did not answer his incoherent letter, but forwarded it to me. In this way, Prabhupāda supported his faithful leaders and did not accept unfounded criticisms.

During the same period, it struck me how we have to “tackle” untrained, crazy boys. Not long after, some of the temple presidents met on the East Coast, and we resolved that crazy persons should not be allowed to live in our temples. When we sent this resolution to Prabhupāda, he replied by criticizing our policy of rejecting unfit persons as too stiff. He wrote that it is easy to turn someone away from the temple and back into the arms of *māyā,* but the difficult thing was to train that person. “Our whole program is to train the untrained. Nobody is in Krishna Consciousness, and we are trying our best to bring people to that platform.”

Prabhupāda also gave us similar advice when we opened the *gurukula.* One of the teachers wrote Prabhupāda how we were carefully screening the students before enrolling them. Prabhupāda replied that we should not be interested in screening students, but in understanding that they are all untrained. Thus we will make them fit.

This was Prabhupāda’s own attitude toward preaching in the beginning of his missionary work at 26 Second Avenue. Of course, a new person cannot be strange or disconcerting to others in the *āśrama.* Prabhupāda used to allow the Bowery derelicts to sit in the temple, even though they stank; but if they made a ruckus, then they were not allowed to stay. We should all be tolerant.

\* \* \*

June 16, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for your letter of June 12, 1968. Yes, I am getting some good opportunities to meet here several learned scholars. Last night we had a meeting in the house of Mr. Abdul Rabbi, and there were some university professors and a Dr. Abbot, a Dr. MacMillan, and many others—two clergymen and their wives. One Father Lanlais was without a wife. So there was very good discussion, and by the grace of Krishna I was able to give them some impression of this philosophy, that it is nicer than anything. Professor Abdul is Mohammedan and is writing a thesis on Sufi religion. And he was also impressed. Unfortunately, I had to eat there, but I accepted a little fruits only, while they were eating all sorts of nonsense, but at least they did not drink. We are two persons only, Janardan and myself. We avoided all kinds of nonsense.

Brahmacharis and Brahmacharinis can live in the temple provided there is a separate arrangement. The restriction is there because if they live together there might be sex impulses agitated. The whole principle is especially for the Brahmacharis to avoid causes which may give impetus for the sex desire. But in your country it is very difficult to make aloof the boys and girls because they are accustomed to mixing together. So it is not possible for me to introduce this new system very strictly, because they are trained differently.

Some way or other, if you are an intelligent boy you should make arrangement for the boys and girls to live separately; Brahmacharis at one place all together, and Brahmacharinis all together at another place. We should always remember that sex life is a great impediment to spiritual advancement. So keeping this point of view in front, we should intelligently manage things with great care so that our aim of life may not be missed.

Maharaj Dasarath was a great devotee of the Lord, but because he was a kshatriya king, keeping his promise was inevitable for him. He preferred to banish Ramachandra on the request of his wife on the principle of keeping his promise. In higher states of spiritual life, one can break even promises also, but there are devotees of comparative merit. In the case of Vasudeva, we find that he was spiritually more advanced than Maharaj Dasarath. Vasudeva was also in agreement with Kamsa that he would deliver all his sons to Kamsa’s hand, as soon as the children were born. But in the case of Krishna, Vasudeva broke his promise. The point is that Krishna appeared as the full-fledged Supreme Personality of Godhead. But Ramachandra appeared as an ideal King. Therefore, in the lila of Ramachandra, principles of morality and ethics, as they are to be followed by an ideal king and ruler, were followed.

In the same sense, Ramachandra banished Sita in order to prove Himself an ideal king who wanted to make His subjects always happy. The whole program was on the basis of an ideal King. But in the case of Lord Krishna, He played as the full, independent Supreme Personality of Godhead. Apparently, therefore, He transgressed so many moral and ethical principles. The comparative studies on the life of Krishna and Ramachandra are very intricate, but the basic principle is that Ramachandra appeared as an ideal King and Krishna appeared as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, although there is no difference between the two.

A similar example is Lord Chaitanya. He appeared as a devotee and not as the Supreme Personality of Godhead, although He is Krishna Himself. So we should accept the Lord’s mood in particular appearances, and we should worship Him in that mood. Sometimes Lord Chaitanya was in the role of being enjoyed. So the party known as Gouranga Nagari, they are considered to be deviated from pure devotional service on account of their giving Lord Chaitanya the same facility as Krishna, which Lord Chaitanya did not like. Our service mood should be compatible to the attitude of the Lord. Not that we should overlap the attitude of Krishna and Lord Chaitanya, or Ramachandra and Krishna. Therefore, in the Sastras specific instructions are there. Just like the worship of Chaitanya is done by the method of chanting Hare Krishna.

Yes, it is true that Ramachandra banished Sita later on. Ayodhya is not bound up with any material worlds. Just like Vrindaban is not bound up by any material limitations as much as Krishna is not bound up by any material limitations. So the kingdom of Ayodhya historically was a tract of land as we see at the present moment, but at that time the king of Ayodhya was the emperor of the world.

Yes, that is very nice, your statement that, “I am offering special flowers and prayers to Lord Jagannatha every morning because I know He is liberal to crippled and mistake-making devotees of His lotus feet.” If we are always afraid of our mistakes, Krishna will save us from all such misgivings and even if we imperceptibly commit some mistake, He will forgive us. But we should be always careful not to commit mistakes.

You are trying your best at that center, and Krishna will help you certainly. I am very glad to see that you are not disappointed. This is a good sign. One should work with courage and patience, and certainly Krishna will be pleased. For the time being you have four or five devotees there, so you try to maintain them there so that you can carry on your program.

Hoping you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda’s description of his meeting at the house of Mr. Abdul Rabbi is one of humanness. When we received this letter, we delighted in reading Prabhupāda’s description of how he avoided all nonsense. Of course, we know Prabhupāda always avoided all types of nonsense, but that he reported it to us in such a charming way was delightful. Although Prabhupāda was wise, he always maintained a wonderful innocence and purity about him. By reading this letter, we can almost see Prabhupāda seated among the smoking, meat-eating clergymen—he alone refraining from nonsense, situated like a lotus on water. Prabhupāda doesn’t report this meeting in a haughty way, but as a humble, pure devotee who avoids sinful activities.

Thinking of the contrast between Śrīla Prabhupāda and these clergymen, I am reminded of another incident when Prabhupāda met Reverend Gary Davis at 26 Second Avenue. Reverend Davis was a black minister and a blues singer. He and his wife came to see Prabhupāda. We provided them upright chairs, and they sat on a higher level than Prabhupāda, who sat with his back against a mat and a pillow. Reverend Davis discussed prayer. He said it was difficult to know what to pray for. He was blind, and to emphasize his question “what to pray for” he repeatedly stomped his cane on the floor. Although “what to pray for” was puzzling to Reverend Davis, it was not puzzling to Prabhupāda. In a humble and soft-spoken manner, Prabhupāda leaned back against his pillow and making a graceful gesture he said, “We have to pray to God, ‘Please, how can I love you?’”

Prabhupāda was similarly humble in the presence of Mr. Abdul Rabbi, even though Prabhupāda possessed superior knowledge.

This letter also contains practical advice from Prabhupāda about the separation of men and women in the temple. Of course, they should not live in the same room together, but here “separate arrangement” refers to separate buildings. Prabhupāda recommends this as better than separate rooms in the same temple. Whatever the arrangement, though, men and women should live separately.

Certain caste Hindus have sometimes accused Prabhupāda of allowing his men and women disciples to mingle too freely, but Prabhupāda could not simply deny American women spiritual life because they were single and wanted equal status with men. Everyone was welcome to take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and to insure a devotional environment, separate accommodations for men and women had to be provided.

As for the reference to Mahārāja Daśaratha: I had written to Prabhupāda about an article I was writing on the *Rāmāyaṇa.* I had read how Mahārāja Daśaratha felt compelled to keep his promise to banish Rāma, his beloved son. I couldn’t understand why Daśaratha did not simply abandon the vow and defend his son. In this letter, Prabhupāda not only explains the importance of adhering to one’s words, but he goes beyond that conception; thus we learn that “Vasudeva was more spiritually advanced than Maharaja Dasarath,” because Vasudeva, who had made an agreement with Kaṁsa, did not hesitate to break his promise to save his son.

Prabhupāda’s explanation that one should approach each incarnation of God according to the mood in which He appears is significant. I have not seen this explanation given in any of his purports as clearly as he gives it here. I myself regularly preach from this letter about this point. Thus, Prabhupāda informs us that to worship Lord Caitanya, who appeared as Kṛṣṇa’s pure devotee, we should chant Hare Kṛṣṇa, and that will please Lord Caitanya.

While Prabhupāda was in Boston, I had also asked him a question about Lord Rāmacandra. I had read how Lord Rāmacandra was glorified in the *Rāmāyaṇa* by comparing Him to different demigods. (For instance, the *Rāmāyaṇa* compares Lord Rāmacandra’s strength to Indra’s.) If Rāmacandra were actually God, how do the scriptures favorably praise Him by comparing Him to a mere demigod? Prabhupāda replies that Lord Rāma appeared in the mood of a human being, the ideal king, and to compare a human being to a demigod is favorable praise.

While giving this explanation, however, Prabhupāda goes further and says that although Lord Kṛṣṇa appears to be a human being, He is so great that all the skies appear present in His mouth. People may think that greater than any person is something vast like the sky, but Lord Kṛṣṇa is so great that even as a child He contains all the universes in His mouth.

Prabhupāda also comments on my prayer—“I am offering special prayers and flowers to Lord Jagannatha every morning because I know he is liberal to crippled and mistake-making devotees at His lotus feet.” Prabhupāda responds to my expression of being afraid of making mistakes: “We should pray to the Lord, asking Him to save us from mistakes. Be sure that He will save us, but be careful not to commit mistakes.”

During this time in Boston we had six-inch Deities of Lord Jagannātha. They had not been properly installed, yet they were our only Deities. Once in 1968, we Boston devotees went to see Śrīla Prabhupāda in New York. We locked the temple and left Lord Jagannātha behind. In New York when we were with Prabhupāda in his apartment, I mentioned Lord Jagannātha, and Prabhupāda asked where He was. I said that we had left Him behind. Prabhupāda then expressed disappointment: never again should we close the temple entirely and leave the Deities alone. “What if you were to leave me alone in a room?” he asked. I felt bad and thought that perhaps I should return immediately to Boston, but Prabhupāda relented and said, “That’s all right.”

I also used to have a small Lord Jagannātha of my own that I kept in a box. I took Him with me every day to the welfare office, both in New York and later in Boston. At that time, I would buy some fruits for lunch, sit outside on a park bench, and offer my foodstuffs to Lord Jagannātha. Even in the winter, I would sit on a park bench amid the snow and eat my lunch before the figure of Lord Jagannātha. That I was making prayers to the Lord of the universe—and that Prabhupāda was encouraging me—shows how merciful Prabhupāda was. I was a young devotee, my devotional creeper fragile, yet I had prayed to the Lord of the universe and Prabhupāda encouraged me. This is the meaning of the verse:

*chāḍiyā vaiṣṇava-sevā*  
*nistara pāyeche kebā.*

**“Without serving a bona fide spiritual master, an ideal Vaiṣṇava, who can be delivered from the clutches of** ***māyā*****?”**

Where else could I have possibly gotten the idea to worship the Lord of the universe except from Prabhupāda? Where else could I have gained the *mūrti* or faith in the Deity? Where else could I have gained practical direction that I should offer my food and worship to the Lord? Even if I had theoretically received information about worshiping the Deity, certainly I would have ordinarily thought, “What is this nonsense of offering flowers to a statue of God?” However, by the grace of my spiritual master, I imbibed firm faith and conviction in worshiping the Deity, chanting the holy name, and performing devotional service. We trust that the spiritual master sees Kṛṣṇa and that when we follow him, we will also see Kṛṣṇa.

In the last paragraph of this letter Prabhupāda writes, “You are trying your best at that center, and Krishna will help you certainly. I am very glad to see that you are not disappointed.” Frankly, our Boston center was growing rather slowly at that time. I didn’t have to say so; Prabhupāda knew, because he had been in Boston for a month. Our temple was small, our activities insignificant, Yet here I was exchanging letters with Prabhupāda, a great *mahā-bhāgavata,* and I was brightly reporting to him our minor activities.

Even in 1968, Prabhupāda had full vision of projects he wanted to build in Bombay, Vṛndāvana, and Māyāpur—huge projects that would take millions of dollars and many expert devotees to construct. Yet he did not appear dissatisfied to spend a month with us and see our small conception of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He encouraged us, without scoffing. He might have easily said, “Why aren’t you doing more? What’s the matter? Why not more men, more money? Why not more books? Why such a small temple? You are a good-for-nothing and should be kicked out of the temple president’s post!” Rather than criticizing, however Prabhupāda encouraged us and saw the bright side.

He said we should work with courage and patience. Then Kṛṣṇa would certainly be pleased. These words encouraged me. I realized my activities were actually small, but Prabhupāda was not disappointed. He accepted my service.

Let us always work in the present to please our spiritual master. There may be difficulties or reduced situations, but if Prabhupāda accepts our service, then it is perfect. We should be realistic about trying to expand our service. Still, we should offer whatever service we can. Then there is hope for our success.

\* \* \*

June 27, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated June 23, 1968, and I have noted the contents. I am very much pleased to hear your good success in the park on Sunday. This is very much encouraging to me, to hear so many people are hearing the philosophy and chanting, and also taking nice prasadam as well. Yes, it is very good, and you please continue this sort of program! This system should be followed, and you will be successful. In New York also Hansadutta is following this system, holding Kirtan at Central Park every day, and he is collecting in the same way. All together he is collecting fifty to seventy dollars a day by contribution and by selling *Back to Godhead.* This is actually our successful propaganda.

We want to distribute our literature and books as well as our prasadam and inject our Hare Krishna medicine within the ear. So, reading of the literature and hearing the chanting is the medicine, and prasadam is the diet. So, if the diet and medicine are properly administered, the disease of Maya will be cured. But the physician must always be healthy. Preachers must be of highly elevated character, following strictly the rules and regulations and chanting regularly in the temple. There may be so many odds in the progressive march of Krishna Consciousness, but if we pin our faith in Krishna, everything will come out successful in due course.

Yes, Krishna didn’t like that place, for $350, so you could not acquire it.

Please convey my blessings to Jadurany, Madhavi Lata, and Devananda. You are all good souls; please work together combinedly for spreading Krishna Consciousness there in that city.

Hoping you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In his previous letter to me, Prabhupāda encouraged me not to be disappointed. He predicted success would come. By the time I received this letter, success had arrived—in the form of Kṛṣṇa’s revelation to us about the opportunity of chanting in the parks. As I had written Prabhupāda in my previous letter, things were difficult in Boston, and our preaching activities didn’t seem to be progressing. But now, by his grace, a new chapter was beginning: “I am very much pleased to hear of your good success in the park on Sunday. This is very encouraging to me to hear so many people are hearing the philosophy and chanting, and also taking nice prasadam as well. Yes, it is very good, and you please continue with this sort of program!”

The program was to go to the Boston Common and chant Hare Kṛṣṇa for several hours. At first we did not know what to expect. Five of us went, stood in one place on the grass, and began *kīrtana*. Within a few minutes, hundreds of people had gathered around us. In those days many young hippies wandered through the Common. Many of them even used to live and sleep there. On Sunday all sorts of middle-class people, young rowdies, and other Bostonians would gather in the Common. Few had seen anything like Hare Kṛṣṇa chanting; it aroused a lot of interest. In general the people were lowclass, and there were always some hecklers. Few took us seriously.

Once in a while there were incidents. Hippies threw bottles at us, and once the Hell’s Angels disrupted our *kīrtana.* But on the whole we were able to go on chanting hour after hour. After a while, we developed the idea that one or two devotees could circulate among the crowd and ask for donations while the others chanted. This proved successful. The whole experience was purifying for the devotees, and we would go back to our little temple feeling thoroughly satisfied and ecstatic. Devotees in other cities had simultaneously begun the same program. We had started it as a once-a-week program; then daily parties began going to places like Harvard Square.

Prabhupāda’s tone was especially exclamatory. Naturally we responded by understanding that public *kīrtana* must be our main program. Prabhupāda was directly pushing us to preach in public places. Now we could understand that this was the right formula; this was what we should have been doing. If there had been any hesitancy on our part, upon receiving this letter we gave it up and committed ourselves to the order of the spiritual master.

At the same time Prabhupāda pushed us forward to meet people and share *kīrtana* with them, he also stressed that the “position” of the preacher himself must always be healthy. Prabhupāda therefore stresses that the preacher should both publicly preach *and* strictly follow the rules and regulations (“chanting regularly in the temple”). By this time we knew, to some degree, what Prabhupāda referred to when he said, “There may be so many odds in the progressive march of Krishna Consciousness.” But with Śrīla Prabhupāda behind us, we too were eager to “pin our faith in Krishna,” and in following his instruction we trusted that everything would come out successfully.

## July 1968

July 2, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings and offer the same to Jadurany and others. Jadurany may be informed that I have received her letter and shall reply to her very soon.  
In the meantime, in my last letter I requested you to see the District Director Mr. J. H. Hamilton of the Boston office U.S. Immigration (223–2361). You know that I have sent him one letter of June 11, 1968 per Reg’d Post No. 00619. It cannot be that he has not received my above letter, but why has he not replied to my letter? Please inquire.

Please try to convince him that my presence in the U.S.A. is essential because I have got to supervise at least eight branches in the U.S.A. I am a qualified religious minister. I have got sufficient money to maintain myself without being a public charge, and my health is fit, as it has already been examined by the public health department. Under the circumstance, I am feeling that by denying my application on some technical grounds—on which I was not at all responsible—injustice has been done to me. Simply request the District Director to give me the right direction for the next step so that I may take the right action. Please, therefore, see him and take a written reply to my letter dated 11 June 1968. I am very much surprised that letters are not properly replied to in such an important office. Please, therefore, inquire in the matter, and let me know why the letter is not replied to, which is so important in this case.

Hope you will do the needful and let me know the result immediately. Hope everything is well there.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In response to Śrīla Prabhupāda’s direct order in this letter, I went to a government building in downtown Boston to see the District Director of U.S. Immigration. I was able to get an appointment with the Assistant Director Mr. Patrick Coomey in his private office, where he sat behind a big desk, a U.S. flag behind him.
