He had Śrīla Prabhupāda’s file before him and told me that they were not denying Prabhupāda’s residency on the basis of his not being qualified as a religious minister; rather, it was a technical matter. He told me he would be replying to Prabhupāda’s letter within a day or two. He assured me there was no legal case against Śrīla Prabhupāda, but we had to go through some procedures.

I remember thinking that he was a rather highly-placed official. I expressed my concern about the case to him, and I felt that there was nothing more I could do.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, however, had expressed in a letter to Brahmānanda that he felt there was some prejudice against him.

I understand that the government of the U.S.A. is disgusted with so-called swamis because they have exploited the people in so many ways. That is a fact. And if I would have been in the government, I would have also considered like that. So they have not a very good opinion about these rascal swamis. Under the circumstances, it will be difficult to get me admitted as a swami, although I am not a swami of the rascal group. But we have to prove it by action that this swami is not like those swamis. This remark was made by Mr. Allen Burke of the television company: He introduced me to the public as “Here is a real swami,” and he showed me all respectful compliments. Anyway, I am not after respectful compliments by the public. But I am concerned about my disciples. I want to see them quite able to preach this sublime doctrine of Krishna Consciousness, and therefore I wish to stay. Otherwise, I am not attracted for any place, either hell or heaven.

I had executed a simple errand, yet representing Prabhupāda that day I felt close to him. I reported my results to Prabhupāda my mail.

In his letter, Prabhupāda reasonably states his case, as he was always able to do in legal disputes or in cases of civil disagreements. Prabhupāda’s behavior was faultless, and when he appeared to become entangled in bureaucracies or legalities, or when he was unfairly treated like a common man by the worldly powers of Kali-yuga he could always reasonably and convincingly explain his case.

Once while Prabhupāda was away from New York, he had some of his disciples live in his apartment at 26 Second Avenue. A legal dispute for possession of the apartment began between the devotees and the members of an opposing party, who felt confident they could get a judge to remove us. Śrīla Prabhupāda, although not present when the case was heard, wrote a letter explaining his behavior, and it was read aloud in court to the judge. When the judge heard Śrīla Prabhupāda’s letter, he said, “That sounds reasonable to me,” and dismissed the case in Prabhupāda’s favor.

Similarly, Prabhupāda covered the points in his immigration case and clearly showed that there was no reason the immigration department should not consider him a qualified religious minister.

Also in this letter, Śrīla Prabhupāda firmly criticizes the U.S. immigration office, which was not answering his correspondence or dealing with his case efficiently, although he had said it was important to him. Prabhupāda had presented his case expertly. I had only to deliver the message to the immigration officials, thus showing them that Prabhupāda was represented by somewhat upstanding young American citizens, who indeed needed Prabhupāda present to supervise them. Little did they know *how essential* his presence was; they did not know the depth of Prabhupāda’s influence against the forces of *māyā* and the benefit he could give America if they allowed him to stay in their country.

Prabhupāda later told us how he had been denied residency in the United States, even after having spent so much money; yet he got it easily and inexpensively through the U.S. Consulate in Canada. In Canada he met with a black man, a U.S. official, and showed him the published copy of the *Teachings of Lord Caitanya,* which impressed him. The man must have been pious, because he made a point of passing Prabhupāda’s case.

If Prabhupāda’s work with American youth had been written down and fairly presented, it would have certainly been an impressive case for residency. He was doing meritorious work in a way that should have been appreciated by those concerned with good citizenship and character-building. That Prabhupāda had not received significant recognition by any official representative of the United States government was a testimony to its bankruptcy in values of goodness. Someone like me, a petty civil service worker, had to approach the U.S. government and inquire why they would not even answer Prabhupāda’s letters against their attempts to deny him American residency.

\* \* \*

July 3, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated June 28, 1968, and I thank you very much for it. Regarding Bali Maharaj: He was born in the same atheistic family of Maharaj Prahlad. He happened to be the grandson of Maharaj Prahlad, and his great grandfather Hiranyakasipu, was very powerful. There was animosity between the demigods and the demons, and Bali Maharaj also defeated the demigods several times and was occupying all the planets. At the time Bamandev appeared as the son of Kasyapamuni, Bali Maharaj was very charitably disposed.

Sometimes the atheists are also very charitable. Persons who believe that “We are doing pious activities, giving charity and performing welfare work to the human society. Why should we bother about God?”—such persons, even though very moral and pious in the estimation of the materialists, are also demons on account of their apathy for Krishna Consciousness.

So, Bali Maharaj was a man of that type. Under the circumstances he was not averse to accepting charity and other pious activities. He was being guided by his spiritual master, Sukaracharya. Sukara means the semina. In other words, one who claims to become acharya on the principle of being born of a Brahmin father, he may call himself sukaracharya, or acharya, or preacher—not by disciplic succession, but on the right of heredity.

In India there are still superstitions that one should be initiated by someone from such a sukaracharya family. They are called generally the *jatigosain*. *Jatigosain* means the caste spiritual masters. All over India, especially in Bengal, this *jatigosain* spiritual mastership is very prevalent. But really goswami means one who is master of the influence of different senses, namely the influence of anger, the influence of the belly, the influence of the genitals, and the influence of talking. So one who is master of these influential webs of sense gratification, he is called goswami. Goswami is not by hereditary chart. So Sukaracharya posed himself as such a goswami spiritual master. He had many mystic powers; therefore, he was considered to be a very influential spiritual master of the demons.

So when Bamandev appeared, Bali Maharaj was attracted by His beauty as a dwarf Brahmin, and as he was charitably disposed he wanted to give Him some charity. But Sukaracharya, being elevated in mystic yogic powers, could understand that Bamandev was Vishnu. And in order to favor the demigods, He had come there to cheat Bali Maharaj in the shape of begging some charities. Bali Maharaj was puffed up with his material vanities, and Bamandev, as He is Vishnu, all-peaceful, without interrupting his attitude just approached him in the form of a Brahmin, who has a right to beg something from the princely order. And the princely order also is always disposed to giving charity to the Brahmins.

Question, paragraph two answer: Sukaracharya, as spiritual master of Bali, taught him that everything should be offered to Vishnu. But when Vishnu actually appeared before Bali, Sukaracharya was afraid of Bali Maharaj’s charitable disposition. He warned Bali Maharaj that this Bamandev had come there to take everything from him in the shape of charity; therefore, he should not promise to give Him anything. This advice revolted Bali Maharaj, because he was formerly instructed that everything should be offered to Vishnu; now why was Sukaracharya asking him not to act in accordance with his previous instructions? Sukaracharya was afraid of his own position. He was living at the cost of Bali Maharaj, so if Bamandev would take everything from Bali Maharaj, he was wondering how he would live. That is the materialistic temperament. The materialist does not want to serve or to give to Vishnu, because he thinks that by giving away to Vishnu he will be put into a poverty-stricken condition. This is materialistic estimation. But actually that is not the fact, as it will be proven by the dealings of Bali Maharaj and Bamandev.

Question one, answer: That is the materialistic way of worshiping. Materialists are always careful for maintaining their material status quo first and then pleasing Vishnu, although they may profess to be devotees of Vishnu. Therefore, the Krishna Conscious persons are greater than such materialistic worshipers. Materialistic persons perform all pious activities or devotional activities for some material gain, and as soon as there is any hindrance in the path of material gain, they at once become demons. Therefore, bhakti means without any material desires. That is the sign of a pure devotee. He has no motive to satisfy his material desires by devotional service.

Question two, answer: That is just his military spirit, that Bali Maharaj says, “If He be the illustrious Lord Vishnu, not desirous of foregoing His own fame, He shall wrest from me this Earth after slaying me in battle, or *He shall be slain by me.”* The last portion of this question is not very clear.

Question three, answer: Why is Bali Maharaj considered a Mahajan? Bali Maharaj is a Mahajan because he wanted to serve Vishnu by disobeying his bona fide spiritual master. As explained above, Sukaracharya was a hereditary spiritual master by seminal discharge. But Bali Maharaj first revolted against this stereotype-seminal-succession spiritual master, and therefore he is a Mahajan. Śrīla Jiva Goswami has described in his *Karamasandharva* that one should be anxious to accept a spiritual master who is bona fide in spiritual knowledge. And if need be, one should relinquish the connection of a hereditary spiritual master and accept a real, bona fide spiritual master.

So when Sukaracharya advised him contrary to his previous instructions—specifically he checked Bali Maharaj in the matter of worshiping Vishnu—Sukaracharya became at once fallen down from the position of spiritual master. Nobody can become a spiritual master who is not a devotee of Vishnu. A Brahmin may be very expert in the matter of performing Vedic rituals, accepting charity, and distributing wealth—all these are the exalted qualifications of the Brahmins; but the Vedic injunction is, in spite of possessing all these qualities, if somebody is against Lord Vishnu, he cannot be a spiritual master.

So when Sukaracharya advised Bali Maharaj against Vishnu, Sukaracharya at once became unqualified as a spiritual master. Bali Maharaj disobeyed such an unqualified spiritual master, and therefore he is accepted as Mahajan. Mahajan means a personality whose footprints should be followed. So, his exemplary behavior in rejecting a non-Vaishnava spiritual master is ideal, and he is considered a Mahajan.

If Jadurany wants to paint the picture of Bali Maharaj, it should be like this: 1) The hall must be very nicely decorated, a princely hall. 2) In one side of the hall the royal throne should be presented as vacant. 3) Bali Maharaj should pose himself bowing down before Lord Bamandev; and Bamandev should be painted with one leg on the Earth and one leg high in the sky and one leg coming out of his navel and put on the head of Bali Maharaj. This means that giving one’s possessions to the Supreme Personality of Godhead is not full charity, but when one’s personal body and head is given to the service of the Lord, then one becomes perfect in offering everything to the Lord. This is called complete surrender of everything that a devotee may possess.

Bali Maharaj may be shown as not more than forty years old, a very nice-looking king, well-dressed, like the royal order, and with a mustache but no beard. Sukaracharya should be wearing Shaivaite telok, and also Maharaj Bali can have Shaivaite telok until after he meets with Bamandev, and then you can change Bali Maharaj’s telok into a Vaishnava one.

Yes, Pradyumna is typing that Third Canto and will send it to you soon. Hoping you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

P.S. While posting this letter, I have received your letter from 29 June 1968. I am glad to learn that Kirtan preaching in the park is going on well and you received $50.00 last Sunday. This is encouraging, as in New York also they are following the same process with success. I have received one letter from Rayrama also, and he is not going to Boston, but he is in L.A. He will write you.

Regarding immigration: I am sending herewith the copy of the letter of Mr. Hamilton. I think you should see Mr. Hamilton and take from him another letter advising me about the right direction.

Prabhupāda gave me the information about Bali Mahārāja to use in a comic strip, “The Mahajans,” which appeared in *Back to Godhead.* I wrote the script. At the time there was no published description of Bali Mahārāja, and Prabhupāda supplied us the information in this letter. I tried to incorporate all that he said. Prabhupāda later commented that he liked “The Mahajans” comic series.

In this letter Prabhupāda spends considerable time discussing the caste guru, the *jāti gosañis,* and other unqualified persons who claim to be guru (even though this detailed information was not important for the comic strip). Śukarācārya thought that by giving to Viṣṇu he would lose, but history proves otherwise. Prabhupāda also points out that by rejecting such a non-Vaiṣṇava guru, Bali Mahārāja became a *mahājana.*

\* \* \*

July 12, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated July 10, 1968, and I have noted the contents about Mr. Coomey, assistant district director of Boston department of Justice. As you say that within a day or two he is going to reply to my letter, I shall wait for his reply until next week, and then I shall take steps as it may be needed. In the meantime, I have noted his instructions, as conveyed by you.

I understand that you are trying to organize a Brahmacharini ashram. A Brahmacharini ashram is certainly a great necessity, because there are so many girl devotees who are attached to our Krishna Consciousness movement. Those who are married couples, there is nothing to be said—they can simply live together as husband and wife. But those who are not married, certainly such Brahmacharis and Brahmacharinis should not live together. That is a special restricted term of our cult. But because in your country there is no distinction between boys and girls or man and woman, and they can freely mix without any restriction, I did not give too much stricture on this point, because by such stricture they might be annoyed, and whatever Krishna Consciousness they were trying to develop might have been checked.

But factually, if you can organize a Brahmacharini ashram, it will be a very nice idea. And I think that our Jadurany or similar other girl students who are a little bit advanced, they can manage such an ashram. But there is another difficulty, that when the girls live together they will pick up quarrels. Anyway, that sort of quarreling will continue wherever there is a little bit of individuality. That is the nature. Even such quarreling is visible in the spiritual world also.

But the main thing is that in Boston you are the only earning member. How will you maintain such a Brahmacharini ashram separately unless there is some source of income? I expect that the pictures painted by the Brahmacharinis will be a source of income to the society. If some arrangement for sales organization can be made, then it will be a very excellent idea. The Brahmacharinis cannot go, of course, for begging, but if some of them agree to go out and sell our books and literature, that will also be helpful. Some source of income by honest endeavor must be there; otherwise how can a nice Brahmacharini ashram be maintained?

In the ashram we must supply all inmates necessary nutritious food. Especially in your country, because the devotees were accustomed to take meat and some protein food, regular supply of dahl, chapatis, rice, fruits and milk must be properly administered. There is no need of eating more than necessary, but the minimum demands must be supplied.

But if you can organize such a nice Brahmacharini ashram, it will be a great success of our society. There is a great need for this. And I wish sincerely that, except for husband and wife, everyone should live separately—man separate from woman, and woman separate from man. I shall be glad to hear from you about further developments. But one thing can be very nicely utilized: if the Brahmacharinis learn to use a typographic machine, that will be a great help, because printing is one of our most important lines of activities. And if the Brahmacharinis help us in the making of letter-printing sheets for photo offset printing, that will be a great help.

Hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This letter consists almost entirely of instructions for organizing a *brahmacāriṇī-āśrama.* We should not superficially think this letter is dated or that we have already completely established the unmarried women’s *āśrama* in ISKCON.

Prabhupāda stresses the necessity for providing suitable living arrangements for unmarried women. “Those who are married couples,” Śrīla Prabhupāda writes, “there is nothing to be said—simply live together as husband and wife.” But unmarried women, usually composed of young girls waiting to be married and women already married but who have since lost their husbands, will continue to join ISKCON. Some women may prefer to remain single or some, though married previously, may not wish to remarry. There will always be a need for the *brahmacāriṇī-āśrama,* and here Śrīla Prabhupāda gives many suggestions for how the women’s *āśrama* should be conducted.

Prabhupāda doesn’t describe an *āśrama* of married women mixed with unmarried women. It is better for the *brahmacāriṇīs* ṇsprit de corps that they have their own place and unmarried status and that they don’t have to mix constantly with household affairs, wives and their children, or men. I remember a *brahmacāriṇī* who was sixteen and living with a householder couple. Śrīla Prabhupāda advised her not to remain with them; if she did, she would practically become a *gṛhastha* herself. When the women marry, “there is nothing to be said,” but while they are *brahmacāriṇīs,* the temple authorities can organize separate living quarters for them.

The main point Prabhupāda makes here is that men should live separately from women. One might object that the whole *brahmacāriṇī* concept is a concoction. In Vedic society a young girl lived at home with her father until she was married; then her husband took care of her. There were no *brahmacāriṇīs.* But Americans know nothing of Vedic culture, and according to Vedic culture, American women live like prostitutes. Śrīla Prabhupāda did not turn away such wayward girls who showed sincere interest. He thought if he did that, “They might be annoyed, and whatever Krishna Consciousness they are trying to develop might be checked.” He therefore said, “Let there be a brahmacharini asrama.” But this was bound to cause a dilemma. The *brahmacāriṇīs* would quarrel with each other; they would not be able to live together peacefully in Kṛṣṇa consciousness . Śrīla Prabhupāda, however, viewed things philosophically and said that because we are individuals, of course there would be quarrels.

Still, he was not deterred in his desire to establish a women’s *āśrama.* He did not say, “Forget the *brahma-cāriṇī-āśrama.* Women will fight like cats, so let them go to hell. We cannot provide for them. Let them live at home.” Rather, Prabhupāda gave practical suggestions: who would pay for such an *āśrama*? He suggested the *brahmacāriṇīs* should distribute books and with that income they could be provided with proper food. Persons conditioned by Western upbringing would require a more substantial diet than the Indian *brahmacārīs* living in Swami Bon’s Institute for Oriental Studies, when Prabhupāda saw when he went to Vṛndāvana in 1967.

Prabhupāda also mentions that it would be useful for the *brahmacāriṇīs* to type and assist in printing his books. This is an instructive letter for engaging and protecting women in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, while at the same time pushing on the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

## August 1968

August 8, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated July 16, as well as July 30, 1968, which I received together through Vaikunthanatha das. The press cuttings were nice. The pictures appeared to be very attractive, and it is learned that it is the biggest daily paper in New England. So it is good publicity. Janmastami is to be performed on the 16th of August, and the next day is my birthday. The feasting should be held on the 17th so that you can finish two festivals in one—namely Nandotsav and Vyasapuja (spiritual master’s birthday).

I know you are doing your best to maintain the establishment of the Boston center, and Krishna will certainly help you in some way or other. Do not worry. I am a little anxious to understand about Jadurany. When I was in Boston I saw her weaker, and I asked her what was the trouble. She said that it was not very serious. But now I can understand that the ailment is increasing. So she must have complete rest—no work for the time being, and positively she should not play mridanga. I understand that she is determined to keep going at top capacity, and it is good that you have restricted her.

I do not know whether Sachi Suta is still there, but if he is there, show him this letter, and I am asking herewith that he may stay with you for some time. Uddhava has already joined the printing work, and I have decided that as soon as Advaita and Uddhava give me assurance that they will be able to conduct a regular press with other assistants, I shall immediately start one press in New York. You will be pleased to know that I have already my immigration visa in Canada. So there will be no difficulty anymore for going to the U.S.A. It may be that I may also get an American visa.

Regarding collection from the public: I think you should take special license or permission from the authorities, because as a religious society we can collect in such a way. Simply the authorities have to be informed that we are a serious institute for spreading God consciousness and that we are not professional beggars. The method of chanting in the park, distributing prasadam, and selling our literatures, and making some collection is just the way by which we can make both propaganda and maintain our institution. So this process must be continued with necessary sanction from the authority. I think there will be no difficulty in getting such sanction.

Hope this meets you well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

The press cuttings referred to in this letter concerned our park *kīrtanas.* Again Prabhupāda makes a statement that I was doing my best. I had expressed anxiety about whether I could financially maintain the Boston center. This problem is still current in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. Prabhupāda assures, “Krishna will certainly help you some way or other.” Although governments and businesses collapse, ISKCON centers rarely close because of economic bankruptcy, because Kṛṣṇa provides for His sincere servants.

An important section of this letter is Prabhupāda’s direction to us regarding public collections. We were performing *kīrtana* in Boston, and devotees in Montreal, New York, and San Francisco were doing the same. Prabhupāda approves of this and says we have the right to collect from the public just as other charitable organizations do. This is an ideal method of earning money, because *brāhmaṇas* should not have to do other work besides studying and preaching. Prabhupāda says we should do this with government sanction. His instruction is simple and pure, and there is no indication here that he wants us to engage in flim-flam techniques, falsely presenting ourselves before the public.

Previously I wrote Prabhupāda and complained that the students in our neighborhood were not cooperative. They lived like animals, dressed in dirty clothes, and the hallways of their apartments and the streets of the neighborhood were filthy and filled with garbage. They completely ignored our presence and immersed themselves in loud rock music, drugs, sex, and school; Prabhupāda sympathized our neighbors were not "happy," but cautioned us not to be overly concerned with getting the best location for our temple. He indicated that since everywhere people are unreceptive, we should not expect to find receptive audiences. We had thought we could solve this problem by moving into a students’ center where there were the so-called best candidates, but *saṅkīrtana* in the park was our most important method for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

\* \* \*

August 19, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated August 12, 1968, and I have noted the contents carefully. Regarding the missing first fifteen chapters, rest assured there will be no difficulty to get them back, and I am just remembering where they are lying. Most probably they are in India, so in my letter I shall inquire from Achyutananda, or if you desire you can inquire from him also. His address is c/o Radha Press, 993/3 Main Road, Gandhi Nagar, Delhi–31.

Regarding collections from the park: please try to get the permit. This will help us in getting such a permit in all other centers. This responsive contribution to our Kirtan is a very nice program for our propaganda. If you are successful in this matter, then there will be no difficulty with our financial position. If we can sell our books and literature and have some collection from the public, then our economic problem is solved. So try to get this permit with your great endeavor.  
Another thing: You will be pleased to know that I have advised Sachi Suta to go to you and join you. He is now in San Francisco, and you can write to him. As I have advised him to join you, you can also send him an invitation letter. That boy is searching out a nice place of peace so he can execute Krishna Consciousness; I think he was not satisfied in Los Angeles, so he proposed to go to Buffalo, but I have advised him to go to you, and if you invite him, it will be nice.

So far as Jadurany is concerned, inform her that her body is Krishna’s body. Therefore, she should take care of her health. Of course, it is very encouraging that she puts forward service of Krishna first, more than all other considerations. It is very nice, and I very much appreciate this attempt. But still, we should not neglect our health, because the body of a devotee is not material. This has been warned against by the Goswamis, that we should not neglect any material thing if it can be used for Krishna Consciousness. So her body, because it is engaged in Krishna’s service, is valuable. So not only she, but all of you should take care of this poor girl. She has left her parents, and she is unmarried—no husband. So, of course she is not poor, because she has got many Godbrothers and sisters, and above all, Krishna. She is not at all poor. In spite of that, we should take care about her health. That is our duty, and inform her that she may not strain beyond her capacity.

Of course, such kinds of trouble may come and go; a devotee is not afraid of such things. But still it is our duty to think always that our body is Krishna’s and we must not neglect it.

So far as the Mahajan comic pictures, it was very nicely drawn, but actually it is not so distinct. Next time I request that pictures must be made very distinct and clear. There is no question of hurrying. I have given some hints to Brahmananda about a story of a mouse and a tiger, and you can ask him for the hints, and he will give them to you. In the meantime, if Brahmananda asks you to join in some important meeting, I think you will be able to go for a day or two. I am so glad that Sriman Sri Nivas is already contributing some money for the activities of the society, and please convey my sincere thanks to him. Krishna will be very much pleased for his mentality.

Regarding your question: “Is it not true that by service I am with you?” It is very correct. You are not only in connection with me, but you are connected with the whole parampara, up to Krishna. It is so nice a thing. By service only we are connected. As stated in the *Srimad Bhagavatam*: sevon mukhe hi jihvadau svayam eva sphuraty adah. So the presence of Krishna is always everywhere, but it is simply manifested by your service. That is the technique. Sarvan khalv idam brahman: everywhere Brahman is present. Just like everywhere there is electricity. It is the technical art that brings out electric power from everywhere. So by service you are not only in touch with me but my predecessors, the acharyas, up to Krishna.

The Fourth Canto of *Bhagavatam* is already there, and I do not know how far you have made progress, but do not be in a hurry. The temple organization is your first business and editing secondary, because there are many others also who can do that. But to organize the temple and to make the Boston center a nice center is important, because there are so many young male students, and we are especially interested in the younger generation because they can accept this philosophy very quickly.

In the *Srimad Bhagavatam* it is recommended by Prahlad Maharaj that unmarried boys should accept this Bhagavat dharma, or Krishna Consciousness, for their life’s benefit. We have to convince them about this, that this life is very valuable. Material education had misled them, because it is without Krishna Consciousness. So when Krishna Consciousness is added to material advancement, it becomes aromatic gold. Gold is very beautiful, but if it has some aroma—aromatic gold—if that is available in the market, it will have greater value. So material civilization is very good for the comforts of the body. Now if we do not utilize the strengths and comforts of our body for Krishna Consciousness, our body will be used for sense gratification. And that will degrade our position. So the younger section of this country should take very seriously that they should take to Krishna Consciousness, and the next generation will be a different public. Those in the Western world who are materially and spiritually advanced will be happy in this life as well as in the next.

Hope you are all well there, and please take care that Jadurany shall remain healthy and not overextend herself.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

The police had stopped us from taking collections in the park and told us, we had to get a permit. Prabhupāda obviously wanted us to continue our *kīrtana* and solicit donations. Therefore, in a personal way, he insisted that I take it upon myself to secure the proper permission. Performing *kīrtana,* asking for donations, and distributing magazines—these combined together would solve our economic problems while providing good facility for preaching. “So,” Prabhupāda concludes, “try to get this permit with your great endeavor.” Upon receiving this order, I began the involved processes of getting permission.

Meanwhile, we gradually resumed our collections, even without local permission. I went to the library and studied Supreme Court cases; I understood that there was solid precedence in the U.S. Constitution for public collections by bona fide religious groups. We had already obtained our state incorporation and federal tax exemption for ISKCON as a religious organization. Therefore, even without the local permit we continued our chanting. We were stopped sometimes by the police, but we always pursued our objective. Well over a year later, we also got permission from the municipal government, but if I had not received this direct order, I probably would not have directly investigated the matter. The force of the spiritual master’s order makes us perform activities that we ourselves would not think of or that may not suit our “tendency.” If Prabhupāda directly asked us to do something, there was no question of refusing, and every sincere disciple knows well that from undertaking the austerity to follow that order comes the greatest spiritual reward and satisfaction. There is no alternative. Śrīla Prabhupāda combined affectionate, fatherly concern for our well-being with his serious intention to push forward the *saṅkīrtana* movement. Thus he has written in a *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* purport:

The only purpose of the preachers of the *saṅkīrtana* movement must be to go on preaching without restriction. That is the way in which Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu introduced this *saṅkīrtana* movement to the world.

—Cc. Ādī [9.29](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/9.29), purport

Prabhupāda’s instruction here about Jadurāṇī’s behavior is significant, not only in the specific context. She had a passionate but unintelligent desire to continuously work at her paintings—without regard for her ill health. Her attitude was that since she was not her body, she might as well continue working in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, even without properly resting, eating, or treating her ill health. She was so headstrong that she would not listen to anyone. therefore, as temple president, I wrote Prabhupāda about her.

Here Prabhupāda replies that she should not neglect her body or think of it as despicable matter. Rather, she should think of her body as something to be dedicated to Kṛṣṇa’s service.

Prabhupāda appreciated her statement of being willing to risk anything—even health—for Kṛṣṇa, but actually, we should not neglect Kṛṣṇa’s property. A devotee’s body is Kṛṣṇa’s, not his own, either to enjoy or neglect.

At the time, we did not have the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*, in which this instruction is given in the example of Sanātana Gosvāmī’s wanting to commit suicide. He thought his body despicable, but the Lord instructed him that as a devotee, his body was not under his own jurisdiction; it had to be directed by Kṛṣṇa. We did not yet know of this example in the early days before Prabhupāda had written all his instructions in his books. A teaching like the one in this letter was an eye-opener and revealed another important facet of the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy.

Before 1970, Prabhupāda wrote many letters in which he expressed his concern for different devotees’ health, sometimes giving his own personal remedies for indigestion, coughs, and headaches. In my own case, he repeatedly told me that I should eat more, as I appeared thin.

The devotees in the early days of ISKCON had the unique advantage of being personally directed by Prabhupāda. His direct instruction was as good as *śāstra.* Thus the disciples of Śrīla Prabhupāda who have personally received different instructions from him can never forget or neglect those instructions, even if other advanced Vaiṣṇavas in our *sampradāya* give different śāstric references.

Aside from Prabhupāda’s asserting the real position about how a devotee should take care of his health, he was simultaneously concerned for and affectionate toward his devotee. Certainly as spiritual father, he did not want to see his son or daughter’s health neglected. We were also his instruments, meant to serve his cause. For these reasons we had become dear to him. We felt that his main intention was simply to give us his blessings. If the few disciples Prabhupāda was engaging were to leave Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Kṛṣṇa would send Prabhupāda others. There was ultimately no reason for his concern for us except his causeless mercy.

As I will describe in an upcoming letter, Prabhupāda soon asked me to marry Jadurāṇī, mainly because she would be better protected if she were married. I foresaw a hint of this in this present letter when Prabhupāda wrote, “All of you should take care of this poor girl. She has left her parents, and she is unmarried—no husband. So, of course, she is not poor, because she has got so many Godbrothers and sisters, and above all, Krishna. She is not at all poor. In spite of that, we should care about her health.”

We may sometimes have different intimations about the future the spiritual master has planned for us. We cannot always anticipate his plans, but we may try to understand his mind. As Prabhupāda told a few of us one evening just before beginning the first *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* class, “This Kṛṣṇa consciousness is revolutionary. You will see. But just be patient.” Nor can we say we know our spiritual master’s plan for us when he gives us a particular order. His order may be the first in a series of steps he plans to lead us through. For example, he may ask us to marry only to have us released from that situation soon after. Our job is not to speculate whether our spiritual master actually knows our best interests. We must assume that he does, and with eager submission, follow his instructions.

Prabhupāda once advised a very thin devotee to eat more. Much later Prabhupāda met the same devotee in a different place. The devotee had become overweight, and Prabhupāda told him that he had eaten too much. The devotee protested, “But Prabhupāda, you told me I should eat more.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda replied to the devotee that as he had obeyed formerly, he should now obey again—even though the second order was exactly opposite the first.

This letter also contains an answer to a philosophical question I had asked Prabhupāda: “Is it true that I am connected with you through my service, even though we are apparently separated?”

Prabhupāda replied, “It is very correct. You are not only in connection with me, but you are connected with the whole parampara, up to Krishna.” By service only are we connected. Prabhupāda gives several śāstric quotes in this connection. In a philosophical light, he stresses service and how that service connects us not only to our own spiritual master, but to the whole disciplic line. By personally giving me this instruction, I was as satisfied as if I had achieved Śrīla Prabhupāda’s personal presence. To understand the concept of service in separation–or anything in Kṛṣṇa consciousness—we have to have love and trust in our spiritual master.

By the time I received this letter, Prabhupāda had finished dictating the Third Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, and I was inquiring about his progress with the Fourth Canto tapes. Prabhupāda cautioned me, “Do not be in a hurry.” I sometimes used to look ahead and calculate how soon Prabhupāda would finish later cantos. Once I told him how many months it had taken him to complete the Third Canto, and I therefore wondered how long it would take for him to finish the whole *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. Prabhupāda began to calculate, based on my estimates, but after figuring out how long the next few cantos would take (compensating for their various sizes), he stopped and said, “Anyway, it’s all up to Kṛṣṇa.” I may have wanted to push my spiritual master forward in his work out of the joy of seeing his books published and distributed. Here, however, he indicates that there was no question of his work being rushed; neither was it a merely mechanical process.

In this letter he also wrote that my main concern should be temple organization and preaching to the younger generation in Boston. Prabhupāda gave a philosophical description in this letter of how youth should practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness, which is the best benefit of life. This was the kind of thing we would immediately incorporate into our lectures. Any disciple of Prabhupāda who received some letters from him is guided even today—just as the mariner is guided by the stars—by the specific philosophical conclusions Prabhupāda gave him in the form of instructions.

## September 1968

September 20, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your two letters dated September 8 and 17, 1968, and I am so happy to learn that you are proud of being husband of Jadurany. Yes, you should be proud of having a nice wife like that. And I have handed over my daughter unto you, because I knew that you would be the perfect boy to take care of her. Recently she was deteriorating in her health, so I was very much anxious about her; therefore I decided to put her in your charge. Of course, even when you were not married, you were taking care of her always, but when the relationship is there as husband and wife, there is the question of responsibility. Anyway, everyone in our society thinks that your combination is very much appropriate. So be happy with your nice wife, and take care of her properly.

In your letter of the 17th instant you have frankly inquired from me about householder life, especially in the matter of sex relationship. A sannyasi is not supposed to be asked about anything sexual. But still, because you are so much dependent on my instruction, so I must give you information as far as possible. Married life is not for sex indulgence. The principle of marriage is on the background of getting good children. So the householder is allowed to have sex once in a month, just after the menstrual period. The menstrual period prolongs at least for five days, so after this five days one can have sex life, provided he desires to get a child. And as soon as the wife is pregnant, no more sex life until the child is born and is grown up at least for six months.

After that, one may have sex life on the same principle. If one does not want more than one or two children, he should voluntarily stop sex life. But one strictly should not use any contraceptive method and at the same time indulge in sex life. That is very much sinful. If the husband and wife can voluntarily restrain by powerful advancement of Krishna Consciousness, that is the best method. It is not necessary that because one has got a wife, therefore he must have sex life. The whole scheme is to avoid sex life as far as possible. And if one can avoid it completely, then it is a great victory for him.

Married life is a sort of license for sex life on the condition of raising children. So you should try to understand these principles of married life and use your discretion. You should not imitate great personalities like Bhaktivinoda Thakur, but you must follow his footprints. But it is not always possible to have the same success as great personalities like Bhaktivinoda Thakur achieved. So in all circumstances, you should try to follow the footprints of authorities, but never imitate them. Unless Jadurany develops better health and strength, I do not advise her to become pregnant. I think you will understand the instruction as I have given and try to follow as far as possible.

Our relationship is eternal. But if somebody lags behind, in spite of our eternal relationship, he may not meet the others at the destination. Just like a flock of birds—although very intimately related, every one of them has to fly in the sky by individual strength. If one is less strong, the others cannot keep him in the sky. That is the law of nature. So long as every one of us is strong in Krishna Consciousness, there is no doubt that you can fly to the spiritual sky and we can meet together without failure. Therefore, individual strength is most important. And that individual strength is achieved in the association of devotees also. So you can make your own judgment.

Anyway, please take care of your health, and I hope you are both well and happy.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda wrote me this letter not long after he asked me to marry. He was in New York in September, and we members of the Boston temple went to see him there. One day during our visit he called me into his room and asked me to marry. I asked him whether this was what he actually wanted for me and said that otherwise, I was planning to remain *brahmacārī.* Prabhupāda said that this was what he wanted. Therefore I agreed.

Prabhupāda then told me that my wife and I should first go through the civil legal procedures for becoming married. Then on the appearance day of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura (an auspicious day) Prabhupāda performed a fire sacrifice at the New York temple for my wedding, and an initiation. After returning to Boston, I wrote Prabhupāda stating my satisfaction with his arrangement. In his reply he revealed why he had asked me to marry. My wife, his spiritual daughter, was sick, and out of concern for her he wanted her protected. I was available, the likely candidate.

In my letter I asked some straightforward questions about sex in Kṛṣṇa conscious marriage. Although it was not exactly appropriate, I didn’t hesitate to ask Śrīla Prabhupāda the questions that were on my mind. Prabhupāda, as my spiritual master, did what no other Vaiṣṇava was willing to do. That he, a *sannyāsī,* was arranging marriages was revolutionary. Prabhupāda himself describes this:

In our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement we follow this policy of Lord Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu. For example, since boys and girls in the Western countries freely intermingle, special concessions regarding their customs and habits are necessary to bring them to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The *ācārya* must devise a means to bring them to devotional service. Therefore, although I am a *sannyāsī,* I sometimes take part in getting boys and girls married, although in the history of *sannyāsa* no *sannyāsī* has personally taken part in marrying his disciples.

—Cc. Ādi [7.37](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/7.37), purport

Arranging a marriage, however, did not mean Śrīla Prabhupāda merely officiated at the ceremony. Because his disciples were dependent, he would have to give them instructions, even in how they restricted sex, since he was their only source of knowledge. I did not know anything about marriage in Kṛṣṇa consciousness; there was no question of Prabhupāda’s asking me to look to a source of knowledge other than himself: “Still, because you are so much dependent on my instruction, so I must give you information as far as possible.”

As for his explicit advice, Prabhupāda does not allow any liberty for wanton, illicit sex life. He states that a householder can engage in sex only with his wife once a month for producing a child. After conception, he must abstain from sex for almost a year and a half. He can only have sex again if he wants to have another child (and he must be able to support that child). When one knows this, it is obvious that marriage in Kṛṣṇa consciousness is not an arrangement to “satisfy” lusty desires. The path of Kṛṣṇa consciousness recommends renunciation from sex—for *gṛhasthas, brahmacārīs,* and *sannyāsīs.* In this letter Prabhupāda severely restricts the *gṛhastha’s* “license.”

Prabhupāda’s final instruction in this letter about “meeting at the destination” is wonderful. I had asked (probably on my wife’s insistence) whether our marriage was eternal. If it was not eternal, then it was material. This presented a dilemma. If we had not become united in an eternal situation, then what was the use of that situation in the first place? Why should we invest our time and energy in something temporary and therefore, material and useless? Prabhupāda answers the question expertly, stressing that it is individual. A Kṛṣṇa conscious husband and wife are linked in the transcendental system. Our Godbrothers are also linked in the transcendental system. Whether they will meet in their next lives, however, depends on each devotee’s individual strength.

This explanation reveals both the individual and group benefits of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The example of the flock of birds flying in the sky—“although very intimately related, every one of them has to fly in the sky by individual strength”—was perfectly clear and a pleasing and enlightening metaphor. Following this example, I try to bring out both aspects in my own preaching. Each of us is alone on the spiritual path, while at the same time we are drawing strength from our intimate connection with our Godbrothers (or, in this case, spouse) and ultimately from our intimate connection with our spiritual master and Kṛṣṇa.

\* \* \*

September 28, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. This is to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated September 24, 1968, and I am so pleased that Devananda is doing marvelous work in propagating our Krishna Consciousness movement by lecturing in the Boston University and he is being paid $25 per lecture. It is a great credit for me also. And I feel proud that Devananda is so nicely representing my views.

By Krishna’s grace everything is gradually coming to the fulfillment of my dreams. The Sankirtan party is walking in the streets and is getting good response from the public. They are selling *Back to Godhead* nicely and getting good sympathy from the public by considerable collections. We are not after collecting money, but when people offer something out of love and sympathy for our mission, it becomes a great asset. I am sure if all of you, my beloved disciples, combine together and try to preach Krishna Consciousness in this spirit, Krishna will give the necessary strength.

By the grace of Krishna and the spiritual master, the dumb man can speak like a great orator, and a lame man can cross over a great mountain. So we must have the necessary unflinching faith in Krishna and the spiritual master, and the strength will be supplied by Krishna. After all, anything which we experience is but a fraction of the unlimited energy of Krishna. And even by a fragmental touch of such spiritual energy of the Lord, we can make our lives successful.

Regarding yourself and Jadurany: You are both very sincere servitors of the cause of Krishna, and Krishna will give you the necessary intelligence how to behave with one another. I am glad to learn that the girls under the guidance of Jadurany are doing nice painting work, and I am very much inclined to have many, many Sankirtan pictures painted like the one which was on the wall in your Boston temple while I was there. I have one photograph of this picture, and it is so nice that I feel my joy is unlimited. (See enclosed sketch for identifying the exact painting I am speaking of.)

Our Madhavi Lata is also a good artist. She is painting a very nice picture of Radha Krishna seated on a swing. But she is a little independently spirited and wants to wander with me. Anyway, she is a sincere, good soul, and let her do the work in her own way. But it is a good asset for her that she has attraction to hear me. That will make her all right in due course of time, and it may be that she will also come out a great painting assistant in our society.

I am sending herewith the certificate required by you, and I hope Krishna will help you in your desire to get more help for your executive function.

Enclosed please find also one letter of thanks for Devananda. Please deliver it to him with my blessings. Hope you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

P.S. I am a little perturbed by hearing about your accident, but I am sure Krishna will help you in your sincere service. I am glad to learn that Jadurany is improving in her health, and I shall always pray to Krishna for her health and prolonged life.

Prabhupāda was pleased that we were receiving twenty-five dollars per lecture at an evening education program we were conducting at Emerson College. We sometimes reported our progress to Prabhupāda as a favorable success, even when the preaching program was at its outset or even only recently contemplated. I wrote Prabhupāda of this particular success of my Godbrother after his first lecture in the series, because I had seen Devananda preach potently. Prabhupāda’s response, like Kṛṣṇa’s, was to come forward ten steps after we had come forward only one (with a program that soon stopped because of poor attendance).

This letter also contains advice about *saṅkīrtana.* Prabhupāda was encouraged by our recent attempts at public *kīrtana* in the streets and parks and confident that all his U.S. centers should begin this, as well as a program for collecting donations. “We are not after collecting money.” This was a subtle instruction guiding us to behave properly, not as professional beggars. Prabhupāda assured us, “Krishna will give you the necessary strength.”

But experience was a strong influence. When the devotees saw that the *karmīs* were willing to freely give us money, accepting us as worthy recipients of charity, some of the devotees approached them more aggressively to get larger donations. Some devotees couldn’t help but approach the *karmīs* crudely . Gradually our image changed—to one of hustlers and beggars.

As the temple president, I was grateful for the devotees’ collections, which gradually, by their combined efforts, outdid what I was earning by working five days a week at the welfare office. We were not aware that our aggressive money collection would eventually have negative effects. The other centers were also using aggressive techniques for collecting money that were callous toward the people’s sentiment. “After all,” we reasoned, “the money they would have spent on illicit sex and drugs is now going to Kṛṣṇa, so it will be used properly, and the *karmīs* will also benefit from their donations. They might be saved at the time of death.” (As Kṛṣṇa states in the *Bhagavad-gītā*: *trāyate mahato bhayāt*—even a little bit of Kṛṣṇa consciousness can save one from the greatest danger.)

While our philosophical understanding was correct, our techniques were shortsighted. Prabhupāda, however, gave us simple instruction in this letter: “The whole process of Kṛṣṇa consciousness is under the spiritual energy. We should therefore depend on Kṛṣṇa, and our endeavors will be successful.” My understanding of this statement is that Prabhupāda was not simply encouraging us that Kṛṣṇa would send money if we tried, but he was instructing us by stressing the preaching spirit—not just the money-collecting spirit—and that would help keep us in the spiritual energy.

We had to learn these things, however, by our mistakes. Nevertheless, Prabhupāda’s instruction that despite these problems we should collect donations remains an indelible confirmation. Whatever money we collected was only a fraction of Kṛṣṇa’s energy. And Prabhupāda said if we tried this process of *kīrtana* and selling books we would enter spiritual exchange with Kṛṣṇa, and even “by a fragmental touch of such spiritual energy of the Lord, we can make our lives successful.”

I again wrote Prabhupāda for advice on marriage. I was baffled. Was the husband-wife relationship material or spiritual? If it was spiritual, then what was the necessity of a man and woman living together as husband and wife? And if it was material, then why would Prabhupāda have engaged us in it? Prabhupāda informed us here to pray to Kṛṣṇa and He would guide us. Prabhupāda was also hopeful, that because we were “both very sincere servitors of the cause of Krishna, Krishna will give you the necessary intelligence how to behave with one another.” We could not expect Prabhupāda to instruct us in every domestic detail, but if we served sincerely, we would be directly following Prabhupāda, and thus Kṛṣṇa would help us.

In this letter Prabhupāda also referred to an independent girl living in our center, who later left Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He no doubt saw that this girl was not going to be able to live in ISKCON as a submissive devotee. The only way she could stay was to follow Prabhupāda from center to center, depending on his presence to maintain her. He spent time with her and was concerned for her, as he was with other disciples who were risks. Here, however, Prabhupāda offered an explanation: “She has a good asset—her attraction to hear me. That will make her all right in due course of time, and it may be that she will also come out a great painting assistant in our society.” This girl (Mādhavī-latā) liked to wander from place to place without serving, seriously but it was to her credit that she wandered to where Prabhupāda was and regularly heard his lectures and informal conversations. Prabhupāda saw the bright side: her wandering was her attraction to hear from her spiritual master.

Prabhupāda was always optimistic, making favorable predictions for things that, in some cases, later appeared to prove failures. How can we understand this? Actually, these predictions did not fail: whatever little Prabhupāda gained was his permanent success. Also, when Prabhupāda said, “It may be that she will come out all right in due course of time,” that was his encouragement for that devotee so that she would attempt to fulfill Prabhupāda’s favorable prediction. His statement was true: a devotee who attempted something, would emerge successful. However, each bird in the flock has to fly on its own.

The accident Prabhupāda referred to in this letter happened to me in the course of service. As a result, I was hospitalized for emergency treatment. I did not want to bother Prabhupāda with this, but it had significance because I was temporarily decommissioned, and I wanted to inform him of something that was obviously consuming my time and attention. I must admit, I was always craving Prabhupāda’s personal attention, and when he acknowledged my report of my accident, it was a great solace for me.

\* \* \*

September 29, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am just in receipt of your air mail special delivery letter dated September 26, 1968, and I am very much pleased to know that the students of different universities in Boston are taking some interest in our missionary activities.

You write to say that it appears the students are more discontent than ever and are openly stating that they do not have any solution to their discontent with the present civilization. It is a very good opportunity, therefore, for presenting our manifesto on the basis of *Bhagavad Gita As It Is* and *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya.* These books are also coming out very soon, by the end of next month. If there is good opportunity for rousing the student community in Boston, I must take advantage of the opportune moment, if actually they are eager to know about it. I am sure they will appreciate the techniques of our movement, if they patiently hear for some time.

If they are ready to spare such time, I am prepared to deliver lectures on the *Bhagavad Gita As It Is* as well as *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya* once a week in different colleges and universities. But I shall appreciate their genuine desire if I am invited directly—so that I can know they are actually eager about it. In that case, I can cancel all my engagements in the western part of your country, and I may spare one or two months in Boston for executing this urgent and important work. The thing is that they must be serious; they must hear the philosophy for some time, and then I am prepared to spare my time for this purpose. So I shall be glad to hear from you further in this matter, and then I shall chalk out my program.

Regarding the Deities: the quotation by Overseas Exporters appears to be very high. I have inquired from several other places also, so let us wait. Don’t put any order just now unless you hear from me. Harivilas brought two pairs of small Deities from Vrindaban. One pair is with me, so They are also very nice. So you can ask Harivilas, and he will give you information where to get Them and what the price is. In that case, you can immediately write to Achyutananda and Jai-Govinda, who are now in Delhi at the following address: c/o Indraprastha Goudiya Math, Dinaka-talab, Malkanganj, Subji-mandi, Delhi 7, India. And arrange to take a supply from Vrindaban directly, not only one pair but a few pairs.

Hope you are all well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In my letter to Prabhupāda, I wrote that the students in Boston were becoming disappointed in their own attempts for liberation. During this time the Vietnam War was in progress and students were agitated. The students’ cynicism was evident. I felt it when I would speak with them. The students in our neighborhood were losing hope and becoming nihilistic. Prabhupāda replied to my report by saying that the spirit of unrest and cynicism toward traditional material values could be used favorably for introducing Kṛṣṇa consciousness. When someone is materially bereft, he is often more receptive to spiritual knowledge than someone who is striving optimistically for material success. “Take advantage,” Prabhupāda said. If the students were really interested in hearing Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy, he would cancel his program wherever he was in the world and come to Boston to speak to the student population there. That I wrote him about my general observations of the students and how they were taking some interest in hearing about spiritual life was one thing but for Prabhupāda to actually come to Boston, he would have to be convinced the students actually had a genuine desire to hear from him. Otherwise, it would not be worth his time.

Prabhupāda had already experienced that the Boston University students were mostly unenthusiastic to inquire extensively about Kṛṣṇa consciousness. To show their interest practically, therefore, Prabhupāda wanted them to invite him personally *and* pay for his trip.

Prabhupāda refers here to little Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities, which we desired and later received in Boston.

## October 1968

October 9, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I have received your letter without date stating about the situation in Boston. By the statement of your letter, I can understand that it is a political situation. Of course, although everything is included in Krishna Consciousness, we do not wish to take part directly in politics and controversial politics. We can take part in politics only when it is sanctioned by Krishna. This political struggle by the students is a different thing. It is a reaction of the karma. Because both the opposing parties, namely the students who have taken shelter in the churches and the government force, which is coming to arrest them—both are in the same category, because when there is the question of meat-eating or supporting the slaughterhouses, both of them will agree.

So the present situation is a reaction to man’s sinful activities. We specially recommend one restrain from four kinds of primary sinful activities, namely illicit sex, intoxication, meat-eating, and gambling. But all these fighting people, they are cent percent addicted to all these habits. So if they are serious about mitigating the unpalatable situation of the society, they must agree to accept Krishna Consciousness; otherwise, there is no possibility of peace in the world.  
Anyway, the situation in Boston as you have described is not very much favorable for preaching our Krishna Consciousness, but if there is possibility of selling our *Back to Godheads,* then that is very nice. You can take advantage.

So far as my going to Boston is concerned, unless the students agree to hear patiently for some time *Bhagavad Gita* and *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya*, the casual lectures will not be very beneficial. So they are not very much interested; otherwise they would have to come to our classes. But I told you previously that I am prepared to go provided they pay the expenses. But I think that this is also not possible. So why should you take the risk of getting me there and then with great difficulty maintain my expenses? I think it is not very much favorable, so we shall see later on what is to be done; for the time being let it be dropped.

Students’ prayers twenty-four hours a day, listening to the political talks, and desiring the war should stop is useless. God cannot be their order-supplier. First of all they act sinfully, and when there is reaction of war, pestilence, famine, and so many other natural disturbances, they pray to God for stopping them. This is not possible. Just like a criminal first of all commits theft, burglary, and debauchery, and when he is captured by the police force, if he prays to the government to stop his punishment, that is not possible. So they are engaged in all sinful activities, and by nature’s law there must be reaction. If I am now encouraging cow killing or animal killing, and when by nature’s law the turn comes upon me to be killed, if I pray to God that I want Him to stop it, how can it be stopped?

So their process is not very genuine. They want to make God their order-supplier. God is not the order-supplier. He is the order-giver. He orders everyone to surrender unto Him, and the fools and rascals who do not surrender unto Him, in the form of so-called prayer they want to order God that He should ask material nature to stop her legal activities. That is not possible.

So the situation is not very favorable, but if somebody agrees to hear *Bhagavad Gita* and the *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya* continually for some time, even if they do not pay me anything I am prepared to go and lecture—but not to take part in some political agitation. So I was awaiting your letter indicating whether I was to go to Boston; now I shall prepare my next program for going to Los Angeles, I think. And my going to Europe is not yet fixed-up.

Regarding Jadurany: I am very glad that you are taking all precaution and all care for the poor girl, whose health is very deteriorated. And you first of all see that she is quite strong enough, that she has improved health; then there can be a Krishna Conscious baby. Otherwise, forget this. Better you and Jadurany become Krishna Conscious children directly than producing a Krishna Conscious baby at the present moment.

Hope this will find you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

P.S. Most probably we shall have our secretarial office in U.N. Church by the next month. I shall require some nice paintings for this important office.

I later wrote a *Back to Godhead* article based on the text of this letter from Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Do you remember 1968 when churches offered “sanctuary” to draft resisters? I was in Boston at that time, and there it was big news for a while, part of the protest against the Vietnam War. Every few days you’d see in the headlines that yet another young man was telling the government, “I won’t go!” by going into a church. The church officials would refuse to allow federal authorities to enter and arrest him, and for a few days the tension mounted. People would throng to the church to hold mass meetings and voice their Christian anti-war sentiments, and at last federal police would move in and, after a scuffle, take the man away.

As a member of the Boston center of the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement, I attended one such meeting at Boston University’s campus chapel. Despite government threats, the protesters were sheltering a draft resister and holding a twenty-four-hour public prayer meeting. The church was crowded and noisy. Several people in the audience carried large signs with political slogans or verses from the Bible. I had hoped to speak about Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but I couldn’t break through the crowd of politicians, ministers, and monitors surrounding the pulpit. Speaker after speaker denounced the imminent invasion of the church by federal agents. A minister quoted verses and explained Christian duty. A little bewildered by the intense mixture of political rhetoric and Biblical quotes, and unsure what was the proper course of action for me, I returned to our center and wrote a query to our spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda. His reply (dated October 9, 1968) arrived a few days later. I have always considered Śrīla Prabhupāda’s analysis of the “sanctuary” situation a perfect response by a fully God conscious person. It showed me how to react not just to that political struggle but to the many others since then.

Now, ten years later, the “sanctuary” protest is all but forgotten. There are new political struggles, new allies and enemies. But I think Śrīla Prabhupāda’s analysis is still the devastating truth. Unfortunately, the world’s leaders are not listening to his advice. Most political and military leaders are completely unaware of the laws of karma—action and reaction—that Śrīla Prabhupāda and the Vedic literatures describe, but they should know that such laws actually exist—as surely as they know of nature’s other unbreakable laws.

My BTG article went on to define *karma* and how it works individually and collectively: people must pay the karmic toll for their sinful acts of abortion and cow slaughter by suffering natural disasters, war, and horrible deaths. Neither through diplomacy nor “prayer” can one avoid reaction to sinful life, but “morality and peace will come naturally to us only when we become Krishna Conscious and awaken to the higher taste of spiritual pleasure.”

I was especially struck by Prabhupāda’s example of the burglar who upon being captured by the police prayed to the government to stop his punishment. I pictured a thief running down the street pursued by police: when the thief saw that he was going to be caught, he stopped voluntarily , turned around, and offered to return the stolen goods in exchange for a full pardon. This is ludicrous, and yet Prabhupāda said that those who make prayers to petition God for peace, while on the other hand continuing to accumulate heavy karma were just as ludicrous.

I also appreciated how strongly Prabhupāda exposed that both left-wing and right-wing political adherents are in complete agreement with each other because they mutually break God’s laws against illicit sex, intoxication, meat-eating, and gambling. Acting in the theater of *māyā,* the students who took shelter in the churches and the government forces that came to arrest them appeared to be at complete odds with one another, locked in conflict; yet concerning the issues that really make a difference in life, these so-called opposing parties were arm-in-arm like friends in their disdain for God's laws. “Because if it were a question of meat-eating or supporting the slaughterhouses, both of them would agree.”

In the material world, our political position is of crucial importance. Which side we are on in local, national, and international politics determines the actual quality of our life, but Prabhupāda saw through this illusion. The passionate politicians are like the *brāhmaṇa* who argued that dry stool is good whereas wet stool is bad. He overlooked the reality: both dry and wet stool are stool. The real distinction among human beings, therefore, is whether we act as devotees of Kṛṣṇa and follow His injunctions, or whether we transgress them. As stated in the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam:*

*yasyāsti bhaktir bhagavaty akiñcanā*  
*sarvair guṇais tatra samāsate surāḥ*  
*harāv abhaktasya kuto mahad-guṇā*  
*manorathenāsati dhāvato bahiḥ*

**All the demigods and their exalted qualities, such as religion, knowledge, and renunciation become manifest in the body of one who has developed unalloyed devotion for the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Vāsudeva. On the other hand, a person devoid of devotional service and engaged in material activities has no good qualities. Even if he is adept at the practice of mystic yoga or the honest endeavor of maintaining his family and relatives, he must be driven by his own mental speculations and must engage in the service of the Lord’s external energy. How can there be any good qualities in such a man? —Bhāg.** [**5.18.12**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-5/18.12)

Although Kṛṣṇa conscious politics does exist (formerly engaged in by *rājarṣis* and Kṛṣṇa Himself in the role of a *kṣatriya* king), nevertheless, in this letter Prabhupāda stressed, “We do not wish to take part directly in controversial politics.” The devotees’ political stand should be upon something sanctioned by Kṛṣṇa. They should not take either side in mundane issues in which both sides are based on illusion.

After reading my letter, Prabhupāda advised us that the situation was not favorable for our participation. He himself would not take part “in some political agitation.” He advised, “If there is possibility of selling our *Back to Godheads*, that is very nice.” On his order, as his disciple, I approached the scenes of political conflict and distributed Prabhupāda’s magazines to all persons. The Kṛṣṇa conscious literature is relevant to those in conflict, but they have to understand it from the transcendental level.

## November 1968

November 1, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of October 28, 1968, along with Jadurany’s. There is some difficulty at our temple here; therefore, my return address is given to my residential apartment, as above. So you can write me letters to this address. I hope the enclosed statement of certificate of your being an ordained Brahmin will help you.

As mentioned in the letter, you will be pleased to know that I am now admitted as ordained minister of religion in the U.S.A. Now there is no difficulty with my staying in your country, and if the students in different universities and colleges want to hear from me about the *Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, Bhagavad Gita As It Is,* and *Srimad Bhagavatam*, I shall be glad to accept such an invitation, provided such students pay reasonable contributions. I am getting gradually older; therefore, even if I remain in your country I shall restrain my moving, and my main business will be henceforward to train all of you, my disciples, for preaching work. I think you will approve of my program. Hope this meets you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter, Prabhupāda states that as he is getting older, his disciples should take on more of the preaching work. Of course, we all tried to do this, but it was not to be. Śrīla Prabhupāda never gave up active preaching. He felt he had to stay on the front lines, partly because he found us unqualified to manage things without him, but also because his spirit to fight on behalf of Lord Caitanya Mahāprabhu was irrepressible. However, he felt that at least he shouldn’t have to do cheap engagements, where sometimes people didn’t show up or where the audience wasn’t interested. It was a sign of respectable intent when a group invited and paid him. Prabhupāda continued to travel and lecture, but he wanted us to take over some of the active preaching.

Along with this November 1 letter, there is a second letter to Jadurāṇī that shows Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expertise in dealing with both his husband and wife disciples. A disciple’s first allegiance is to guru, not to the spouse. Sometimes a spouse may want the spiritual master to support his or her position in a marital disputes. Jadurāṇī did not like me to restrict her, especially when I tried to modify her behavior to help her recover from illness. Here Śrīla Prabhupāda assures her of both her ultimate relationship with him and freedom from encumbrances from her restricting husband. Yet he tells her to deal submissively with her husband:

I confirm herewith my statement that the wife is the shareholder of the husband’s pious activities. So you should know it well that Sriman Satsvarupa, his life being fully dedicated to Krishna, whatever he desires you to act, you should kindly follow his instructions. Actually, he cannot check you in the matter of your service either to Krishna or to me, but because we have to work with our present body, we must take care of it by following the ordinary rules.

\* \* \*

November 14, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for your letter of November 9, 1968, and I have noted the contents carefully. It is good to hear that the temple is going on nicely and you are selling *Back to Godheads* also, 400 per month. I understand you are trying to get more lecture engagements at colleges, also in sociology and political classes and philosophy classes. You should not give political or social discussions; while speaking on Krishna Conscious philosophy from *Bhagavad Gita* and *Srimad Bhagavatam,* you can simply touch on some socio-political implications. Krishna Consciousness is so broad and all-inclusive that naturally when speaking in Krishna Consciousness we get so many opportunities to deal in politics, although that is not our subject matter. To do so requires expert handling. You can take part in such classes and present the subject matter in such a way that it may first appear like socio-political-philosophical affairs but actually introduce our Krishna Consciousness philosophy. That requires tactful handling. You can give it a political, etc. heading and then end it with our philosophy.

*Visokumbhan Payomuckhan.* That means to give somebody a jar of poison with a label of nectarean or milk preparation on it. So we have to become politicians in this way, just like in old politics there was the presentations of *Visokanya.* This *Visokanya* is a poisonous, beautiful girl. The idea was that a baby girl was from the beginning administered little doses of poison every day which she could tolerate. They gradually increased the doses, culminating in large doses when she was a youth. Such a girl was engaged in killing enemies. The enemy would be received as a guest, and this girl would be engaged for looking after his comfort, and when they were friendly, as soon as the enemy kisses the girl in the mouth, the poisonous effect immediately has effect, because with her tongue the poison is very quickly effective. And the enemy would die. Similarly, we have to introduce Krishna Consciousness in such a way that through politics and sociology the people will be injected and become Krishna Conscious. But instead of being a victim, they will be immortalized. That is the difference.

The story of Ramanujacharya, as stated by you, is not one of disobedience. It was a great fervor for the disciple to advance the missionary cause of the spiritual master. Such rare instances are sometimes visible in many places. Just like there is one instance in Chaitanya Mahaprabhu’s life, that His personal servitor, Govinda, crossed Him while Chaitanya Mahaprabhu was lying on the floor. The story is like this: Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, after taking His lunch, would rest for a while, and Govinda would daily massage His feet. So one day Chaitanya Mahaprabhu fell asleep very soon and kept His head on the threshold of the room.

So while He was sleeping, Govinda came and crossed the body of Chaitanya and massaged His feet. He sat there, and when Chaitanya Mahaprabhu awoke, He saw Govinda was sitting there, although it was late. So He inquired, “Govinda, you have not taken your prasadam? Why are you sitting here?”

So he replied, “Yes, I shall now go to take prasadam. I could not go on the other side because You were lying on the threshold.”

Chaitanya Mahaprabhu said, “How did you come in?”

He flatly replied, “By crossing You.”
