Then Chaitanya inquired, “Why did you not go out by also crossing Me?”

Govinda said, “That I cannot do. I crossed You to come inside the room for Your service, but I cannot go out crossing You to take my lunch; that would be for my sense gratification.”

The idea is for service of the superior, sometimes such violation appears to be in the scene. Ramanujacharya violated the order of the spiritual master to advance the cause of the spiritual master. The spiritual master’s mission was to deliver the fallen souls; therefore, Ramanujacharya considered that if the mantra was so powerful to deliver all persons, why not deliver it to everyone as soon as possible. He used tactics to advance the cause of the spiritual master. So everything has to be judged by the motive, and as the motive of such apparent violation was very good, Ramanuja’s spiritual master embraced him. This is not actually violating the order of the spiritual master. But such actions are to be done very rarely.

Yes, there is definitely a vast difference between initiated and non-initiated. One who is initiated is authorized, and one who is not initiated is not authorized. Just like, for example, Pradyumna’s attending class in Sanskrit in a college. He is given a chance to learn Sanskrit, but he is not equal with the regular students. One who becomes initiated is channelized to the authorities in disciplic succession. One who isn’t initiated may chant Hare Krishna (and should certainly be encouraged to do so) and serve in his own way; otherwise, he may fall away from following the rules and regulations.

The clothing is already painted on the Jagannathas, even if there is no cloth. And even if Krishna is naked, that does not make any difference. But when we dress Him, our service is appreciated. From Krishna’s side, naked or dressed, He is Krishna. And from our side, if we nicely dress Krishna and nicely feed Him, He is appreciating our service, and we are certainly benefited. Krishna is the same; He does not require us to dress Him or feed Him, etc., but the more I serve by dressing, feeding, and caring for Him, I will become more Krishna Conscious, as He appreciates the service.

Yes, it is all right to continue painting Lord Chaitanya and the Gopis as you have been, in flesh tone.

It is not mentioned how many hands the different Brahmas from the different universes have. Maybe four hands. Yes, they ride on swans.

I am sending herewith a tape, so you do the work on your dictaphone, and send me regularly the typed copies. Don’t stop sending them; it makes me stop also. Unless I get back the typed copies, it doesn’t encourage me. So you send me the typed copies regularly, and I shall send you the tapes regularly.

What about the Third Canto? I know that I have done up to the Fourth Canto, Seventh Chapter, and the present tape is a continuation, but I am in darkness about where the papers are. So give me an account of where the papers are.

I am concerned about Jadurany’s health condition—has she got any fever? If she has a feverish condition, she should simply lie down and do no work.

Hope you are well, and please keep me informed of your temple affairs, as well as about Jadurany’s health condition.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

P.S. I have received the Indian letter sent by you.

Here Prabhupāda takes considerable time to explain how we should present Kṛṣṇa consciousness in a university setting. I had written Prabhupāda that we were trying to give lectures in sociology, political science, and philosophy classes. Sometimes college professors are willing to invite guest lecturers into their classrooms. Over the years I have given many college lectures as a devotee, and my practice began in 1968 under the culturing of Śrīla Prabhupāda. I soon learned that college professors were often willing to have the devotees speak in classes particularly relevant to their syllabus. Therefore, we were invited to speak in classes on “new religions” or Hinduism, or Indian religion, or Indian philosophy. However, I thought it would be better if we spoke in other classes as well, since the philosophy of the Absolute Truth, Kṛṣṇa consciousness, is needed and is applicable everywhere.

In trying to present Kṛṣṇa consciousness in these wider areas, there are at least two difficulties: (1) To secure a speaking engagement with a professor who really doesn’t want anything to do with Kṛṣṇa consciousness, a devotee sometimes misrepresents himself, claiming he will give a mundane talk on Indian culture. If a devotee avoids informing the professor that he will talk about Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and if he then speaks on *Bhagavad-gītā* with no attempt to relate it to the subject of the college course, the professor will feel the devotee has misrepresented himself and has cheated. As a result, the professor will never have a devotee speak in his class again. (2) To please a professor or an audience of students without raising their fears that they will be proselytized, a devotee may speak a watered-down version of Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy. However, speaking about only mundane Indian sociology, vaguely introducing spiritual topics such as “goodness,” or even giving a lecture that has deteriorated to presenting impersonal philosophy of self-realization without mentioning Kṛṣṇa—are all worthless.

Here Śrīla Prabhupāda gives advice that first of all “you should not give political or social discussions; while speaking on Krishna Consciousness philosophy from *Bhagavad Gita* and *Srimad Bhagavatam*, you can simply touch on some socio-political implications.” Any presentation of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, written or spoken, should be strictly according to the *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. From the objective position of presenting *śāstra*, we can comment on other subjects, such as those taught in the university. But we must always speak based on *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. Then we can discuss the *Bhāgavatam’s* version of sociology *(varnāśrama dharma*), politics *(īśāvāsya*), and philosophy. (“Religion without philosophy is sentimental and sometimes fanatical, but philosophy without religion is only dry mental speculation.”)

How do you attract students without inciting their prejudices while you simultaneously avoid overreacting to the pressure of a mundane audience, which could make you pander to them or avoid presenting the clear conclusions of Kṛṣṇa consciousness? The answer to these questions is to “label the jar of poison.” The “poison” is what they don’t want to hear, Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Vedic literature has much to say about many subjects. Kṛṣṇa is everything; Kṛṣṇa consciousness has a jurisdiction over all subjects of knowledge. You only have to present a subject with some expertise. By introducing a talk as “Systems of Acquiring Knowledge,” you may give a lecture on the relative merits of the epistemologies of direct perception, theory, and hearing from authority as given in the Sanskrit *Vedas.* Or you may talk in a sociology class on “The Goals of Human Society.” You could also give a talk to feminists on the role of women according to the *Vedas.* You will satisfy your audience’s keen interest in hearing about women; at the same time, you can present the philosophy without watering it down at all. There are glorious histories in the Vedic literatures of valiant women, and the place of women in the Vedic society is exalted, but the real conclusion (although different from what feminists in your audience would expect) will ultimately satisfy them.

You can make a presentation in a sociology class on “The Importance of the Family,” or you could give a talk in political science on “Which Political System is Best?” The title of your talk should contain something that appears mundane, which the ordinary audience is hankering for. When a devotee speaks, the heart of his presentation is Kṛṣṇa conscious *siddhānta;* but he presents it in the context of sociology (or as Śrīla Prabhupāda writes, “While speaking on Krishna Consciousness philosophy from *Bhagavad Gita* and *Srimad Bhagavatam,* you can simply touch on some socio-political implications.”)

We should never avoid the conclusion of Kṛṣṇa consciousness—*sarva-dharmān parityajya,* “Give up all other religions and surrender to Kṛṣṇa.” If we do not mention the holy name or conclude that surrender to Kṛṣṇa is a principle above all religions, what is the use of writing or speaking? There are already thousands of professors and books vaguely describing God in a mundane way. We don’t want to contribute our own to them.

In this letter Prabhupāda tells how the *viṣakanyā* girls poisoned their political enemies. He comments, “Similarly, we have to introduce Krishna Consciousness in such a way that through politics and sociology \[the students\] will be injected and become Krishna Conscious. But instead of being a victim, they will be immortalized. That is the difference.”

In this letter, Prabhupāda also told an account of Rāmānuja, who transgressed the etiquette toward his spiritual master, Yamunācārya. Rāmānuja appeared to directly disobey his spiritual master, but he was actually motivated by the desire to carry out the essence of his guru's order. I had heard, vague second hand stories about these pastimes, so I asked Prabhupāda for the actual story. We should not be content to hear second or third hand accounts of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes or explanations of the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy, but should have them corroborated by our spiritual master. There are millions of Vedic stories, and our spiritual master may not wish to tell all of them in his presentation. We are on the safe side if we repeat only the stories he has told.

Here Prabhupāda also relates how Govinda, Lord Caitanya's servant, stepped over the Lord’s body to serve Him, but refused to cross His body a second time for his own sense gratification. According to Vaiṣṇava etiquette, stepping over the body of a devotee is an offense, but Govinda abandoned that etiquette to serve the Lord.

Once, Prabhupāda was seated at his desk in his room at 26 Second Avenue. One of his disciples was adjusting a picture on the wall near his head. To hang the picture, the disciple wanted to step on Prabhupāda's sitting mat, but the disciple first asked, “Swamijī, may I stand on this rug?”

Prabhupāda replied, “To serve me, you can step on my head if necessary.” However, to cross the body of the spiritual master or to step on his seat or body would be completely offensive if done to further our sense gratification.

Prabhupāda also related an account of how Yamunācārya once gave Rāmānuja a secret mantra. Rāmānuja knew that his spiritual master wanted to deliver *all* fallen souls, and he therefore delivered the mantra to the public in the marketplace. On other occasions, Prabhupāda told us more about this story. He said Yamunācārya later reprimanded Rāmānuja, “Do you know that for disobeying my order and spreading the mantra I gave you, you could go to hell?”

Rāmānuja replied, “I am prepared to go to hell, but I wanted to spread your mission.” By Rāmānuja’s saying, “I am prepared to go to hell,” we can understand that very rarely should a disciple attempt to transgress the injunctions of *śāstra* or the guru's orders unless he is willing to risk endangering his own spiritual life. Similarly, the *śāstras* advise the guru not to accept many disciples as he may suffer a reaction. When a guru takes on many disciples despite the injunction, he does so at personal risk.

Similarly, the *gopīs* gave the dust of their feet when Nārada told them by doing this they would cure Kṛṣṇa’s headache. When Nārada saw the *gopīs* freely offer their foot dust although other devotees had refused, he questioned them. “Don’t you know,” Nārada asked, “that giving the dust of your feet is considered offensive and you could go to hell?” The *gopīs* replied they were prepared to go to hell if they could relieve Kṛṣṇa’s headache. Kṛṣṇa was their only concern.

These stories of devotees’ “transgressions” cannot be cited to support our own disobedience to the spiritual master.

I had also asked Prabhupāda about the difference between initiated and uninitiated devotes. The example he gave in this letter has become a classic response to this question. Prabhupāda stresses that initiation makes us “channelized to the authorities in the disciplic succession.” Sometimes we are asked about the benefits of hearing from authorities, or chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa, or following the guru's orders *prior* to initiation. The answer is that prior activities *(puraścaryā*) are certainly important, as they form the basis for qualifying ourselves for becoming initiated. At the time we determine in our heart that we will follow the spiritual master’s orders, accepting the spiritual master as our own eternal guru, then our initiation is sure to take place. Actual *dīkṣā,* however, which is a consummation of the prior following, must take place. A boy may be attracted to a girl and even request her hand in marriage, but the consummation of their relationship occurs when they are formally married. Initiation means to begin. By *dīkṣā* we are linked to our spiritual master and can begin becoming an authorized representative of *paramparā.*

I often repeat the statements Prabhupāda made near the end of this letter. He asked me to keep him informed about the work I was doing for him. I was regularly receiving tapes from Prabhupāda and typing them into manuscripts. However, Prabhupāda criticized me for not regularly sending him the typed transcripts. Sometimes I accumulated many pages before sending them to Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda’s statements always remind me that the relationship with the spiritual master is one of personal reciprocation. Disciples sometimes wonder whether the spiritual master can read their minds. Here, however, we see that the relationship between the spiritual master and disciple is not based on mind-reading. If Prabhupāda were to have employed such mystic techniques, he could have already seen or acquired the manuscript pages. Rather he said, “I am in darkness. Where are the papers?”

We should feel the pressure to inform our spiritual master of pertinent matters and to inquire from him about our service. We should never think, “Oh, he is a liberated person, so I don’t have to tell him. He’ll know through Kṛṣṇa in the heart.” Out of love, the disciple wants to reveal his mind and does not remain passive, thinking that the spiritual master can get knowledge by reading his disciples’ minds. This shows how Prabhupāda also asked for reciprocation to remain enthusiastic in his immediate writing projects. “Don’t stop sending them,” he wrote, referring to the manuscript pages. “It makes me stop also. So give me an account where the papers are.”

\* \* \*

November 18, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated November 16, 1968, and it is very encouraging that Devananda is combating Mr. Jarvis, who is representing Maharishi Mahesh. Yes, in each and every such meeting you should go and challenge these persons, but you must be equipped very strongly with the conclusions of the *Bhagavad Gita.* In the *Bhagavad Gita* it is clearly said that the first class yogi is he who has in his heart always the eternal form of Krishna. I could not follow what Devananda said to the Jerry Jarvis when Devananda said that Mr. Jarvis was also worshiping Krishna. It is very good to argue with him, even at the suspension of meetings, and it shows very good enthusiasm.

Your question: “Is it to be understood that once the yogi sees the Narayana form, his desires for travel and material perfections cease (as did the desires of Dhruva Maharaj)? Or do the yogis still persist in desiring to do wonders even after seeing the Narayana form? You say in your essay ‘Super Consciousness’ that the topmost perfection is to see Narayana’s form and take dictation from Him. In short, the question is, is it true that the yogi who is traveling to planets and performing siddhis has never seen Narayana?”

Dhruva Maharaj was a devotee. The yogis are not devotees. They are, more or less, after some material perfection. But generally, one who sees Narayana, he becomes a devotee. And sometimes such devotees, before entering the supreme abode of Krishna or Narayana, they want to see some of the wonders of this material world. Just like I have come to your country, and I have no interest for sight-seeing, but my main business is preaching Krishna Consciousness; but still, when I go to a new place, sometimes devotees take me to some important sight-seeing place. Similarly, a yogi after becoming a devotee sometimes goes to other planets also, and Narayana fulfills such material desires. In other words, a devotee, even if he wants to fulfill some material desires, he is given the chance by the Lord; but a pure devotee, who is in a higher status, does not desire like that. A pure devotee does not care for any siddhis.

I am glad to learn that Jadurany is feeling a little better, and I hope you will take proper care for the girl. I understand that Govinda dasi has recommended Jadurany to take iron pills, and almost all the girls here are taking them. It is compensating the deficiency due to their past habit of meat-taking. This is a good recommendation.

Hope this meets you well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda described that in less than a second we can go to Vaikuṇṭha. We don’t have to take a slow train on which the conductor announces, “Now passing Janaloka! Take a look outside! Anyone want to get off?” We don’t have to become anxious, “Oh no, I hope I don’t get attracted to this place!” When we leave our bodies, we can go at once to Kṛṣṇa. Some *yogīs,* however, even while in their bodies, develop powers to travel to other planets. Looking at different places they consider, “This is a fabulous place. I’ve always wanted to see what the demigods on Mount Meru are like.” Even though liberated and eligible to go back to Godhead, some devotees still want to see the varieties in the material universe;. The pure devotee doesn’t want to visit other planets. He surrenders exclusively to Kṛṣṇa and he goes to Kṛṣṇa.

The devotee goes past all higher planets, through all subtle existences—through the layers of earth, water (which is ten times thicker than the layer of earth) fire (which is ten times thicker than water), air, ether, and so on (each layer ten times thicker than the preceding one), Crosses the Virajā River, through the Brahmajyoti, past the Vaikuṇṭhas , and stops only at the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa in Goloka Vṛndāvana.

\* \* \*

November 22, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of November 20, 1968, along with the first tape of this time. The second tape is already sent to you, and the third tape is enclosed herewith; please find. When typing please mention the tape number. I hope to send you new tapes regularly, and I hope you will do the needful.

I understand that the entire Third Canto and Fourth Canto up to date are with you. Please keep them with you, and let me know when they are ready. Most probably we shall start our own press very soon. And as soon as the press is started, we shall immediately begin printing the *Bhagavatam* and other books.

Please let me know if Devananda has any binding experience and whether he can teach some boys here to bind nicely. Unless I am certain that everything in the press department will be done by ourselves, I am not inclined to start the press. I have duly received the copies of prospectus sent by you.

Thanking you ever more.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

By December of 1968, Prabhupāda suddenly began to write at a doubled rate, compiling two books at once. He thought both books would be extremely important for setting the foundation of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. One book was *Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead,* a translation of and commentary on the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. Prabhupāda wanted the book to contain colorful paintings, and he turned to the two or three devotee artists among his disciples. He issued a kind of challenge to them that as fast as they painted, he would write:

As soon as possible, I will require some artist who will paint pictures from the *Bhagavatam* as I give hints on what to paint. But the artist must be very quick. Two or three pictures must be done every week. These pictures will be used for my new book *Kṛṣṇa,* which I will begin as soon as I get the assistance of a quick painter. The Tenth Canto contains forty chapters about Krishna in Vrindaban and fifty chapters about Krishna in Dvaraka. So our book will most likely have in the first volume forty chapters of Krishna in Vrindaban, with one picture for each chapter.

Prabhupāda described the subject he wanted for each of the first five illustrations. For the first picture, pregnant Devakī, the mother of Kṛṣṇa, should be shown sitting in the palace. Near the ceiling of the room, the demigods should appear surrounding her and praying for the Lord's appearance. For the second picture, he wanted Devakī displayed sitting in a different place in the palace room, and Lord Viṣṇu, in His four-armed form and yellow dress, to appear before her Devakī and Vasudeva should be shown bowing down before Lord Viṣṇu. The third illustration should show baby Kṛṣṇa lying happily on Devakī's lap just like an ordinary child. Prabhupāda instructed that the action in the fourth painting should be Vasudeva carrying baby Kṛṣṇa in his arms across the Yamunā River. The fifth painting should show Vasudeva coming to the house of Nanda Mahārāja in Vṛndāvana, where he exchanged Kṛṣṇa for the new born child of Yaśodā.

In *Prabhupāda-līlā* I describe how Śrīla Prabhupāda wrote *Kṛṣṇa* book and *Nectar of Devotion* simultaneously, wherever he was:

Day after day he went deeply into the mellow of the Vaiṣṇava literature, rendering the learned Sanskrit poetry of the ancient Vyāsadeva and the realizations of Rūpa Gosvāmī into language comprehensible to the modern English reader. Only externally and superficially, his life seemed less eventful, as he was not going out much or seeing his disciples as much. But he was feeling full urgency and satisfaction that he was, by Kṛṣṇa’s grace, now making the most important contribution. Sitting in a simple room in a small Los Angeles suburban house, he was gradually delivering the books that he knew would form the basis for a movement that could go on for thousands of years. He sensed the victory of Vedic enlightenment over the darkness of the age.

Years later, Śrīla Prabhupāda described in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* why he wrote the *Kṛṣṇa* book even though he was in the process of translating the Fourth Canto:

Following in the footsteps of Śrīla Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī, I am trying to translate the *Bhāgavata* literatures as quickly as possible. However, knowing myself to be an old man and almost an invalid because of rheumatism, I have already translated the essence of all literatures, the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, as the summary study in English. I started the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement at the age of seventy. Now I am seventy-eight, and so my death is imminent. I am trying to finish the translation of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* as soon as possible, but before finishing it, I have given my readers the book *Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Personality of Godhead,* so that if I die before finishing the whole task, they may enjoy this book, which is the essence of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*.

As the humble, blissful receiver of the first *Kṛṣṇa* book tapes, I happily began reciting *Kṛṣṇa* book pastimes in my morning classes to the few devotees gathered in our Boston storefront. For a brief time, I was the only devotee in our movement who knew these stories; before the books and manuscripts were available, the devotees first heard Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes from my reciting what Prabhupāda had dictated. More than ever, I attempted to carefully repeat everything he had said, since it gave the hearers much pleasure. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s devotees did not know much about Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes until he began to describe them in *Kṛṣṇa* book.

Sometimes in his talks, Prabhupāda described Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, but not in such detail and sustained literary expression as in the *Kṛṣṇa* book. This was the perfect way to hear about Kṛṣṇa. We were aware that many Indians and scholars of Indian religion knew these stories, and that they sometimes came to our temples and indulged in telling stories about Kṛṣṇa, but we never wanted to hear about Kṛṣṇa from them. We saw it as a test whether we could restrain ourselves from turning away from them or stopping them when they spoke stories about Kṛṣṇa we had not heard from Prabhupāda. We knew we had to hear about Kṛṣṇa from His pure devotee. The ordinary Hindu or nondevotee professor would tell a story of Kṛṣṇa without the conviction that *kṛṣṇa-kathā* was the literal Absolute Truth, and they could not realize or explain the science of Kṛṣṇa. Hearing about Kṛṣṇa from such persons could be dangerous:

*avaiṣṇava-mukhodgīrṇaṁ*  
*pūtaṁ hari-kathāmṛtam*  
*śravaṇaṁ naiva karavyaṁ*  
*sarpocchiṣṭam yathā payaḥ*

**One should not hear anything about Kṛṣṇa from a non-Vaiṣṇava. Milk touched by the lips of a serpent has poisonous effects; similarly, talks about Kṛṣṇa given by a non-Vaiṣṇava (no matter how great a scholar he might be) are also poisonous. —Padma Purāṇa**

*Kṛṣṇa-kathā* must be heard from Kṛṣṇa’s representative, who thoroughly understands how Kṛṣṇa is Bhagavān and how He declares Himself in *Bhagavad-gītā*: *mattaḥ parataraṁ nānyat,* “There is no truth higher than Me.” The authorized speaker or writer of *kṛṣṇa-kathā,* therefore, has to know Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes in the context of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, where they were spoken in an authorized manner by Śukadeva Gosvāmī to Mahārāja Parīkṣit.

All the proper conditions for hearing about Kṛṣṇa were fulfilled when Śrīla Prabhupāda began speaking the *Kṛṣṇa* book on tapes. The speaker, the audience, and the place of recitation—all become sanctified when Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes are properly delivered. I eagerly received and relished every one of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s expressions in his *Kṛṣṇa* book dictation. I began making copies of his dictation tapes so his original phrasing and voice would be retained.

These reminiscences are sweet, but the important thing is that now the world has *Kṛṣṇa* book, and it has been translated into many languages. Speaking the tapes in Los Angeles, Śrīla Prabhupāda intended to produce handsome volumes. The period when he delivered the *Kṛṣṇa* book month after month (starting from the date of this letter) is glorious.

Prabhupāda’s *Kṛṣṇa* book consists mostly of free paraphrasing of each verse of every chapter throughout the Tenth Canto, and this is accompanied by Prabhupāda’s philosophical explanations, woven artfully into the narration. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s books are like a transcendental horn of plenty delivering to the world the fruits of love of God in overflowing abundance. In his relatively few years on earth, Prabhupāda delivered a wealth of Kṛṣṇa conscious literature, making the otherwise impoverished world filled with voidism into a world of spiritual prosperity. In some ways his gift of *Kṛṣṇa* book was the most merciful presentation of all his books. Prabhupāda’s practical statement in this letter, “I hope to send you new tapes regularly,” is filled with auspicious meaning that benefited the whole world.

It is stated in many *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* verses that *kṛṣṇa-kathā* is the prime medicine for the people of Kali-yuga. With Prabhupāda’s personal statement, “I hope you will do the needful,” I received my call to duty to participate in a menial way in this glorious epoch.

In the rest of this letter, Prabhupāda remains concerned with his literary endeavors. The *Kṛṣṇa* book was just beginning; the Third Canto of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* was done, and the Fourth was on its way (although Prabhupāda was pausing in translating the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* to execute *Kṛṣṇa* book).

Here also, Prabhupāda expresses plans for his own press. Prabhupāda was intent on delivering Kṛṣṇa’s message. Just as he was in India with the first volumes of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, Prabhupāda was concerned not only with the writing and editing but also with the actual printing of his books—along with the distribution to the people. *Saṅkīrtana* was Prabhupāda’s chief method of delivering Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* describes the literary mission of Śrīla Vyāsadeva:

**The material miseries of the living entity, which are superfluous to him, can be directly mitigated by the linking process of devotional service. But the mass of people do not know this, and therefore the learned Vyāsadeva compiled this Vedic literature, which is in relation to the Supreme Truth. —Bhāg.** [**1.7.6**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/7.6)

The first speakers and audiences of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* carried Vyāsadeva’s concern for delivering the conditioned souls from repeated birth and death. When Mahārāja Parīkṣit inquired from Śukadeva, he asked him about the duty of a person about to die. Mahārāja Parīkṣit was inquiring not just for himself, but for all people, who are in the clutches of impending death. He wanted to spend his last days hearing Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes as told in the *Bhāgavatam*. Śukadeva Gosvāmī replied to Mahārāja Parīkṣit’s question: “My dear king, your question is glorious because it is very beneficial to all kinds of people. The answer to this question is the prime subject matter for hearing and is approved by all transcendentalists.”

Similarly, the sages at Naimiṣāraṇya performed their sacrifice of hearing the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* with a plan to benefit all people in Kali-yuga by distributing knowledge of Kṛṣṇa. When the sages requested the head speaker, Sūta Gosvāmī, to speak about Kṛṣṇa, Sūta Gosvāmī replied, “Your questions are worthy because they relate to Lord Kṛṣṇa and so are of relevance to the world’s welfare.” Rūpa Gosvāmī also advocated that Kṛṣṇa consciousness be spread widely to benefit the unfortunate people of the modern age, who although thinking themselves advanced because they enjoy technology and material comforts, are actually bereft in the worst sense, for they don’t know the goal of human life. Śrīla Prabhupāda prepared the Kṛṣṇa book tapes as well as his other literature exactly in *paramparā* from the previous *ācāryas* who delivered *kṛṣṇa-kathā* in writing. Prabhupāda was not an ordinary writer; he performed the highest humanitarian work. Prabhupāda did what no one else could do—he presented Kṛṣṇa's pastimes. Even Sanskrit or scholars of Vaiṣṇavism don’t know how to present Kṛṣṇa consciousness in Kali-yuga, nor do they have conviction in the effect of Kṛṣṇa’s words. Prabhupāda’s sincere disciples and grand disciples should assist him in his mission. Thus they will benefit themselves and everyone by distributing *Kṛṣṇa* book and profuse and authorized editions of *Bhagavad-gītā* and *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*.

## December 1968

December 31, 1968

My dear Satsvarupa,

Please accept my blessing. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter of December 27, 1968, along with one very beautiful picture of Jadurany’s. I am so pleased to see this wonderful portrait, and please convey my heartfelt thanks to Jadurany for her fine endeavors.

Regarding the article suggested by Rayrama, you are correct in your doubts that Radha-Krishna-leela should not be discussed in *Back to Godhead*. In the *Srimad Bhagavatam* there are so many philosophical discussions, and we should concentrate on these philosophical aspects. Otherwise, the less intelligent will surely understand Radha-Krishna-leela as simply nonsense boy-and-girl sex life. Hanuman Prasad Poddar and company are all mundane types, and they have no entrance into actual spiritual activities. Superficially they pose themselves as great devotees, but we can feel their pulse; ultimately they are impersonalists. We should not depend upon writings from such unauthorized sources. Strictly we should avoid publishing these confidential topics in *Back to Godhead*. This is dangerous for the conditioned soul.

Although such Krishna-leela can do some good to the mundane people in the long run, to understand the philosophical aspects of *Bhagavad Gita* and *Srimad Bhagavatam* in the beginning is essential. I do not know why Rayrama has asked you to send such an article. It is not to my sanction. We shall be very careful about mundane sex life. That is the pivot of conditioned life. You are intelligent enough, and I hope that Krishna will help you in these matters.

I hope you are well.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter Śrīla Prabhupāda forbids us to present confidential topics in *Back to Godhead*. It is interesting that he wrote this while writing Kṛṣṇa’s confidential pastimes in *Kṛṣṇa* book.

Throughout Śrīla Prabhupāda’s life, he was dedicated to delivering the *rāja-vidyā rāja-guhya,* the most confidential knowledge of the Absolute Truth—*kṛṣṇa-kathā.* Yet he was also dedicated to protesting the misuse of *kṛṣṇa-kathā* by Māyāvādīs or other materially motivated persons (professional *Bhāgavatam* reciters and *prākṛta-sahajiyās*).

In this letter Prabhupāda is concerned that unless persons who speak *kṛṣṇa-kathā* carefully present Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, those reciters can ruin the proper understanding of the hearers. Descriptions of the *rāsa* dance are especially not meant for the public. Śrīla Prabhupāda was preparing to translate *all* the chapters of the Tenth Canto in his *Kṛṣṇa* book, but he was compiling the book carefully and beautifully, always giving philosophical explanations so the reader would not be misled.

When we reads Prabhupāda’s *Kṛṣṇa* book, we must not mistake the pastimes of Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa for mundane love affairs between a young man and woman. The affairs of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa are on the highest level of spiritual reality, but when the spiritual world is reflected into the material world, the highest point becomes the lowest. In the perverted reflection (the material world), the love affairs of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa become the abominable affairs of illicit sex between man and woman in the mood of mundane enjoyment. The Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa pastimes of the *rāsa* dance are the highest technology of the science of Kṛṣṇa, and they can be understood only by the topmost devotees. Only *they* can deliver these pastimes to others. Innocent persons can benefit from hearing *kṛṣṇa-kathā* only from a Kṛṣṇa conscious master like Śrīla Prabhupāda, who knows the means of presenting topics about Kṛṣṇa according to time and place. If one reads all of Prabhupāda’s books—from *Bhagavad-gītā* through the *Bhāgavatam*—only then can he appreciate the *rāsa* dance.

In *Back to Godhead* we should present philosophical topics frequently discussed by the previous *ācāryas.* Śrīla Prabhupāda later told us how we should present in *Back to Godhead* the first two levels of transcendental realization: (1) Information to awaken people’s awareness of God. People have no information of God and are therefore sunk in the worst condition of ignorance. The first service, therefore, in delivering Kṛṣṇa consciousness to them is to awaken their knowledge of their relationship with God by philosophical presentations. There are many philosophical arguments and presentations made by the previous *ācāryas* that oppose the atheistic and impersonal version of reality. Before a person can understand anything in transcendental knowledge, he has to first understand that he is not his body, but spirit soul and an eternal servant of God.

(2) We must present information which instructs people in the activities of devotional service. When we acknowledge that Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Personality of Godhead and that we have a relationship with Him, then we are eligible to hear about the activities of devotional service—hearing, chanting, and the many practical ways we can engage our senses in the Lord's service under the spiritual master's direction.

The third and fourth levels of Kṛṣṇa consciousness—to hear Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes and to directly absorb ourselves in love of God—should not be prematurely presented in a magazine such as *Back to Godhead*, which is intended for wide distribution. As we see later in this letter, Prabhupāda does not take this transgression of introducing *rāsa-līlā* into *Back to Godhead* casually; rather, he is uncompromising: “It is not to my sanction.”

Prabhupāda gave me a charge of his own confidence by saying, “You are intelligent enough, and I hope that Krishna will help you in these matters.” We should not be overconfident or think we maturely understand Kṛṣṇa consciousness and how to deliver it to others. Rūpa Gosvāmī says we should never think of ourselves as great preachers, but should simply try to be an instrument in serving the previous *ācāryas;* but even serving as that instrument requires some confidence. If we have no confidence, we will hesitate and won’t be able to speak. Our confidence has to rest in faith in our spiritual master, the previous *ācāryas,* and the message we are delivering. However, by practicing and receiving encouragement from our spiritual master, we will be able to deliver the message properly and understand various intricacies, such as which topics of *kṛṣṇa-kathā* are suitable to a particular audience according to time and place.

Prabhupāda’s saying, “You are intelligent,” meant to me, “I benedict you with intelligence.” His stating that he hoped Kṛṣṇa would help me meant that I required help from Kṛṣṇa because I was prone to making the same mistakes that Prabhupāda mentions in his letter. Prabhupāda’s saying he hoped Kṛṣṇa would help me meant that with the good wishes of a pure devotee, surely Kṛṣṇa’s blessings would follow.

We have to remain vigilant, using our keen intelligence as far as possible to remember the instructions of our spiritual master, never discussing Kṛṣṇa conscious topics in a way he has not authorized. In this way, each of us is responsible for safeguarding the purity of the *paramparā*. Prabhupāda never spoiled his cornucopian distribution of the fruits of love of God by including in it anything inappropriate or tinged with the mundane.
